**A:** Five components, each scored 0 or 2 (max 10): 1) Fetal breathing movements (≥1 episode of ≥30 sec in 30 min), 2) Gross body movements (≥3 in 30 min), 3) Fetal tone (≥1 episode of flexion/extension), 4) Amniotic fluid volume (single deepest pocket ≥2 cm), 5) Reactive NST (≥2 accelerations in 20 min). Score 8–10 = reassuring; 6 = equivocal (repeat or deliver if ≥37 wks); ≤4 = abnormal (deliver).

The BPP assesses acute (breathing, movement, tone, NST) and chronic (amniotic fluid) fetal well-being. The SOGC recommends BPP for surveillance in high-risk pregnancies (IUGR, post-dates, diabetes). A modified BPP (NST + AFI only) is a common screening alternative due to shorter assessment time.

**Q:** A woman at 8 weeks gestation reports exposure to a cat litter box. Which TORCH infection is she at risk for, and what is the recommended workup?

**A:** Toxoplasmosis (from Toxoplasma gondii in cat feces). Workup: IgG and IgM serology. If IgM positive, confirm with IgG avidity testing (low avidity = recent infection). If acute infection confirmed, treat with spiramycin (to reduce vertical transmission) or pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine + folinic acid (if fetal infection confirmed).

Congenital toxoplasmosis can cause chorioretinitis, intracranial calcifications, and hydrocephalus. Risk of fetal transmission increases with gestational age but severity decreases. The SOGC recommends counselling pregnant women to avoid handling cat litter, consuming undercooked meat, and gardening without gloves.

**Q:** List the TORCH infections and the classic congenital findings associated with each.

**A:** T – Toxoplasmosis: chorioretinitis, diffuse intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus. O – Other (syphilis, VZV, parvovirus B19): syphilis → snuffles, Hutchinson teeth, saddle nose; parvovirus → hydrops fetalis. R – Rubella: cataracts, deafness, PDA, blueberry muffin rash. C – CMV: periventricular calcifications, sensorineural hearing loss, microcephaly (most common congenital infection). H – HSV: skin vesicles, encephalitis, disseminated disease.

CMV is the most common congenital infection in Canada, affecting 0.5–1% of newborns. Rubella has become rare due to MMR vaccination. The CPS recommends targeted CMV testing in neonates with compatible symptoms and universal hearing screening to detect CMV-related sensorineural hearing loss early.

**Q:** What is the non-stress test (NST) and how is a reactive result defined?

**A:** The NST monitors fetal heart rate in response to fetal movement. A reactive NST is defined as ≥2 accelerations of ≥15 bpm above baseline lasting ≥15 seconds within a 20-minute window (for gestational age ≥32 weeks). Before 32 weeks, accelerations of ≥10 bpm for ≥10 seconds are acceptable.

A reactive NST is reassuring and indicates intact fetal autonomic function. A non-reactive NST may be due to fetal sleep cycle (up to 40 minutes), prematurity, or fetal compromise. The SOGC recommends extending monitoring to 40 minutes or using vibroacoustic stimulation before classifying as non-reactive.

**Q:** During which period of embryonic development is the fetus most vulnerable to teratogens, and why?

**A:** The critical period is weeks 3–8 of embryonic development (weeks 5–10 gestational age), during organogenesis. Exposure during this period causes major structural malformations because organs are actively forming. Before week 3, the effect is typically all-or-none (miscarriage or no effect). After week 8, teratogens may cause functional defects or growth restriction but fewer structural anomalies.

This explains why early prenatal care and pre-conception counselling are essential. The SOGC recommends folic acid supplementation starting at least 3 months before conception (0.4–1 mg daily, or 4–5 mg if high risk) to prevent neural tube defects, which form during the critical period at weeks 3–4.

**Q:** A pregnant woman at 20 weeks has a positive quadruple screen for trisomy 21. She asks about definitive diagnostic testing. What are the options and their risks?

**A:** Amniocentesis (performed at 15–20+ weeks): aspiration of amniotic fluid for karyotype/microarray; procedure-related pregnancy loss risk approximately 0.1–0.3%. Chorionic villus sampling (CVS, performed at 10–13 weeks): biopsy of placental tissue; similar loss risk (~0.1–0.2%). Both provide definitive chromosomal diagnosis. CVS allows earlier diagnosis but carries a small risk of confined placental mosaicism.

The SOGC recommends that all women with positive screening results be offered genetic counselling and invasive diagnostic testing. NIPT can be used as a secondary screen to reduce unnecessary invasive procedures, but it remains a screening test, not a diagnostic one. Chromosomal microarray is now preferred over conventional karyotype for higher diagnostic yield.

**Q:** What prenatal genetic screening options are available in Canada?

**A:** First trimester: enhanced FTS (nuchal translucency US + PAPP-A + free beta-hCG, 11-14 weeks). Integrated Prenatal Screening (IPS): first trimester markers + second trimester quad screen (most sensitive non-invasive screen, ~85-90% detection for trisomy 21). NIPT (cell-free fetal DNA): >99% detection rate for trisomy 21, offered to all women, increasingly funded provincially.

SOGC recommends offering screening to all pregnant women regardless of age. NIPT is the most accurate screening test but does not replace diagnostic testing (amniocentesis/CVS) for confirmation. Provincial funding for NIPT varies — some fund for high-risk only, others universally. Genetic counselling should accompany all screening.

**Q:** What is Rh isoimmunization and how is it prevented?

**A:** Rh-negative mother develops anti-D antibodies after exposure to Rh-positive fetal blood, risking hemolytic disease of the fetus/newborn (HDFN) in subsequent pregnancies. Prevention: anti-D immunoglobulin (RhIG/WinRho) 300 mcg IM at 28 weeks and within 72 hours of delivery if newborn is Rh-positive. Also after any sensitizing event (amniocentesis, trauma, miscarriage, ectopic).

SOGC recommends routine anti-D prophylaxis at 28 weeks for all Rh-negative women. Kleihauer-Betke test quantifies fetal-maternal hemorrhage to determine if additional RhIG doses are needed (standard dose covers 30 mL fetal blood). Cell-free fetal DNA can now determine fetal Rh status non-invasively.

**Q:** What are the indications and protocol for Group B Streptococcus (GBS) prophylaxis in pregnancy?

**A:** Universal screening: GBS culture at 36-37 weeks (vaginal-rectal swab). Intrapartum IV penicillin G (5 million IU loading, then 2.5-3 million IU q4h) indicated for: positive GBS culture, GBS bacteriuria this pregnancy, previous infant with GBS disease, unknown status with risk factors (preterm <37 weeks, ROM >18 hours, intrapartum fever).

SOGC recommends universal culture-based screening over risk-based approach. GBS is the leading cause of neonatal early-onset sepsis. Penicillin is first-line; cefazolin for mild penicillin allergy; clindamycin or vancomycin for severe allergy (based on susceptibility). IAP reduces early-onset GBS disease by 80%.

**Q:** What are the components of the biophysical profile (BPP) and how is it scored?

**A:** 5 components, each scored 0 or 2 (max 10): fetal breathing movements (>=1 episode >=30 sec in 30 min), gross body movements (>=3 in 30 min), fetal tone (>=1 flexion/extension), amniotic fluid volume (single deepest pocket >=2 cm), and reactive NST. Score >=8 is reassuring, 6 is equivocal, <=4 is abnormal.

SOGC recommends BPP for fetal surveillance in high-risk pregnancies. The modified BPP (NST + amniotic fluid index) is a common alternative for routine surveillance. BPP <=4 generally warrants delivery depending on gestational age. Each component reflects different aspects of fetal CNS function and oxygenation.

**Q:** How is intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR/FGR) diagnosed and managed?

**A:** Diagnosis: estimated fetal weight <10th percentile on ultrasound. Classify as early-onset (<32 weeks, often placental insufficiency) vs late-onset (>=32 weeks). Management: serial growth US every 2-3 weeks, umbilical artery Doppler (absent/reversed end-diastolic flow is ominous), BPP, antenatal corticosteroids if preterm delivery anticipated, timing of delivery based on Doppler and gestational age.

SOGC defines FGR as EFW <10th percentile with abnormal Dopplers or <3rd percentile. Absent end-diastolic flow warrants delivery consideration at 34 weeks; reversed flow at 28-32 weeks after steroids. Ductus venosus Doppler is the best predictor of perinatal death in early-onset FGR. Underlying causes include preeclampsia, infection, chromosomal anomaly.

**Q:** What is a non-stress test (NST) and how is it interpreted?

**A:** External cardiotocography monitoring fetal heart rate and uterine activity for 20-40 minutes. Reactive (normal): >=2 accelerations (>=15 bpm above baseline, lasting >=15 seconds) in 20 minutes with normal baseline (110-160 bpm) and moderate variability. Non-reactive: does not meet criteria after 40 minutes — requires further evaluation (vibroacoustic stimulation, BPP, or extended monitoring).

SOGC recommends NST for fetal surveillance beginning at 32 weeks in high-risk pregnancies. Before 32 weeks, criteria are modified (10 bpm rise for 10 seconds). A reactive NST is reassuring for 1 week. A non-reactive NST does not always indicate compromise — fetal sleep cycle (20-40 min) is the most common cause. Decelerations on NST require further evaluation.

**Q:** What is the role of antenatal corticosteroids and at what gestational age are they given?

**A:** Betamethasone 12 mg IM x2 doses 24 hours apart (or dexamethasone 6 mg IM q12h x4). Given when preterm delivery anticipated at 24+0 to 34+6 weeks. Reduces neonatal RDS by 50%, IVH by 50%, NEC, and mortality. A single rescue course may be given if >14 days since first course and <34 weeks.

SOGC recommends antenatal corticosteroids for all women at risk of preterm delivery 24-34 weeks. Benefits are maximal 2-7 days after administration. Late preterm (34-36 weeks) steroid use is considered in some centres (ALPS trial) but is not universally recommended in Canada due to concerns about neonatal hypoglycemia.

**Q:** What are the first-trimester screening recommendations for preeclampsia?

**A:** SOGC recommends first-trimester screening (11-14 weeks) combining maternal risk factors, mean arterial pressure, uterine artery pulsatility index, and PAPP-A/PlGF. Women identified as high-risk should start low-dose aspirin (162 mg daily) before 16 weeks, continued until 36 weeks.

The ASPRE trial demonstrated that aspirin 150 mg/night starting before 16 weeks reduces preterm preeclampsia by 62% in screen-positive women. SOGC recommends aspirin for women with >=1 high-risk factor (prior preeclampsia, chronic HTN, diabetes, renal disease, autoimmune disease) or >=2 moderate risk factors.

**Q:** What routine prenatal blood tests are recommended at the first antenatal visit in Canada?

**A:** CBC, blood group and Rh type, antibody screen, rubella immunity, syphilis (RPR/VDRL), HIV (opt-out), hepatitis B surface antigen, urinalysis and culture, cervical cytology (if due), chlamydia/gonorrhea NAAT. Consider: hepatitis C (if risk factors), TSH (if symptoms or risk), hemoglobin electrophoresis (if at-risk ethnicity), varicella immunity.

SOGC recommends these investigations at the initial prenatal visit (ideally <12 weeks). Repeat antibody screen at 28 weeks for Rh-negative women. Repeat GC/chlamydia in third trimester if risk factors. Ontario Prenatal Record and equivalent provincial tools standardize documentation. Group B strep culture is done at 36-37 weeks.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for preeclampsia per SOGC?

**A:** New-onset hypertension (>=140/90) after 20 weeks gestation PLUS >=1 adverse condition: proteinuria (>=0.3 g/24h or PCR >=30), end-organ dysfunction (elevated creatinine, liver enzymes, low platelets <100, hemolysis), pulmonary edema, cerebral/visual symptoms, or adverse fetal conditions (FGR, oligohydramnios, abnormal Dopplers).

SOGC 2022 guidelines removed the requirement for proteinuria — preeclampsia can be diagnosed with hypertension + any end-organ dysfunction. Severe features include SBP >=160, symptoms (headache, visual changes, RUQ pain), HELLP syndrome, or eclampsia. sFlt-1/PlGF ratio is an emerging biomarker for prediction and diagnosis.

**Q:** What folic acid supplementation is recommended for low-risk women of childbearing age in Canada?

**A:** Low-risk women should take a daily oral multivitamin containing 0.4 mg (400 mcg) folic acid for at least 2-3 months before conception, throughout pregnancy, and 4-6 weeks postpartum (or as long as breastfeeding continues). They should also eat a diet rich in folate.

Folic acid supplementation prevents neural tube defects (NTDs). High-risk women (personal or partner history of NTD, previous NTD pregnancy) require 5 mg folic acid starting at least 3 months before conception through 12 weeks post-conception. Women with additional risk factors (poor medication compliance, teratogen exposure) also need 5 mg.

**Q:** What is high-risk folic acid supplementation for women planning pregnancy who are at increased risk of neural tube defects?

**A:** Women with personal history of NTD, previous NTD pregnancy, or male partner with personal NTD history or previous NTD pregnancy: 5 mg folic acid at least 3 months before conception through 12 weeks post-conception, then 0.4-1.0 mg until postpartum/end of breastfeeding.

The higher 5 mg dose (vs. 0.4 mg for low-risk) is required because the risk of NTD recurrence or occurrence is substantially elevated in these high-risk groups. Women on antiepileptic medications, those with diabetes, or those with poor medication compliance also warrant higher supplementation and counselling.

## Gynaecologic Conditions & Cancer

**Q:** What are the SOGC guidelines for cervical cancer screening (Pap test) in Canada?

**A:** Start at age 21 or 3 years after first sexual intercourse; Pap test every 3 years for ages 21-69; HPV primary testing every 5 years is being phased in — stop screening at age 69 (or after total hysterectomy for non-cancer indication)

SOGC/Canadian Cancer Society Cervical Cancer Screening Guidelines 2013: Pap testing every 3 years with normal results. HPV co-testing or primary HPV testing is being adopted in Canadian provinces for higher sensitivity. Women vaccinated against HPV should still have regular cervical screening.

**Q:** A 45-year-old woman has heavy menstrual bleeding for 6 months. She is nulliparous, obese, and has irregular cycles. What is the priority investigation?

**A:** Endometrial biopsy (pipelle) to exclude endometrial hyperplasia or carcinoma; also pelvic ultrasound and CBC

SOGC AUB (Abnormal Uterine Bleeding) guidelines: endometrial biopsy indicated for AUB in women ≥40, or in younger women with risk factors for endometrial cancer (obesity, PCOS, chronic anovulation, tamoxifen use, Lynch syndrome). Hysteroscopy with directed biopsy is gold standard.

**Q:** What is the PALM-COEIN classification of abnormal uterine bleeding?

**A:** Structural (PALM): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma (fibroids), Malignancy/hyperplasia; Non-structural (COEIN): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not classified

FIGO PALM-COEIN classification guides investigation and management of AUB. Most common structural cause: fibroids (leiomyomas). Ovulatory dysfunction (anovulation): the most common non-structural cause. Treatment based on underlying etiology.

**Q:** What is the treatment for uterine fibroids causing heavy menstrual bleeding?

**A:** Medical: tranexamic acid, NSAIDs, combined OCP, progestin IUD (levonorgestrel IUD); Procedural: uterine artery embolization (UAE), focused ultrasound; Surgical: myomectomy (fertility-preserving) or hysterectomy

Uterine fibroids (leiomyomas) are the most common benign pelvic tumors in women. Management depends on symptoms, fertility desires, and fibroid size/location. SOGC guidelines: LNG-IUS (Mirena) is highly effective for AUB from fibroids and adenomyosis.

**Q:** A 25-year-old woman has severe dysmenorrhea, deep dyspareunia, and pelvic pain. Ultrasound shows endometriomas bilaterally. Diagnosis and management?

**A:** Endometriosis; treat with combined OCP (first-line), progestins, GnRH agonists (leuprolide); surgery for severe cases or endometriomas; infertility treatment if planning pregnancy

SOGC endometriosis guidelines: medical treatment suppresses endometriosis but does not cure it; symptoms return when treatment is discontinued. Definitive diagnosis requires laparoscopy with biopsy. Endometriomas >4 cm: surgical drainage and ablation before IVF improves outcomes.

**Q:** What is polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS) and how is it diagnosed per Rotterdam criteria?

**A:** PCOS diagnosis requires 2 of 3: oligo/anovulation, clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism, polycystic ovaries on ultrasound (≥12 follicles 2-9mm per ovary OR ovarian volume ≥10 mL) — exclusion of other causes

PCOS is the most common endocrinopathy in women of reproductive age (5-10%). Associated with insulin resistance, metabolic syndrome, T2DM, and endometrial cancer risk. Management: lifestyle modification, metformin, combined OCP for menstrual regulation and hyperandrogenism.

**Q:** A 65-year-old postmenopausal woman has a pelvic mass, elevated CA-125, and ascites. What is the diagnosis until proven otherwise and the management?

**A:** Ovarian cancer until proven otherwise; management: surgical staging and debulking (cytoreduction) by gynecologic oncologist, followed by platinum-based chemotherapy (carboplatin + paclitaxel)

Ovarian cancer has 5-year survival of 30-40% because most are diagnosed at advanced stage. Risk factors: BRCA1/2, nulliparity, endometriosis, HRT. No effective screening test. OCP use reduces lifetime ovarian cancer risk by 30-50%.

**Q:** What is the management of cervical cancer based on stage?

**A:** Stage IA1: cone biopsy or simple hysterectomy; Stage IA2-IIA: radical hysterectomy + pelvic LN dissection (or chemoradiation); Stage IIB-IVA: concurrent chemoradiation (cisplatin + radiotherapy)

Cervical cancer is HPV-related in >99% of cases. The most common histology is squamous cell carcinoma (75%), adenocarcinoma (20%). Sentinel lymph node biopsy increasingly used for early-stage cervical cancer. Bevacizumab added to chemotherapy for recurrent/metastatic disease.

**Q:** What is the management of postmenopausal uterine bleeding?

**A:** Any postmenopausal bleeding requires immediate investigation with transvaginal ultrasound (endometrial thickness) and endometrial biopsy to exclude endometrial cancer — endometrial stripe >4mm warrants biopsy

SOGC and ACOG guidelines: endometrial cancer is the most common gynecological malignancy in Canada. Presenting symptom in 90%: postmenopausal bleeding. Endometrial biopsy (pipelle) should be performed regardless of ultrasound findings if clinical suspicion exists.

**Q:** What is menopause and when does it occur?

**A:** Menopause: permanent cessation of menstruation, diagnosed retrospectively after 12 consecutive months of amenorrhea in a woman ≥45 years without other cause; average age in Canada: 51 years

SOGC menopause guidelines: FSH >30 IU/L and low estradiol support diagnosis. Perimenopause: irregular cycles, vasomotor symptoms (hot flashes), sleep disturbance, mood changes. Premature ovarian insufficiency (POI): menopause before age 40.

**Q:** What are the indications and contraindications for hormone replacement therapy (HRT/MHT)?

**A:** Indications: vasomotor symptoms (hot flashes), genitourinary syndrome of menopause (GSM), osteoporosis prevention, POI (premature ovarian insufficiency); Contraindications: breast cancer, endometrial cancer, unexplained vaginal bleeding, active liver disease, VTE or stroke history

SOGC Menopause Guidelines: MHT has the best benefit-risk profile for healthy women <60 or within 10 years of menopause (timing hypothesis). Combined estrogen + progestogen for women with intact uterus (prevents endometrial hyperplasia). Estrogen alone for post-hysterectomy.

**Q:** What is pelvic organ prolapse (POP) and how is it managed?

**A:** POP: descent of pelvic organs through pelvic floor (cystocele, rectocele, uterine prolapse); classified by POP-Q system; management: pelvic floor physiotherapy, pessary, or surgical repair

SOGC Urogynaecology guidelines: risk factors for POP: vaginal delivery, obesity, constipation, heavy lifting, advancing age, family history. Pelvic floor physiotherapy is first-line for mild-moderate prolapse. Pessaries provide non-surgical support. Surgical repair for severe or symptomatic POP.

**Q:** What is the BRCA1/2 risk-reducing surgical strategy?

**A:** Risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy (RRSO) at 35-40 years (BRCA1) or 40-45 years (BRCA2) after childbearing — reduces ovarian cancer risk by 80-95%, breast cancer risk by 50%

SOGC hereditary gynecologic cancer guidelines: BRCA1/2 carriers should be referred to hereditary cancer programs. RRSO also induces surgical menopause — MHT until age 50 does not negate breast cancer risk reduction. Risk-reducing mastectomy (bilateral) reduces breast cancer risk by >90%.

**Q:** What are the symptoms and treatment of vulvovaginal candidiasis?

**A:** Vulval pruritus, burning, thick white cottage cheese discharge, dyspareunia; treat uncomplicated VVC with topical azole (clotrimazole, miconazole) or fluconazole 150 mg PO single dose

Most VVC is caused by Candida albicans. Recurrent VVC (≥4 episodes/year): investigate for underlying cause (diabetes, immunosuppression), treat with fluconazole weekly x6 months as maintenance. Azole-resistant Candida glabrata: intravaginal boric acid.

**Q:** What is the clinical presentation and treatment of ovarian torsion?

**A:** Ovarian torsion: sudden onset severe unilateral pelvic pain, nausea/vomiting, pelvic mass; Doppler ultrasound may show absent/reduced blood flow; emergency laparoscopy for detorsion — if viable, do not remove ovary

SOGC guidelines: ovarian torsion is a surgical emergency. Doppler absence does not confirm torsion (can have torsion with flow). Detorsion is preferred to oophorectomy — even if ovary appears necrotic, blood flow may return. Cystectomy at same time if ovarian cyst present.

**Q:** What is cervical cancer screening in Canada?

**A:** Age 25-69: Pap smear q3 years (or HPV primary screening q5 years where available). No screening <25 or >69 if adequate prior screening

CTFPHC 2013 recommends cytology. Several provinces transitioning to HPV primary screening. Colposcopy for HSIL or persistent LSIL. HPV vaccination reduces but does not eliminate need for screening.

**Q:** What is the Canadian cervical cancer screening guideline?

**A:** Age 25-69: cytology (Pap) every 3 years OR HPV primary screening every 5 years (where available). Start at age 25 (not at onset of sexual activity). Stop at 70 if adequate recent negative screening. Post-hysterectomy (with cervix removal for benign indication): stop screening. Immunocompromised: annual screening.

CTFPHC 2013 guidelines recommended starting screening at 25, which differs from some US guidelines that start at 21. The shift to HPV primary screening reflects evidence that HPV testing is more sensitive than cytology. Several Canadian provinces are transitioning to HPV-based screening programs.

**Q:** What is the management algorithm for abnormal cervical cytology?

**A:** ASCUS: HPV co-testing (if HPV negative, return to routine; if HPV positive, colposcopy) OR repeat cytology in 12 months. LSIL: colposcopy. HSIL: colposcopy + ECC. AGC: colposcopy + ECC + endometrial biopsy (if >=35 or abnormal bleeding). CIN1: observation (70% regress). CIN2: treat if persistent >2 years or excision. CIN3: excision (LEEP or cold knife cone).

SOGC colposcopy guidelines outline management algorithms. LEEP (loop electrosurgical excision procedure) is the most common treatment for CIN2/3 in Canada. CIN2 in women <25 may be observed due to high regression rates. HPV vaccination does not change screening recommendations.

**Q:** What is the approach to abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB)?

**A:** PALM-COEIN classification: Structural (PALM): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia. Non-structural (COEIN): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified. Workup: pregnancy test, CBC, TSH, pelvic US, endometrial biopsy (age >=40 or risk factors for hyperplasia). Management by cause: OCPs/IUS for ovulatory dysfunction, resection for polyps/fibroids, progestins for hyperplasia.

SOGC guidelines use the FIGO PALM-COEIN classification system. Endometrial biopsy is recommended for AUB in women >=40 years or <40 with risk factors (obesity, anovulation, tamoxifen). Levonorgestrel IUS (Mirena) is first-line medical treatment for HMB.

**Q:** What are the types of uterine fibroids and their management options?

**A:** Types (FIGO): submucosal (Type 0-2, cause heavy bleeding), intramural (Type 3-5), subserosal (Type 6-7, cause bulk symptoms). Medical: tranexamic acid, OCPs, LNG-IUS, GnRH agonists (preoperative shrinkage), ulipristal acetate. Surgical: myomectomy (fertility-preserving), hysterectomy (definitive), uterine artery embolization (UAE), MRI-guided focused US.

SOGC guidelines recommend medical management first-line for symptomatic fibroids. GnRH agonists cause temporary menopause (max 6 months due to bone loss). UAE is effective but not recommended for women desiring future fertility. Hysterectomy is the only definitive treatment.

**Q:** What is the workup and management of an adnexal mass?

**A:** Premenopausal: most are functional cysts (<6cm, simple-observe, repeat US in 6-8 weeks). Concern for malignancy: complex/solid, >6cm, ascites, elevated CA-125, postmenopausal. Postmenopausal: any adnexal mass warrants investigation. Tumor markers: CA-125 (epithelial ovarian), AFP/hCG/LDH (germ cell), inhibin (granulosa cell). Imaging: pelvic US first, CT/MRI for characterization.

SOGC guidelines and IOTA (International Ovarian Tumor Analysis) simple rules guide management. CA-125 is not specific (elevated in endometriosis, PID, pregnancy, peritoneal irritation). Postmenopausal women with complex mass and elevated CA-125 should be referred to gynecologic oncology. Risk of Malignancy Index (RMI) combines US, menopausal status, and CA-125.

**Q:** What are the causes and workup of secondary amenorrhea?

**A:** Definition: absence of menses for >=3 cycles or >=6 months. Most common cause: pregnancy (always test first). Other causes: hypothalamic (stress, weight loss, exercise), PCOS, thyroid dysfunction, hyperprolactinemia, premature ovarian insufficiency, Asherman syndrome, medication (OCPs, depot medroxyprogesterone). Workup: pregnancy test, TSH, prolactin, FSH, estradiol. Progestin challenge test.

SOGC guidelines recommend systematic evaluation. Elevated FSH (>40) suggests premature ovarian insufficiency (karyotype if <40 for Turner mosaicism). Elevated prolactin requires MRI sella for prolactinoma. Hypothalamic amenorrhea (low FSH, low estradiol) warrants bone density assessment due to estrogen deficiency.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS)?

**A:** Rotterdam criteria (>=2 of 3): (1) oligo/anovulation, (2) clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism, (3) polycystic ovaries on US (>=12 follicles 2-9mm in one ovary or ovarian volume >10mL). Exclude: thyroid disease, CAH (17-OHP), hyperprolactinemia, Cushing syndrome.

SOGC guidelines use Rotterdam criteria. PCOS affects 6-10% of reproductive-age women and is the most common cause of anovulatory infertility. Management: weight loss if overweight, OCPs for cycle regulation and hyperandrogenism, metformin for metabolic features, letrozole for ovulation induction (first-line over clomiphene).

**Q:** What is the approach to endometriosis diagnosis and management?

**A:** Clinical diagnosis: cyclic pelvic pain, dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia, dyschezia, infertility. Gold standard diagnosis: laparoscopy with histological confirmation. US may show endometriomas (chocolate cysts). Medical: NSAIDs, combined OCPs (continuous), progestins, GnRH agonists (with add-back therapy), dienogest. Surgical: laparoscopic excision/ablation of implants, adhesiolysis.

SOGC guidelines recommend empiric medical treatment before laparoscopy in suspected endometriosis. Dienogest is a newer progestin specifically approved for endometriosis in Canada. Deep infiltrating endometriosis requires expert surgical management. Endometriosis affects 6-10% of reproductive-age women.

**Q:** What is the assessment and management of pelvic organ prolapse?

**A:** POP-Q staging: Stage 0-IV based on descent relative to hymen. Presentation: vaginal bulge, pressure, urinary symptoms (incomplete emptying, stress incontinence), bowel symptoms. Types: cystocele (anterior), rectocele (posterior), uterine prolapse, vault prolapse. Conservative: pelvic floor physiotherapy, pessary. Surgical: anterior/posterior colporrhaphy, sacrocolpopexy, hysterectomy + vault suspension.

SOGC guidelines recommend pessary as first-line for symptomatic prolapse. Pelvic floor physiotherapy (Kegel exercises) can improve Stage I-II prolapse. Surgical mesh for transvaginal prolapse repair carries significant complication risk. In Canada, vaginal mesh for prolapse has been largely restricted.

**Q:** What is the approach to a postmenopausal woman with vaginal bleeding?

**A:** ALWAYS investigate-endometrial cancer until proven otherwise. Workup: pelvic exam, transvaginal ultrasound (endometrial thickness <4-5mm has high NPV), endometrial biopsy (Pipelle). If biopsy insufficient: hysteroscopy with D&C. Causes: atrophic vaginitis (most common), endometrial polyp, endometrial hyperplasia/cancer, cervical pathology, HRT.

SOGC guidelines recommend endometrial biopsy for all postmenopausal bleeding. Endometrial thickness >4mm on TVUS requires tissue sampling. Endometrial cancer is the most common gynecologic malignancy in Canada. Risk factors: obesity, unopposed estrogen, nulliparity, late menopause, tamoxifen, PCOS.

**Q:** What are the SOGC guidelines for menopause management and hormone replacement therapy?

**A:** Vasomotor symptoms: HRT is most effective treatment. Oral or transdermal estrogen + progestogen (if uterus present). Transdermal preferred (lower VTE risk). Lowest effective dose for shortest duration needed. Contraindications: breast cancer, active VTE, active liver disease, unexplained vaginal bleeding, coronary heart disease. Alternatives: venlafaxine, paroxetine, gabapentin for vasomotor symptoms.

SOGC Menopause guidelines state HRT initiated <60 years or within 10 years of menopause (window of opportunity) is safe for most women and may reduce cardiovascular risk. WHI data reinterpreted: risks primarily in older women (>60). Vaginal estrogen for genitourinary syndrome of menopause is safe even in breast cancer survivors.

**Q:** What is the approach to female infertility?

**A:** Definition: failure to conceive after 12 months of regular unprotected intercourse (6 months if >=35 years). Workup: ovulatory assessment (day 3 FSH/estradiol, day 21 progesterone, AMH), tubal patency (HSG or laparoscopy), semen analysis (male factor 40%). Treatment by cause: anovulation-letrozole (first-line), tubal-IVF, unexplained-IUI with ovulation induction then IVF.

SOGC infertility guidelines recommend simultaneous evaluation of both partners. Letrozole replaced clomiphene as first-line ovulation induction (NEJM trial 2014). AMH (anti-Mullerian hormone) predicts ovarian reserve. IVF is publicly funded in some Canadian provinces (Ontario, Quebec) with specific criteria.

**Q:** What are the types of ovarian cancer and their staging?

**A:** Epithelial (90%): serous (most common, high-grade associated with BRCA), mucinous, endometrioid, clear cell. Germ cell (5%): dysgerminoma, teratoma, yolk sac tumor. Sex cord-stromal (5%): granulosa cell, Sertoli-Leydig. Staging: I-confined to ovary, II-pelvic extension, III-peritoneal spread/nodes, IV-distant metastases. Treatment: cytoreductive surgery + platinum-based chemotherapy.

Ovarian cancer is the most lethal gynecologic cancer. No effective screening exists for average-risk women (CA-125 + TVUS screening does NOT reduce mortality). BRCA1 carriers: 40-50% lifetime ovarian cancer risk; BRCA2: 15-20%. SOGC recommends risk-reducing bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy by age 40-45 for BRCA1 carriers.

**Q:** What is the management of ectopic pregnancy vs miscarriage in early pregnancy bleeding?

**A:** Key distinguishing features: ectopic-adnexal mass, peritoneal signs, hCG rising abnormally (<35% increase in 48h). Miscarriage types: threatened (cervix closed, ongoing pregnancy), inevitable (cervix open, membranes intact), incomplete (cervix open, partial tissue passage), complete (tissue passed, cervix closing), missed (no cardiac activity, cervix closed). Management of miscarriage: expectant, medical (misoprostol 800mcg vaginal), or surgical (suction D&C).

SOGC guidelines define miscarriage management options. Discriminatory zone: hCG level (typically 1500-3000 IU/L) above which a viable IUP should be seen on TVUS. If hCG above discriminatory zone with empty uterus: ectopic until proven otherwise. Rh-negative women receive RhIg for any early pregnancy bleeding.

**Q:** What is the PALM-COEIN classification for abnormal uterine bleeding?

**A:** Structural (PALM): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy. Non-structural (COEIN): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified

FIGO system. Workup: pregnancy test, CBC, TSH, coagulation studies, pelvic US. Ovulatory dysfunction most common in reproductive age. Endometrial biopsy if >45 or risk factors for endometrial cancer.

**Q:** What are the SOGC-recommended criteria for diagnosing endometriosis?

**A:** Clinical suspicion based on cyclical pelvic pain, dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia, dyschezia, and/or infertility. Definitive diagnosis historically requires laparoscopy with histological confirmation. However, SOGC now supports empirical treatment based on clinical diagnosis without mandatory laparoscopy.

Average diagnostic delay for endometriosis is 7-10 years. SOGC guidelines acknowledge that imaging (transvaginal US for endometriomas, MRI for deep infiltrating disease) can support diagnosis non-invasively. CA-125 is not recommended as a screening tool due to poor sensitivity and specificity.

**Q:** What are the medical management options for endometriosis?

**A:** First-line: combined OCP (continuous preferred) or progestins (dienogest, norethindrone, medroxyprogesterone). Second-line: GnRH agonists (with add-back HRT to limit bone loss) or GnRH antagonists (elagolix). NSAIDs for pain. Levonorgestrel IUD for pain and bleeding.

SOGC recommends hormonal suppression as first-line medical therapy. GnRH agonists without add-back therapy should not exceed 6 months due to bone density loss. Dienogest 2 mg daily is specifically approved for endometriosis in Canada. Surgical excision is considered for refractory cases or infertility.

**Q:** What are the Rotterdam criteria for diagnosing PCOS?

**A:** Need 2 of 3: 1) oligo/anovulation, 2) clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism (hirsutism, acne, elevated free testosterone), 3) polycystic ovarian morphology on ultrasound (>=12 follicles 2-9mm or ovarian volume >10mL). Must exclude other causes (thyroid disease, CAH, Cushing, hyperprolactinemia).

SOGC and Endocrine Society guidelines use Rotterdam criteria. In adolescents, all 3 criteria should be present (US morphology is unreliable peri-menarchally). Metabolic screening (OGTT, lipids, BP) is recommended at diagnosis since 50% develop metabolic syndrome.

**Q:** How is PCOS managed depending on the patient's reproductive goals?

**A:** Not seeking pregnancy: combined OCP (first-line for menstrual regulation and anti-androgen effect), spironolactone for hirsutism, metformin for metabolic features. Seeking pregnancy: letrozole (first-line ovulation induction, superior to clomiphene per NEJM trial), weight loss, metformin as adjunct.

SOGC recommends letrozole over clomiphene citrate as first-line for ovulation induction based on the NICHD trial showing higher live birth rates. Lifestyle modification with 5-10% weight loss improves ovulation rates. Spironolactone requires contraception due to teratogenicity (anti-androgen).

**Q:** What is the PALM-COEIN classification for abnormal uterine bleeding?

**A:** Structural (PALM): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia. Non-structural (COEIN): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified. This FIGO system standardizes terminology and guides workup.

SOGC adopted PALM-COEIN to replace imprecise terms like menorrhagia and metrorrhagia. Workup includes CBC, ferritin, TSH, pregnancy test, transvaginal US, and endometrial biopsy (if age >=45, or risk factors for hyperplasia). Coagulopathy screening is recommended in adolescents with heavy menstrual bleeding.

**Q:** When should endometrial biopsy be performed in a patient with abnormal uterine bleeding?

**A:** Age >=45 with AUB, age <45 with risk factors (obesity, PCOS, chronic anovulation, tamoxifen use, family history of endometrial/colon cancer), failed medical management, or postmenopausal bleeding (any episode warrants investigation).

SOGC recommends endometrial sampling to exclude malignancy/hyperplasia. Pipelle biopsy has >95% sensitivity for endometrial cancer in postmenopausal women. Transvaginal US showing endometrial thickness <4-5mm in postmenopausal women has high negative predictive value for malignancy.

**Q:** What is the current approach to ovarian cancer screening in average-risk and high-risk women?

**A:** Average-risk: routine screening NOT recommended (UKCTOCS trial showed no mortality benefit). High-risk (BRCA1/2 carriers): consider transvaginal US + CA-125 every 6 months starting age 30-35, but risk-reducing bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy (RRBSO) by age 35-40 (BRCA1) or 40-45 (BRCA2) is the definitive prevention.

SOGC and Canadian guidelines do not recommend population-based ovarian cancer screening. RRBSO reduces ovarian cancer risk by ~80% and is recommended after completion of childbearing. Serous tubal intraepithelial carcinoma (STIC) is now recognized as the precursor of high-grade serous ovarian cancer.

**Q:** A 16-year-old presents with heavy menstrual bleeding since menarche. What coagulopathy workup is indicated?

**A:** CBC, ferritin, PT/INR, aPTT, fibrinogen, von Willebrand panel (vWF antigen, ristocetin cofactor activity, factor VIII), platelet function testing. Von Willebrand disease is the most common inherited bleeding disorder, found in up to 20% of adolescents with heavy menstrual bleeding.

SOGC recommends screening for bleeding disorders in adolescents presenting with heavy menses at menarche. VWD type 1 is most common. Testing should occur off hormonal therapy and ideally not during acute bleeding. Tranexamic acid and combined OCP are first-line treatments for menorrhagia in VWD.

**Q:** What is the medical management of adenomyosis?

**A:** Levonorgestrel IUD (Mirena) is first-line — reduces menstrual blood loss by 70-90%. Alternatives: combined OCP (continuous), GnRH agonists (short-term), NSAIDs for pain, tranexamic acid for bleeding. Definitive treatment is hysterectomy for women not desiring future fertility.

Adenomyosis is a common cause of dysmenorrhea and heavy bleeding in parous women aged 35-50. Transvaginal US shows an enlarged globular uterus with heterogeneous myometrium. MRI is the gold standard for diagnosis. SOGC guidelines recommend LNG-IUD as first-line conservative approach.

**Q:** What is the significance of opportunistic salpingectomy at the time of benign gynecologic surgery?

**A:** SOGC recommends discussing bilateral salpingectomy at time of hysterectomy or as an alternative to tubal ligation for sterilization. Evidence suggests most high-grade serous ovarian cancers originate in the fallopian tube (STIC lesions). Risk reduction estimated at 40-65% for serous ovarian cancer.

The OGCS (Ovarian Cancer Screening) paradigm has shifted from early detection to prevention. Canadian gynecologic oncology societies endorse opportunistic salpingectomy. No adverse effect on ovarian reserve or timing of menopause has been demonstrated. This is a population-health strategy unique to Canadian practice.

**Q:** What cervical cancer screening is recommended for women ages 25-29?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid cytology every 3 years (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

For women 25-29, Pap smear q3yr is recommended as a weak recommendation. Earlier ages are not recommended for routine screening.

**Q:** What cervical cancer screening is recommended for women ages 30-69?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

For women 30-69, Pap smear q3yr is a strong recommendation with high quality evidence supporting reduced cervical cancer mortality.

**Q:** Should women under age 20 be screened for cervical cancer?

**A:** No, recommend not routinely screening women under age 20 (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

Cervical cancer is rare in this age group, and screening leads to unnecessary procedures and anxiety without clear benefit.

**Q:** When can routine cervical cancer screening cease for women age 70 and older?

**A:** When they have had 3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years (adequately screened)

Women ≥70 who have been adequately screened (3 negative Pap tests in last 10 years) can stop routine screening.

**Q:** What is the cervical cancer screening recommendation for women ages 20-24?

**A:** Recommend not routinely screening for cervical cancer (weak recommendation; moderate quality evidence)

Unlike the strong recommendation for ages <20, this is a weak recommendation based on moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** What is the cervical cancer screening recommendation for women ages 25-29?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid cytology every 3 years (weak recommendation; moderate quality evidence)

This is the age group where screening begins, with cytology-based testing every 3 years.

**Q:** What is the cervical cancer screening recommendation for women ages 30-69?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation; high quality evidence)

This is a strong recommendation with high quality evidence, reflecting the proven benefit in this age group.

**Q:** When can routine cervical cancer screening cease in women age ≥70?

**A:** Women who have been adequately screened (3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years) can stop routine screening

For women age ≥70 who have not been adequately screened, continue until 3 negative test results are obtained.

**Q:** What are the cervical cancer screening recommendations for women ages 25-29 according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Papanicolaou (Pap) smear or liquid cytology every 3 years (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

For women ages 25-29, Pap smear or liquid-based cytology q3yr is recommended as a weak recommendation based on moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** What are the cervical cancer screening recommendations for women ages 30-69 according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

For women ages 30-69, Pap smear q3yr is a strong recommendation based on high quality evidence.

**Q:** What is the recommendation for cervical cancer screening in women under age 20?

**A:** Recommend NOT routinely screening for cervical cancer (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

The Canadian Task Force strongly recommends against routine cervical cancer screening for women under 20 based on high quality evidence.

**Q:** What is the recommendation for cervical cancer screening in women ages 20-24?

**A:** Recommend NOT routinely screening for cervical cancer (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

For ages 20-24, the recommendation against routine screening is weak based on moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** According to the Ontario Cervical Screening Program 2022, at what age should cervical cancer screening begin for average-risk women?

**A:** Age 25 (if sexually active)

Ontario recommends screening women age ≥25 who are or have ever been sexually active, every 3 years if cytology is normal.

**Q:** What is the recommended cervical cancer screening approach for women ages 30-69 according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

This is a strong recommendation with high quality evidence supporting screening every 3 years in this age group.

**Q:** According to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines, should women ages 20-24 be routinely screened for cervical cancer?

**A:** No - recommend NOT routinely screening (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

Evidence does not support routine screening in this age group due to low prevalence of clinically significant disease and potential harms from over-treatment.

**Q:** When can routine cervical cancer screening cease according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** For women age 70+ who have been adequately screened (3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years), routine screening may cease

Women who have had adequate negative screening can stop at 70, while those not adequately screened should continue until 3 negative results are obtained.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency for cervical cancer screening in Ontario for women age 25+ with normal cytology?

**A:** Every 3 years

Ontario's Cervical Screening Program (2022) recommends screening q3yr if cytology is normal for women age 25+ who are or have ever been sexually active.

**Q:** At what age should cervical cancer screening begin according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Ages 25-29: Pap smear or liquid cytology q3yr (weak recommendation); Ages 30-69: Pap smear or liquid cytology q3yr (strong recommendation)

Screening is not recommended for women under 20 (strong) or ages 20-24 (weak). Starting at age 25, Pap smear every 3 years is recommended.

**Q:** Should women under 20 be screened for cervical cancer?

**A:** No - recommend not routinely screening (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

The Canadian Task Force strongly recommends against cervical cancer screening in women under 20 due to high quality evidence showing no benefit.

**Q:** When can routine cervical cancer screening cease according to Canadian guidelines?

**A:** For women age 70+ who have been adequately screened (3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years), routine screening may cease

Women who have had adequate negative screening can stop at age 70, while those not adequately screened should continue until 3 negative results.

**Q:** What is the recommended cervical cancer screening interval with normal cytology in Ontario?

**A:** Every 3 years

The Ontario Cervical Screening Program recommends screening q3yr for women age 25+ with normal cytology results.

**Q:** What are the Canadian Task Force cervical cancer screening recommendations for women ages 25-29?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid cytology every 3 years (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

Screening is not routinely recommended for ages <25 but may be offered at age 25.

**Q:** What are the Canadian Task Force cervical cancer screening recommendations for women ages 30-69?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

This is a strong recommendation based on high-quality evidence demonstrating mortality reduction.

**Q:** What does the Canadian Task Force recommend for cervical cancer screening in women under age 20?

**A:** Recommend NOT routinely screening (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

Cervical cancer is extremely rare in this age group, and screening leads to unnecessary procedures.

**Q:** When should women who have not been adequately screened continue cervical cancer screening beyond age 70?

**A:** Continue until 3 negative test results have been obtained

Women who started screening late or had gaps require completion of adequate screening before stopping.

**Q:** According to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines, at what age should routine cervical cancer screening begin for average-risk women?

**A:** Age 25 (weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence)

Pap smear or liquid cytology recommended q3yr starting at age 25. For ages 20-24, screening is not routinely recommended.

**Q:** According to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines, what is the recommended cervical cancer screening interval for women ages 30-69?

**A:** Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years (strong recommendation, high quality evidence)

This is a strong recommendation based on high quality evidence showing adequate screening frequency.

**Q:** When can cervical cancer screening cease according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Women ages 70+ who have been adequately screened (3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years) may stop routine screening

Women 70+ with inadequate screening should continue until 3 negative test results are obtained.

**Q:** At what age is routine cervical cancer screening NOT recommended according to 2013 Canadian Task Force guidelines?

**A:** Under age 20 (strong recommendation) and ages 20-24 (weak recommendation)

Under 20: strong recommendation, high quality evidence. Ages 20-24: weak recommendation, moderate quality evidence.

**Q:** Which age group has STRONG recommendation for cervical cancer screening in Canada?

**A:** Ages 30-69

Strong recommendation with high quality evidence for Pap smear or liquid-based cytology every 3 years in this age group.

**Q:** What are associated conditions that increase diabetes risk?

**A:** PCOS, acanthosis nigricans, psychiatric disorders, HIV

Certain conditions like PCOS and acanthosis nigricans are associated with increased diabetes risk.

**Q:** At what age does the Canadian Task Force recommend NOT routinely screening for cervical cancer?

**A:** Ages <20 (strong recommendation) and ages 20-24 (weak recommendation)

No screening is recommended for women under 20 (strong), and routine screening is not recommended for ages 20-24 (weak).

**Q:** When may routine cervical cancer screening cease for women age ≥70?

**A:** If they have been adequately screened (3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years)

Women ≥70 who have had 3 successive negative Pap tests in the last 10 years may stop routine screening.

**Q:** What is the Pap test screening recommendation for cervical cancer prevention?

**A:** Pap test every 3 years for anyone with a cervix aged 25 to 69 who has ever been sexually active

Regular Pap testing detects precancerous changes from HPV infection.

**Q:** What is the cervical cancer screening recommendation (Pap test)?

**A:** Pap test every 3 years for anyone with a cervix aged 25-69 who has ever been sexually active

Regular cervical cancer screening is recommended for at-risk individuals.

**Q:** What conditions are associated with increased diabetes risk?

**A:** PCOS, acanthosis nigricans, psychiatric disorders, HIV

These conditions are associated with insulin resistance and higher diabetes risk.

**Q:** What is the most common gynecological cause of pelvic pain, and how is it managed?

**A:** Ruptured ovarian cysts are the most common cause. Management: if unruptured or ruptured but hemodynamically stable — analgesia and follow-up. If ruptured with significant hemoperitoneum — may require surgery.

Ovarian cysts can rupture spontaneously, especially at mid-cycle (mittelschmerz). Most are managed conservatively. Key differentials requiring urgent action include ovarian torsion (requires surgical detorsion), ectopic pregnancy, and TOA. β-hCG must always be checked to exclude pregnancy.

**Q:** What are the causes of non-pregnant abnormal uterine bleeding using PALM-COEIN?

**A:** PALM (structural): Polyps, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia. COEIN (non-structural): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not yet classified.

PALM-COEIN is the FIGO classification system for abnormal uterine bleeding in non-pregnant women. Structural causes are diagnosed by imaging/hysteroscopy; non-structural causes require laboratory investigation. Initial management aims to establish hemostasis (TXA, hormonal therapy) before definitive diagnosis.

**Q:** What were the key results of the Women's Health Initiative (WHI) trial on combined hormone therapy?

**A:** WHI (2002): 16,608 postmenopausal women with intact uterus randomized to combined estrogen + progesterone vs. placebo. Results: Increased risk of CHD and breast cancer in the treatment group. Conclusion: Overall health risks exceed benefits of combined hormone therapy in healthy postmenopausal women.

The WHI trial significantly influenced practice regarding hormone replacement therapy (HRT). Combined HRT is now not recommended as primary prevention for cardiovascular disease. It remains an option for symptomatic menopausal women with informed consent and individualized risk-benefit assessment.

**Q:** What is the gold standard medical method for termination of pregnancy up to 9 weeks GA, and how does it work?

**A:** Mifepristone 200 mg PO on day 1, followed by misoprostol 800 mcg PV/BUC on day 2 or 3. Efficacy: 95–98% up to 49 days after LMP.
- Mifepristone: blocks progesterone receptor → alters endometrium, softens cervix, induces bleeding
- Misoprostol: synthetic prostaglandin → stimulates uterine contractions and expulsion of products of conception

This two-drug regimen is highly effective and avoids surgical risks. Mifepristone primes the uterus; misoprostol provides the expulsive force. Side effects include expected bleeding, pain, nausea, diarrhea, and chills/fever (prostaglandin effects). D&C backup must be accessible if medical abortion fails.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to mifepristone-based medical abortion?

**A:** Absolute contraindications:
1. Ectopic pregnancy
2. Chronic adrenal failure
3. Ambivalence about the decision

Relative contraindications:
- Unconfirmed GA
- IUD in situ
- Long-term steroid therapy
- Bleeding disorder or anticoagulation
- Porphyria

Mifepristone blocks glucocorticoid receptors as well as progesterone receptors, so adrenal insufficiency is dangerous (adrenal crisis risk). Ectopic pregnancy will not be treated by this regimen. Patients must confirm and be certain of their decision.

**Q:** What is the definition of infertility and when should investigations begin based on age?

**A:** Infertility: inability to conceive or carry to term after 1 year of regular unprotected intercourse.
- <35 yr: investigate after 12 months
- 35–40 yr: investigate after 6 months
- >40 yr: investigate immediately

Investigate earlier if: history of PID, prior infertility in a previous relationship, prior pelvic surgery, chemotherapy/radiation in either partner, recurrent pregnancy loss, or moderate-to-severe endometriosis.

Fertility declines with age, especially after 35, and the threshold for investigation is lowered accordingly. Infertility affects 10–15% of couples; both partners must be investigated. 75% of couples achieve pregnancy within 6 months and 85% within 1 year of trying.

**Q:** What day 3 hormonal investigations are ordered to assess ovarian reserve and ovulatory function in female infertility?

**A:** Day 3 labs: FSH, LH, estradiol, TSH, prolactin, free and total testosterone, androstenedione, DHEAS.
Day 21–23: serum progesterone (high levels confirm ovulation).
Any day: Anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) — marker of ovarian reserve; higher = better reserve.

Day 3 FSH/LH/estradiol assess ovarian reserve and baseline gonadotropin status. Elevated day 3 FSH suggests diminished ovarian reserve. AMH reflects antral follicle count and is the most reliable independent marker of ovarian reserve. Progesterone on day 21–23 confirms the luteal phase and ovulation.

**Q:** What imaging and procedural investigations assess tubal patency in female infertility?

**A:** 1. Hysterosalpingogram (HSG): dye insufflated into uterus with X-ray — shows uterine cavity shape and tubal patency; can be therapeutic (opens tubes)
2. Sonohysterogram (SHG): saline insufflated with U/S — shows uterine cavity and tube patency; can be therapeutic
3. Laparoscopy with dye insufflation (tubal dye test): rarely used as diagnostic; also evaluates peritoneal cavity for endometriosis/adhesions
4. Hysteroscopy: evaluates uterine cavity (submucosal fibroids, polyps, adhesions)

HSG is the standard first-line imaging for tubal patency. It is minimally invasive and may itself be therapeutic by flushing debris. SHG is better for evaluating the uterine cavity. Laparoscopy is reserved for cases where peritoneal pathology (endometriosis, adhesions) is suspected.

**Q:** What ovulation induction agents are used in female infertility, and which is preferred in PCOS?

**A:** - Letrozole (aromatase inhibitor): preferred first-line in PCOS — associated with higher live birth rates vs. clomiphene
- Clomiphene citrate (estrogen antagonist): not available in Canada; blocks estrogen perception → increases FSH/LH release; better if anovulatory
- Metformin: used alone or combined with clomiphene for PCOS
- Human menopausal gonadotropins (HMG, e.g., Pergonal), recombinant FSH, LHRH: for more complex cases
- Bromocriptine/cabergoline: if elevated prolactin

Letrozole is now the preferred first-line agent in PCOS-related anovulatory infertility per Canadian and international guidelines, showing higher live birth rates than clomiphene with fewer multiple pregnancies. Metformin improves insulin sensitivity in PCOS, reducing androgen production and restoring ovulation.

**Q:** What are the Rotterdam diagnostic criteria for Polycystic Ovarian Syndrome (PCOS)?

**A:** PCOS requires 2 of 3 criteria:
1. Oligomenorrhea/irregular menses for ≥6 months
2. Hyperandrogenism:
   - Clinical: hirsutism or acne
   - Biochemical: raised free testosterone
3. Polycystic ovaries on U/S (not appropriate in adolescents): ≥12 small follicles 2–9 mm ("string of pearls") or ovarian volume >10 cc

Note: In adolescents, wait at least 1–2 years before diagnosing as puberty normally resembles PCOS.

The Rotterdam criteria are the most widely used diagnostic criteria for PCOS. Since any 2 of 3 criteria suffice, patients can have PCOS without polycystic ovaries on U/S. The diagnosis requires excluding other causes (late-onset CAH, Cushing's, hyperprolactinemia, hypothyroidism).

**Q:** What are the long-term health consequences and metabolic complications of PCOS?

**A:** Long-term consequences:
- Hyperlipidemia
- Endometrial hyperplasia (from unopposed estrogen due to anovulation)
- Subfertility
- Obesity
- Sleep apnea
- Insulin resistance → impaired glucose tolerance (35%), T2DM (10%)
- Acanthosis nigricans (marker of insulin resistance)

Chronic anovulation in PCOS leads to unopposed estrogen stimulation of the endometrium, increasing risk of endometrial hyperplasia and cancer. Insulin resistance drives androgen overproduction and perpetuates the cycle. Annual OGTT (75g every 2 years if obese) is recommended for metabolic surveillance.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of PCOS?

**A:** Insulin resistance → compensatory hyperinsulinemia → increased ovarian androgen secretion + decreased FSH secretion and increased LH secretion → anovulation → infertility, oligomenorrhea. Peripheral conversion of androgens to estrogens (by adipose) contributes further. Obesity worsens insulin resistance, creating a positive feedback loop. LH:FSH ratio >2:1 is characteristic (low sensitivity/specificity).

Hyperinsulinemia acts synergistically with LH to stimulate ovarian theca cells to produce androgens. This excess androgen impairs follicle maturation, causing multiple small arrested follicles ("string of pearls"). FSH suppression prevents dominant follicle selection. The LH:FSH ratio >2:1 reflects the chronic LH elevation.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for PCOS hirsutism?

**A:** - Any combined OCP (suppresses LH and androgens)
- Diane-35 (cyproterone acetate): anti-androgenic OCP
- Yasmin (drospirenone + ethinyl estradiol): spironolactone analogue (inhibits steroid receptors)
- Spironolactone: androgen receptor inhibitor
- Flutamide: androgen receptor blocker/reuptake inhibitor
- Finasteride: 5-alpha reductase inhibitor
- Mechanical hair removal

Hirsutism results from androgen excess stimulating terminal hair follicles. Combined OCPs suppress pituitary LH (reducing ovarian androgen production) and increase SHBG (reducing free testosterone). Anti-androgens block peripheral androgen action. Mechanical removal addresses existing terminal hair.

**Q:** What investigations are ordered at PCOS presentation, and what surveillance is recommended?

**A:** At presentation: assess BMI, BP, fasting lipid profile.
Laboratory: prolactin, TSH, free T4; 17-hydroxyprogesterone (to exclude late-onset CAH); LH:FSH ratio; DHEAS, androstenedione, free testosterone (most sensitive for hyperandrogenism).
Transvaginal or transabdominal U/S for polycystic ovaries.
Surveillance: 75g OGTT every 2 years (especially if obese).
Consider endometrial biopsy if long-standing abnormal uterine bleeding (to rule out hyperplasia).

PCOS is a diagnosis of exclusion — hypothyroidism, hyperprolactinemia, and late-onset CAH (17-OHP elevated) must be ruled out. Free testosterone is the most sensitive biochemical marker for hyperandrogenism. Regular OGTT surveillance addresses the significant metabolic risk in PCOS.

**Q:** What is the significance of acanthosis nigricans in PCOS?

**A:** Acanthosis nigricans — darkening of skin folds in intertriginous zones (neck, axillae, groin) — is a cutaneous marker of insulin resistance. It occurs in both lean and obese patients with PCOS and indicates significant hyperinsulinemia.

Hyperinsulinemia stimulates keratinocyte and fibroblast proliferation via IGF-1 receptors, causing the characteristic velvety hyperpigmented plaques. In PCOS, its presence should prompt evaluation for glucose intolerance and guide treatment toward insulin sensitizers (metformin).

**Q:** What is physiologic vaginal discharge and how does it differ from pathologic discharge?

**A:** Physiologic: clear/white, flocculent, odourless, pH 4.0–4.5; contains epithelial cells and Lactobacilli; increases with elevated estrogen states (pregnancy, OCP, mid-cycle, PCOS, premenarchal).
Pathologic: suggests infection (vulvovaginitis, chlamydia, gonorrhea, PID), non-infectious inflammation (chemical irritants, atrophic vaginitis, foreign body), neoplasia, or systemic conditions. If increased in a perimenopausal/postmenopausal woman, investigate for excess estrogen effects (e.g., endometrial cancer).

The acidic pH of normal vaginal discharge is maintained by Lactobacillus-produced lactic acid, which protects against pathogens. Elevated pH (>4.5) suggests BV or trichomoniasis. Postmenopausal women with new discharge require evaluation for estrogen-secreting tumours or endometrial pathology.

**Q:** What are the causes and management of prepubertal vulvovaginitis?

**A:** Cause: non-specific vulvovaginitis in 25–75% of cases. Contributing factors: lack of labial fat pads, non-estrogenized thin alkaline mucosa (pH >7), obesity, poor hygiene, proximity of anus to vagina, foreign bodies (toilet paper), bubble baths/perfumed soaps, lichen sclerosis.
Infectious causes: pinworms, Candida (if diapers/antibiotics), Group A Streptococcus, S. aureus, Shigella.
Important: discovery of an STI should raise suspicion of sexual abuse.

Management: enhanced hygiene (wipe front to back, white cotton underwear, sitz baths, mild detergent, no bubble baths, no fabric softener, avoid tight clothes). Antibiotics for identified infectious organism.

The prepubertal vaginal environment is susceptible to irritation due to low estrogen, which results in thin mucosa and higher pH. Non-specific vulvovaginitis is most commonly due to hygiene issues. Clinicians must be alert to the possibility of sexual abuse when STIs are found in prepubertal children.

**Q:** What are the causes of female infertility organized by category?

**A:** Ovarian: diminished ovarian reserve (age, chemotherapy, radiation, Fragile X), ovulatory dysfunction (hypogonadotropic hypogonadal anovulation — Kallmann's, hyperprolactinemia; normogonadotropic anovulation — PCOS, obesity [70–85%]; hypergonadotropic hypoestrogenic — primary ovarian insufficiency, Turner's syndrome [10–30%]), luteal phase defect.
Tubal: PID, peritubal adhesions, tubal ligation, hydrosalpinx.
Uterine: submucosal fibroids/polyps, congenital anomalies (bicornuate, septate, didelphys, MRKH), Asherman's syndrome, intrauterine adhesions, DES exposure.
Cervical: hostile cervical mucus, anti-sperm antibodies, structural defects (cone biopsy, cryotherapy).
Other: endometriosis, adenomyosis, medications, systemic disease (renal/hepatic failure, Cushing's, DM).

PCOS is the most common cause of normogonadotropic anovulation (70–85%). PID is the most common cause of tubal infertility. Asherman's syndrome (intrauterine adhesions) is a complication of uterine surgery. Systematic evaluation of all categories is essential — 10–15% of couples are affected.

**Q:** What is the most common histological type of cervical cancer, and what is the second most common type that is increasing in incidence?

**A:** Squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) accounts for ~90% of cervical cancers. Adenocarcinoma accounts for ~10% and is increasing in incidence.

SCC arises predominantly from the transformation zone (TZ) at the squamocolumnar junction. Adenocarcinoma arises from endocervical glandular cells; its relative increase is partly attributable to better SCC prevention via Pap screening.

**Q:** What is the transformation zone (TZ) of the cervix, and why is it clinically significant?

**A:** The TZ is the area between the original squamocolumnar junction and the new (current) squamocolumnar junction that forms after squamous metaplasia. It is the site where the majority of cervical dysplasias and cancers arise, because active metaplasia in the presence of an inducing agent (e.g. HPV) leads to dysplasia.

During puberty, estrogen causes eversion of columnar epithelium (ectopy), which is then exposed to the acidic vaginal pH and undergoes metaplasia to squamous epithelium. This active metaplastic process in the TZ is vulnerable to HPV-induced dysplastic transformation.

**Q:** Which HPV types are considered high-risk for cervical neoplasia, and what proportion of cervical cancers contain a high-risk HPV type?

**A:** High-risk HPV types: 16, 18, 31, 33, 35, 39, 45, 51, 52, 56, 58, 59, 68, 73, and 82. >99% of cervical cancers contain one of these high-risk HPV types. Low-risk types (6, 11) are associated with condylomata but not malignancy.

HPV types 16 and 18 together account for approximately 70% of cervical cancers. The near-universal presence of high-risk HPV in cervical cancers underpins prophylactic HPV vaccination as the primary prevention strategy.

**Q:** What are the early and late clinical features of cervical cancer?

**A:** Early: often asymptomatic; watery to brown/red vaginal discharge; postcoital bleeding. Late: vaginal bleeding (postcoital, postmenopausal, or irregular) in 80-90%; pelvic or back pain (tumour extension to pelvic walls); bladder/bowel symptoms. Signs: friable, raised, reddened, or ulcerated area on cervix.

The majority of patients present late with abnormal vaginal bleeding. SCC typically appears as an exophytic fungating tumour, whereas adenocarcinoma is often endophytic with a barrel-shaped cervix. Early disease is detected through screening (Pap/colposcopy), underscoring the importance of cervical cancer screening programs.

**Q:** Describe the indications for LEEP (loop electrosurgical excision procedure) in the workup of cervical cancer.

**A:** LEEP is indicated for: unsatisfactory colposcopy (poor visualization/access to TZ); discrepancy between cytology, colposcopy, and histological findings; positive findings/glandular abnormalities on endocervical curettage (ECC); recurrence of lesion post-ablation or excision; inability to rule out invasive disease (large lesions extending into endocervical canal, widely on cervix, or onto vaginal epithelium). Cold-knife conization is preferred if glandular abnormality (AIS) is suspected.

LEEP is both diagnostic and therapeutic for CIN lesions. It provides a histological specimen with interpretable margins. ECC is performed when the entire lesion is not visible or when no lesion is visible on colposcopy.

**Q:** According to FIGO 2018 staging, what defines Stage IB1, IB2, and IB3 cervical cancer?

**A:** Stage IB1: stromal invasion ≥5 mm and <2 cm wide. Stage IB2: stromal invasion ≥5 mm and 2 cm to <4 cm wide. Stage IB3: ≥4 cm in width. All Stage IB lesions are limited to the cervix.

Stage I cervical cancer is confined to the cervix. Substaging of IB reflects tumour size because it influences choice of treatment (surgery vs. chemoradiation) and prognosis. Stage IA is diagnosed only by microscopy (IA1: <3 mm deep; IA2: 3 to <5 mm deep).

**Q:** What is the management of Stage IA2, IB1, and IB2 cervical cancer?

**A:** Typically treated with radical hysterectomy and pelvic lymphadenectomy (sentinel node or pelvic LN dissection). If high chance of adjuvant radiation, consider primary chemoradiation to avoid double-modality morbidity. For fertility preservation with tumour <2 cm: radical trachelectomy (removal of cervix and parametria) + nodes instead of radical hysterectomy. Adjuvant chemoradiation if high-risk pathological features: positive pelvic LNs, positive parametria, or positive margins.

Equal cure rates can be achieved with surgery or radiation; surgery allows accurate staging, sparing of ovaries (preserving endocrine function), and more targeted adjuvant therapy. Trachelectomy is an option in carefully selected patients with early-stage disease who desire future fertility.

**Q:** What is the treatment for Stage IB3, II, III, and IV cervical cancer?

**A:** Primary chemoradiation therapy. CT is used to assess extent of disease and evaluate pelvic and para-aortic nodes. If PET shows positive nodes: primary chemoradiation with extended field radiation therapy. Hysterectomy is generally not recommended following primary curative-intent chemoradiation.

Advanced cervical cancer (stage IB3 and beyond) is not amenable to primary surgical cure due to the extent of disease. Concurrent cisplatin-based chemotherapy with external beam radiation (+ brachytherapy) is the standard of care for locally advanced disease.

**Q:** What are the 5-year survival rates by FIGO stage for cervical cancer?

**A:** Stage 0: 99%. Stage I: 85%. Stage II: 55%. Stage III: 30%. Stage IV: 7%. Overall: 50-60%.

Prognosis is strongly dependent on stage at diagnosis. The dramatic drop in survival from Stage I to Stage II reflects parametrial or vaginal extension. This underscores the critical importance of early detection through cervical cancer screening programs.

**Q:** Compare Gardasil 9 and Cervarix HPV vaccines in terms of strains covered, dosing schedule, and approved age.

**A:** Gardasil 9: covers types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58. 2-dose schedule (second dose 6-12 months after first) for ages <15; 3-dose (0, 2, 6 months) for ages ≥15. Approved for females and males ages 9-45. Cervarix: covers types 16, 18 only. Schedule: 0, 1, 6 months. Approved for females ages 9-45. Both given IM; contraindicated in pregnancy.

Gardasil 9 (9-valent) provides broader protection than Cervarix (bivalent), covering the two low-risk condyloma-causing types (6, 11) in addition to five additional high-risk oncogenic types beyond 16 and 18. Vaccination before sexual debut provides maximum benefit but is still recommended for sexually active individuals.

**Q:** What are the key features of CIN/HSIL management based on patient age according to Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Women ≥25 years with HSIL/CIN II-III: excisional procedures (cold knife, LEEP, or laser) preferred; positive margins require follow-up colposcopy with directed biopsies ± ECC. Women <25 years with HSIL: colposcopy every 6 months for 2 years or treatment may be acceptable based on patient preference. For LSIL in women ≥25: if HPV negative → routine Pap every 3 years; if HPV positive → follow-up colposcopy with cytology and HPV test (if ≥30 years) in 1 year.

The age-stratified approach for CIN management reflects the higher likelihood of spontaneous regression in younger women and concerns about excisional procedures increasing preterm birth risk in nulliparous patients. Canadian guidelines emphasize risk-based screening and management.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of fallopian tube carcinoma, and what is the most specific symptom?

**A:** Classic triad: watery discharge ('hydrops tubae profluens' — most specific), vaginal bleeding or discharge (50%), and crampy lower abdominal/pelvic pain. Most patients actually present with a pelvic mass rather than the classic triad.

Fallopian tube carcinoma is the least common female reproductive tract malignancy (0.3%), usually serous epithelial type. Emerging evidence suggests some serous ovarian cancers actually originate in the fallopian tube. Treatment is the same as for malignant epithelial ovarian tumours.

**Q:** Compare lichen sclerosus, lichen simplex chronicus, and lichen planus of the vulva.

**A:** Lichen sclerosus: 'figure of 8' distribution, thin atrophic membrane-like epithelium, labial fusion, postmenopausal (any age), risk of SCC transformation; treat with high-potency topical steroid (clobetasol). Lichen simplex chronicus: thickened hyperkeratotic 'leather-like' labia majora, pruritus/burning especially at night, typically postmenopausal; treat with medium-potency steroid + nighttime antihistamines. Lichen planus: autoimmune (T cells attack basal keratinocytes), 3 variants (erosive, papulosquamous, hypertrophic), peak age 30-60, can extend into vagina causing desquamative vaginitis, associated with oral lichen planus; treat with ultrapotent steroid BID.

All three are non-neoplastic disorders of vulvar epithelium requiring biopsy to exclude malignancy. Lichen sclerosus carries the highest risk of progression to SCC and requires long-term monitoring. Every suspicious vulvar lesion should be biopsied.

**Q:** What are the two types of vulvar SCC (Type I and Type II), and how do they differ in etiology and patient demographics?

**A:** Type I (HPV-related): 50-70% of vulvar SCC; caused by HPV types 16 and 18 (90% of VIN contain HPV DNA); associated with usual/classical vulvar intraepithelial neoplasia (VIN); more common in younger patients. Type II (non-HPV-related): associated with vulvar dystrophy, differentiated VIN, and lichen sclerosus; typically postmenopausal patients.

Recognizing the two distinct etiological pathways has clinical implications: HPV-related vulvar SCC may be preventable via HPV vaccination, while non-HPV-related disease requires surveillance of at-risk conditions like lichen sclerosus. Both types are predominantly SCC (90% of vulvar malignancies).

**Q:** What is the most important prognostic factor in vulvar carcinoma, and what are the key features of the FIGO 2022 staging system?

**A:** Nodal involvement is the single most important prognostic factor, followed by tumour size. Lesions >4 cm are associated with poorer prognosis. FIGO 2022: Stage I = confined to vulva (IA: <2 cm and invasion <1 mm; IB: >2 cm or invasion >1 mm). Stage II = extension to lower 1/3 of urethra/vagina/anus with negative nodes. Stage III = any size with extension to adjacent structures or non-fixed/non-ulcerated lymph node metastases (IIIA: LN metastases <5 mm; IIIB: >5 mm; IIIC: extracapsular spread). Stage IV = fixed to bone, fixed/ulcerated LNs, or distant metastases. Overall 5-year survival: 79%.

The updated FIGO 2022 staging emphasizes nodal characteristics (size and extracapsular spread) in the Stage III substaging. Treatment is stage-dependent: Stage I → radical local excision; Stage II → modified radical vulvectomy; Stages III-IV → sentinel LN biopsy followed by surgical resection ± adjuvant chemotherapy/radiation.

**Q:** What are the most common site and histological type of primary vaginal carcinoma, and what are its risk factors?

**A:** Most common site: upper 1/3 of the posterior wall. Most common histology: SCC (80-90%). Risk factors: HPV infection (analogous to cervical cancer), prior history of cervical or vulvar cancer. More than 50% diagnosed between age 70-90. Primary vaginal carcinoma represents 2-3% of female genital tract malignancies; most vaginal cancers are metastatic from cervix, endometrium, ovary, or colon.

Because most vaginal cancers are secondary (metastatic), workup must always rule out disease on the cervix, vulva, or anus as the primary source. Schiller test (Lugol's iodine staining of normal squamous epithelium) and colposcopy with biopsy are used in diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the primary FIGO staging system used for ovarian cancer versus cervical cancer, and why?

**A:** Ovarian cancer uses surgical (pathological) staging because laparotomy is needed to fully evaluate the extent of disease. Cervical cancer uses clinical staging because it is most prevalent in developing countries that lack advanced imaging technologies (CT, MRI), facilitating consistent international staging.

Clinical staging of cervical cancer (FIGO 2018) allows all imaging modalities and pathological findings to inform staging, but the system was designed so that examination, colposcopy, biopsy, and basic imaging (CXR, IVP) alone are sufficient. Surgical staging of ovarian cancer accounts for the need for cytoreduction and allows assessment of retroperitoneal nodes.

**Q:** What is the Risk of Malignancy Index (RMI) and how is it calculated?

**A:** RMI = U × M × CA-125. U (ultrasound score): U=1 if 0 or 1 ultrasound features; U=4 if 2-5 features. Ultrasound features scored: multilocular cyst, solid areas, metastases, ascites, bilateral lesions. M (menopausal status): M=1 premenopausal; M=4 postmenopausal. CA-125: actual serum level in U/mL. RMI >200 warrants referral to gynecologic oncology.

The RMI combines three factors to stratify risk of ovarian malignancy before surgery. An RMI >200 has a sensitivity of approximately 70-80% and specificity of 90% for malignancy. It guides appropriate surgical triage: low-risk patients can be managed by general gynecologists, while high-risk patients require gynecologic oncologists.

**Q:** What is the goal of primary surgical treatment for advanced epithelial ovarian cancer, and what does evidence show about cytoreduction margins?

**A:** The goal of primary surgery is optimal cytoreduction with residual disease <1 cm (ideally 0 cm). Evidence shows: suboptimal cytoreduction (residual >1 cm) vs. optimal (<1 cm) results in reduced survival estimates favouring lower-volume disease. Borderline difference in progression-free survival when residual disease >2 cm vs. <2 cm is compared (p=0.05). No significant difference in overall survival between suboptimal debulking approaches.
