Cytoreductive surgery (debulking) for advanced epithelial ovarian cancer reduces tumour burden, improving sensitivity to subsequent platinum-based chemotherapy. Optimal debulking (ideally no residual macroscopic disease) is the primary surgical goal. The surgeon should attempt maximal resection including bowel resection, diaphragm stripping, and splenectomy when needed.

**Q:** What treatment is recommended for vulvar intraepithelial neoplasia (VIN) and what is the risk of progression to cancer?

**A:** VIN presents as multicentric white or pigmented plaques on the vulva (may only be visible at colposcopy). Treatment options: local excision (superficial vulvectomy ± split-thickness skin grafting for defects), ablative therapy (laser, cauterization), or local immunotherapy (imiquimod). Progression to invasive cancer rarely occurs with appropriate management.

VIN is a precancerous change; 90% of lesions contain HPV DNA (types 16, 18) in Type I disease. The choice of treatment depends on lesion size, location, and patient characteristics. Imiquimod (topical immune response modifier) is a non-surgical option that has shown efficacy in clearing HPV-associated VIN.

**Q:** What colposcopic features are used to identify suspicious cervical lesions requiring biopsy?

**A:** After application of acetic acid, suspicious features include: acetowhite lesions (abnormal cells appear white due to increased nuclear protein density), punctation (stippled red dots representing capillary loops), mosaicism (mosaic tile-like vascular pattern), and abnormal blood vessels (irregular, corkscrew-shaped vessels). Endocervical curettage (ECC) is performed if the entire lesion is not visible or no lesion is visible.

Colposcopy is a clinical procedure that facilitates identification and biopsy of suspicious cells. It is indicated after abnormal Pap results. The Bethesda Classification System is based on cytological results of Pap testing, while carcinoma is a histological diagnosis requiring tissue biopsy.

**Q:** What is the role of salpingectomy in ovarian cancer prevention?

**A:** Opportunistic salpingectomy (removal of fallopian tubes at time of other pelvic surgery) is recommended to prevent ovarian cancer, as emerging evidence shows that some serous ovarian cancers originate in the fallopian tube (specifically the fimbriated end). This is a prevention strategy offered to women who have completed childbearing.

The 'fallopian tube origin hypothesis' for high-grade serous ovarian cancer is supported by the finding of STIC (serous tubal intraepithelial carcinoma) lesions in the fallopian tubes of women with BRCA mutations. Bilateral salpingectomy with delayed oophorectomy is one risk-reduction strategy for BRCA carriers who wish to preserve ovarian function.

## Contraception & Family Planning

**Q:** What is the most effective reversible contraception method and how effective is it?

**A:** Intrauterine devices (LNG-IUS: 0.1-0.2% failure rate; copper IUD: 0.6-0.8% failure rate) and implants (etonogestrel implant: 0.05% failure rate) — all >99% effective as LARC (long-acting reversible contraception)

SOGC Contraception Guidelines: LARC methods (IUDs, implants) are most effective because they eliminate user error. Copper IUD is also the most effective emergency contraception (>99% if inserted within 5 days of unprotected intercourse).

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to combined oral contraceptive pills (COCPs)?

**A:** WHO Category 4: breastfeeding <6 weeks postpartum, hypertension ≥160/100, migraine with aura, stroke/TIA/DVT/PE history, ischemic heart disease, active liver disease/cancer, breast cancer, smoking ≥15 cigarettes/day age ≥35, major surgery with prolonged immobilization

SOGC/WHO MEC (Medical Eligibility Criteria) classify contraceptive use by condition. Category 4 = absolute contraindication; Category 3 = relative contraindication (risks usually outweigh benefits). Combined pills increase VTE and stroke risk in high-risk groups.

**Q:** What is emergency contraception and what options are available in Canada?

**A:** Emergency contraception (EC) options: 1) Levonorgestrel (Plan B One-Step) 1.5mg within 72h (reduced efficacy by 120h); 2) Ulipristal acetate (Ella) within 120h (more effective than LNG especially 72-120h); 3) Copper IUD within 120h (most effective >99%)

SOGC EC guidelines: levonorgestrel EC reduces pregnancy risk by 85-95% when used within 72h. Less effective with higher BMI (>75 kg). Ulipristal acetate is superior to LNG for women >75 kg. Copper IUD: most effective EC and provides ongoing contraception.

**Q:** A 16-year-old requests contraception. What are the ethical and legal considerations in Canada?

**A:** Minors can consent to contraception without parental involvement in all Canadian provinces — physician must assess for capacity and coercion; maintain confidentiality

Canadian law and SOGC guidelines support provision of contraceptive care to mature minors without parental consent. Mature minor doctrine applies. Assess for coercion or abuse. Maintain confidentiality. Ontario's Personal Health Information Protection Act (PHIPA) protects adolescent health information.

**Q:** What is progestin-only contraception and when is it preferred over combined pills?

**A:** Progestin-only pill (POP), progestin IUD (LNG-IUS), progestin implant, DMPA injection; preferred when estrogen is contraindicated: breastfeeding, migraine with aura, VTE history, cardiovascular disease, smoking ≥35 years

SOGC guidelines: progestin-only methods do not contain estrogen and are safe in situations where estrogen is contraindicated. LNG-IUS (Mirena): most effective progestin-only method, also treats heavy menstrual bleeding and dysmenorrhea.

**Q:** How does DMPA (Depo-Provera, medroxyprogesterone acetate) injection affect fertility?

**A:** DMPA (150 mg IM every 12 weeks): fertility return delayed up to 12-18 months after last injection; bone density temporarily reduced (reversible after discontinuation)

SOGC guidelines: DMPA is a highly effective (>99%) injectable contraceptive. Counseling about delayed fertility return is essential for women planning pregnancy within 12-18 months. Adolescents and women who may want pregnancy soon may prefer other methods.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and efficacy of copper IUD as an intrauterine device?

**A:** Copper IUD (e.g., Mona Lisa NT, IUD380): spermicidal (copper ions inhibit sperm motility), prevents fertilization, possible anti-implantation effect; 10-year efficacy >99%, failure rate 0.6-0.8%/year

SOGC IUD guidelines: copper IUD is inserted by trained providers during any phase of menstrual cycle or up to 5 days after unprotected sex (EC use). Side effects: heavier, more painful periods (opposite of LNG-IUS). No hormones — preferred by women who want hormone-free contraception.

**Q:** A patient asks about sterilization (tubal ligation). What should the counseling include?

**A:** Tubal ligation is a permanent surgical method (assume irreversible); very effective (failure rate <0.5%); inform about failure risk (ectopic pregnancy risk if failure), alternatives (IUD equally effective and reversible), no protection against STIs

SOGC sterilization guidelines: patients should be counseled that sterilization is intended to be permanent. Regret rates: 5-26%, higher in women <30 and those having the procedure postpartum or after a recent pregnancy loss. Vasectomy for male partner is safer with lower failure rate.

**Q:** What contraceptive options are safest for a breastfeeding mother 6 weeks postpartum?

**A:** Progestin-only methods (LNG-IUS, etonogestrel implant, POP, DMPA) or copper IUD — combined OCP contraindicated in first 6 weeks postpartum (breastfeeding and VTE risk)

SOGC Postpartum Contraception Guidelines: Breastfeeding provides some contraception (LAM: lactational amenorrhea method) — >98% effective if exclusive BF, amenorrheic, and <6 months postpartum. LARC methods (IUD, implant) can be inserted immediately postpartum (within 10 min of placental delivery) or delayed to 4 weeks.

**Q:** A woman with lupus (SLE) asks about contraception options. What is appropriate?

**A:** Active severe SLE: combined OCP is WHO category 3-4 (avoid if antiphospholipid antibodies, thrombocytopenia, or nephritis); progestin-only IUD or copper IUD are preferred

SOGC/WHO MEC guidelines for SLE: SLE without severe features and negative antiphospholipid antibodies — combined OCP may be used (category 2-3). SLE with antiphospholipid antibodies: combined OCP is contraindicated (category 4) due to VTE/thrombosis risk. LARC methods are safest.

**Q:** What are the available contraceptive methods ranked by effectiveness?

**A:** Most effective (>99%): IUD (copper and LNG), implant (Nexplanon), sterilization. Very effective (91-99% typical use): DMPA injection, combined OCP, patch, ring. Moderate (80-91%): condoms, diaphragm, withdrawal, fertility awareness. Emergency: copper IUD (most effective), ulipristal (ella, up to 5 days), levonorgestrel (Plan B, up to 3 days).

SOGC contraception guidelines emphasize long-acting reversible contraception (LARC: IUD, implant) as first-line due to highest effectiveness and no adherence issues. Canadian data shows 9% typical-use failure rate for OCPs. Contraceptive counseling should use shared decision-making approach.

**Q:** What are the absolute and relative contraindications to combined hormonal contraception (CHC)?

**A:** Absolute (WHO Category 4): migraine with aura, VTE history/active, known thrombophilia, SLE with antiphospholipid antibodies, breast cancer, smoking >=15 cig/day AND age >=35, uncontrolled HTN, major surgery with prolonged immobilization, <21 days postpartum. Relative (Category 3): controlled HTN, migraine without aura in >=35, obesity, smoking <15 cig/day AND >=35.

SOGC uses WHO Medical Eligibility Criteria (MEC) framework. Category 1: no restriction. 2: generally use. 3: risks usually outweigh benefits. 4: unacceptable risk. CHC is safe for most women. Estrogen component increases VTE risk 3-4x (absolute risk still low: 3-9 per 10,000 woman-years).

**Q:** What are the types of IUDs available in Canada and their key features?

**A:** Copper IUD (Mona Lisa, Flexi-T): hormone-free, effective for 5-10 years, may increase menstrual bleeding/cramping, can be used for emergency contraception. LNG-IUS 52mg (Mirena): 5 years, reduces menstrual bleeding (therapeutic for HMB), amenorrhea in 20-50%. LNG-IUS 19.5mg (Kyleena): 5 years, smaller, less amenorrhea. LNG-IUS 13.5mg (Jaydess): 3 years, smallest.

SOGC recommends IUDs as first-line contraception for all ages including nulliparous women and adolescents. IUDs do NOT increase PID risk (only slightly elevated in first 20 days after insertion). IUDs can be inserted at any time in the cycle. Expulsion rate: 2-10% in first year.

**Q:** What is the approach to emergency contraception in Canada?

**A:** Copper IUD: most effective (>99%), can insert up to 7 days post-intercourse. Ulipristal acetate (ella) 30mg: effective up to 5 days, progesterone receptor modulator, more effective than LNG for BMI >26. Levonorgestrel (Plan B) 1.5mg: available OTC, effective up to 3 days (best <24h), less effective with BMI >25. Yuzpe method: less effective, more side effects.

SOGC emergency contraception guidelines recommend copper IUD as most effective option. Ulipristal requires prescription in Canada. LNG (Plan B) is available without prescription. After LNG, hormonal contraception can start next day. After ulipristal, wait 5 days before starting hormonal contraception.

**Q:** What is the quick-start method for initiating contraception?

**A:** Start hormonal contraception on the day of the visit regardless of cycle day (not waiting for next menses). Requirements: reasonably exclude pregnancy (negative urine hCG, no intercourse since last period, or consistent contraception use). Use backup method (condoms) for first 7 days. Preferred approach per SOGC for improving uptake and continuation.

SOGC guidelines endorse quick-start to reduce unintended pregnancy during the waiting period. Traditional Sunday-start or first-day-of-period start methods lead to unnecessary delays and missed opportunities. Same-day IUD insertion is also recommended when pregnancy is reasonably excluded.

**Q:** What is the management of a missed combined oral contraceptive pill?

**A:** 1 missed pill: take immediately, continue pack, no backup needed. 2 missed pills (or 1 missed in first week): take most recent missed pill, continue pack, use backup x 7 days, consider EC if unprotected intercourse in past 5 days. >=3 missed pills (or >=2 in first week): take most recent, continue pack, backup x 7 days, EC if indicated, skip placebo if in week 3.

SOGC missed pill guidelines simplified in 2015. First-week missed pills are highest risk due to extending the hormone-free interval. The 7-day rule (backup for 7 days after any significant disruption) is the unifying principle. Extended or continuous regimens reduce missed-pill risk.

**Q:** What postpartum contraception options are available and when can they be started?

**A:** Immediately postpartum: copper IUD or LNG-IUS (within 10 min of placenta delivery or within 48h). Progestin-only methods (POP, implant, DMPA): anytime postpartum. CHC (estrogen-containing): NOT before 21 days postpartum (VTE risk), not before 42 days if additional VTE risk factors. Breastfeeding: progestin-only preferred; CHC may reduce milk supply (avoid <6 weeks).

SOGC recommends postpartum contraception counseling antenatally and before hospital discharge. Immediate postpartum IUD insertion has higher expulsion rates (10-20%) but improves contraception uptake. Ovulation can occur as early as 21 days postpartum in non-breastfeeding women.

**Q:** What contraceptive counseling is needed for women with medical comorbidities?

**A:** Epilepsy on enzyme-inducers: avoid CHC or use higher-dose, consider IUD/DMPA. Migraine with aura: no estrogen (IUD, POP, implant, DMPA). Diabetes: CHC acceptable if no vascular complications. HTN: progestin-only if uncontrolled. VTE history: no estrogen (IUD, POP, implant). Breast cancer: IUD copper only (no hormonal).

SOGC uses WHO MEC criteria for medical eligibility. The copper IUD has no medical contraindications except active PID, uterine anomalies, or Wilson disease. LNG-IUS has fewer systemic effects than other hormonal methods. DMPA may decrease bone density with long-term use.

**Q:** What are the non-contraceptive benefits of hormonal contraception?

**A:** CHC: reduces dysmenorrhea, menorrhagia, acne, hirsutism, ovarian cysts, endometrial and ovarian cancer risk (50% reduction with 5 years use), PMS/PMDD. LNG-IUS: treats menorrhagia (90% reduction), endometrial hyperplasia protection, dysmenorrhea. DMPA: reduces endometriosis pain, menorrhagia, sickle cell crises.

SOGC and CFPC guidelines encourage discussion of non-contraceptive benefits. Ovarian cancer risk reduction persists 30+ years after CHC discontinuation. LNG-IUS is first-line treatment for heavy menstrual bleeding per SOGC guidelines. Continuous OCP use eliminates withdrawal bleeding and associated symptoms.

**Q:** What is the approach to contraception in adolescents?

**A:** All methods are safe for adolescents. SOGC recommends LARC (IUD, implant) as first-line. Confidentiality is paramount-mature minor doctrine applies in most Canadian provinces. Dual protection (hormonal + condoms for STI prevention) recommended. DMPA: use with caution in <18 due to bone density effects. Emergency contraception: should be discussed proactively.

Canadian law generally allows minors to consent to contraception without parental involvement if they understand the risks and benefits (mature minor doctrine). School-based health centres in many Canadian provinces provide contraception. SOGC emphasizes removing barriers to adolescent contraception access.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to IUD insertion (USMEC category 4)?

**A:** Current pregnancy, current PID/STI (delay insertion), unexplained vaginal bleeding (investigate first), cervical/endometrial cancer, uterine cavity distortion. Copper IUD: Wilson disease

Hormonal IUD (Mirena/Kyleena): additional benefit of reduced menstrual bleeding. Can be inserted immediately postpartum or post-abortion. Nulliparity is NOT a contraindication. Expulsion rate 2-10%.

**Q:** What are the USMEC (or Canadian equivalent) categories for contraceptive eligibility?

**A:** Category 1: no restriction. Category 2: advantages generally outweigh risks. Category 3: risks generally outweigh advantages (relative contraindication). Category 4: unacceptable health risk (absolute contraindication). SOGC uses WHO Medical Eligibility Criteria adapted for Canadian practice.

MEC categories guide contraceptive prescribing in women with medical conditions. Key Category 4 examples for combined hormonal contraception: migraine with aura, VTE history, breast cancer, smoking >=15 cig/day if age >=35. Progestin-only methods have fewer Category 4 conditions.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to copper IUD and levonorgestrel IUD insertion?

**A:** Both: current pregnancy, current PID/STI (treat first then insert), unexplained uterine bleeding (investigate first), uterine cavity distortion (large submucosal fibroids), cervical/endometrial cancer. Additional for LNG-IUD: current breast cancer (Category 4). Copper IUD: Wilson disease.

SOGC guidelines emphasize that IUDs are safe for nulliparous women, adolescents, and immediately post-abortion. Past PID is NOT a contraindication. STI screening can be done at time of insertion without delaying placement. Quick start (same-day) insertion improves uptake.

**Q:** What are the emergency contraception options available in Canada and their time windows?

**A:** Levonorgestrel 1.5 mg (Plan B): up to 72 hours, efficacy decreases with time. Ulipristal acetate 30 mg (Ella): up to 120 hours, maintains efficacy throughout. Copper IUD: up to 7 days post-unprotected intercourse, most effective method (>99%), can serve as ongoing contraception.

SOGC recommends copper IUD as most effective EC. Ulipristal is more effective than levonorgestrel at 72-120 hours. Levonorgestrel is less effective in women >75 kg; ulipristal is less effective >90 kg — copper IUD preferred for higher BMI. Hormonal EC is available OTC in Canada.

**Q:** What is the recommended timing for postpartum contraception initiation?

**A:** IUD (copper or LNG): immediately post-placental or at 4-6 weeks. Progestin-only methods (POP, implant, DMPA): immediately postpartum regardless of breastfeeding. Combined hormonal contraception: avoid until 6 weeks postpartum (VTE risk) if breastfeeding, or 3 weeks if not breastfeeding and no VTE risk factors.

SOGC guidelines emphasize that rapid-repeat pregnancy (<18 months interpregnancy interval) increases adverse outcomes. Immediate post-placental IUD insertion is safe and reduces loss to follow-up. Etonogestrel implant (Nexplanon) can be inserted before discharge. DMPA does not impair lactation.

**Q:** A 38-year-old smoker (20 cigarettes/day) with migraines with aura requests contraception. What methods are appropriate?

**A:** Combined hormonal methods (pill, patch, ring) are Category 4 (absolutely contraindicated) due to both migraine with aura AND smoking >15 cig/day at age >=35 — each independently is Category 4. Safe options: copper IUD, LNG-IUD, progestin-only pill, DMPA, etonogestrel implant.

Migraine with aura increases ischemic stroke risk 2-4 fold; combined estrogen further increases this risk. SOGC and WHO MEC are clear that estrogen-containing methods are contraindicated. Progestin-only methods do not increase stroke risk. IUDs are first-line long-acting options for this patient.

**Q:** What is the 'quick start' method for initiating contraception?

**A:** Start contraception on the day of the visit regardless of menstrual cycle day, after reasonably excluding pregnancy (negative urine hCG, no unprotected intercourse in past 14 days). Use backup method (condoms) for 7 days. Pregnancy test repeated in 3-4 weeks.

SOGC supports quick start to reduce barriers and improve contraception uptake. Traditional advice to wait for next menses leads to interval pregnancies. Quick start applies to OCPs, patch, ring, DMPA, and implant. IUDs can also be inserted same-day with similar approach.

**Q:** What are the advantages and counselling points for the etonogestrel subdermal implant (Nexplanon)?

**A:** Advantages: >99% efficacy, lasts 3 years, rapid return to fertility, progestin-only (safe with estrogen contraindications), low maintenance. Counselling: irregular bleeding is the most common reason for removal (occurs in up to 30%), weight gain is minimal, no protection against STIs.

SOGC recommends LARC (IUDs, implant) as first-line contraception for all women including adolescents. The implant has the lowest failure rate of any reversible method. Anticipatory counselling about bleeding pattern changes reduces dissatisfaction and early removal. Available in Canada since 2020.

**Q:** A woman on combined OCP requires treatment with rifampin for latent TB. What contraceptive counselling is needed?

**A:** Rifampin is a potent CYP3A4 inducer that significantly reduces OCP efficacy — this is a Category 3 interaction. Switch to a non-affected method: copper IUD, LNG-IUD, or DMPA (depot is adequate dose). If continuing OCP, use additional barrier method throughout treatment and for 28 days after stopping rifampin.

SOGC and WHO MEC specifically address enzyme-inducing drug interactions. Rifampin is the strongest inducer and uniquely affects DMPA less. Other enzyme inducers (certain anticonvulsants: carbamazepine, phenytoin) also reduce efficacy. IUDs are unaffected by drug interactions and are preferred.

**Q:** What is the SOGC recommendation on DMPA (Depo-Provera) and bone mineral density?

**A:** DMPA is associated with decreased BMD (2-8% over 2 years), partially reversible after discontinuation. SOGC does not restrict use duration in adult women. In adolescents (<18), other methods preferred but DMPA acceptable if alternatives unsuitable. Routine DEXA scanning not recommended.

Health Canada issued a black box warning in 2005 regarding BMD loss, but SOGC and WHO concluded that benefits generally outweigh risks even for prolonged use. BMD loss stabilizes and partially recovers. No evidence of increased fracture risk has been demonstrated in epidemiologic studies.

**Q:** What contraceptive options are safe for a woman with a history of VTE currently on anticoagulation?

**A:** Safe options (Category 1-2): copper IUD, LNG-IUD, progestin-only pill, etonogestrel implant. Contraindicated (Category 4): all combined hormonal contraception (OCP, patch, ring). DMPA is Category 2-3 depending on guideline.

Estrogen-containing methods increase VTE risk 3-4 fold and are absolutely contraindicated with VTE history. SOGC recommends LARC as first-line. LNG-IUD is not associated with systemic levels of progestin sufficient to affect coagulation. Copper IUD is the most evidence-based non-hormonal option.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to combined hormonal contraceptives (CHC)?

**A:** 1. <4 weeks postpartum if breastfeeding, or <21 days postpartum if not breastfeeding
2. Major surgery with prolonged immobilization
3. Known/suspected pregnancy
4. Undiagnosed abnormal vaginal bleeding
5. Prior thromboembolic events or thromboembolic disorders (Factor V Leiden mutation; protein C, S, or antithrombin III deficiency; active thrombophlebitis)
6. Cerebrovascular or coronary artery disease
7. Estrogen-dependent tumours (breast, uterus)
8. Impaired liver function with acute liver disease
9. Congenital hypertriglyceridemia
10. Smoker age >35 yr
11. Migraines with focal neurological symptoms (excluding aura)
12. Uncontrolled HTN

Combined hormonal contraceptives increase thrombotic risk due to estrogen. These absolute contraindications reflect situations where that additional risk is unacceptable. Smokers over 35 and those with migraines with aura have substantially elevated stroke risk. These align with Canadian and WHO Medical Eligibility Criteria (MEC) Category 4 conditions.

**Q:** A 38-year-old smoker requests combined oral contraceptive pills (OCP). What is the contraindication, and what alternative should be offered?

**A:** Smoking at age >35 is an absolute contraindication to CHC due to markedly elevated risk of stroke and MI. Offer progestin-only methods (progestin-only pill, Depo-Provera, Nexplanon implant, or levonorgestrel IUS) as they have no estrogen-related thrombotic risk.

The combination of estrogen-containing contraceptives and smoking in women >35 yr creates a synergistic increase in arterial thrombotic events. Progestin-only contraceptives are safe in this population as progestins do not significantly increase arterial thrombotic risk.

**Q:** What are the advantages of combined oral contraceptive pills (OCPs) beyond contraception?

**A:** Non-contraceptive benefits include:
- Cycle regulation
- Decreased dysmenorrhea and heavy menstrual bleeding (less anemia)
- Decreased benign breast disease and ovarian cyst development
- Decreased risk of ovarian and endometrial cancer
- Increased cervical mucus (may lower STI risk)
- Decreased PMS symptoms
- Less acne
- Possible osteoporosis protection

OCPs suppress ovulation and regulate hormonal fluctuations, which confers protection against hormone-sensitive cancers and cycle-related symptoms. These benefits inform clinical decisions when recommending OCPs for non-contraceptive indications (e.g., dysmenorrhea, PCOS, endometriosis).

**Q:** What drugs can decrease the efficacy of combined hormonal contraceptives (CHC), requiring use of a back-up method?

**A:** Rifampin, phenobarbital, phenytoin, griseofulvin, primidone, and St. John's wort can decrease CHC efficacy via hepatic enzyme induction (CYP450 induction increases OCP metabolism).

These drugs induce hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, accelerating estrogen and progestin metabolism and reducing contraceptive blood levels below effective thresholds. Patients on these medications should use a barrier method or consider a non-hormonal IUD.

**Q:** What is the missed pill protocol for a combined OCP (e.g., Alesse) if 1 pill is missed within the first week of the cycle?

**A:** Take 1 pill as soon as possible and continue taking 1 pill daily. Emergency postcoital contraception (EPC) may be necessary if unprotected intercourse occurred in the preceding week.

Missing a pill in the first week is highest risk because the hormone-free interval just ended and ovulation suppression may be insufficient. EPC should be considered if unprotected sex occurred. Missing in weeks 2–3 requires taking the missed pill ASAP and continuing the pack without a hormone-free interval.

**Q:** What is the Slynd progestin-only pill (POP), and what is its key advantage over older POPs?

**A:** Slynd contains 4 mg drospirenone (24 active + 4 placebo pills). Its key advantage is a 24-hour missed pill window, compared to the previous 3-hour window of older POPs (e.g., norethindrone). It is best for patients who need to avoid estrogen.

The longer missed-pill window improves adherence and reduces user error. Drospirenone has anti-androgenic and anti-mineralocorticoid properties. Slynd is suitable for breastfeeding women, those with hypertension, or those with contraindications to estrogen.

**Q:** Compare Mirena and Kyleena levonorgestrel IUS — which patients is each best suited for?

**A:** Mirena IUS (LNG 52 mcg): best for patients with heavy or painful periods, parous patients; 20% amenorrhea rate; approved up to 8 years.
Kyleena IUS (LNG 19.5 mcg): best for nulliparous patients who want a light period monthly; primarily looking for birth control with lower hormone dose.

Mirena delivers a higher daily dose of levonorgestrel, leading to greater endometrial suppression and higher amenorrhea rates — ideal for menorrhagia. Kyleena's lower dose suits patients wanting lighter but persistent periods. Both are highly effective reversible long-acting contraceptives (LARCs).

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to insertion of either a copper IUD or progesterone IUS?

**A:** Absolute contraindications for BOTH:
- Known or suspected pregnancy
- Undiagnosed genital tract bleeding
- Acute or chronic PID
- Lifestyle risk for STIs
- Known distorted uterine cavity
- Immediately post-septic abortion

Additional for Copper IUS only:
- Known allergy to copper or Wilson's disease

IUS insertion in the setting of infection risks ascending infection and sepsis. An undisturbed uterine cavity is required for proper IUS positioning. Copper IUS is contraindicated with Wilson's disease due to impaired copper metabolism leading to systemic copper toxicity.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action and success rate of Plan B (levonorgestrel) emergency postcoital contraception?

**A:** Plan B (levonorgestrel 750 mcg q12h for 2 doses, or both together): suppresses ovulation, alters endometrium, affects sperm/ova transport. 75–95% effective if used within 24h. Can be used up to 5 days after unprotected intercourse. Less effective if weight >75 kg; not recommended if >80 kg.

Plan B primarily works by delaying or preventing ovulation — it does not disrupt an established pregnancy. Efficacy decreases significantly with time. Body weight affects efficacy because the fixed dose results in lower serum levels in heavier individuals. Ulipristal or copper IUD should be considered in those over 75 kg.

**Q:** What is ulipristal acetate (Ella) and how does it differ from Plan B for emergency contraception?

**A:** Ulipristal (30 mg PO) is a selective progesterone receptor modulator (SPRM). It delays ovulation by up to 5 days. Used within 5 days of unprotected intercourse. After taking ulipristal, combined OCP must be delayed by 5 days due to mechanism of action. It is not less effective based on weight (unlike Plan B).

Ulipristal's antiprogestin activity allows it to delay ovulation even after the LH surge has begun, potentially making it more effective than Plan B when taken later. Concurrent progestins compete at the receptor, reducing ulipristal's efficacy — hence the delay before resuming hormonal contraception.

**Q:** What is the most effective emergency postcoital contraception method and its time window?

**A:** The copper IUD (postcoital IUD) is the most effective emergency contraception, with a 1% failure rate. It must be inserted within 7 days of unprotected intercourse. It prevents implantation via a foreign body reaction and sperm toxicity.

The copper IUD provides near-complete protection and has the added benefit of providing ongoing long-term contraception. It is the preferred option for patients >80 kg where levonorgestrel EPC has reduced efficacy. Cervical swabs for gonorrhea and chlamydia should be done prior to insertion.

**Q:** What is the Depo-Provera contraceptive, and what are its advantages and disadvantages?

**A:** Depo-Provera: depot medroxyprogesterone acetate 150 mg IM every 12 weeks.
Advantages: suppresses ovulation, 70% amenorrhea after 1–2 years, does not affect breast milk, effective for dysmenorrhea.
Disadvantages: irregular breakthrough bleeding, weight gain, controversial decreased bone density (often reversible), return to fertility may take up to 1–2 years.
Not recommended for long-term use due to bone density concerns.

Depo-Provera is highly effective and convenient (quarterly dosing). The delayed return to fertility (up to 2 years) is important to counsel patients who may desire pregnancy soon. Bone density concerns (reduced due to estrogen suppression) have led to recommendations against long-term use, particularly in adolescents.

**Q:** What is Nexplanon and what are its advantages and disadvantages as a contraceptive?

**A:** Nexplanon: 4 cm etonogestrel 68 mg subdermal implant placed in inner arm; lasts up to 3 years.
Advantages: does not affect breast milk, no intrauterine placement needed, good bleeding and pain control, no change in bone density, quick return to fertility after removal.
Disadvantages: irregular breakthrough bleeding, weight gain.

Nexplanon is one of the most effective reversible contraceptive methods (>99% efficacy). Unlike Depo-Provera, it does not suppress estrogen enough to cause bone density loss. It is an excellent option for patients wanting long-term contraception without intrauterine placement.

## Obgyn Ob

**Q:** What conditions prolong Succinylcholine blockade?

**A:** Decreased plasma pseudocholinesterase quantity (hereditary deficiency, liver disease, pregnancy, malignancy, malnutrition, collagen vascular disease) or impaired enzyme activity (genetically inherited)

These conditions reduce the rate of SCh metabolism, extending its duration of action.

**Q:** A spider angioma is associated with which hyperestrogenic states?

**A:** Liver disease, pregnancy, and oral contraceptive pill use

Spider angiomas develop due to hyperestrogenic states that cause arteriolar dilation, though they can also occur idiopathically.

**Q:** What conditions are associated with spider angioma (Campbell Telangiectasia)?

**A:** Hyperestrogenic states such as liver disease, pregnancy, and oral contraceptive use

Spider angiomas are associated with hyperestrogenic states. While they can be associated with liver disease, pregnancy, or OCP use, they are more often idiopathic with no underlying pathology.

**Q:** What condition is associated with spider angiomas in the setting of hyperestrogenic states?

**A:** Liver disease, pregnancy, and oral contraceptive use

Spider angiomas (Campbell telangiectasias) can be associated with hyperestrogenic states including chronic liver disease, pregnancy, and OCP use. However, they also commonly occur without any underlying pathology.

**Q:** What conditions are associated with spider angiomas (Campbell telangiectasia)?

**A:** Hyperestrogenic states including liver disease, pregnancy, and oral contraceptive use. However, spider angiomas are often idiopathic.

Spider angiomas occur when a central arteriole dilates with slender radiating branches. They blanch with pressure applied to the center.

**Q:** What laboratory finding is characteristic of Sézary Syndrome?

**A:** WBC >20 x 10^9/L with Sézary cells (atypical circulating T-cells), and CD4:CD8 ratio >10 on flow cytometry.

Sézary syndrome is the leukemic variant of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma characterized by erythroderma and circulating malignant T-cells.

**Q:** What monitoring is required before and during acitretin therapy?

**A:** Baseline: LFTs, fasting lipid panel, pregnancy test ×2, glucose, radiography if long-term. Ongoing: LFTs and lipids q1-2wk until stable, then as indicated. Pregnancy test q3mo if >3yr after discontinuation.

Acitretin is highly teratogenic. Women of childbearing potential require strict contraception. The drug has significant interactions with methotrexate, tetracyclines, and other systemic retinoids.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for methotrexate therapy?

**A:** Pregnancy test at baseline, CBC at baseline then q6mo or more frequently, BUN/Cr and LFTs at baseline then q4-8wk

Methotrexate 10-25mg weekly (max 30mg/wk) is used for psoriasis and AD. Folic acid supplementation is given to minimize side effects. More frequent monitoring is needed with dose changes or elevated serum levels.

**Q:** What monitoring parameters are required for methotrexate in psoriasis?

**A:** Pregnancy test at baseline, CBC, BUN/Cr, and LFTs at baseline then q4-8 weeks

Methotrexate requires regular hematologic and hepatic monitoring due to risks of bone marrow suppression and hepatotoxicity, especially with weekly dosing.

**Q:** What are the critical monitoring requirements for isotretinoin in females of childbearing age?

**A:** Pregnancy test x2 at baseline, then monthly during treatment; TWO forms of reliable contraception required (highly teratogenic); fasting lipid panel and LFTs q1-2 weeks until stable; glucose if risk factors

Isotretinoin is highly teratogenic (category X) causing severe birth defects. Strict pregnancy prevention protocols are mandatory including dual contraception and monthly pregnancy testing.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for methotrexate in dermatology?

**A:** Baseline: pregnancy test, CBC, BUN/Cr, LFTs; then CBC q6 months or more frequently if dose changes or risk factors; BUN/Cr, LFTs q4-8 weeks

Methotrexate is a folate antagonist used for psoriasis and atopic dermatitis. Regular monitoring is essential due to risks of myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, and pulmonary toxicity.

**Q:** What monitoring is required for acitretin therapy?

**A:** Baseline LFTs, fasting lipid panel, pregnancy test (x2), then LFTs and lipids q1-2 weeks until stable, pregnancy test q3 months during treatment and >3 years after discontinuation

Acitretin requires extensive monitoring due to hepatotoxicity, hyperlipidemia, and teratogenicity. Women of childbearing potential require strict contraceptive measures

**Q:** What are the contraindications for isotretinoin?

**A:** Pregnancy - teratogenic. Sexually active females require 2 forms of reliable contraception

Isotretinoin is highly teratogenic. Females of childbearing potential must use two forms of contraception. Generally unsafe in lactation

**Q:** What monitoring is required for methotrexate?

**A:** Pregnancy test at baseline; CBC at baseline then q6 months; BUN/Cr, LFTs at baseline then q4-8 weeks

Methotrexate requires monitoring for bone marrow suppression, hepatotoxicity, and renal toxicity. Folic acid is given to minimize side effects

**Q:** What is HELLP syndrome and when is it seen?

**A:** Hemolytic anemia, elevated liver enzymes, low platelets - seen only in preeclampsia/eclampsia

HELLP syndrome is a severe form of preeclampsia affecting multiple organ systems and requires urgent obstetric management.

**Q:** What are the causes/precipitants of hypertensive emergencies?

**A:** Causes include: essential HTN, emotional exertion, pain, sympathomimetic drugs (cocaine, amphetamines), MAOI with tyramine, pheochromocytoma, and pregnancy (preeclampsia/eclampsia).

Identifying the precipitant is essential for targeted treatment.

**Q:** What are the posterior pituitary hormones and their functions?

**A:** ADH (antidiuretic hormone - regulates water balance) and oxytocin (needed for labor and breastfeeding)

Unlike anterior pituitary hormones, these are made in the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior pituitary.

**Q:** What is the role of oxytocin and what deficiency symptoms occur?

**A:** Needed for labor and breastfeeding; deficiency is usually asymptomatic

Oxytocin deficiency is rare and primarily affects uterine contraction during labor and milk ejection during breastfeeding.

**Q:** What is the recommended first-line treatment for smoking cessation in pregnancy?

**A:** Counselling is recommended as first-line; NRT should be made available if non-pharmacologic methods fail

While there's no strong evidence of major positive or negative outcomes with bupropion or varenicline in pregnancy, benefits must outweigh risks.

**Q:** What does GGT and MCV laboratory testing assess in alcohol use disorder?

**A:** Baseline and follow-up monitoring

GGT is sensitive to alcohol-induced liver injury; MCV reflects macrocytosis from alcohol.

**Q:** When should pregnant individuals be screened for gestational diabetes?

**A:** Between 24-28 weeks gestation

All pregnant individuals should be screened for gestational diabetes between 24-28 weeks gestation using a 75g oral glucose tolerance test.

**Q:** When should gestational diabetes be screened?

**A:** Between 24-28 weeks gestation

All pregnant individuals should be screened for gestational diabetes during this gestational window.

**Q:** What should home glucose monitors be calibrated against?

**A:** Laboratory blood glucose

Home glucose monitors should be calibrated against laboratory blood glucose measurements periodically.

**Q:** What is the gestational diabetes screening recommendation?

**A:** Screen all pregnant individuals between 24-28 weeks gestation

Gestational diabetes screening is recommended for all pregnant individuals between 24-28 weeks gestation.

**Q:** What are the five components of the sexual history (5 P's)?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal insertive/receptive), Protection, Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention

The 5 P's provide a structured approach to obtaining a comprehensive sexual history.

**Q:** What are the 5 P's of sexual history?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal insertive/receptive), Protection, Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention

Structured approach to sexual history taking for STI risk assessment.

**Q:** What is the '5 P's' approach to sexual history?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal insertive/receptive), Protection, Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention

The 5 P's provide a structured framework for comprehensive sexual history taking.

**Q:** What does the 5 P's sexual history include?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal), Past history of STIs, Protection, Pregnancy prevention

This framework ensures comprehensive sexual history taking.

**Q:** What laboratory tests are useful for monitoring alcohol use?

**A:** GGT, MCV, AST, ALT, CBC, INR

GGT and MCV are sensitive markers. Elevated AST with AST:ALT ratio ~2:1 suggests alcohol use. CBC may show anemia or thrombocytopenia; INR assesses liver synthetic function.

**Q:** When is treatment for bacterial vaginosis (BV) required?

**A:** Treatment is not required for asymptomatic patients unless high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

BV is caused by overgrowth of Gardnerella vaginalis, Mycoplasma hominis, and anaerobes. Asymptomatic BV does not require treatment but should be treated in certain high-risk obstetric/gynecologic scenarios.

**Q:** What did the CHAP trial conclude about treating mild chronic hypertension in pregnancy?

**A:** Treating mild chronic hypertension (BP <140/90 mmHg) with antihypertensives improved pregnancy outcomes, reducing the primary outcome (preeclampsia, preterm birth, placental abruption, fetal death) from 37% to 30.2%

CHAP showed that treating mild chronic hypertension (traditionally only severe HTN was treated) improves maternal and fetal outcomes.

**Q:** What are the five 'P's of sexual history?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal insertive/receptive), Protection (pregnancy prevention), Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention

The 5 P's provide a structured approach to obtaining a comprehensive sexual history.

**Q:** What is Kussmaul breathing?

**A:** Deep, labored breathing pattern seen in metabolic acidosis

Kussmaul breathing is a compensatory mechanism to expel CO2 and correct metabolic acidosis in DKA.

**Q:** When should asymptomatic bacterial vaginosis be treated?

**A:** Treatment is unnecessary unless high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

BV can be a normal vaginal flora variant in asymptomatic patients. Treatment is indicated in specific high-risk scenarios to prevent complications.

**Q:** What did the CHAP trial find about treating mild chronic hypertension in pregnancy?

**A:** Treating mild chronic hypertension (BP <140/90mmHg) reduced the primary outcome (preeclampsia, preterm birth, placental abruption, fetal/neonatal death) from 37.0% to 30.2%

The CHAP trial established that treating mild chronic hypertension in pregnancy improves maternal and fetal outcomes.

**Q:** What is the treatment for herpes during pregnancy with prior infection in the past year?

**A:** Acyclovir 800mg PO BID x 5 days OR 800mg PO TID x 2 days OR Valacyclovir 500mg PO BID x 3 days OR 1g PO daily x 5 days

Suppressive therapy at 36 weeks is recommended for patients with prior herpes infection to reduce recurrence and viral shedding at delivery.

**Q:** What does the 5P's sexual history represent?

**A:** Partners (numbers, gender), Practices (vaginal, oral, anal), Protection, Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention

The 5P's provides a structured approach to sexual history taking for comprehensive STI assessment.

**Q:** When should bacterial vaginosis be treated?

**A:** Treat only if symptomatic, unless high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

Asymptomatic BV does not require treatment in most cases. Treatment is indicated in specific high-risk situations to prevent complications.

**Q:** What is the treatment for first-episode genital herpes during pregnancy?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID for 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1 g PO BID for 7-10 days

First-episode herpes during pregnancy requires antiviral treatment to reduce viral load and risk of transmission.

**Q:** What is the suppressive therapy regimen for genital herpes in pregnancy at 36 weeks?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID daily starting at 36 weeks gestation

Suppressive therapy at 36 weeks reduces viral shedding and risk of neonatal herpes at delivery.

**Q:** What did the CHAP trial demonstrate about treating mild chronic hypertension in pregnancy?

**A:** Treating mild chronic hypertension (BP 140/90 mmHg) during pregnancy reduced primary outcome (preeclampsia, preterm birth, placental abruption, fetal death) from 37% to 30.2%

CHAP changed practice by supporting treatment of mild (not just severe) hypertension in pregnancy.

**Q:** When is screening for gestational diabetes recommended?

**A:** Between 24-28 weeks gestation for all pregnant individuals

Universal screening for gestational diabetes occurs between 24-28 weeks gestation to identify and manage glucose intolerance in pregnancy.

**Q:** When is treatment required for asymptomatic bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Treatment is unnecessary unless high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

Asymptomatic BV does not require treatment except in specific high-risk obstetric or gynecologic situations.

**Q:** What is the treatment for herpes genitalis in pregnancy with primary infection?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1g PO BID x7-10 days

Treatment of primary HSV in pregnancy is recommended to reduce viral shedding and risk of transmission.

**Q:** What is the treatment for herpes in pregnancy with primary episode?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID for 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1g PO BID for 7-10 days

Treatment of primary HSV in pregnancy is important to reduce viral shedding and risk of neonatal transmission.

**Q:** What is suppressive therapy for herpes in pregnancy at 36 weeks?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID at 36 weeks

Suppressive therapy near term reduces HSV recurrence and viral shedding at delivery, reducing need for cesarean section.

**Q:** When is treatment for bacterial vaginosis (BV) indicated in asymptomatic patients?

**A:** Treat asymptomatic BV only if: high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

BV is treated in symptomatic patients. Asymptomatic non-pregnant patients typically do not require treatment due to risks outweighing benefits.

**Q:** What is the treatment for genital herpes in pregnancy (first episode)?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1 g PO BID x 7-10 days

First-episode herpes in pregnancy requires treatment to reduce viral shedding and potential transmission to newborn.

**Q:** When is treatment for bacterial vaginosis required in asymptomatic patients?

**A:** Treatment is unnecessary unless: high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

BV is associated with preterm delivery and pelvic inflammatory disease in high-risk scenarios, warranting treatment even when asymptomatic.

**Q:** What is the treatment for primary herpes episode during pregnancy?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1g PO BID x 7-10 days

Antiviral treatment reduces viral shedding and may decrease neonatal transmission risk in primary infections.

**Q:** What was the CHAP trial finding regarding treating mild chronic hypertension in pregnancy?

**A:** Treatment targeting BP <140/90 mmHg reduced primary outcome (preeclampsia, preterm birth <35wk, placental abruption, fetal/neonatal death): 30.2% vs 37.0% in control

CHAP supports treating mild chronic hypertension (BP 140/90 mmHg) in pregnancy, previously only severe HTN was treated.

**Q:** A 28-year-old pregnant woman at 20 weeks presents with BV. She has no symptoms. Should she be treated?

**A:** Yes, treat with metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 7 days - high-risk pregnancy warrants treatment despite being asymptomatic

BV in pregnancy is associated with preterm delivery. High-risk pregnancy is an indication for treatment even in asymptomatic patients.

**Q:** What does the 5 P's represent in sexual history?

**A:** Partners, Practices, Past history of STIs, Pregnancy prevention, Protection

The 5 P's framework ensures comprehensive sexual history taking.

**Q:** What is the treatment for herpes in pregnancy with primary infection?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1g PO BID x 7-10 days

Primary HSV infection during pregnancy carries risk of neonatal herpes and requires aggressive treatment.

**Q:** When is treatment for bacterial vaginosis indicated?

**A:** Treatment is unnecessary unless symptomatic, or if high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

Bacterial vaginosis is caused by overgrowth of Gardnerella vaginalis, Mycoplasma hominis, and anaerobes. Treatment is typically reserved for symptomatic patients or those undergoing procedures.

**Q:** What is the treatment for herpes during pregnancy with primary infection?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 7-10 days, OR valacyclovir 1 g PO BID x 7-10 days

Primary HSV infection in pregnancy requires antiviral treatment to reduce viral shedding and risk of neonatal herpes.

**Q:** What is the treatment for recurrent herpes in pregnancy in patients with prior infection within the previous year?

**A:** Acyclovir 800 mg PO BID x 5 days, OR 800 mg PO TID x 2 days, OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID x 3 days, OR 1 g PO daily x 5 days

Recurrent episodes in pregnancy are treated with shorter antiviral courses.

**Q:** When is treatment required for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Treat only if symptomatic; asymptomatic patients require treatment only if high-risk pregnancy, prior IUD insertion, gynecologic surgery, induced abortion, or upper tract instrumentation

BV is caused by overgrowth of Gardnerella vaginalis, Mycoplasma hominis, and anaerobes. Treatment is generally unnecessary unless specific obstetric/gynecologic risks exist.

**Q:** How is first-episode herpes simplex virus treated in pregnancy?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID x 7-10 days OR valacyclovir 1 g PO BID x 7-10 days

First-episode HSV in pregnancy requires treatment to reduce viral shedding and risk of neonatal transmission.

**Q:** How is recurrent HSV managed in pregnancy for patients with prior infection within the previous year?

**A:** Acyclovir 800 mg PO BID x 5 days OR 800 mg PO TID x 2 days OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID x 3 days or 1 g daily x 5 days; suppressive therapy at 36 weeks: acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily or valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID

Suppressive therapy at 36 weeks reduces HSV recurrence at delivery and need for cesarean section.

**Q:** What are the screening laboratory values for hemochromatosis?

**A:** Transferrin saturation (Fe/TIBC) >45% and serum ferritin >400 ng/mL

90% of primary hemochromatosis involves the C282Y allele of the HFE gene.

**Q:** What laboratory findings are characteristic of Primary Biliary Cholangitis?

**A:** Increased ALP, GGT, bilirubin (later), elevated IgM, increased cholesterol (especially HDL)

The cholestatic pattern (high ALP/GGT) is hallmark. Bilirubin rises in later stages. Elevated IgM and cholesterol (particularly HDL) are common.

**Q:** What are the screening laboratory criteria for hemochromatosis?

**A:** Transferrin saturation >45%, Serum ferritin >400 ng/mL

Elevated transferrin saturation (>45%) and ferritin (>400 ng/mL) are initial screening tests indicating possible iron overload and warrant further investigation.

**Q:** What is the approach to evaluating chronic diarrhea?

**A:** Chronic diarrhea evaluation requires systematic assessment including history, physical exam, laboratory tests, endoscopy, and imaging based on clinical presentation

A review (NEJM 1995) outlines the diagnostic approach to chronic diarrhea including differential diagnosis and appropriate investigations

**Q:** What laboratory findings are characteristic of active SLE?

**A:** Low complement (C3, C4), low hemolytic complement (CH50), and often false positive VDRL

SLE can also present with leukopenia, lymphopenia, and proteinuria.

**Q:** What is the hallmark laboratory finding in polymyositis/dermatomyositis?

**A:** Elevated CK (creatine kinase) in 80% of patients

Diagnosis is confirmed with muscle biopsy, EMG, and sometimes MRI.

**Q:** Can hydronephrosis be physiologic?

**A:** Yes, for example in pregnancy (gravid uterus compresses ureters and progesterone decreases ureteral tone)

Physiologic hydronephrosis in pregnancy typically resolves postpartum and may not require intervention.

## General

**Q:** What are the first-line treatments for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 7 days, OR metronidazole 0.75% gel intravaginally nightly x 5 days, OR clindamycin 2% cream intravaginally nightly x 7 days

BV is caused by anaerobic overgrowth. Metronidazole and clindamycin are both effective against the anaerobic organisms involved.

**Q:** When should vulvovaginal candidiasis be treated?

**A:** Only treat if patient is symptomatic

Asymptomatic candidiasis does not require treatment as Candida can be a normal commensal in the vaginal flora.

**Q:** What is first-line treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500mg PO BID x 7 days OR Metronidazole 0.75% gel intravaginally nightly x 5 days OR Clindamycin 2% cream intravaginally nightly x 7 days

Metronidazole is the preferred oral treatment. Alternative regimens include topical clindamycin for patients who cannot tolerate metronidazole.

**Q:** What are alternative treatments for bacterial vaginosis (second-line)?

**A:** Metronidazole 2g PO single dose OR Clindamycin 300mg PO BID x 7 days

Second-line options are used when first-line treatment fails or is not tolerated.

**Q:** What is the recommended suppressive therapy for herpes at 36 weeks gestation?

**A:** Acyclovir 400mg PO TID daily OR Valacyclovir 500mg PO BID at 36 weeks

Suppressive therapy near term reduces the need for cesarean delivery due to active lesions at delivery.

**Q:** What is the first-line oral treatment for vulvovaginal candidiasis?

**A:** Fluconazole 150 mg PO single dose

Fluconazole is the oral treatment of choice. Treatment is only indicated if the patient is symptomatic.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500 mg PO BID for 7 days

Metronidazole is the first-line oral therapy. Alternatives include metronidazole gel or clindamycin cream.

**Q:** What are the alternative treatments for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 0.75% gel: one applicator (5 g) intravaginally at bedtime for 5 days, OR clindamycin 2% cream: one applicator (5 g) intravaginally at bedtime for 7 days

Topical alternatives are available for patients who cannot tolerate oral metronidazole.

**Q:** What are the three most common STIs associated with vaginal discharge in adult women?

**A:** Bacterial vaginosis (BV), vulvovaginal candidiasis (VVC), and trichomoniasis

These three infections account for the majority of vaginal discharge complaints in adult women and require different treatment approaches.

**Q:** What is the first-line oral treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500 mg PO twice daily for 7 days

Metronidazole 500mg BID x7 days is the preferred oral regimen for BV.

**Q:** What is the first-line topical treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 0.75% gel: one applicator (5g) intravaginally at bedtime for 5 days OR Clindamycin 2% cream: one applicator (5g) at bedtime for 7 days

Topical alternatives to oral metronidazole are effective for BV treatment.

**Q:** What is suppressive therapy dose for herpes genitalis at 36 weeks gestation?

**A:** Acyclovir 400 mg PO TID daily OR valacyclovir 500 mg PO BID starting at 36 weeks

Suppressive therapy in late pregnancy reduces viral shedding and need for cesarean delivery.

**Q:** What is the cause of vulvovaginal candidiasis?

**A:** Candida species (most commonly Candida albicans)

Candida is a yeast that can cause vulvovaginal infection when it overgrows.

**Q:** What is the second-line oral treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 2g PO single dose OR clindamycin 300 mg PO BID x7 days

**Q:** What is the topical treatment for bacterial vaginosis using metronidazole gel?

**A:** Metronidazole 0.75% gel - one applicator (5 g) intravaginally at bedtime for 5 days

Topical metronidazole is an alternative for patients who cannot tolerate oral therapy.

**Q:** What is the dosing regimen for metronidazole in bacterial vaginosis (alternative regimen)?

**A:** Metronidazole 0.75% gel one applicator (5g) intravaginally at bedtime x 5 days

Topical metronidazole is an alternative to oral therapy and has fewer systemic side effects.

**Q:** What is the second-line treatment for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 2 g PO single dose OR clindamycin 300 mg PO BID x 7 days

Second-line options used when first-line fails or is not tolerated.

**Q:** What is the oral treatment for vulvovaginal candidiasis?

**A:** Fluconazole 150 mg PO as a single dose

Single-dose oral fluconazole is as effective as topical azoles and more convenient for patients.

**Q:** What is the intravaginal treatment for bacterial vaginosis using metronidazole gel?

**A:** Metronidazole 0.75% gel: one applicator (5g) intravaginally at bedtime for 5 days

Alternative to oral metronidazole for patients who cannot tolerate systemic side effects or prefer local therapy.

**Q:** What is the treatment for bacterial vaginosis using clindamycin cream?

**A:** Clindamycin 2% cream: one applicator (5g) intravaginally at bedtime for 7 days

Useful alternative for patients allergic to metronidazole. Avoid concurrent use with condoms as clindamycin weakens latex.

**Q:** What are first-line treatment options for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 7 days OR metronidazole 0.75% gel intravaginally nightly x 5 days OR clindamycin 2% cream intravaginally nightly x 7 days

Metronidazole targets anaerobic overgrowth. Alternative regimens include single-dose metronidazole 2g PO or clindamycin 300 mg PO BID x 7 days.

**Q:** When is treatment for vulvovaginal candidiasis recommended?

**A:** Treat only if patient is symptomatic

Asymptomatic candidiasis does not require treatment as Candida is a normal vaginal commensal. Treatment is indicated for symptomatic infection.

**Q:** What are alternative second-line treatments for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 2 g PO single dose, OR clindamycin 300 mg PO BID x 7 days

These are second-line options when first-line treatments (7-day metronidazole or clindamycin cream) fail or are not suitable.

**Q:** What are the first-line oral treatment options for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 500 mg PO twice daily x 7 days; alternatives include metronidazole 0.75% gel intravaginally x 5 days or clindamycin 2% cream intravaginally x 7 days

Metronidazole targets anaerobic overgrowth. Oral therapy has higher compliance than 7-day topical regimens.

**Q:** What are the second-line treatment options for bacterial vaginosis?

**A:** Metronidazole 2 g PO as single dose OR clindamycin 300 mg PO twice daily x 7 days

Second-line options are used for treatment failure or intolerance to first-line regimens.

## Gynecology

**Q:** What are the most common causes of primary amenorrhea?

**A:** 1. Gonadal dysgenesis (e.g., Turner syndrome 45,X0) — most common overall
2. Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea
3. Müllerian agenesis (MRKH syndrome)

Primary amenorrhea is defined as no menses by age 13 without secondary sexual characteristics, or by age 15 with secondary sexual characteristics. Turner syndrome (45,X0) is the most common cause, presenting with streak gonads and elevated FSH.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of secondary amenorrhea?

**A:** Pregnancy (must be ruled out first). Among non-pregnant patients, functional hypothalamic amenorrhea is the most common cause.

Secondary amenorrhea is the absence of menses for >6 months or 3 cycles after documented menarche. A beta-hCG must always be the first investigation. Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea is often related to stress, excessive exercise, and/or anorexia.

**Q:** How is the progesterone withdrawal challenge interpreted in the workup of amenorrhea?

**A:** Medroxyprogesterone 10 mg PO daily x 10-14 days. A positive withdrawal bleed (within 2-7 days) indicates adequate estrogen and a patent outflow tract. No bleeding suggests hypoestrogenism, excessive androgens/progesterone, pregnancy, or structural causes (e.g., Asherman syndrome).

The progesterone challenge assesses whether the endometrium has been primed by estrogen. Withdrawal of progesterone from an estrogen-primed endometrium causes shedding and bleeding. Absence of bleeding narrows the differential considerably.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of postmenopausal bleeding, and what must always be ruled out?

**A:** Most common cause: genitourinary syndrome of menopause (vaginal atrophy). Must rule out: endometrial cancer and hyperplasia with an endometrial biopsy before treatment.

While atrophy is the most frequent cause, postmenopausal bleeding is endometrial cancer until proven otherwise. Endometrial thickness >5 mm on transvaginal ultrasound in a postmenopausal woman is considered abnormal and warrants biopsy.

**Q:** What are the '4 Dys' of endometriosis?

**A:** 1. Dysmenorrhea
2. Dyspareunia (cul-de-sac/uterosacral ligament involvement)
3. Dyschezia (uterosacral ligament/rectosigmoid involvement)
4. Dysuria (bladder involvement)

Endometriosis is the presence of endometrial glands and stroma outside the uterine cavity. It is a chronic condition affecting 15-30% of premenopausal patients. The 4 Dys represent the cardinal symptoms reflecting ectopic endometrial tissue causing cyclic inflammation.

**Q:** What is the gold standard for diagnosing endometriosis, and what are the characteristic laparoscopic findings?

**A:** Gold standard: direct visualization at laparoscopy with biopsy. Findings: mulberry spots (dark blue/brownish-black implants), 'chocolate' cysts (endometriomas), powder-burn lesions, white lesions, clear blebs, and peritoneal pockets.

Histologic confirmation requires 2 or more of: endometrial epithelium, glands, stroma, or hemosiderin-laden macrophages. However, SOGC guidelines state that surgical confirmation is NOT required before starting medical management; a suggestive history is adequate for presumptive diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the first-line medical treatment for endometriosis?

**A:** 1st line: Cyclic/continuous estrogen-progestin (OCP), progestin (Depo-Provera or dienogest/Visanne), or Mirena IUS. NSAIDs for pain. 2nd line: GnRH agonist (Lupron) or antagonist (elagolix/Orlissa, relugolix/Myfembree), or danazol.

Medical management is the mainstay for endometriosis-associated pain. Surgical confirmation is not required before initiating treatment. GnRH agonists used >6 months require add-back progestin or estrogen to prevent bone mineral density loss and vasomotor symptoms.

**Q:** What is adenomyosis, and how does it differ from endometriosis in presentation?

**A:** Adenomyosis is endometrial glands and stroma extending into the myometrium ('endometriosis interna'). It presents at an older age (40-50 years) vs. endometriosis (25-30 years), with a symmetrically bulky uterus usually <14 cm and Halban's sign (tender, softened uterus on premenstrual bimanual exam).

Adenomyosis is found in 20-40% of hysterectomy specimens and 15% of females >35 years. Definitive diagnosis requires pathology, but clinical diagnosis supported by U/S or MRI is adequate. Treatment of choice for completed childbearing is hysterectomy.

**Q:** What is the most common type of uterine fibroid and how are fibroids classified by the FIGO system?

**A:** Most common: intramural (within myometrial wall). FIGO classification: Submucosal (types 0-2), Intramural (types 3-4), Subserosal (types 5-7), Cervical (type 8). Type 0 = pedunculated intracavitary, Type 7 = pedunculated subserosal.

Fibroids are benign smooth muscle tumours diagnosed in 40-50% of premenopausal patients >35. They are estrogen- and progesterone-responsive and typically regress after menopause. Submucosal fibroids are most likely to cause AUB and affect fertility.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to combined estrogen-progestin contraceptives?

**A:** Postpartum <4 wk (breastfeeding) or <21 d (not breastfeeding), major surgery with prolonged immobilization, pregnancy, undiagnosed vaginal bleeding, prior/active thromboembolic events, thrombophilic disorders (Factor V Leiden, protein C/S deficiency), cerebrovascular/coronary disease, estrogen-dependent tumours, acute liver disease, congenital hypertriglyceridemia, smoker >35 years, migraines with focal neurological symptoms, uncontrolled HTN.

Combined hormonal contraceptives increase the risk of VTE, stroke, and hypertension. The estrogen component is responsible for most thromboembolic risk. For patients with contraindications, progestin-only methods (IUS, implant, Depo-Provera, POP) are alternatives.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and timing of emergency postcoital contraception with Plan B (levonorgestrel)?

**A:** Levonorgestrel 750 μg q12h x 2 doses (can take both together). Mechanism: suppresses ovulation, causes deficient luteal phase, alters endometrium/sperm transport. Use within 72h (ideally 24h), can use up to 5 days. 75-95% effective within 24h. Less effective if >75 kg, not recommended if >80 kg.

Plan B has greater efficacy and fewer side effects than the Yuzpe method because it lacks estrogen. Ulipristal (Ella) is a selective progesterone receptor modulator effective up to 5 days and may delay ovulation by up to 5 days. Copper IUD is the most effective EPC (99.9%, up to 7 days).

**Q:** What is the medical abortion regimen and up to what gestational age is it the gold standard?

**A:** Gold standard up to 9 weeks GA. Mifepristone 200 mg PO on day 1 (progesterone receptor blocker) + misoprostol 800 μg PV/buccal on day 2-3 (synthetic prostaglandin causing uterine contractions). 95-98% effective up to 49 days after LMP.

Mifepristone blocks progesterone receptors needed for early pregnancy maintenance, alters the endometrium, induces bleeding, and softens the cervix. Misoprostol causes uterine contractions and expulsion. Absolute contraindications include ectopic pregnancy and chronic adrenal failure.

**Q:** How do you classify spontaneous abortions, and what defines an inevitable vs. incomplete abortion?

**A:** Inevitable: bleeding + cramping + ruptured membranes, fetus low in uterus on U/S, cervix OPEN. Incomplete: bleeding + cramps + passage of tissue, residual tissue in uterus on U/S, cervix OPEN. Key difference: passage of tissue has occurred in incomplete but not in inevitable abortion.

Threatened abortion has bleeding with closed cervix and live fetus (<5% abort). Complete abortion shows empty uterus with closed cervix. Missed abortion has no fetal heart rate but no bleeding, with closed cervix. Management of all includes checking Rh status and giving Rhogam if negative.

**Q:** What is the most common location for ectopic pregnancy, and what is the classic triad of presentation?

**A:** Most common: ampullary region of fallopian tube (70%). Classic triad: abdominal pain + vaginal bleeding + amenorrhea. It is an ectopic pregnancy until proven otherwise in any patient with a uterus presenting with this triad.

Ectopic pregnancy occurs in 1/100 pregnancies and is the leading cause of maternal death in the first trimester. 50% are due to tubal damage from PID. Serial β-hCG levels with <35% rise every 2 days and an empty uterus on TVUS with β-hCG >2000-3000 suggest ectopic pregnancy.

**Q:** What are the indications and contraindications for methotrexate treatment of ectopic pregnancy?

**A:** Dose: 50 mg/m² IM single dose. Contraindications: hemodynamic instability, tubal rupture signs, poor compliance, renal/liver dysfunction, bone marrow suppression, active pulmonary disease, peptic ulcer, immunosuppression, heterotopic pregnancy with viable IUP, breastfeeding. Cannot conceive for 3 months after due to teratogenicity.

Methotrexate is a folic acid reductase inhibitor affecting rapidly dividing trophoblastic cells. Follow β-hCG on days 4 and 7, then weekly until non-detectable. Up to 25% require a second dose. Stop prenatal vitamins as folic acid decreases efficacy. Success rate is 82-95%.

**Q:** What are the Rotterdam diagnostic criteria for PCOS?

**A:** 2 of 3 required: 1) Oligomenorrhea/irregular menses for 6 months, 2) Hyperandrogenism (clinical: hirsutism/acne; or biochemical: raised free testosterone), 3) Polycystic ovaries on U/S (≥12 follicles 2-9 mm or ovarian volume >10 cc; not appropriate in adolescents).

PCOS is a chronic endocrine disorder involving insulin resistance, elevated LH:FSH ratio (>2:1), and increased androgens. Long-term consequences include dyslipidemia, endometrial hyperplasia, subfertility, obesity, and sleep apnea. In adolescents, wait 1-2 years to diagnose as adolescence resembles PCOS.

**Q:** What are the key distinguishing features of candidal vaginitis, bacterial vaginosis, and trichomoniasis?

**A:** Candida: white cottage-cheese discharge, pH <4.5, KOH shows hyphae/spores, intense pruritus. BV: grey thin fishy discharge, pH >4.5, clue cells on wet mount, positive whiff test, no inflammation. Trichomoniasis: yellow-green frothy discharge, pH >4.5, motile flagellates on wet mount, cervical petechiae ('strawberry cervix').

BV is not an STI but a replacement of Lactobacilli by anaerobes. Trichomoniasis IS an STI requiring partner treatment. Candida risk factors include immunosuppression, antibiotics, and increased estrogen. Metronidazole (for BV and trichomoniasis) has a disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chlamydia, and what are the complications of untreated infection?

**A:** Treatment: doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 7 days OR azithromycin 1 g PO single dose (doxycycline contraindicated in 2nd/3rd trimester). Complications: PID with low-grade salpingitis and adhesions, infertility, ectopic pregnancy, chronic pelvic pain, Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome, reactive arthritis (HLA-B27), perinatal conjunctivitis and pneumonia.

Chlamydia trachomatis is the most common bacterial STI in Canada. 80% of patients are asymptomatic. It is often co-infected with N. gonorrhoeae. It is a reportable disease, and all sexual partners within 60 days must be tested and empirically treated.

**Q:** What are the minimum diagnostic criteria for PID?

**A:** Minimum criteria (all 3 required): cervical motion tenderness + uterine tenderness + adnexal tenderness. Additional criteria: oral temperature >38.3°C, leukocytosis on saline microscopy, elevated ESR/CRP, cervical infection with N. gonorrhoeae or C. trachomatis.

PID is infection of the upper genital tract primarily affecting young sexually active women ages 15-25. The most common causative organisms are C. trachomatis and N. gonorrhoeae. Treatment requires polymicrobial coverage. One episode of PID causes 13% infertility, two episodes cause 36%.

**Q:** What is the outpatient antibiotic regimen for PID?

**A:** 1st line: ceftriaxone 500 mg IM x 1 dose + doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 14 days, OR cefoxitin 2 g IM x 1 dose + probenecid 1 g PO + doxycycline 100 mg PO BID + metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 14 days.

Inpatient treatment is indicated for moderate-severe illness, adnexal/tubo-ovarian mass, failed oral therapy, immunocompromised, pregnant, or adolescent patients. Inpatient regimen includes cefoxitin 2g IV q6h + doxycycline 100 mg PO/IV q12h, continuing IV until 24h after symptom improvement.

**Q:** What are the stages of syphilis and the treatment for primary/secondary syphilis?

**A:** Primary: painless chancre 3-4 wk post-exposure. Secondary: maculopapular rash (palms/soles), condylomata lata, diffuse lymphadenopathy. Latent: positive serology only. Tertiary: tabes dorsalis, aortic aneurysm, gummas. Treatment (primary/secondary/<1yr latent): benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM single dose.

Dark field microscopy is the most sensitive/specific test for primary syphilis. Non-treponemal tests (VDRL, RPR) are screening tests that become non-reactive after treatment. Treponemal tests (FTA-ABS) are confirmatory and remain reactive for life. Syphilis is a reportable disease.

**Q:** What is the average age of menopause, and what are the hallmark clinical features?

**A:** Average age: 51 years. Features: vasomotor instability (hot flashes, night sweats, palpitations), urogenital atrophy/GUSM (dyspareunia, vaginal dryness, urinary symptoms), skeletal effects (osteoporosis), psychological symptoms (anxiety, depression, decreased libido). Pathophysiology: degenerating theca cells fail to respond to gonadotropins → decreased estrogen → increased FSH/LH.

Premature ovarian insufficiency is menopause before age 40. For women >45 with menopausal symptoms, no testing is necessary. Increased FSH >35 IU/L on day 3 confirms menopause. Osteoporosis is the single most important health hazard, while cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death post-menopause.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to menopausal hormone therapy (MHT)?

**A:** Acute liver disease, undiagnosed vaginal bleeding, history of or known/suspected breast cancer, known/suspected uterine cancer, acute vascular thrombosis, history of severe thrombophlebitis/thromboembolic disease, cardiovascular disease. Mnemonic: ABCD — Acute liver disease, undiagnosed Bleeding, Cancer (breast/uterine)/CVD, DVT.

MHT is safest when initiated <10 years after menopause and/or <60 years old. Combined estrogen-progesterone increases breast cancer risk after 4-5 years. Estrogen alone does not increase breast cancer risk for at least 8 years. Progestin must be added for patients with intact uterus to prevent endometrial hyperplasia.

**Q:** What are the stages of pelvic organ prolapse (POP-Q system)?

**A:** Stage 0: no prolapse. Stage 1: most distal portion >1 cm above hymen. Stage 2: within 1 cm above or below hymen. Stage 3: >1 cm below hymen but <2 cm less than total vaginal length. Stage 4: complete procidentia/vault eversion.

Risk factors include pregnancy, aging, decreased estrogen, increased intra-abdominal pressure, and connective tissue disorders. Conservative management includes weight loss, pelvic floor muscle training (Kegels), vaginal estrogen, and pessary. The only true pelvic hernia is vault prolapse.

**Q:** What are the risk factors and key features of Type I vs. Type II endometrial cancer?

**A:** Type I (80%): estrogen-related endometrioid, well-differentiated, associated with PCOS, obesity, unopposed estrogen, tamoxifen, late menopause, nulliparity. Type II (20%): non-estrogen related, includes serous/clear cell/carcinosarcoma, associated with p53 mutation, HER2 overexpression, poorer prognosis, more common in Black patients.

Endometrial cancer is the most common gynecological malignancy in North America. 85-90% 5-year survival for stage I. Screening with annual endometrial sampling (age 30-35) is only for patients with HNPCC/Lynch II syndrome. Any postmenopausal bleeding requires endometrial biopsy.

**Q:** What tumour markers are used for different ovarian tumour types?

**A:** Epithelial: CA-125 (serous/endometrioid). Sex-cord stromal: inhibin (granulosa cell). Germ cell: LDH (dysgerminoma), AFP (yolk sac tumour), β-hCG (choriocarcinoma), AFP + β-hCG (embryonal). No marker for immature teratoma.

Ovarian cancer causes more deaths than all other gynecologic malignancies combined. 85% are epithelial. CA-125 has only 50% sensitivity in early-stage disease, making it a poor screening test. Any adnexal mass in a postmenopausal woman should be considered malignant until proven otherwise.

**Q:** What is the Risk of Malignancy Index (RMI) formula for evaluating an ovarian mass?

**A:** RMI = U × M × CA-125. U = ultrasound score (1 point each for: multilocular cyst, solid areas, metastases, ascites, bilateral); U=1 if score 0-1, U=4 if score 2-5. M = menopausal status (premenopausal M=1, postmenopausal M=4). RMI >200: gynecologic oncology referral recommended.

The RMI is the best available tool to assess likelihood of ovarian malignancy and the need for preoperative gynecologic oncology referral. Population-based screening with CA-125 and/or transvaginal ultrasound has NOT been shown to reduce ovarian cancer mortality.

**Q:** What HPV types are covered by Gardasil 9, and which types are most oncogenic?

**A:** Gardasil 9 covers types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, 58. Types 6 and 11: associated with anogenital warts. Types 16 and 18: most oncogenic (classically associated with cervical HSIL). >99% of cervical cancers contain a high-risk HPV type.

HPV is the most common viral STI in Canada. The vaccine should be administered before onset of sexual activity for optimal benefit but is still indicated for sexually active individuals. Condoms may not fully protect as HPV can be transmitted through areas not covered.

**Q:** What is the most important risk factor for cervical cancer in Canada?

**A:** Poor screening uptake. At-risk groups include: immigrant Canadians, Indigenous peoples, geographically isolated Canadians, sex-trade workers, low socioeconomic status, and immunocompromised individuals.

The majority of cervical cancers are SCC (90%), with adenocarcinoma increasing (10%). The transformation zone between original and current squamocolumnar junction is where most dysplasias/cancers arise. The slow progression from dysplasia to cancer takes ~10 years on average.

**Q:** What differentiates a complete from a partial hydatidiform mole?

**A:** Complete mole: diploid (46XX/46XY, all paternal), diffuse trophoblastic hyperplasia, NO fetus, 'snowstorm' on U/S, theca-lutein cysts, 15-20% risk of GTN. Partial mole: triploid (69XXX/69XXY, 1 maternal + 2 paternal sets), focal hyperplasia, fetal tissue present, <4% risk of GTN.

Complete moles present with vaginal bleeding, uterine size large for dates, hyperemesis, and preeclampsia. Treatment is suction D&C. Follow-up requires serial β-hCG weekly until negative x3, then monthly for 6 months (complete) or 1 month (partial). Reliable contraception is required throughout follow-up.

**Q:** How is gestational trophoblastic neoplasia (GTN) diagnosed?
