**A:** Any of: β-hCG rise >10% over 2 weeks (3 values), β-hCG plateau (<10% change) over 3 weeks (4 values), histologic diagnosis of choriocarcinoma, metastatic disease, or β-hCG ≥20,000 mIU/mL >4 weeks post-evacuation.

GTN is the malignant form of gestational trophoblastic disease. Stage I (confined to uterus) is treated with single-agent chemotherapy (actinomycin D or methotrexate) for low-risk (WHO score ≤6). High-risk (score ≥7) requires combination chemotherapy with EMA-CO. Lungs are the primary site for metastases.

**Q:** What is the Meigs syndrome triad?

**A:** Benign ovarian tumour (typically fibroma/thecoma) + ascites + pleural effusion. The effusion and ascites resolve after tumour removal.

Meigs syndrome is a paraneoplastic phenomenon associated with sex-cord stromal tumours, most commonly ovarian fibroma. It is important to distinguish from malignant ovarian tumours with ascites and pleural effusions, which carry a much worse prognosis.

**Q:** What are the granulosa cell tumour characteristics, tumour marker, and histologic hallmark?

**A:** Estrogen-producing tumour causing feminizing effects (precocious puberty, menorrhagia, PMB). Tumour marker: inhibin. Histologic hallmark: Call-Exner bodies (small groups of cells arranged around central cavity). Risk of concurrent endometrial cancer due to estrogen stimulation.

Granulosa-theca cell tumours are sex cord-stromal tumours that may be benign or malignant. Their estrogen production can stimulate the endometrium, leading to hyperplasia and cancer. Treatment is surgical resection, with chemotherapy for unresectable metastatic disease.

**Q:** What are Krukenberg tumours?

**A:** Metastatic ovarian tumours (usually bilateral, 80%) from GI tract (commonly stomach or colon), breast, or endometrium. Histologic hallmark: 'signet-ring' cells (mucin-filled cells that push the nucleus to the periphery).

Krukenberg tumours represent 4-8% of ovarian malignancies. They are secondary tumours rather than primary ovarian cancers. The presence of bilateral ovarian masses with signet-ring cells should prompt investigation for a GI primary tumour.

**Q:** What is lichen sclerosus of the vulva, and why is monitoring important?

**A:** Subepithelial fat loss causing thin, atrophic labia with membrane-like epithelium in a 'figure of 8' distribution. Symptoms: pruritus, dyspareunia, burning, ulceration. Most common in postmenopausal women. Treatment: high-potency topical steroid (clobetasol). Must monitor for SCC due to increased malignancy risk.

Lichen sclerosus is a non-neoplastic vulvar disorder that can occur at any age but is most common postmenopausally. Biopsy is often necessary to confirm diagnosis and rule out malignancy. Long-term topical steroid treatment is usually necessary.

**Q:** How do you differentiate stress urinary incontinence from overactive bladder?

**A:** Stress incontinence: involuntary urine loss with increased intra-abdominal pressure (cough, laugh, sneeze). Overactive bladder: urgency ± urge incontinence, usually with frequency and nocturia, caused by involuntary detrusor contraction. Treatment: stress = surgery (midurethral sling); OAB = behavioral modification + anticholinergics (oxybutynin) or β-agonist (mirabegron).

Stress incontinence affects up to 37.5% of women, with risk factors including age, obesity, vaginal delivery, and hypoestrogenic states. OAB affects up to 22% of patients. Initial workup for both includes U/A, post-void residual, and pelvic exam assessing for prolapse.

**Q:** A 55-year-old postmenopausal woman asks about hormone therapy for vasomotor symptoms. What are the key benefits and risks of combined estrogen-progestin HT?

**A:** Benefits: decreases risk of osteoporotic fractures and colorectal cancer, relieves vasomotor symptoms. Risks: increases DVT/PE risk; can increase breast cancer and stroke risk if initiated >60 years old or >10 years post-menopause.

HT should not be initiated if >10 years post-menopause. The WHI trial showed overall health risks exceed benefits for combined HT. Alternatives for vasomotor symptoms include venlafaxine, SSRIs, or gabapentin.

**Q:** What are the four paired ligaments supporting the uterus and their primary functions?

**A:** (1) Round ligaments: anteversion/suspension; travel through broad ligament and inguinal canal to labia majora. (2) Uterosacral ligaments: mechanical support, prevent prolapse, contain autonomic nerve fibres; arise from sacral fascia, insert into posterior inferior uterus. (3) Cardinal ligaments: mechanical support, prevent prolapse; extend from lateral pelvic walls to lateral cervix and vagina. (4) Broad ligaments: contain fallopian tube, round ligament, ovarian ligament, nerves, vessels, and lymphatics.

The round ligaments maintain uterine anteversion but are NOT the primary support against prolapse. The cardinal and uterosacral ligaments are the main supports preventing uterine prolapse and are often damaged during childbirth or surgery, leading to pelvic organ prolapse.

**Q:** What is the blood supply to the uterus, ovaries, and fallopian tubes, and what is the anatomical relationship between the ureter and uterine artery?

**A:** Uterus: uterine artery (branch of internal iliac artery, anterior division). Ovaries: ovarian arteries (branches off aorta); left ovarian vein drains to left renal vein, right drains to IVC. Fallopian tubes: uterine and ovarian arteries. The ureter passes UNDER the uterine artery ('water under the bridge') — critical to avoid ureteral injury during hysterectomy.

The mnemonic 'water under the bridge' reminds surgeons that the ureter (water) runs inferior to the uterine artery (bridge). Ureteral injury is a known complication of hysterectomy. The round ligament contains Sampson's artery (branch of uterine artery).

**Q:** What is the lymphatic drainage of the external genitalia, and which structures drain to deep versus superficial inguinal nodes?

**A:** Most external genitalia drain to superficial inguinal lymph nodes. The clitoris drains to deep inguinal lymph nodes. The ovaries drain via the infundibulopelvic ligament to para-aortic lymph nodes.

Lymphatic drainage determines spread patterns of vulvar and clitoral cancers. Clitoral melanoma or cancer may involve deep inguinal and pelvic nodes, requiring more extensive lymph node dissection compared to other vulvar cancers that primarily spread to superficial inguinal nodes.

**Q:** Describe the segments of the fallopian tube from medial to lateral.

**A:** From medial to lateral: (1) Interstitial (intramural) — within uterine wall; (2) Isthmic — narrow segment adjacent to uterus; (3) Ampullary — widest segment, most common site of fertilization and ectopic pregnancy; (4) Infundibular — funnel-shaped, terminates at the fimbriae.

The ampullary segment is where fertilization normally occurs. It is also the most common site of ectopic implantation (~70%). The mesosalpinx is the peritoneal fold attaching the fallopian tube to the broad ligament. Total fallopian tube length is 8-14 cm.

**Q:** What is the role of the infundibulopelvic ligament (suspensory ligament of the ovary)?

**A:** The infundibulopelvic ligament connects the ovary to the pelvic wall and contains the ovarian artery, ovarian vein, ovarian plexus, and lymphatic vessels. It is the main vascular pedicle of the ovary and must be ligated during oophorectomy.

The infundibulopelvic ligament is surgically critical — it is divided when performing salpingo-oophorectomy. The ureter runs close to this ligament and can be injured if not clearly identified. The mesovarium is a separate peritoneal fold attaching the ovary to the broad ligament.

**Q:** Summarize the hormonal events of the follicular (proliferative) phase of the menstrual cycle.

**A:** Low estrogen and progesterone from the previous cycle stimulate FSH and LH release (via GnRH pulsatility). FSH acts on ovarian granulosa cells to stimulate follicular growth in 3-30 follicles. Rising estrogen from the dominant follicle provides negative feedback on FSH (reducing it) but positive feedback just before ovulation triggers the LH surge. The endometrium proliferates under estrogen stimulation. Cervical mucus becomes clear, increased, and stringy (Spinnbarkeit 8-10 cm).

The follicular phase is variable in duration (unlike the fixed 14-day luteal phase). The switch from negative to positive feedback by estrogen at the mid-cycle peak triggers the LH surge, leading to ovulation approximately 36-48 hours later. This understanding is essential for fertility assessment and contraception counseling.

**Q:** What are the hormonal events and endometrial changes during the luteal (secretory) phase of the menstrual cycle?

**A:** After ovulation (triggered by LH surge ~36h prior), the dominant follicle becomes the corpus luteum, which secretes progesterone and estrogen. Progesterone provides negative feedback on LH and FSH. The endometrium undergoes secretory changes (glandular secretions, cessation of mitoses). Cervical mucus becomes opaque, scant, and less stretchy (Spinnbarkeit 1-2 cm). Without fertilization, corpus luteum degenerates → progesterone withdrawal → menses.

The luteal phase has a fixed duration of 14 days (± 2 days), unlike the variable follicular phase. Progesterone is the dominant hormone, preparing the endometrium for implantation. Progesterone also raises basal body temperature by ~0.5°C (useful for fertility tracking). Corpus luteum inadequacy can cause recurrent pregnancy loss.

**Q:** What are the effects of estrogen on the endometrium, follicles, and target tissues during the menstrual cycle?

**A:** Endometrium: proliferation of glandular and stromal tissue. Ovarian follicles: reduces atresia (promotes follicle survival). Target tissues: decreases estrogen receptors (downregulation). Main hormone of the follicular/proliferative phase; stimulated by FSH; majority secreted by the dominant follicle.

Estrogen's proliferative effect on the endometrium is opposed by progesterone in the luteal phase ('organize and stabilize'). Unopposed estrogen leads to endometrial hyperplasia and increases cancer risk. This is why progestin is added in postmenopausal hormone therapy for women with an intact uterus.

**Q:** What are the effects of progesterone on the endometrium and target tissues?

**A:** Endometrium: cessation of mitoses (stops proliferation), organizes glands (initiates secretions), inhibits macrophages, interleukin-8, and degradative enzymes. Target tissues: decreases both estrogen receptors AND progesterone receptors (anti-estrogen effect). Secreted by corpus luteum; stimulated by LH.

Progesterone's 'anti-estrogen' effect explains why combined oral contraceptives with progestin component reduce endometrial and ovarian cancer risk. In the absence of progesterone (anovulatory cycles, PCOS), unopposed estrogen drives endometrial hyperplasia. Progesterone also stabilizes the endometrium to prevent breakthrough bleeding.

**Q:** What is the sequence of pubertal development in females, and what is the mnemonic to remember it?

**A:** Mnemonic 'Boobs, Pubes, Grow, Flow': (1) Thelarche (breast development), (2) Pubarche (pubic and axillary hair), (3) Growth spurt, (4) Menarche (onset of menses). Adrenarche (adrenal androgen secretion) precedes gonadarche by ~2 years. Gonadarche begins at ~age 8.

Menarche typically follows the peak height velocity and/or 2 years after breast budding. Average age of menarche in Canada is 12.2 years (normal range 10-15 years). Thelarche before age 8 or menarche before age 9 suggests precocious puberty requiring investigation.

**Q:** What are the Tanner stages of breast development?

**A:** Stage 1: Prepubertal. Stage 2: Breast bud (thelarche). Stage 3: Further enlargement of areolae and breast with no separation of contours. Stage 4: Secondary mound of areolae and papilla above breast level. Stage 5: Areolae recessed to general contour of breast (mature).

Tanner staging provides a standardized method to assess pubertal progression. Thelarche at Stage 2 is typically the first sign of puberty in females. Premature thelarche (isolated breast development <8 years without other puberty signs) is usually benign but requires monitoring.

**Q:** What are the Tanner stages of pubic hair (pubarche) development in females?

**A:** Stage 1: Prepubertal (no hair). Stage 2: Downy hair along labia only. Stage 3: Darker, coarser hair extending over pubis. Stage 4: Adult-type hair with no thigh involvement. Stage 5: Adult hair in distribution and type, with spread to medial thighs.

Pubarche is driven by adrenal androgens (adrenarche) and ovarian androgens. It typically follows thelarche by 6-12 months. Premature pubarche (isolated pubic hair <8 years) requires evaluation to rule out congenital adrenal hyperplasia, androgen-secreting tumour, or precocious puberty.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Premenstrual Syndrome (PMS)?

**A:** At least one AFFECTIVE symptom (depression, angry outbursts, irritability, anxiety, confusion, social withdrawal) AND one SOMATIC symptom (breast tenderness/swelling, bloating, headache, extremity swelling, joint/muscle pain, weight gain, fatigue) during the 5 days before menses in each of 3 prior cycles; symptoms relieved within 4 days of onset of menses; do not recur until at least day 13; present without pharmacologic therapy; occur reproducibly during 2 cycles of prospective recording; cause identifiable dysfunction.

PMS is multifactorial with serotonergic dysregulation as the most plausible theory. Prospective symptom tracking (e.g., PRISM calendar) is required to confirm the diagnosis and distinguish PMS from other mood disorders with premenstrual exacerbation. Symptoms must be ABSENT from day 13 to late follicular phase.

**Q:** What distinguishes Premenstrual Dysphoric Disorder (PMDD) from PMS, and what are the diagnostic criteria?

**A:** PMDD is more severe than PMS and causes significant functional impairment. Requires at least 5 of 11 symptoms during most cycles in the past year (with at least 1 of the first 4 core symptoms): (1) depressed mood/hopelessness, (2) anxiety/tension, (3) affective instability, (4) anger/irritability, plus any of: decreased interest, difficulty concentrating, lethargy, appetite change, hypersomnia/insomnia, overwhelmed feeling, or physical symptoms. Symptoms present the week before menses and resolve within days of onset.

PMDD is classified as a depressive disorder in DSM-5. The serotonergic mechanism means SSRIs (either continuous or luteal-phase dosing) are first-line treatment. PMDD can be superimposed on other psychiatric disorders, but must be distinguished from simple premenstrual exacerbation of an existing disorder.

**Q:** What is the first-line imaging modality for pelvic structures, and when is transvaginal versus transabdominal ultrasound preferred?

**A:** Ultrasound (US) is first-line. Transvaginal US provides better resolution of uterus and adnexal structures. For detecting early pregnancy: transvaginal US can detect if β-hCG ≥1500 IU/L, while transabdominal US requires β-hCG ≥6500 IU/L. Transvaginal US is preferred for most gynecologic indications.

The discriminatory zone (β-hCG level at which a pregnancy should be visible) is clinically important in evaluating possible ectopic pregnancy. If β-hCG exceeds the discriminatory zone and no intrauterine pregnancy is seen on transvaginal US, ectopic pregnancy must be strongly considered.

**Q:** What are the indications for endometrial biopsy in gynecological practice?

**A:** Abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB) or postmenopausal bleeding (PMB); age >40 with AUB; risk factors for or history of endometrial cancer; failure of medical treatment; significant intermenstrual bleeding; infrequent menses suggesting anovulatory cycles; obesity. Detection rate >97% for endometrial pathology.

Endometrial biopsy using a pipelle suction curette is an office-based procedure. Pre-treatment with misoprostol (cervical ripening) is optional and less effective in postmenopausal women. If office biopsy is not possible or inconclusive, D&C ± hysteroscopy in the OR is indicated. Postmenopausal bleeding always requires endometrial evaluation.

**Q:** What are the indications, routes, and SOGC recommendations for hysterectomy?

**A:** Indications: uterine fibroids, endometriosis/adenomyosis, uterine prolapse, pelvic pain, AUB, cancer (endometrium, ovaries, fallopian tubes, cervix). SOGC recommends: (1) most minimally invasive approach (vaginal > laparoscopic > open); (2) correct preoperative anemia (Hb <120 g/L), antibiotic prophylaxis, VTE prevention; (3) full endometriosis excision concurrently; (4) consider opportunistic salpingectomy to reduce ovarian cancer risk; (5) low threshold for cystoscopy if ureteral injury suspected.

Opportunistic salpingectomy at hysterectomy is endorsed by SOGC as a strategy to reduce serous ovarian cancer risk (many 'ovarian' cancers originate from fallopian tube fimbriae). The approach should NOT be changed solely for salpingectomy. Ureteral injury is the most feared complication; the ureter passes under the uterine artery.

**Q:** What are the complications of hysterectomy that must be discussed in informed consent?

**A:** General anesthetic risks; bleeding; infection; injury to other organs (ureter, bladder, rectum); loss of ovarian function if ovaries removed (iatrogenic menopause) or early menopause; venous thromboembolism (VTE).

Ureteral injury is particularly important and SOGC recommends routine cystoscopy if injury is suspected. Patients must be counseled about risks of removing ovaries (immediate surgical menopause with cardiovascular and bone health implications) versus leaving them (residual ovarian cancer risk). VTE prophylaxis is recommended perioperatively.

**Q:** What is the menstrual cycle length, typical blood loss, and when does menarche normally occur?

**A:** Cycle length: 28 ± 7 days (21-35 days). Bleeding duration: 1-6 days. Normal blood loss: 25-80 mL per cycle. Menarche: occurs between 10-15 years, average 12.2 years in Canada, typically 2 years after breast budding and/or following peak height velocity.

Blood loss >80 mL/cycle is defined as heavy menstrual bleeding (formerly menorrhagia). Cycle length <21 days (polymenorrhea) or >35 days (oligomenorrhea) is abnormal. Menarche before age 10 or after age 15 warrants investigation for precocious or delayed puberty respectively.

**Q:** What is the etiology of Premenstrual Syndrome and which neurotransmitter theory is most plausible?

**A:** PMS is multifactorial: genetics likely play a role; CNS-mediated neurotransmitter (serotonin, dopamine, GABA) interactions with sex steroids (progesterone, estrogen, testosterone) are involved. Serotonergic dysregulation is currently the most plausible theory.

The serotonergic hypothesis explains why SSRIs (selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors) are effective first-line pharmacotherapy for moderate-to-severe PMS and PMDD. They can be used continuously or in the luteal phase only. Symptoms occur only in the luteal phase (ovulatory cycles required) — anovulation eliminates PMS.

**Q:** What is the significance of cervical mucus changes throughout the menstrual cycle?

**A:** Follicular/periovulatory phase: cervical mucus becomes clear, increased in amount, and stringy (Spinnbarkeit 8-10 cm) — fertile mucus that facilitates sperm penetration. Luteal/secretory phase: mucus becomes opaque, scant, and less stretchy (Spinnbarkeit 1-2 cm) — under progesterone influence, forms barrier to sperm.

Cervical mucus assessment is used in fertility awareness methods and assists in timing of insemination. Spinnbarkeit (the ability to stretch) peaks at ovulation. Clomiphene and other anti-estrogens may impair cervical mucus quality despite inducing ovulation, potentially reducing fertility treatment success.

**Q:** What is the LH surge and what triggers it?

**A:** The LH surge is a sudden large increase in LH that occurs approximately 36 hours before ovulation. It is triggered by a switch from negative to positive feedback at mid-cycle: when rising estrogen (from the dominant follicle) reaches a peak, it switches from inhibiting to stimulating GnRH/LH release. The dominant follicle releases its oocyte ~36 hours after the LH surge begins.

This positive feedback is unique to estrogen — progesterone always exerts negative feedback. The LH surge causes final oocyte maturation, stigma formation on the follicle, and ovulation. Luteal phase cells (granulosa cells) then luteinize and produce progesterone. OPKs (ovulation predictor kits) detect this LH surge.

**Q:** What is the blood supply to the vagina?

**A:** Primary supply from the vaginal branch of the internal pudendal artery with anastomoses from the uterine artery, inferior vesical artery, and middle rectal artery.

The vagina's rich anastomotic blood supply from multiple sources explains why vaginal surgery can involve significant bleeding. The vagina is a muscular canal extending from the cervix to the vulva, lined by rugated stratified squamous epithelium. The upper vagina is divided by the cervix into anterior, posterior, and lateral fornices.

**Q:** What is the clinical implication of the 'water under the bridge' anatomical relationship in pelvic surgery?

**A:** The ureter passes UNDER (inferior to) the uterine artery. During hysterectomy or ligating the uterine artery, the ureter can be inadvertently ligated or transected if not clearly identified. The ureter is most commonly found at the pelvic brim (passing over the iliac vessels) and in the medial leaf of the broad ligament (passing under the uterine artery).

Ureteral injury occurs in approximately 0.1-0.5% of hysterectomies and is more common with laparoscopic than open surgery. SOGC recommends a low threshold for intraoperative cystoscopy if injury is suspected. Unrecognized ureteral ligation causes hydronephrosis; ureteral transection causes urinoma.

**Q:** Which ovarian vein drains to the left renal vein vs. the inferior vena cava, and what is the clinical significance?

**A:** Left ovarian vein drains to the left renal vein. Right ovarian vein drains directly to the inferior vena cava. Clinical significance: left-sided nutcracker syndrome (compression of left renal vein) can cause left ovarian vein hypertension and pelvic congestion syndrome. This asymmetry mirrors the left testicular vein drainage in males.

This anatomical difference also explains why primary varicosities are more common on the left side in both males and females. In oncology, lymphatic drainage of the ovaries follows the ovarian vessels to para-aortic/para-caval nodes, not the pelvic nodes (unlike the uterus and cervix).

**Q:** What is adrenarche and how does it relate to gonadarche in female puberty?

**A:** Adrenarche is increased secretion of adrenal androgens (DHEA, DHEAS), responsible for pubic and axillary hair (pubarche). Gonadarche is increased secretion of gonadal sex steroids from the ovaries. Adrenarche typically precedes gonadarche by approximately 2 years. Gonadarche begins at approximately age 8.

Adrenarche and gonadarche are independently regulated — one can occur without the other. Premature adrenarche (adrenarche before age 8) may be associated with PCOS or insulin resistance later in life. Gonadarche is regulated by the HPO axis, while adrenarche is regulated by ACTH/adrenal mechanisms.

**Q:** What are the four main surgical approaches to hysterectomy?

**A:** 1. Open (abdominal): transverse (Pfannenstiel/Maylard) or midline laparotomy
2. Vaginal hysterectomy: entire procedure through vagina, no abdominal incision
3. Laparoscopic-assisted vaginal hysterectomy (LAVH)
4. Total laparoscopic hysterectomy (TLH) / Robotic

Minimally invasive approaches (vaginal, laparoscopic, robotic) reduce recovery time and complications compared to open surgery. Robotic may be advantageous in high-BMI patients but is more costly.

**Q:** What tissues are removed in a Radical Hysterectomy (Type 3) and for what indication?

**A:** Tissues removed: uterus, cervix, entire parametria, uterine artery ligated at its origin from internal iliac artery, uterosacral ligament at most distal attachment (rectum), and upper 1/3 to 1/2 vagina.
Indication: cervical cancer

Radical hysterectomy removes a wider margin of tissue than total hysterectomy, including the full parametria and uterosacral ligaments, which is necessary for adequate surgical management of cervical cancer.

**Q:** What is the difference between a Modified Radical Hysterectomy (Type 2) and a Radical Hysterectomy (Type 3)?

**A:** Modified Radical (Type 2): removes proximal 1/3 parametria, uterine artery ligated medial to ureter, midpoint of uterosacral ligaments, upper 1-2 cm vagina — used for cervical cancer up to stage IB1.
Radical (Type 3): removes entire parametria, uterine artery ligated at its origin from internal iliac artery, uterosacral ligament at most distal attachment, upper 1/3–1/2 vagina — used for cervical cancer.

The extent of parametrial resection and the level at which the uterine artery is ligated distinguish these two procedures. Greater radicality is required for more advanced cervical cancer.

**Q:** What are the two most common causes of primary amenorrhea?

**A:** 1. Gonadal dysgenesis (e.g., Turner syndrome, 45,X0)
2. Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea

Turner syndrome (45,X0) is the most common cause of primary amenorrhea with hypergonadotropic hypogonadism. Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea is due to inadequate GnRH pulsatility, often secondary to stress, low body weight, or excessive exercise.

**Q:** Define primary amenorrhea. What are the three diagnostic criteria?

**A:** 1. No menses by age 13 in the absence of secondary sexual characteristics
2. No menses by age 15 with secondary sexual characteristics
3. No menses following the normal developmental sequence (regardless of age)

Primary amenorrhea is distinguished from secondary amenorrhea by the absence of a prior menstrual period. The criteria account for whether secondary sexual development has occurred, as this guides the differential diagnosis.

**Q:** Define secondary amenorrhea.

**A:** No menses for >6 months OR 3 cycles after documented menarche.

Secondary amenorrhea implies a previous history of menstruation. The most common cause is pregnancy, which must always be excluded first (B-hCG). Functional hypothalamic amenorrhea is the most common non-pregnancy cause.

**Q:** In a patient with primary amenorrhea and normal breast development but absent uterus on exam, what are the two main diagnoses to consider?

**A:** 1. Androgen insensitivity syndrome (AIS) — 46,XY karyotype, testes produce testosterone but end-organs are unresponsive; breast development due to peripheral aromatization
2. Müllerian agenesis (MRKH syndrome) — 46,XX, normal ovaries, absent uterus/upper vagina

Both conditions present with normal secondary sexual characteristics (breasts) but absent uterus. Karyotype differentiates: 46,XY = AIS; 46,XX = MRKH. In AIS, management includes gonadal resection after puberty (to prevent malignancy) and creation of neo-vagina.

**Q:** Describe the progesterone (progestin) challenge test and how to interpret it in the workup of amenorrhea.

**A:** Give medroxyprogesterone acetate (Provera®) 10 mg PO daily for 10–14 days.
• Positive result (withdrawal bleed within 2–7 days): indicates adequate estrogen levels (anovulatory cause, e.g., PCOS)
• Negative result (no withdrawal bleed): suggests hypoestrogenism, excessive androgens/progesterone, pregnancy, obstructive cause (cervical stenosis), or structural cause (uterine adhesions/Asherman's)

A withdrawal bleed confirms the endometrium has been primed by estrogen and the outflow tract is patent. Absence of a bleed after progesterone challenge suggests either inadequate estrogen priming (e.g., hypothalamic, pituitary, or ovarian failure) or an outflow tract obstruction.

**Q:** What initial investigations should be ordered for secondary amenorrhea?

**A:** 1. B-hCG (rule out pregnancy — first step)
2. FSH, LH
3. Prolactin
4. TSH (thyroid dysfunction)
5. Androgens (testosterone, DHEA)
6. Estradiol
7. Pelvic U/S

A systematic hormonal workup categorizes amenorrhea as hypergonadotropic (high FSH = primary ovarian insufficiency), hypogonadotropic (low FSH = hypothalamic/pituitary cause), or normogonadotropic (PCOS, outflow obstruction). Pregnancy must be excluded first.

**Q:** When is a CT/MRI head indicated in the workup of hyperprolactinemia causing amenorrhea?

**A:** CT head if prolactin >100 ng/dL (or ng/mL). MRI of hypothalamus and pituitary if lesion suspected or prolactin markedly elevated.

Mildly elevated prolactin may be idiopathic or medication-related, but levels >100 ng/mL strongly suggest a prolactinoma. Neuroimaging is required to identify a pituitary macroadenoma or other CNS lesion causing pituitary stalk compression.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of a prolactinoma?

**A:** Galactorrhea, visual changes (bitemporal hemianopsia from optic chiasm compression), and headache.

Prolactinomas are the most common secretory pituitary tumours. Elevated prolactin inhibits GnRH pulsatility, causing hypogonadotropic hypogonadism and amenorrhea. Large macroadenomas can compress surrounding structures causing visual field defects and headache.

**Q:** What is the management of hyperprolactinemia-related amenorrhea when the patient desires fertility vs. does not desire fertility?

**A:** • Fertility desired: Bromocriptine or cabergoline (dopamine agonists) to reduce prolactin and restore ovulation
• No fertility desired: Combined OCPs
• Demonstrable lesion on MRI: surgical management (transsphenoidal resection)

Dopamine agonists (cabergoline preferred over bromocriptine due to better tolerability) suppress prolactin secretion and can shrink prolactinomas. OCPs regulate cycles but do not normalize prolactin or restore ovulation. Surgery is reserved for medication-refractory cases or large macroadenomas.

**Q:** What is the management of Asherman's syndrome?

**A:** Hysteroscopy with excision of intrauterine synechiae (adhesions). Evaluation with hysterosalpingography or sonohysterography.

Asherman's syndrome results from intrauterine adhesions (most commonly post-dilation and curettage), causing amenorrhea or hypomenorrhea. Hysteroscopic lysis of adhesions restores the endometrial cavity and can restore menstruation and fertility.

**Q:** What is the PALM-COEIN classification of abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB)?

**A:** Structural (PALM):
• P — Polyp
• A — Adenomyosis
• L — Leiomyoma (Lsm = submucosal; Lo = other)
• M — Malignancy and Hyperplasia

Non-structural (COEIN):
• C — Coagulopathy
• O — Ovulatory dysfunction
• E — Endometrial
• I — Iatrogenic
• N — Not yet classified

The FIGO PALM-COEIN classification provides a systematic framework for evaluating AUB. PALM causes are structural and typically identified on imaging or pathology; COEIN causes are functional and require lab/hormonal evaluation.

**Q:** What defines heavy menstrual bleeding (AUB)?

**A:** Any of:
• ≥80 cc of blood loss per cycle
• ≥8 days of bleeding per cycle
• Menstrual flow that significantly affects quality of life

In clinical practice, quantifying blood loss is difficult, so patient-reported impact (e.g., soaking pads/tampons, passage of clots, disruption to daily activities) is used. Quality of life impact is now a key criterion.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of postmenopausal bleeding and what must be ruled out?

**A:** Most common cause: genitourinary syndrome of menopause (atrophy).
Must rule out: endometrial cancer and hyperplasia — requires endometrial biopsy before treating for atrophy.

Any postmenopausal bleeding is endometrial cancer until proven otherwise. Even though atrophy is more common, endometrial sampling (biopsy or D&C) must be performed first to exclude malignancy before attributing bleeding to atrophy.

**Q:** When should an endometrial biopsy be performed for AUB?

**A:** • All patients ≥40 years with AUB
• Patients <40 years with persistent AUB
• Patients <40 years with endometrial cancer risk factors (type 2 DM, obesity, chronic anovulation, PCOS, Lynch syndrome)

Endometrial biopsy is the key investigation for AUB-M (malignancy). Risk factors for endometrial cancer include prolonged unopposed estrogen exposure (obesity, anovulation, PCOS, tamoxifen use).

**Q:** What is the medical management for acute, severe AUB (stepwise approach)?

**A:** a) Antifibrinolytic (tranexamic acid/Cyklokapron®) 500–1000 mg PO BID-TID PRN (up to 4 g/d)
b) IV antifibrinolytic 10 mg/kg IV q8h AND/OR IV conjugated estrogen (Premarin® 25 mg IV q4h × 24h) with antiemetic
c) Tapering OCP regimen: 35 mcg pill TID × 7d, then taper to once daily for 3 weeks
d) More commonly: taper OCP to TID × 2d → BID × 2d → once daily (after a or b)
Also consider: high-dose progestins, GnRH agonists, progesterone IUD

Acute severe AUB requires rapid hemostasis. IV estrogen promotes endometrial proliferation to stop bleeding. Antifibrinolytics inhibit plasminogen activation. Tapering OCP stabilizes the endometrium. Resuscitation and fluid replacement are priorities if hemodynamically unstable.

**Q:** What surgical options are available for AUB in patients who have completed childbearing?

**A:** • Endometrial ablation (definitive for AUB; repeat may be needed if <40 yr and symptoms recur)
• Hysterectomy: definitive treatment
• Uterine artery embolization (UAB-L)
• Myomectomy (if fibroid-related)
• Polypectomy (if polyp-related)

Endometrial ablation destroys the endometrial lining and is effective for AUB, but is contraindicated in patients desiring future fertility. Hysterectomy is the only truly definitive treatment. UAE is effective for fibroid-related bleeding but has a higher re-intervention rate.

**Q:** What is the definition of primary dysmenorrhea and how does it differ from secondary dysmenorrhea?

**A:** Primary dysmenorrhea: recurrent, crampy lower abdominal pain during menses in the ABSENCE of demonstrable disease. Begins hours before onset of bleeding, persists 48–72 hours. Associated NV, altered bowel habits, headache (prostaglandin-mediated).

Secondary dysmenorrhea: pain during menses attributed to an underlying disorder (endometriosis, adenomyosis, fibroids, PID, IUD-copper, etc.).

Primary dysmenorrhea is caused by excess prostaglandin F2-alpha production, causing myometrial contractions and uterine ischemia. NSAIDs (started before onset of pain) are first-line treatment. Secondary dysmenorrhea requires treating the underlying cause.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for primary dysmenorrhea?

**A:** • Regular exercise, local heat
• NSAIDs: should be started BEFORE onset of pain (e.g., naproxen, ibuprofen)
• Combined hormonal contraceptives (CHCs) with continuous or extended use: suppress ovulation, reduce menstrual flow

NSAIDs work by inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis. Starting them 1–2 days before expected menses is more effective than waiting for pain onset. CHCs reduce endometrial prostaglandin production by suppressing ovulation and thinning the endometrium.

**Q:** What causes of secondary dysmenorrhea should be considered in a patient with dyspareunia and abnormal bleeding?

**A:** Endometriosis, adenomyosis, uterine fibroids (leiomyoma), PID/chronic pelvic inflammatory disease, uterine polyps, ovarian cysts, intrauterine synechiae, copper IUD.

The triad of dysmenorrhea + dyspareunia + infertility/abnormal bleeding strongly suggests endometriosis. Physical exam findings (uterosacral nodularity, fixed retroversion, adnexal tenderness) help distinguish secondary from primary causes.

**Q:** Define endometriosis and its proposed etiology.

**A:** Definition: Presence of endometrial tissue (glands and stroma) OUTSIDE the uterine cavity; a chronic condition resolving only with menopause.

Proposed mechanisms (combination likely):
1. Retrograde menstruation (Sampson's theory) — most widely accepted
2. Coelomic metaplasia
3. Immunologic: decreased NK cell activity limiting clearance of ectopic endometrial cells
4. Vascular/lymphatic dissemination (explains extra-pelvic disease)

No single mechanism explains all cases. Retrograde menstruation occurs in most women, but only those with immunologic defects (decreased NK cell activity) develop endometriosis. Lymphatic spread explains rare extra-pelvic sites (lung, diaphragm).

**Q:** What are the risk factors for endometriosis?

**A:** • Family history (7–10× increased risk if 1st-degree relative affected)
• Obstructive genital tract anomalies (earlier onset — relates to retrograde menstrual mechanism)
• Nulliparity
• Age >25 years
• Early menarche (<11–13 y/o)
• Shorter menstrual cycles (<27 days)
• Low BMI
• Low birth weight
• Increased menstrual flow

Risk factors that increase retrograde menstrual flow or extend endometrial exposure increase risk of endometriosis. Protective factors include multiparity, lactation, and late menarche (fewer lifetime ovulations/menstrual cycles).

**Q:** What is the incidence of endometriosis and its relationship to infertility?

**A:** • Incidence: 15–30% of premenopausal patients
• Mean age at presentation: 25–30 years
• 30–40% of patients with endometriosis will be infertile
• 15–30% of infertile patients will have endometriosis

Endometriosis causes infertility through multiple mechanisms: distorted pelvic anatomy, adhesions affecting tubal function, altered peritoneal fluid environment, and impaired implantation. Diagnosis is often delayed by 5 years from symptom onset.

**Q:** What are the three main clinical presentations of endometriosis?

**A:** 1. Pain (80%): secondary dysmenorrhea, deep dyspareunia, dyschezia, dysuria, sacral backache, chronic pelvic pain worsening perimenstrually
2. Infertility (25%): 30–40% of endometriosis patients; 15–30% of infertile patients have endometriosis
3. Mass (endometrioma) (20%): ovarian 'chocolate' cyst containing old blood; may be asymptomatic

Endometriosis is a multifaceted condition often presenting with a combination of these features. Diagnosis is frequently delayed because symptoms overlap with other conditions and endometriosis can be asymptomatic.

**Q:** What are the laparoscopic findings of endometriosis and what is the gold standard for diagnosis?

**A:** Gold standard: Direct visualization at laparoscopy + biopsy showing ≥2 of: endometrial epithelium, glands, stroma, hemosiderin-laden macrophages.

Laparoscopic findings:
• Mulberry spots: dark blue/brownish-black implants on uterosacral ligaments, cul-de-sac
• Endometriomas: 'chocolate cysts' on ovaries
• 'Powder-burn' lesions on peritoneal surface
• Early white lesions and clear blebs
• Peritoneal 'pockets'

Endometriotic lesions vary in appearance from classic dark 'powder-burn' lesions to subtle white/clear lesions. Biopsy confirmation provides histologic diagnosis. CA-125 may be elevated but is not sufficiently sensitive or specific for diagnosis.

**Q:** According to SOGC guidelines, when is surgical confirmation required before starting medical treatment for endometriosis?

**A:** Surgical confirmation of disease is NOT required prior to starting medical management. A suggestive history, even with a negative exam, is adequate for a presumptive diagnosis and initiation of treatment.

SOGC guidelines support empirical medical therapy for presumed endometriosis without mandatory laparoscopic confirmation. This avoids surgical risks in patients with classic presentations. Failure of medical therapy, desire for fertility optimization, or large adnexal masses may then prompt surgery.

**Q:** What is the first-line and second-line medical treatment for endometriosis?

**A:** First-line:
• Cyclic/continuous estrogen-progestin (OCP)
• Progestin alone: IM medroxyprogesterone (Depo-Provera®) or oral dienogest (Visanne®)
• Mirena® IUS
• NSAIDs (e.g., naproxen sodium — avoid selective COX-2 inhibitors if trying to conceive)

Second-line:
• GnRH agonist: leuprolide (Lupron®) or elagolix (Orlissa®) — induces pseudomenopause
• GnRH antagonist: relugolix combo (Myfembree®)
• Danazol (Danocrine®) — weak androgen, rarely used due to side effects

Medical therapy suppresses endometrial implants and prevents disease progression. GnRH agonists require add-back estrogen/progestin if used >6 months to prevent bone mineral density loss and vasomotor symptoms. Danazol is rarely used due to androgenic side effects (acne, hirsutism, voice change).

**Q:** What are the side effects of GnRH agonists (e.g., leuprolide/Lupron®) used for endometriosis, and what add-back therapy is required?

**A:** Side effects: hot flashes, vaginal dryness, reduced libido, decreased bone mineral density (with prolonged use >6 months).

Add-back therapy: required when GnRH agonist used >6 months — add progestin or estrogen to prevent decreased BMD and reduce vasomotor side effects.

Exception: Elagolix (Orlissa®) low dose (150 mg once daily) does NOT require add-back. High dose (200 mg BID) DOES require add-back.

GnRH agonists cause hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (pseudomenopause), reducing estrogen which drives endometriosis but also causes menopausal symptoms and bone loss. Add-back therapy provides enough hormones to prevent side effects without stimulating endometriosis.

**Q:** When is surgery indicated for endometriosis?

**A:** Indications:
• Failure of medical management
• Desire for fertility optimization (best time to conceive is immediately after conservative surgery)
• Large adnexal masses (endometriomas)
• Adhesive disease causing obstruction (bowel/urinary)
• Definitive treatment desired

Surgical options:
• Conservative: laparoscopic ablation/resection of implants, lysis of adhesions, ovarian cystectomy
• Definitive: hysterectomy + bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy

Conservative surgery preserves fertility and is preferred for reproductive-age patients. If not planning pregnancy post-operatively, medical suppression is recommended to prevent recurrence. Medical treatment is often continued postoperatively to reduce recurrence.

**Q:** What is Myfembree® and what is its indication?

**A:** Myfembree® = relugolix 40 mg + estradiol 1 mg + norethindrone acetate 0.5 mg.
It is a GnRH antagonist combination tablet.
Indications:
1. Moderate to severe pain associated with endometriosis
2. Heavy menstrual bleeding associated with uterine fibroids in premenopausal women
The add-back (estradiol + norethindrone) counters vasomotor side effects of GnRH antagonism.

GnRH antagonists (unlike agonists) suppress the HPO axis immediately without an initial flare. Myfembree® includes add-back therapy in a single tablet for convenience. It is not a contraceptive.

**Q:** What is adenomyosis, and how does it differ from endometriosis?

**A:** Adenomyosis: extension of endometrial glands and stroma INTO the myometrium (uterine wall); synonym 'endometriosis interna'.

Key differences from endometriosis:
• Older age group (40–50 yr vs. 25–30 yr)
• Uterus symmetrically bulky (vs. normal or adnexal mass in endometriosis)
• Found in 20–40% of hysterectomy specimens
• Halban's sign: tender, softened uterus on premenstrual bimanual exam
• Final diagnosis: pathologic (post-hysterectomy)

Adenomyosis and endometriosis can coexist. Adenomyosis is a common incidental histologic finding in hysterectomy specimens. It causes heavy menstrual bleeding and secondary dysmenorrhea through disruption of normal myometrial contractility.

**Q:** What is Halban's sign and what condition does it suggest?

**A:** Halban's sign: tender, softened uterus on premenstrual bimanual examination.
Suggests: adenomyosis.

In adenomyosis, the uterus becomes edematous and tender due to cyclical stimulation of ectopic endometrial tissue within the myometrium. The uterus is symmetrically enlarged (<14 cm) and characteristically tender on premenstrual exam.

**Q:** What is the definitive treatment for adenomyosis and what medical options are available?

**A:** Definitive: hysterectomy — treatment of choice in patients who have completed childbearing.

Medical options:
• Iron supplements (for anemia)
• Analgesics, NSAIDs
• Mirena® IUS
• CHC, dienogest (Visanne®), medroxyprogesterone (Depo-Provera®) — limited evidence
• GnRH agonists: leuprolide (Lupron®), elagolix (Orlissa®)
• Danazol 100–200 mg daily (trial × 4 months)

Medical therapy for adenomyosis suppresses symptoms but does not cure the disease. Mirena® IUS reduces heavy bleeding and dysmenorrhea. Hysterectomy is curative and the definitive treatment for patients who have completed childbearing.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and pathogenesis of uterine fibroids (leiomyomata)?

**A:** Epidemiology:
• Diagnosed in ~40–50% of premenopausal patients >35 yr
• More common and larger in Black patients (earlier age of onset)
• Occur in 70–80% of women by age 50
• Typically regress after menopause

Pathogenesis:
• Estrogen stimulates monoclonal smooth muscle proliferation
• Progesterone stimulates proteins that inhibit apoptosis
• Degenerative changes occur when tumour outgrows blood supply

Fibroids are the most common benign gynaecologic tumours. They are estrogen-sensitive and thus shrink after menopause. They have minimal malignant potential (sarcomatous degeneration is rare).

**Q:** What is the FIGO classification of uterine fibroids by location?

**A:** Submucosal (grow toward endometrial cavity):
• Type 0: pedunculated (entirely intracavitary)
• Type 1: <50% intramural
• Type 2: ≥50% intramural

Intramural (most common — grow within muscular wall):
• Type 3: contacts endometrium, 100% intramural
• Type 4: intramural

Subserosal (grow from serosa):
• Type 5: >50% intramural
• Type 6: <50% intramural
• Type 7: pedunculated subserosal

Cervical:
• Type 8: other (cervical location)

Location determines symptoms and management. Submucosal fibroids most commonly cause AUB and infertility (distort endometrial cavity). Subserosal fibroids cause bulk symptoms. Intramural are most common. FIGO classification guides treatment (e.g., hysteroscopic resection for submucosal).

**Q:** What are the clinical features and symptoms of uterine fibroids?

**A:** Majority asymptomatic (incidental finding on exam or U/S).

When symptomatic:
1. Heavy/prolonged menstrual bleeding and dysmenorrhea (most common: 26–29%)
2. Pressure/bulk symptoms: pelvic pressure/heaviness, increased abdominal girth, urinary frequency, difficulty voiding, constipation/bloating
3. Reproductive dysfunction: infertility, recurrent pregnancy loss, pregnancy complications (degeneration, malpresentation, obstructed labour)
• Rare emergency: acute urinary retention (surgical emergency!)

Submucosal fibroids most often cause AUB by disrupting endometrial vascularity and impairing normal haemostasis. Intramural and subserosal fibroids more commonly cause bulk symptoms. Pedunculated fibroids may torse, causing acute pelvic pain.

**Q:** What investigations are used to evaluate uterine fibroids?

**A:** • Bimanual exam: uterus asymmetrically enlarged
• Pelvic/transvaginal U/S: confirms diagnosis, assesses location
• Sonohysterogram (saline infusion): differentiates polyps from submucosal fibroids; assesses size/degree of submucosal component; useful before hysteroscopic resection
• Diagnostic office hysteroscopy: visualizes endometrial cavity; rules out small polyps; plans hysteroscopic resection
• MRI: preoperative planning (e.g., before laparoscopic myomectomy)
• Endometrial biopsy: rules out hyperplasia/cancer if AUB, especially age ≥40 or risk factors
• CBC: assess for anemia

Transvaginal U/S is the first-line imaging. Sonohysterogram improves detection of submucosal fibroids that may impact fertility. Pregnancy must be excluded in all patients of reproductive age.

**Q:** What are the medical treatment options for symptomatic uterine fibroids?

**A:** • Antiprostaglandins: NSAIDs (ibuprofen, naproxen)
• Antifibrinolytic: tranexamic acid (Cyklokapron®)
• CHC (combined hormonal contraceptives), Mirena® IUS, Depo-Provera®
• GnRH agonist: leuprolide (Lupron®) — used 3 months preoperatively to shrink fibroids, increase Hb, correct anemia; can bridge to menopause with add-back
• GnRH antagonist: Myfembree® (relugolix + estradiol + norethindrone acetate) — manages heavy menstrual bleeding from fibroids

Medical therapy controls symptoms but does not eliminate fibroids. GnRH agonists are often used preoperatively to reduce fibroid size and blood loss. Myfembree® is an oral once-daily option with add-back therapy included.

**Q:** What are the surgical options for uterine fibroids and when is each preferred?

**A:** • Hysteroscopic resection: submucosal fibroids (types 0–2), combined with endometrial ablation for AUB-Lsm (NOT if desiring future pregnancy)
• Myomectomy (hysteroscopic, open, or laparoscopic): fibroid removal preserving uterus — preferred when fertility is desired
• Uterine artery embolization (UAE): occludes both uterine arteries, shrinks fibroids by 50% at 6 months, improves bleeding in 90%; NOT for future pregnancy planning; higher surgical re-intervention rate
• Hysterectomy: definitive treatment, no future fertility

UAE is effective for bleeding but has higher re-intervention rates vs. surgical approaches and risks pregnancy complications. Patients may become septic during fibroid degeneration post-UAE requiring emergency hysterectomy. Surgery during pregnancy is traditionally avoided.

**Q:** What are the long-term outcomes of uterine artery embolization (UAE) for fibroids compared to surgery?

**A:** • UAE occludes both uterine arteries, shrinks fibroids ~50% at 6 months
• Improves heavy bleeding in 90% within 1–2 months
• No significant difference in patient satisfaction or live birth rates vs. surgery
• UAE associated with HIGHER risk of minor complications and need for additional surgical re-intervention within 2 years
• NOT recommended for patients planning future pregnancies (risk of pregnancy complications)

Meta-analyses of RCTs show UAE is non-inferior to surgery for patient satisfaction and live birth rates but requires more re-interventions. UAE avoids surgical risks (anesthesia, adhesions) but has unique complications including post-embolization syndrome and rare emergency hysterectomy.

**Q:** What are the effectiveness rates for common contraceptive methods (perfect use)?

**A:** • Abstinence: 100%
• Vasectomy: 99.9%
• Mirena® IUS: 99.9%
• Postcoital copper IUD (EPC): 99.9%
• Ella® (ulipristal acetate): 99.9% within 7d
• Etonogestrel implant (NEXPLANON®): 99%
• OCP/NuvaRing®/Ortho Evra® patch: 99.7%
• Depo-Provera®: 99.7%
• Drospirenone (Slynd®): 99.3%
• Copper IUD: 99.3%
• Tubal ligation: 99.65%
• Condom alone: 98%
• Plan B® (levonorgestrel): 98% (within 24h)
• Diaphragm with spermicide: 94%

Perfect use vs. typical use effectiveness differ significantly for user-dependent methods (condoms: 98% vs. 82%; OCP: 99.7% vs. 92%). Long-acting reversible contraceptives (LARCs) have the highest effectiveness because they are user-independent. Canadian Paediatric Society endorses LARCs as first-line for adolescents.

**Q:** How does the combined oral contraceptive pill (OCP) work? What are the mechanisms of action?

**A:** • Estrogen: suppresses FSH → inhibits follicular development
• Progestin: prevents LH surge → suppresses ovulation; thickens cervical mucus; decreases tubal motility; decidualizes endometrium
• Combined effect: prevents fertilization and implantation

Most OCPs contain ethinyl estradiol (10–35 mcg) plus a progestin. The progestin type varies (norethindrone, levonorgestrel, desogestrel, drospirenone) and determines specific effects (androgenic vs. anti-androgenic). Monophasic pills have consistent progestin throughout; triphasic vary throughout cycle.

**Q:** What are the non-contraceptive benefits of combined OCPs?

**A:** • Decreased menstrual bleeding
• Decreased acne
• Decreased progression of endometriosis (inhibits ovulation)
• Decreased risk of ovarian cancer
• Decreased risk of endometrial cancer
• Relief of dysmenorrhea

OCPs are widely used for non-contraceptive indications. The risk reduction for ovarian and endometrial cancer persists for years after cessation. These benefits must be balanced against risks (VTE, hypertension, stroke in susceptible individuals).

**Q:** What are the risks of combined OCPs?

**A:** • Increased risk of venous thromboembolism (VTE)
• Increased risk of hypertension
• Increased risk of stroke (in susceptible individuals, e.g., migraines with aura)
• Unclear associations with elevated cancer risks
• Side effects: breast tenderness, nausea, bloating, unscheduled bleeding, amenorrhea (no clear evidence of weight gain)

VTE risk is the major concern, particularly with higher-dose estrogen and certain progestins (desogestrel, gestodene). OCPs are contraindicated in women with history of VTE, clotting disorders, smoking >35 years, migraines with aura, uncontrolled hypertension, and certain cardiovascular diseases.

**Q:** What are the characteristics of the transdermal contraceptive patch (Ortho Evra®)?

**A:** • Changed weekly for 3 consecutive weeks, then off 1 week for withdrawal bleed
• Releases norelgestromin 6 mg and ethinyl estradiol 0.60 mg
• Has enough hormone for 9 days (forgiveness window)
• Applied to lower abdomen, back, upper arm, or buttocks (NOT breasts)
• Effectiveness >99% with perfect use
• May be less effective in patients >90 kg
• Can be used continuously or in extended fashion

The patch provides consistent hormone levels through transdermal absorption, avoiding first-pass hepatic metabolism. It should not be used with hepatitis C medications (obmitasvir, paritaprevir, ritonavir) or in patients with skin hypersensitivity/exfoliative dermatologic disorders.

**Q:** What are the characteristics of the NuvaRing® contraceptive ring?

**A:** • Thin flexible plastic ring inserted into vagina by patient
• Left in place for 3 weeks, then removed and discarded
• New ring inserted 7 days later (to allow withdrawal bleed)
• Releases etonogestrel 120 mcg/day and estradiol 15 mcg/day
• Has enough hormone for 35 days
• Effectiveness: 98%
• Side effects: vaginitis, vaginal discharge, mild breast tenderness, mild nausea
• Associated with LESS breakthrough bleeding than other methods
• Risk of toxic shock syndrome is rare

NuvaRing® delivers hormones locally via vaginal mucosa with low systemic dose. It can also be used continuously or in extended fashion. Compared to the patch, it has less systemic estrogen exposure, potentially reducing VTE and other estrogen-related side effects.

**Q:** What are the SOGC guidelines for starting hormonal contraceptives?

**A:** • Thorough history and BP measurement (post-pandemic SOGC guidelines: BP no longer required for virtual appointments)
• Pelvic exam NOT required (STI screening by urine; Pap smear screening starts at >25 years)
• Can start at any time during cycle, ideally within 5 days of LMP
• Follow-up visit 3 months after prescription
• Back-up contraception for 7 days recommended, particularly if initiated >5 days from LMP

SOGC and Canadian guidelines have moved toward reducing barriers to contraceptive access. Eliminating mandatory pelvic exams and BP requirements for virtual prescriptions improves access to hormonal contraception.

**Q:** What is the lactational amenorrhea method (LAM) and how effective is it?

**A:** LAM effectiveness: 98% — but only in first 6 months postpartum.
Requirements for effectiveness: fully/nearly fully breastfeeding AND amenorrhea AND within 6 months of delivery.
Typical use: 98% (first 6 months postpartum).

Breastfeeding suppresses GnRH pulsatility through elevated prolactin, inhibiting ovulation. All three criteria (exclusive breastfeeding, amenorrhea, <6 months postpartum) must be met simultaneously for 98% effectiveness. If any criterion fails, additional contraception is needed.

**Q:** What are the emergency postcoital contraception (EPC) options and their effectiveness?

**A:** 1. Plan B® (levonorgestrel only): 98% within 24 hours; effectiveness decreases by 70% at 72h
2. Yuzpe® method (estrogen + levonorgestrel): 98% within 24h; decreases by 30% at 72h
3. Ella® (ulipristal acetate): 99.9% within 7 days
4. Postcoital copper IUD: 99.9% within 7 days

EPC works primarily by delaying or inhibiting ovulation (not abortifacient). Copper IUD is the most effective EPC and also provides ongoing contraception. Ella® (UPA) is more effective than levonorgestrel, especially for patients >75 kg. Plan B® may be less effective in women >75 kg.

**Q:** What is the 'withdrawal bleed' on combined OCPs and is it medically necessary?

**A:** The withdrawal bleed is an apparent (false) period caused by withdrawal of hormones during the placebo/pill-free interval. It was originally devised so women could hide contraceptive use.

It is NOT medically necessary. Continuous OCP use (no pill-free intervals) results in fewer unintended pregnancies and is safe. Extended cycle preparations with less frequent withdrawal bleeds are also acceptable.

Modern evidence shows no health benefit to monthly withdrawal bleeds. Continuous OCP use can treat endometriosis, dysmenorrhea, and menstruation-related symptoms. Patient autonomy and medical indications guide the decision between cyclic and continuous regimens.

**Q:** What is the most common gynaecologic tumour and what is its malignant potential?

**A:** Uterine leiomyomata (fibroids) are the most common gynaecologic tumours.
Malignant potential: minimal (sarcomatous degeneration is rare).

Fibroids are benign smooth muscle tumours found in 40–50% of premenopausal women >35 years and in up to 70–80% of women by age 50. They are estrogen-sensitive and typically regress after menopause. Despite their prevalence, malignant transformation is extremely rare.

**Q:** A 26-year-old woman presents with progressive dysmenorrhea, deep dyspareunia, and infertility. Pelvic exam reveals uterosacral nodularity. CA-125 is mildly elevated. What is the diagnosis and what does CA-125 tell you?

**A:** Diagnosis: Endometriosis.

CA-125: May be elevated in endometriosis but should NOT be used as a diagnostic test — it is neither sensitive nor specific enough. It is not routinely ordered for diagnosis of endometriosis.

The clinical triad of dysmenorrhea + dyspareunia + infertility with uterosacral nodularity is highly suggestive of endometriosis. CA-125 is a tumour marker primarily used to monitor ovarian cancer (not diagnose it or endometriosis). A suggestive history even with negative exam warrants empiric medical treatment per SOGC guidelines.

**Q:** A 17-year-old presents with primary amenorrhea, normal breast development, and no uterus on pelvic exam. Karyotype is 46,XY. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Diagnosis: Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS) — complete form.

Management:
1. Gonadal resection AFTER puberty (wait until breast development complete — reduces malignancy risk while allowing feminization from aromatization of testosterone)
2. Psychological counselling
3. Creation of neo-vagina with progressive dilation
4. Hormone replacement therapy post-gonadectomy

In AIS, 46,XY individuals have non-functional androgen receptors. Testes produce testosterone (aromatized to estrogen peripherally → breast development) and anti-Müllerian hormone (AMH) → absent uterus/fallopian tubes. Gonads must be removed post-puberty due to gonadoblastoma risk (~2–3%).

**Q:** What is the 'sliding sign' on transvaginal ultrasound and what does its absence indicate?

**A:** The sliding sign assesses mobility between the rectum/sigmoid colon and the posterior uterus/vagina during real-time transvaginal U/S.

Absence of sliding sign = evidence of severe endometriosis involving the rectum and sigmoid colon (deeply infiltrating disease with adhesions).

The sliding sign is an ultrasound dynamic technique where the examiner gently moves the probe and assesses if the bowel slides freely over the posterior uterus. Loss of this mobility indicates posterior compartment adhesions/deeply infiltrating endometriosis, which has major implications for surgical planning.

**Q:** What is an endometrioma ('chocolate cyst') and how is it managed?

**A:** An endometrioma is an endometriotic cyst within the ovary containing old blood ('chocolate'-coloured fluid).

Management:
• Asymptomatic and small (<5 cm): conservative (no surgery needed)
• Symptomatic or expanding: surgical (ovarian cystectomy) — but this can decrease ovarian reserve
• If bilateral endometriomas or concern for fertility: refer to REI before surgery to consider ovarian tissue freezing
• Medical treatment is for pain, NOT for the endometrioma itself

Endometriomas occur in ~20% of endometriosis patients. Surgical cystectomy reduces ovarian reserve (removes healthy ovarian tissue along with cyst). In women desiring future fertility, fertility preservation consultation should occur before bilateral ovarian surgery.

**Q:** What is the most effective emergency contraception option and within what time frame should it be used?

**A:** Most effective EPC options (tied at 99.9%):
1. Postcoital copper IUD: within 7 days — also provides ongoing highly effective contraception
2. Ella® (ulipristal acetate): within 7 days — prescription required

Note: Plan B® and Yuzpe® are less effective (98%) and ideally used within 24 hours (decreasing efficacy at 72h).

The copper IUD is the most effective EPC overall, preventing >99.9% of pregnancies within 7 days of unprotected sex. It also serves as an ongoing LARC. Ulipristal acetate (Ella®) maintains efficacy better across the 5-day window compared to levonorgestrel (Plan B®), which is particularly important for use between 72–120 hours.

**Q:** What are the three most common organisms causing infectious vulvovaginitis, and which discharge characteristic distinguishes each?

**A:** 1. Candida albicans — whitish 'cottage cheese' discharge, intense pruritus, pH <4.5
2. Bacterial vaginosis (BV) — grey, thin, fishy-smelling discharge, pH >4.5
3. Trichomonas vaginalis — yellow-green, frothy, malodorous discharge, pH >4.5

Distinguishing the three common causes of vaginitis is essential for MCCQE1. Candida causes the classic thick white discharge with pruritus; BV causes thin grey discharge with amine odour due to anaerobic overgrowth; Trichomonas is a sexually transmitted flagellated protozoan causing frothy yellow-green discharge with strawberry cervix (petechiae). pH is a key differentiator: Candida keeps normal vaginal pH, while BV and Trichomonas are both alkaline.

**Q:** What is the significance of the 'whiff test' in vaginal discharge evaluation, and in which condition is it positive?

**A:** The whiff test (amine/KOH test) involves adding 10% KOH to vaginal discharge on a slide. A positive result (fishy odour) indicates bacterial vaginosis, caused by the liberation of amines (putrescine, cadaverine) produced by anaerobic bacteria.

The whiff test is a simple bedside test used to diagnose BV. The fishy odour results from volatile amines released when KOH raises the pH and volatilizes amine compounds produced by anaerobic bacteria such as Gardnerella vaginalis, Prevotella, and Mobiluncus. It is one of the Amsel criteria for BV diagnosis. It is not positive in Candida or Trichomonas.

**Q:** What are clue cells and in which condition are they found on saline wet mount?

**A:** Clue cells are squamous epithelial cells covered ('dotted') with coccobacilli (Gardnerella vaginalis), causing a stippled or granular appearance that obscures the cell border. They are the hallmark finding of bacterial vaginosis on saline wet mount, defined as >20% of epithelial cells being clue cells.

Clue cells are one of the Amsel criteria for bacterial vaginosis. On microscopy, the clue cells' border appears obscured by adherent coccobacilli. BV is characterized by replacement of normal Lactobacillus-dominant flora with anaerobic overgrowth. The saline wet mount also shows paucity of WBCs and reduced Lactobacilli in BV.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Trichomonas vaginalis infection, and why must partners be treated?

**A:** Metronidazole 2 g PO single dose (or metronidazole 500 mg BID x 7 days as alternative). Partners must be treated because Trichomonas is a sexually transmitted infection (STI); without partner treatment, re-infection is likely. Patients should be warned to avoid alcohol while on metronidazole (disulfiram-like reaction).

Trichomonas vaginalis is the only one of the three common vaginitides that is sexually transmitted and requires mandatory partner treatment. Treatment is indicated even in asymptomatic cases (25% are asymptomatic). In pregnancy, metronidazole 2 g orally once is also recommended. The disulfiram-like effect of metronidazole with alcohol causes flushing, nausea, and tachycardia.

**Q:** What is the treatment for bacterial vaginosis in a symptomatic pregnant woman?

**A:** Metronidazole 2 g orally once (oral treatment is preferred in pregnancy). BV in pregnancy is associated with recurrent preterm labour, preterm birth, and postpartum endometritis.

Symptomatic pregnant women with BV should be treated due to obstetrical complications. The preferred regimen in pregnancy is oral metronidazole. Vaginal metronidazole gel or clindamycin cream are alternatives. Routine treatment of partners is not recommended for BV (not sexually transmitted). Treatment of asymptomatic non-pregnant women is not indicated unless they are scheduled for pelvic surgery or procedures.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for vulvovaginal candidiasis in pregnancy vs. non-pregnancy?

**A:** In pregnancy: vaginal antifungals only (clotrimazole, butoconazole, miconazole, terconazole suppositories/creams) — oral fluconazole is contraindicated.
Non-pregnancy: topical azoles (1, 3, or 7-day courses) OR fluconazole 150 mg PO single dose.
Prophylaxis for recurrent infection: boric acid vaginal suppositories or luteal-phase fluconazole.

Oral fluconazole is contraindicated in pregnancy due to risk of fetal harm (associated with cardiac defects and spontaneous abortion at higher doses). Topical azoles are safe and effective in pregnancy. Recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis (≥4 episodes/year) warrants longer maintenance therapy. Partner treatment is not routinely recommended as candidiasis is not sexually transmitted.

**Q:** What is the most common bacterial STI in Canada, what percentage is asymptomatic, and what is the first-line treatment?

**A:** Chlamydia trachomatis is the most common bacterial STI in Canada. Approximately 80% of patients are asymptomatic. First-line treatment: doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 7 days, or azithromycin 1 g PO single dose. Note: doxycycline is contraindicated in the 2nd and 3rd trimesters of pregnancy.

Chlamydia is an obligate intracellular parasite. Because most patients are asymptomatic, screening is recommended for sexually active individuals under 25 years, during pregnancy, and in neonates born to infected mothers. Chlamydia often co-exists with gonorrhea, so patients with chlamydia should be tested for gonorrhea. It is a reportable disease in Canada. Sexual partners within 60 days should be tested and given empiric treatment.

**Q:** What are the complications of untreated Chlamydia trachomatis infection in women?

**A:** 1. Pelvic inflammatory disease (PID): low-grade salpingitis causing tubal obstruction
2. Infertility
3. Ectopic pregnancy
4. Chronic pelvic pain
5. Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (perihepatitis/liver capsule inflammation)
6. Reactive arthritis (HLA-B27 associated, male predominance), conjunctivitis, urethritis
7. Perinatal infection: neonatal conjunctivitis and pneumonia

Chlamydia is a major cause of preventable infertility in Canada. Even subclinical or minimally symptomatic infections can cause significant tubal damage. Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome presents with right upper quadrant pain from peri-hepatic adhesions. Reactive arthritis (formerly Reiter's syndrome) is the classic triad of arthritis, urethritis, and conjunctivitis seen predominantly in HLA-B27 positive males.

**Q:** When is test of cure recommended after Chlamydia treatment?

**A:** Test of cure (3 weeks after completing treatment) is recommended when:
1. Compliance with treatment is uncertain/suboptimal
2. An alternative treatment regimen was used
3. Persistent symptoms remain
4. The patient is prepubertal or pregnant
Test of cure is NOT routinely indicated for C. trachomatis or N. gonorrhoeae otherwise.

Routine test of cure is not recommended for most chlamydia infections because standard regimens have high cure rates. However, in vulnerable populations (pregnant, prepubertal) or when treatment compliance is uncertain, retesting 3 weeks after completion ensures eradication. Retesting at 3 months post-treatment is recommended to detect re-infection.

**Q:** What is the best diagnostic test for Chlamydia trachomatis, and what specimens can be used?

**A:** Nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT) is the preferred diagnostic test — equally or more effective than cervical culture. Specimens: cervical swab, vaginal swab (self-administered), rectal swab, pharyngeal swab, or urine. Tissue culture remains the definitive gold standard (obligate intracellular parasite), but NAAT is preferred clinically.

NAAT has largely replaced culture for routine chlamydia testing due to its superior sensitivity, non-invasive specimen collection (urine, self-swab), and ability to detect organisms from multiple sites. Self-administered vaginal swabs are now equally or more effective than clinician-collected cervical swabs, improving screening uptake. Chlamydia can also infect the pharynx and rectum in cases of oral/anal sex.

**Q:** What is the treatment of gonorrhea in a non-pregnant adult, and what is important to co-prescribe?

**A:** Ceftriaxone 250 mg IM single dose PLUS azithromycin 1 g PO single dose. Azithromycin is co-prescribed to treat possible co-existing chlamydia (high rate of co-infection) and to help prevent cephalosporin resistance. Partners must be treated. Gonorrhea is a reportable disease.

Neisseria gonorrhoeae Gram-negative intracellular diplococci are identified on Gram stain. In pregnant patients: ceftriaxone + azithromycin or spectinomycin 2 g IM + azithromycin 1 g PO (avoid quinolones due to resistance and fetal concerns). The dual therapy approach for gonorrhea (ceftriaxone + azithromycin) is recommended to prevent emergence of resistance and cover co-infection with chlamydia.

**Q:** What HPV types cause anogenital warts vs. cervical cancer?

**A:** Anogenital warts (condylomata acuminata): HPV types 6 and 11 (low-risk, non-oncogenic)
Cervical high-grade intraepithelial lesions/cancer: HPV types 16 and 18 (most oncogenic); also types 31, 33, 35, 36, 45 and others associated with cervical and vulvar intraepithelial neoplasia.

HPV is the most common viral STI in Canada with over 200 subtypes, >30 of which are genital. Types 6 and 11 cause condylomata acuminata (anogenital warts) but rarely cause cancer. Types 16 and 18 cause approximately 70% of cervical cancers. The 9-valent HPV vaccine (Gardasil-9) covers types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, and 58, providing broader oncogenic protection.

**Q:** What HPV treatment options are patient-administered vs. provider-administered for anogenital warts?

**A:** Patient-administered: podofilox 0.5% solution/gel BID x 3 days, then 4 days off (repeat x 4 weeks); imiquimod 5% cream 3x/week nightly x 16 weeks; sinecatechins 10% ointment TID x up to 16 weeks.
Provider-administered: cryotherapy (liquid nitrogen, q1-2 wk); podophyllin resin in benzoin (weekly); trichloroacetic acid (TCA) 80-90% or bichloroacetic acid weekly x 4-6 wk (safe in pregnancy); surgical/laser removal.

Anogenital warts require treatment if symptomatic or cosmetically bothersome. TCA is the only topical treatment safe in pregnancy. Imiquimod, podofilox (podofil0x), and podophyllin are contraindicated in pregnancy. Sinecatechins (green tea extract) is also contraindicated in pregnancy. For intraepithelial lesions and cancers, management follows colposcopy-guided protocols in gynecological oncology.

**Q:** What is koilocytosis and what does it represent on Pap smear?

**A:** Koilocytosis is the cytological hallmark of HPV infection on Pap smear: nuclear enlargement and atypia with a perinuclear halo (clearing) of the cytoplasm, giving an 'owl eye' appearance. It represents HPV-induced cytopathic effect and indicates viral infection of squamous epithelial cells.

Koilocytes (from Greek koilos = hollow) are HPV-infected squamous cells with characteristic perinuclear cytoplasmic vacuolization and nuclear atypia. They are found on cervical cytology and biopsy specimens. Detection of koilocytosis on Pap smear indicates subclinical or clinical HPV infection and warrants colposcopy. HPV DNA subtyping can guide management but is not routinely performed.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of HSV vulvar infection, and how does primary differ from recurrent infection?

**A:** Primary infection: average incubation 4 days (range 2-12 d); prodrome (tingling, burning, pruritus); multiple painful shallow ulcerations with small vesicles appearing 7-10 days post-infection; inguinal lymphadenopathy, malaise, fever; dysuria and urinary retention if urethra involved.
Recurrent infections: less severe, less frequent, shorter duration (usually only HSV-2); prodrome precedes lesions; no systemic symptoms typically.

HSV-2 causes 90% of genital herpes; HSV-1 causes 10%. Primary genital herpes is often more severe with constitutional symptoms because it is a new infection without prior immunity. Recurrences are common with HSV-2 (not HSV-1). The lesions are infectious from prodrome onset until complete healing. Education on transmission risk during prodrome and subclinical shedding is essential.

**Q:** What are the treatment regimens for first episode genital herpes, recurrent episodes, and daily suppressive therapy?

**A:** First episode (7-10 days): acyclovir 200 mg PO 5x/day, famciclovir 250 mg PO TID, or valacyclovir 1 g PO BID.
Recurrent episode (5 days): acyclovir 400 mg PO TID, famciclovir 125 mg PO BID, or valacyclovir 1 g PO once daily.
Suppressive therapy (consider if >6 recurrences/yr or recurrence every 2 months): acyclovir 400 mg BID, famciclovir 250 mg BID, valacyclovir 500 mg once daily, or valacyclovir 1 g once daily.
Severe disease: IV acyclovir 5-10 mg/kg q8h x 2-7 days.

Antiviral therapy for genital herpes reduces severity and duration of outbreaks but does not eradicate latent infection. Daily suppressive therapy reduces recurrence frequency, viral shedding, and transmission risk to partners. Suppressive therapy is indicated for patients with frequent recurrences (>6/year) or those who wish to minimize transmission risk. Valacyclovir is preferred for suppression due to better bioavailability and once-daily dosing.

**Q:** What is the most sensitive and specific diagnostic test for primary syphilis, and what are the serological testing options?

**A:** Most sensitive/specific: dark-field microscopy of aspirated ulcer serum or node fluid — detects Treponema pallidum spirochetes directly.
Non-treponemal screening tests: VDRL, RPR — become non-reactive after treatment; may be positive with other conditions (false positives).
Specific anti-treponemal tests: FTA-ABS, MHA-TP, TP-PA — confirmatory; remain reactive for life even after adequate treatment.

Dark-field microscopy is the gold standard for diagnosing primary syphilis when a chancre is present, because serological tests are often negative early in primary syphilis. Non-treponemal tests (VDRL, RPR) are used for screening and monitoring treatment response (titres fall with successful treatment). Specific treponemal tests confirm the diagnosis and remain positive lifelong. This distinction is critical for interpreting syphilis serology.

**Q:** What are the four stages of syphilis and their key clinical features?

**A:** Primary (3-4 wk after exposure): painless chancre on vulva/vagina/cervix; painless inguinal lymphadenopathy; serology usually negative.
Secondary (2-6 mo after initial infection): maculopapular rash on palms/soles/trunk; condylomata lata (broad-based anogenital grey lesions); malaise, lymphadenopathy; serology positive.
Latent: no clinical manifestations; detected by serology only.
Tertiary: tabes dorsalis, general paresis (neurological); aortic aneurysm/dilated aortic root (cardiovascular); vulvar gumma (rare).

Syphilis caused by Treponema pallidum progresses through stages if untreated (25-40% develop late complications). The painless chancre of primary syphilis is a classic MCCQE1 finding — it differs from the painful ulcers of herpes or chancroid. Secondary syphilis produces the classic rash on palms and soles. Condylomata lata (secondary syphilis) must be distinguished from condylomata acuminata (HPV warts). Neurosyphilis (tabes dorsalis) and cardiovascular syphilis are tertiary complications.

**Q:** What is the treatment of syphilis by stage in non-pregnant adults?

**A:** Primary, secondary, or latent syphilis <1 year duration: benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM single dose.
Latent syphilis >1 year duration: benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units IM weekly x 3 weeks.
Neurosyphilis: IV aqueous penicillin G 3-4 million units q4h x 10-14 days.

Penicillin remains the drug of choice for all stages of syphilis. Benzathine penicillin G provides sustained treponemicidal levels for the prolonged treatment needed in late syphilis. Neurosyphilis requires IV penicillin to achieve adequate CSF penetration. Syphilis is a reportable disease in Canada, and partners must be referred for treatment. Screening during pregnancy is mandated to prevent congenital syphilis.

**Q:** What is a Bartholin gland abscess, and how is it managed based on size?

**A:** A Bartholin gland abscess results from infection of an obstructed Bartholin duct (most commonly E. coli, polymicrobial, S. aureus, Group B Strep), presenting as unilateral swelling and pain at the inferior lateral vaginal opening.
Management:
- Large mass >3 cm (1st or 2nd episode): I&D under local anesthesia with Word catheter placement for 2-3 weeks. If recurrent after two failed Word catheter attempts: marsupialization.
- Small mass <3 cm: I&D with Word catheter, sitz baths, warm compresses.
- Antibiotics: reserved for recurrence, high-risk complicated infection, culture-positive MRSA, or systemic infection.

The Bartholin glands are bilateral mucus-secreting glands at the posterior vaginal introitus. Obstruction leads to cyst formation, which can become infected (abscess). The Word catheter is a small balloon catheter inserted to maintain drainage and allow epithelialization of a new duct opening, preventing recurrence. Marsupialization surgically creates a permanent new duct opening. Routine antibiotics are not indicated for uncomplicated abscesses managed with drainage.

**Q:** What is pelvic inflammatory disease (PID), what are the minimum diagnostic criteria, and what are the causative organisms in order of frequency?

**A:** PID is infection of the upper genital tract (above cervix) including endometrium, fallopian tubes, ovaries, and pelvic peritoneum, primarily in young sexually active women.
Minimum diagnostic criteria (one of): cervical motion tenderness, uterine tenderness, adnexal tenderness.
Additional criteria: fever >38.3°C, leukocytosis, abnormal mucopurulent cervical discharge, elevated CRP/ESR, NAAT-documented infection.
Causative organisms (in order): C. trachomatis, N. gonorrhoeae, M. genitalium; E. coli/colonic anaerobes (postmenopausal women only).
