PID is diagnosed clinically because the minimum diagnostic criteria are intentionally sensitive to avoid missing and under-treating subclinical cases that still cause significant tubal damage and infertility. The gold standard is laparoscopy but is only indicated in select cases. PID accounts for up to 20% of all infertility. Risk factors include age 15-25, multiple partners, previous PID, and IUD insertion (risk is highest in the first 3 weeks after insertion).

**Q:** What are the indications for inpatient vs. outpatient management of PID?

**A:** Inpatient indications: moderate-severe illness, atypical infection, adnexal/tubo-ovarian mass or pelvic abscess, failed/intolerable oral therapy, immunocompromised, pregnant, adolescent (first episode), surgical emergency cannot be excluded (e.g., ovarian torsion), PID secondary to instrumentation.
Outpatient: mild-moderate illness with typical findings, oral antibiotics tolerated, compliance ensured, follow-up within 48-72 h.

The decision for hospitalization is guided by clinical severity and risk factors. Tubo-ovarian abscess (TOA) is a classic indication for inpatient treatment given the risk of rupture and sepsis. If symptoms are not improving after 72 hours of inpatient treatment, laparoscopy may be required. IUD removal should be considered after a minimum of 24 hours of antibiotics. All partners should be treated.

**Q:** What antibiotic regimens are used for inpatient and outpatient treatment of PID?

**A:** Inpatient (IV until 24h after improvement, then complete 14d with oral doxycycline):
- Cefoxitin 2g IV q6h + doxycycline 100 mg PO/IV q12h, OR
- Clindamycin 900 mg IV q8h + gentamicin (loading dose 2 mg/kg, then 1.5 mg/kg q8h maintenance)
- Add metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 14d for tubo-ovarian abscess.

Outpatient (1st line): ceftriaxone 500 mg IM x1 + doxycycline 100 mg PO BID x 14d ± metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 14d.
Outpatient (2nd line): ofloxacin 400 mg PO BID x 14d or levofloxacin 500 mg PO once daily x 14d + metronidazole 500 mg PO BID x 14d.

PID must be treated with polymicrobial coverage due to its polymicrobial etiology. IV therapy is continued until clinical improvement, then oral doxycycline completes a 14-day total course. Outpatient regimens must cover chlamydia, gonorrhea, and anaerobes. Consider retesting for C. trachomatis and N. gonorrhoeae 4-6 weeks after completing treatment if documented infection was found.

**Q:** What are the complications of untreated PID, and how does infertility risk relate to number of PID episodes?

**A:** Complications: chronic pelvic pain, persistent hydrosalpinx, abscess/peritonitis, adhesion formation, ectopic pregnancy, infertility, bacteremia, septic arthritis, endocarditis.
Infertility risk: 1 episode PID → 13% infertility; 2 episodes → 36% infertility (cumulative risk increases significantly with each episode).

Tubal damage from PID is the major preventable cause of infertility and ectopic pregnancy in Canada. The scarring and adhesions from repeated infections progressively impair tubal function and patency. Even subclinical PID can cause significant damage. This highlights the importance of early diagnosis and treatment, as well as STI prevention and screening in at-risk populations.

**Q:** What is toxic shock syndrome (TSS) and what are its clinical features?

**A:** TSS is a clinical illness caused by staphylococcal toxin (TSST-1) characterized by: sudden high fever/chills, sore throat, headache, diarrhea, macular erythroderma followed by desquamation 1-2 weeks later, signs of multiorgan failure, and refractory hypotension.
Menstrual TSS: associated with high-absorbency tampon use. Non-menstrual gynecologic TSS: diaphragm/cervical cap/sponge use (>24h), postoperative/postpartum wound infections.

TSS is caused by Staphylococcus aureus exotoxin TSST-1 acting as a superantigen. Withdrawal of highly absorbent tampons from the market significantly reduced menstrual TSS incidence, but tampon use (especially prolonged use or high absorbency) remains a risk factor. The classic skin finding is diffuse macular erythroderma resembling sunburn, followed by desquamation of palms and soles 1-2 weeks later.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic treatment for toxic shock syndrome?

**A:** Vancomycin (load 20-35 mg/kg, maintenance 15-20 mg/kg q8-12h) + clindamycin 900 mg IV q8h + piperacillin-tazobactam 4.5g IV q6h. Continue combination therapy until hemodynamically stable for at least 48-72 hours. Also: remove source of infection, surgical debridement if warranted, treat shock, and ensure adequate hydration.

Clindamycin is included because it inhibits protein synthesis and thus suppresses toxin production by S. aureus, even against resistant strains. Vancomycin covers MRSA. Piperacillin-tazobactam provides broad-spectrum coverage including gram-negative organisms. Removal of the infectious source (e.g., tampon, IUD, packing) is the critical first step. IV fluid resuscitation for shock is essential.

**Q:** What are the four phases of the female sexual response cycle?

**A:** 1. Desire (libido): energy allowing initiation or response to sexual stimulation
2. Arousal (excitement): physical and emotional stimulation leading to breast and genital vasodilation and clitoral engorgement
3. Orgasm: maximized stimulation allowing relinquishment of control
4. Resolution: congestion and tension resolves within seconds; complete resolution may take up to 60 minutes
Note: these phases may vary in sequence, overlap, repeat, or be absent — cannot be applied consistently to all patients.

The Masters and Johnson model of sexual response (desire, arousal, orgasm, resolution) is the classic framework. However, contemporary understanding acknowledges that female sexual response is nonlinear and highly variable. Many women experience phases in different orders, and arousal may precede or follow desire. This is clinically important when assessing sexual dysfunction.

**Q:** What is the most common type of female sexual dysfunction, and what are the categories of etiology?

**A:** Most common: lack of desire (reduced libido).
Categories of etiology:
1. Biological: gynecological (pregnancy, menopause, endometriosis), urological, vascular, neurological, musculoskeletal, systemic (DM, thyroid), medications (SSRIs, beta-blockers, antipsychotics, benzodiazepines, OCPs), behavioural (smoking, alcohol, opioids, obesity)
2. Psychological: history of sexual violence, depression, anxiety, stress, fatigue
3. Relationship: abuse, relationship distress, ineffective sexual stimulation

A biopsychosocial approach to sexual dysfunction is essential. SSRIs are a particularly important pharmacological cause of sexual dysfunction (reduced desire, anorgasmia, delayed orgasm) — sildenafil may help SSRI-induced sexual dysfunction. The dyspareunia cycle illustrates how pain leads to anxiety, which leads to vaginismus, perpetuating the cycle. Thorough history-taking (including medications) is key to identifying reversible causes.

**Q:** What are the types of anorgasmia and how is dyspareunia classified?

**A:** Anorgasmia: Primary anorgasmia — never achieved orgasm under any circumstances. Secondary anorgasmia — was previously able to achieve orgasms but currently unable.
Dyspareunia: painful intercourse; classified as: Superficial (at vaginal introitus) or Deep (with deep penetration, often from pelvic pathology like endometriosis, PID).

Primary anorgasmia often responds to sex therapy, education, and anxiety reduction. Secondary anorgasmia suggests an acquired cause (medications, hormonal changes, relationship issues). Superficial dyspareunia suggests vulvar/vaginal pathology (atrophy, vaginismus, infection); deep dyspareunia suggests pelvic pathology (endometriosis, PID, adhesions). Pelvic floor physiotherapy is a key treatment for dyspareunia.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are available for sexual dysfunction related to lack of desire in postmenopausal women?

**A:** For lack of desire (biological causes) in postmenopausal women: androgens (testosterone, DHEA), estrogens, tibolone, serotonergic/dopaminergic agents (flibanserin, bupropion, buspirone), bremelanotide. For SSRI-induced low libido: consider sildenafil.
For arousal/lubrication issues: non-hormonal water-based lubricants, topical estrogen (cream, tablet, or ring), coconut oil.
For sexual pain: topical estrogen for atrophy, nerve modulators (amitriptyline, gabapentin, pregabalin), pelvic floor physiotherapy.

Hypoactive sexual desire disorder (HSDD) is the most common sexual complaint in women. Treatment is multimodal. Flibanserin (FDA-approved for premenopausal HSDD) acts as a serotonin-1A agonist and 5HT-2A antagonist. Bremelanotide is a melanocortin receptor agonist for premenopausal HSDD. Testosterone supplementation can benefit postmenopausal women with low libido, though not yet Health Canada-approved specifically for this indication.

**Q:** What is the definition of menopause, and at what age does it typically occur?

**A:** Menopause is defined as the absence of menses for 12 consecutive months (1 year). Average age of physiological menopause is 51 years (due to follicular atresia). Premature ovarian insufficiency (POI): before age 40 (causes: autoimmune, infection, Turner syndrome). Perimenopause: the period surrounding menopause characterized by fluctuating hormones, irregular cycles, and symptom onset.

FSH >35 IU/L on cycle day 3 (if still cycling) supports menopausal transition, but FSH is not always predictive due to monthly variation. The diagnosis is clinical: absence of menses for 12 months. For women >45 with irregular cycles and symptoms, no testing is necessary. Ages 40-45: check beta-hCG, prolactin, TSH. Under 40: also check FSH. Iatrogenic menopause results from surgical oophorectomy, radiation, or chemotherapy.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of menopause and how does it explain the hormonal changes observed?

**A:** Degenerating theca cells fail to respond to endogenous gonadotropins (FSH, LH) → less estrogen produced → decreased negative feedback on the hypothalamic-pituitary axis → increased FSH and LH. Stromal cells continue to produce androgens due to increased LH stimulation. Result: high FSH (FSH > LH), low estradiol (later), relative androgen excess.

The primary defect in menopause is ovarian follicular depletion. As follicle number and quality decline, inhibin B (which normally suppresses FSH) falls, allowing FSH to rise — the first hormonal change of menopause. Estradiol falls later. The continued androgen production by ovarian stroma explains why some women maintain some libido and why testosterone replacement may be beneficial in some postmenopausal women.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of menopause organized by system?

**A:** Vasomotor: hot flushes/flashes, night sweats, sleep disturbances, formication, nausea, palpitations (85% of women; tends to dissipate over time).
Urogenital (genitourinary syndrome of menopause/GUSM): vaginal dryness, dyspareunia, pruritus, post-coital bleeding, urinary frequency/urgency/incontinence. Exam: thinning tissues, erythema, petechiae, dryness.
Skeletal: osteoporosis, joint/muscle pain, back pain.
Skin/soft tissue: decreased breast size, skin thinning, loss of elasticity.
Psychological: anxiety, depression, irritability, fatigue, decreased libido, memory loss.

Vasomotor symptoms are the most common reason women seek treatment (20-30% seek medical attention, 10% are unable to work). GUSM (formerly vulvovaginal atrophy/urogenital atrophy) is a chronic progressive condition affecting sexual function, urinary health, and quality of life. Unlike vasomotor symptoms, GUSM does not improve spontaneously and worsens over time without treatment.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for menopausal vasomotor symptoms, and what are the alternatives?

**A:** First-line: Menopause hormonal therapy (MHT/HRT) — most effective for vasomotor symptoms.
Alternatives (for women who cannot take MHT): SSRI (e.g., paroxetine, escitalopram), venlafaxine (SNRI), gabapentin, propranolol, clonidine, acupuncture, behavioural modifications.

MHT is the most effective treatment for vasomotor symptoms and also addresses GUSM, osteoporosis, and mood symptoms. Contraindications to MHT include hormone-sensitive cancers (breast, endometrial), active thromboembolic disease, unexplained vaginal bleeding, and active liver disease. Non-hormonal options are available for women with contraindications. SSRIs are particularly useful for women with co-existing depression.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for genitourinary syndrome of menopause (GUSM/vaginal atrophy)?

**A:** Local (vaginal) estrogen (preferred for isolated GUSM): estrogen cream (Premarin®/Estragyn Vaginal Cream®), vaginal suppository/tablet (VagiFem®), vaginal ring (Estring®).
Non-hormonal: lubricants (Replens®), water-based lubricants, coconut oil.
Vaginal DHEA (prasterone/Intrarosa®): FDA-approved for GUSM.
SERMs: ospemifene (oral, for dyspareunia from GUSM).
Lifestyle: smoking cessation, weight loss.

Local vaginal estrogen is the preferred treatment for isolated GUSM as it provides local benefit with minimal systemic absorption and avoids systemic MHT risks. Unlike vasomotor symptoms that improve over time, GUSM is chronic and progressive. Vaginal DHEA (prasterone) is converted locally to estrogens and androgens and is effective for dyspareunia. Non-hormonal lubricants provide symptom relief but do not reverse the underlying atrophic changes.

**Q:** What are the investigations for menopausal transition and what do the hormonal findings show?

**A:** For women with irregular cycles and menopausal symptoms:
- >45 yr: no testing necessary (clinical diagnosis)
- 40-45 yr: beta-hCG, prolactin, TSH
- <40 yr: beta-hCG, FSH, prolactin, TSH
Findings: increased FSH (>35 IU/L on day 3 of cycle), FSH > LH, decreased estradiol (later). Note: FSH not always predictive due to monthly variation; use clinical criteria (12 months amenorrhea) to diagnose.

FSH >35 IU/L is supportive but not diagnostic of menopause due to fluctuation. In women <40, FSH is measured to diagnose premature ovarian insufficiency (POI), which has different management and long-term health implications. Beta-hCG is essential to rule out pregnancy in perimenopausal women with irregular cycles. TSH rules out thyroid dysfunction as a cause of irregular periods and vasomotor-like symptoms.

**Q:** A 24-year-old sexually active woman presents with lower abdominal pain and mucopurulent cervical discharge. Exam shows cervical motion tenderness. What is the diagnosis, and what workup is needed before starting treatment?

**A:** Diagnosis: Pelvic Inflammatory Disease (PID) — minimum criterion (cervical motion tenderness) met.
Workup: beta-hCG (must rule out ectopic pregnancy), CBC, blood cultures if sepsis suspected, urine R&M, NAAT for N. gonorrhoeae/C. trachomatis/M. genitalium, HIV testing, syphilis serology, pelvic ultrasound (assess for TOA), vaginal swab for Gram stain and C&S.

This is a classic MCCQE1 clinical scenario. The most critical step before diagnosing PID is ruling out ectopic pregnancy (beta-hCG), as both can present with pelvic pain and cervical motion tenderness. Ectopic pregnancy is a surgical emergency. Once ectopic is excluded, empiric treatment for PID should be started promptly — delay increases risk of tubal damage. Ultrasound detects tubo-ovarian abscess, which changes management to inpatient care.

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman has thick white vaginal discharge with intense itching. Microscopy shows hyphae and spores. What is the diagnosis, treatment, and what is contraindicated in pregnancy?

**A:** Diagnosis: Vulvovaginal candidiasis (Candida albicans most common).
Treatment (non-pregnant): topical azoles (clotrimazole, miconazole, terconazole) for 1, 3, or 7 days, OR fluconazole 150 mg PO single dose.
In pregnancy: topical azoles only — oral fluconazole is contraindicated (associated with fetal cardiac defects and spontaneous abortion).
Microscopy: KOH wet mount reveals pseudohyphae and spores.

Candida causes the classic white 'cottage cheese' discharge with intense pruritus, pH <4.5, and KOH wet mount showing hyphae/spores with paucity of WBCs. The absence of WBCs helps distinguish from BV and Trichomoniasis. Fluconazole's teratogenicity is a key safety point for prescribing during pregnancy. Recurrent infection (>4 episodes/year) warrants longer suppressive therapy and investigation for predisposing factors (DM, immunosuppression).

**Q:** What are the notifiable (reportable) STIs to public health in Canada, and which gynecological STIs are reportable?

**A:** CDC Notifiable/Reportable Diseases include: Chancroid, Chlamydia, Gonorrhea, Hepatitis A/B/C, HIV, Syphilis.
Of the gynecological STIs covered: Chlamydia, Gonorrhea, and Syphilis are all reportable. PID is reportable when linked to these organisms. All sexual partners must be notified and offered testing/treatment.

Reporting STIs to public health is a legal requirement in Canada, enabling contact tracing and epidemiological surveillance. For chlamydia, partners within 60 days prior to symptom onset or specimen collection (if asymptomatic) must be tested and empirically treated. Partner notification is essential to break transmission chains. Physicians have a duty to report these infections and may assist with partner notification.

**Q:** What is postoperative pelvic cellulitis after hysterectomy, and how is it managed?

**A:** Pelvic cellulitis is a common post-hysterectomy complication affecting the vaginal vault/cuff. Features: erythema, induration, tenderness, and discharge at the vaginal cuff.
Management: if fever and leukocytosis → broad-spectrum antibiotics (clindamycin + gentamicin); drain if excessive purulence or large mass. Can progress to intra-abdominal and pelvic abscess.

Vaginal cuff cellulitis is one of the most common postoperative complications of hysterectomy, typically occurring within the first week. The vaginal cuff represents a potential entry site for ascending infection. Broad-spectrum coverage targeting polymicrobial flora (including anaerobes) is essential. Progression to pelvic abscess requires drainage. This is distinct from vaginal cuff hematoma or dehiscence.

**Q:** What HPV subtypes are covered by the Gardasil-9 vaccine, and how does this prevent HPV disease?

**A:** Gardasil-9 covers HPV types: 6, 11 (anogenital warts), 16, 18 (most oncogenic, 70% of cervical cancers), 31, 33, 45, 52, 58 (additional oncogenic types). It is a prophylactic vaccine — prevents infection by HPV types covered, thereby preventing warts and HPV-related dysplasia/cancers. It does not treat existing HPV infection.

Gardasil-9 provides the broadest protection, covering 9 HPV types responsible for ~90% of cervical cancers and ~90% of anogenital warts. It is most effective when given before HPV exposure (before sexual debut). In Canada, it is offered as part of publicly funded school immunization programs. Condoms reduce but do not fully eliminate HPV transmission (virus can be shed from uncovered skin).

**Q:** What is Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome and what causes it?

**A:** Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome is perihepatitis (inflammation of the liver capsule) occurring as a complication of pelvic inflammatory disease, most commonly from Chlamydia trachomatis (also Neisseria gonorrhoeae). It presents with right upper quadrant pain that may be confused with hepatobiliary disease. It results from transperitoneal or hematogenous spread of infection from the fallopian tubes to the liver capsule.

Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome is an important complication to recognize because RUQ pain in a young sexually active woman should prompt consideration of PID as an etiology, not just hepatobiliary pathology. Perihepatic 'violin string' adhesions are the characteristic laparoscopic finding. Treatment is the same as for PID (antibiotics targeting chlamydia and gonorrhea). Liver function tests are usually normal.

**Q:** What is the dyspareunia cycle, and why is it clinically important?

**A:** The dyspareunia cycle: Painful intercourse (initially from organic cause) → anxiety with or without sexual response → vaginismus with intercourse → worsening pain → perpetuates cycle.
Clinical importance: even after the organic cause is treated, the psychological and musculoskeletal components (anxiety, vaginismus) may persist, requiring multidisciplinary treatment including pelvic floor physiotherapy and sex therapy.

Understanding the dyspareunia cycle helps clinicians appreciate why addressing only the biological cause of dyspareunia is insufficient. Vaginismus (involuntary contraction of pelvic floor muscles) can develop as a conditioned response to pain, persisting even after the original cause is resolved. Treatment requires addressing all components: biological (treat underlying pathology, topical estrogen for atrophy, nerve modulators), psychological (sex therapy, CBT), and physical (pelvic floor physiotherapy).

**Q:** What is the primary indication for menopausal hormone therapy (MHT)?

**A:** Treatment of menopausal symptoms, specifically vasomotor instability (hot flashes). MHT should NOT be prescribed if the only objective is prevention of chronic disease.

Canadian guidelines emphasize that MHT is appropriate for symptom relief. It should be individualized, using the lowest effective dose, and its use beyond age 60 or more than 5 years requires a careful risk-benefit discussion between clinician and patient.

**Q:** When is menopausal hormone therapy considered safest to initiate?

**A:** MHT is safest when initiated less than 10 years after menopause AND/OR in women younger than 60 years old.

The 'timing hypothesis' underlies this guideline. Initiating HT early in the menopausal transition, before established atherosclerosis, is associated with more favourable cardiovascular risk profiles. For symptomatic women meeting these criteria, benefits of symptom relief generally outweigh risks.

**Q:** Which route of estrogen administration is preferred in MHT and why?

**A:** Transdermal estrogen (patch or gel) is preferred overall, especially for patients with hypertriglyceridemia, impaired hepatic function, and those who smoke.

Oral estrogen undergoes first-pass hepatic metabolism, increasing hepatic production of clotting factors and triglycerides. Transdermal estrogen bypasses this effect, resulting in lower thromboembolic risk and less adverse impact on lipid profiles.

**Q:** Why is progestin added to estrogen therapy in women with an intact uterus?

**A:** Progestin is given in combination with estrogen to prevent development of endometrial hyperplasia and endometrial cancer in women with an intact uterus.

Unopposed estrogen stimulates endometrial proliferation, which over time leads to hyperplasia and malignancy. Adding progestin (or progesterone) counters this effect. Women without a uterus (post-hysterectomy) can safely use estrogen alone.

**Q:** Which breast cancer risk is associated with combined estrogen-progesterone HRT vs. estrogen-only HRT?

**A:** Combined estrogen-progesterone HRT increases breast cancer risk (RR ~1.3) after 4-5 years of use. Estrogen-only HRT has no increased risk for at least 8 years in women without a uterus; it may actually REDUCE the risk of breast cancer.

WHI data demonstrated that the addition of progestin to estrogen was responsible for increased breast cancer risk. Estrogen-alone users (post-hysterectomy) did not show this risk increase and had a reduction in breast cancer incidence in the trial. This distinction is clinically important.

**Q:** What are the benefits listed for MHT in Table 19 of Toronto Notes?

**A:** Benefits of MHT: reduction of vasomotor symptoms, reduction of sleep problems, reduction of mood/anxiety problems, reduction of aches and pains, osteoporosis prevention and treatment, reversal of vulvar and vaginal atrophy.

Local vaginal estrogen therapy is recommended if vulvovaginal atrophy is the only indication. For systemic benefits including vasomotor and mood symptoms, systemic MHT is appropriate. Osteoporosis prevention is a significant benefit of MHT, as osteoporosis is the single most important health hazard associated with menopause.

**Q:** What is the most important health hazard associated with menopause, and what is the leading cause of death post-menopause?

**A:** Osteoporosis is the single most important health hazard associated with menopause. Cardiovascular disease is the leading cause of death post-menopause.

While osteoporosis causes significant morbidity (fractures, disability), cardiovascular disease accounts for more mortality. Both are estrogen-deficiency related and can potentially be mitigated with MHT when initiated at the appropriate time.

**Q:** What are the relative contraindications to MHT?

**A:** Relative contraindications: pre-existing uncontrolled HTN, uterine fibroids and endometriosis, familial hyperlipidemias, migraine headaches, family history of estrogen-dependent cancer, chronic thrombophlebitis, DM with vascular disease, gallbladder disease, hypertriglyceridemia, impaired liver function, fibrocystic disease of the breasts.

For patients with relative contraindications, transdermal estrogen may be safer than oral (avoids hepatic first-pass, reduces triglycerides and clotting factor synthesis). Clinical judgment is required to weigh benefits against individual risks.

**Q:** What does the Mirena IUD provide in MHT regimens?

**A:** The Mirena IUD (levonorgestrel 52 mg, releasing ~20 mcg/day) is commonly used as the progestin component in combined MHT regimens, providing endometrial protection without systemic progestin side effects.

Using a levonorgestrel IUD delivers progestin locally to the endometrium. This reduces systemic progestin side effects (mood changes, bloating, breast tenderness) while still preventing endometrial hyperplasia in women receiving systemic estrogen.

**Q:** What is pelvic organ prolapse (POP)?

**A:** Pelvic organ prolapse is the descent of pelvic structures (bladder, rectum, small intestine, or uterus) into or through the vagina, resulting from weakening or failure of pelvic floor support structures.

POP results from damage to ligaments, fascia, and muscles of the pelvic floor. The primary clinical features are vaginal bulge and pressure. It is staged using the POP-Q system, which is based on the most distal portion of prolapse relative to the hymen.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for pelvic organ prolapse?

**A:** Risk factors: pregnancy, aging, decreased estrogen (post-menopause), increased intra-abdominal pressure (obesity, chronic cough, constipation, ascites, heavy lifting), ethnicity (greater incidence in White women vs. Asian or African), and connective tissue disorders.

Pregnancy and vaginal delivery are the most modifiable risk factors. Estrogen deficiency leads to decreased collagen in pelvic support structures. White ethnicity has a higher incidence for reasons that may relate to inherent differences in pelvic anatomy and connective tissue.

**Q:** How is pelvic organ prolapse staged using the POP-Q system?

**A:** Stage 1: most distal portion >1 cm above hymen. Stage 2: most distal portion between 1 cm above and 1 cm below hymen. Stage 3: most distal portion >1 cm below hymen but no further than 2 cm less than total vaginal length. Stage 4: complete procidentia (complete eversion).

The POP-Q (Pelvic Organ Prolapse Quantification) system is the standardized method to objectively quantify prolapse. Measurements are taken at rest and with straining in the dorsal lithotomy position.

**Q:** What are the general conservative treatments for pelvic organ prolapse?

**A:** Conservative treatments: weight loss, pelvic floor muscle training (Kegel exercises, pelvic physiotherapy), local vaginal estrogen therapy, and vaginal pessary (supportive or space-occupying intravaginal devices).

Conservative management is first-line for most patients with POP. Pessaries provide mechanical support and are an alternative to surgery, especially for patients who are poor surgical candidates or wish to preserve fertility. Local estrogen helps restore tissue integrity in hypoestrogenic patients.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and surgical treatment of anterior vaginal wall prolapse (cystocele)?

**A:** Clinical features: urinary frequency, urgency, nocturia, stress incontinence, incomplete bladder emptying with increased UTI risk. Surgical treatment: anterior colporrhaphy ('anterior repair'); consider additional procedures if documented stress urinary incontinence.

Anterior wall prolapse involves herniation of the bladder into the anterior vaginal wall. It can cause voiding dysfunction because of urethral kinking and bladder compression. Anterior colporrhaphy tightens the anterior vaginal wall and its fascial support.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and surgical treatment of posterior vaginal wall prolapse (rectocele)?

**A:** Clinical features: straining/digitation to evacuate stool, constipation. Treatment: general conservative measures, laxatives and stool softeners; surgical: posterior colporrhaphy ('posterior repair') with plication of endopelvic fascia and approximation of perineal muscles (can cause dyspareunia).

Rectocele is herniation of the rectum into the posterior vaginal wall. Patients may need to apply digital pressure to the posterior vaginal wall to complete defecation. Posterior repair restores the rectovaginal septum but carries a risk of dyspareunia due to tissue tightening.

**Q:** What type of prolapse is a vault prolapse, and why is it considered a true hernia of the pelvis?

**A:** Vault prolapse (previously 'enterocele') is an apical prolapse post-hysterectomy where the apex of the vaginal vault protrudes into the vagina, often containing small bowel. It is the only true hernia of the pelvis because peritoneum herniates with the small bowel.

After hysterectomy, the apex of the vaginal vault requires ligamentous support. If this support fails, the peritoneal sac (containing small intestine) can herniate through the vaginal apex — a true hernia by anatomic definition. Treatment includes sacrocolpopexy, sacrospinous fixation, or uterosacral ligament suspension.

**Q:** What is uterine prolapse and what are its clinical features?

**A:** Uterine prolapse is protrusion of the cervix and uterus into the vagina (apical prolapse). Features: groin/back pain (stretching of uterosacral ligaments), pelvic heaviness/pressure, worse with standing and lifting, worse at end of day, relieved by lying down, possible ulceration/bleeding (especially if hypoestrogenic), urinary incontinence.

Symptoms worsen with increased intra-abdominal pressure and improve when recumbent because gravity is no longer a factor. Treatment is vaginal hysterectomy plus surgical prevention of vault prolapse. Pessary is an alternative for non-surgical candidates.

**Q:** Define stress urinary incontinence and list its risk factors.

**A:** Stress urinary incontinence (SUI) is involuntary loss of urine with increased intra-abdominal pressure (cough, laugh, sneeze, walk, run), affecting up to 37.5% of women. Risk factors: increased age, obesity, pregnancy/vaginal delivery, hypoestrogenic state (post-menopause, post-partum), smoking/chronic cough, neurological conditions, genetics, high-impact exercise.

SUI results from urethral sphincter deficiency or urethral hypermobility. Vaginal delivery is a key modifiable risk factor. Hypoestrogenism leads to urethral mucosal atrophy and reduced urethral closure pressure. Obesity increases resting intra-abdominal pressure.

**Q:** What investigations are used in the diagnosis of stress urinary incontinence?

**A:** Investigations: urinalysis (hematuria, pyuria, glucosuria, proteinuria), urine cytology if hematuria/irritative voiding symptoms, urine culture if pyuria/bacteria, post-void residual (normal <1/3 total volume; abnormal >1/3), and urodynamic testing.

Post-void residual assessment helps distinguish SUI from overflow incontinence or bladder outlet obstruction. Urodynamics can objectively demonstrate urethral sphincter insufficiency and exclude detrusor overactivity. Urinalysis rules out UTI as a cause of symptoms.

**Q:** What are the surgical treatment options for stress urinary incontinence?

**A:** Surgical options: midurethral sling (TVT — tension-free vaginal tape, TOT — transobturator tape), urethropexy (Burch procedure), pubovaginal sling, and urethral bulking agents.

Midurethral slings (TVT/TOT) are the gold standard surgical treatment for SUI with excellent cure rates. The Burch procedure (laparoscopic or open) elevates the bladder neck. Urethral bulking agents (injected periurethrally) are less invasive but less durable. Conservative treatment should precede surgery.

**Q:** Define overactive bladder (OAB) and its symptom complex.

**A:** OAB is a symptom syndrome defined as urgency, with or without urge urinary incontinence (UUI), usually with frequency and nocturia. It affects up to 22% of patients. UUI is involuntary leakage with or immediately preceded by a strong desire to void.

OAB results from involuntary detrusor contractions that overcome the urethral sphincter mechanism or from poor bladder compliance. Unlike SUI (triggered by physical activity), OAB is driven by urgency/detrusor overactivity. Treatment is stepwise: behavioral, pharmacologic, then procedural.

**Q:** What are the causes/etiology of overactive bladder?

**A:** Causes: idiopathic (congenital, aging); medical (CHF, DM, diuretics); neurogenic (MS, Parkinson's, CVD, dementia, spinal cord injury); bladder outlet obstruction (previous bladder neck surgery, POP); gynecologic (UTI, pregnancy, pelvic mass, urethral diverticulum); psychosomatic (habits, anxiety, high fluid consumption).

Neurogenic OAB results from loss of central inhibition of the micturition reflex. Any condition causing increased post-void residual (outlet obstruction, POP) can secondarily cause detrusor instability. Identifying and treating the underlying cause is key to management.

**Q:** What medications are used to treat overactive bladder?

**A:** Anticholinergics: oxybutynin (Ditropan), tolterodine (Detrol), fesoterodine (Toviaz), solifenacin (Vesicare), trospium (Trosec), darifenacin (Enablex). Beta-3 adrenergic agonist: mirabegron (Myrbetriq).

Anticholinergics block muscarinic receptors on the detrusor, reducing involuntary contractions. Mirabegron relaxes the detrusor through beta-3 agonism and is an alternative for patients intolerant to anticholinergics (e.g., due to dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, cognitive effects in elderly). Procedures include sacral neuromodulation and botox injection.

**Q:** What are the behavioural/non-pharmacologic treatments for overactive bladder?

**A:** Behaviour modification: reduce bladder irritants (caffeine, smoking, alcohol, acidic/spicy foods), adequate water intake, regular voiding schedule. Bladder training with a pelvic physiotherapist. Procedures: sacral neuromodulation, detrusor botulinum toxin injection.

Behavioral interventions are first-line for OAB. Bladder retraining gradually extends voiding intervals to increase bladder capacity and reduce urgency. Sacral neuromodulation (InterStim) modulates the sacral reflex arc. Botulinum toxin injection directly inhibits detrusor contraction.

**Q:** What is the most common gynecological malignancy in North America and its typical presentation?

**A:** Endometrial carcinoma is the most common gynecological malignancy in North America (40%), and the 4th most common cancer in women. Postmenopausal bleeding (PMB) is the classic presentation — PMB = endometrial cancer until proven otherwise (95% present with vaginal bleeding). Mean age is 60 years.

Because endometrial carcinoma typically presents with early bleeding symptoms, most cases are diagnosed at Stage I with an 85-90% 5-year survival. The diagnosis should be assumed until ruled out by endometrial biopsy in any postmenopausal woman with vaginal bleeding.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for endometrial cancer (mnemonic COLD NUT)?

**A:** COLD NUT: Cancer (ovarian, breast, colon family history), Obesity, Late menopause, Diabetes mellitus, Nulliparity, Unopposed estrogen (PCOS, anovulation, HRT), Tamoxifen (chronic use). Also: genetic syndromes (HNPCC/Lynch II, Cowden syndrome), prior pelvic radiation, early menarche.

These risk factors all relate to excessive estrogen stimulation (unopposed by progesterone) or genetic predisposition. Obesity increases peripheral conversion of androgens to estrogen. Tamoxifen acts as an estrogen agonist on the endometrium despite being an antagonist in breast tissue.

**Q:** Compare Type I vs. Type II endometrial carcinoma.

**A:** Type I (80%): estrogen-related, endometrioid histology, well-differentiated (grade 1-2), associated with PCOS/obesity/unopposed estrogen, better prognosis. Type II (20%): non-estrogen related, non-endometrioid (serous, clear cell, carcinosarcoma), associated with p53 mutation and HER2 overexpression, more common in Black patients, poorer prognosis.

Type I carcinoma arises through an estrogen-driven hyperplasia-to-carcinoma sequence. Type II carcinomas develop de novo from atrophic endometrium and have more aggressive behavior. This distinction impacts treatment planning and screening strategies.

**Q:** What is the abnormal endometrial thickness threshold on ultrasound for postmenopausal vs. premenopausal women?

**A:** Abnormal endometrial thickness: >5 mm in postmenopausal women (symptomatic), and >11 mm in asymptomatic premenopausal women.

Transvaginal ultrasound is a useful initial investigation for PMB. An endometrial thickness >5 mm in a postmenopausal woman with bleeding warrants endometrial sampling. In asymptomatic premenopausal women, a higher threshold of 11 mm is used before biopsy is indicated.

**Q:** How is endometrial carcinoma investigated?

**A:** Endometrial sampling (office endometrial biopsy) is the primary investigation. Hysteroscopy for persistent uterine bleeding with benign/inadequate sampling. CT and MRI only for high-grade tumours or suspected extrauterine spread. Tumour markers not used for screening.

Office endometrial biopsy is the gold standard for initial investigation — simple, inexpensive, and high sensitivity. Hysteroscopy allows direct visualization and targeted biopsy. MRI is superior to CT for assessing depth of myometrial invasion, which is key for staging and surgical planning.

**Q:** What are the prognostic factors for endometrial carcinoma?

**A:** Key prognostic factors: FIGO stage (most important), histologic grade, histologic subtype, and depth of myometrial invasion.

FIGO stage remains the strongest independent predictor of survival: Stage I has 85-90% 5-year survival, while Stage IV has very poor outcomes. Myometrial invasion >50% (Stage IB) significantly worsens prognosis. High-grade (Type II) tumours have worse outcomes regardless of stage.

**Q:** What is the surgical treatment of endometrial carcinoma?

**A:** Total hysterectomy + BSO (bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy). For more advanced cases: pelvic washings + pelvic and para-aortic node dissection + omentectomy. Adjuvant therapy (radiation and/or chemotherapy) depends on clinical and histological features. Hormone therapy (progesterone IUD or oral progestins) can be used for fertility-sparing treatment.

Surgery provides both treatment and staging information (determining need for adjuvant therapy). Fertility-sparing approaches using progestins are options for young women with well-differentiated, Stage IA disease who wish to preserve fertility. CA-125 is used to monitor treatment response.

**Q:** What is uterine sarcoma, and what is its most common presenting symptom?

**A:** Uterine sarcoma is a rare uterine malignancy (3-9% of all uterine malignancies) arising from stromal components (endometrial stroma, mesenchymal, or myometrial tissues). It behaves more aggressively than endometrial carcinoma with a 5-year survival of 35%. Vaginal bleeding is the most common presenting symptom.

Uterine sarcomas are often diagnosed postoperatively when a uterus removed for presumed fibroids is found to contain sarcoma on pathology. Their aggressive behavior and poor prognosis distinguish them from carcinomas.

**Q:** What histological features distinguish leiomyosarcoma from benign leiomyoma?

**A:** Leiomyosarcoma is distinguished from leiomyoma by: (1) increased mitotic count (>10 mitoses/10 high-power fields), (2) tumour necrosis, and (3) cellular atypia. Clinical clues include rapidly enlarging fibroids in premenopausal patients or enlarging fibroids in a postmenopausal patient.

These three histologic criteria are key to diagnosing leiomyosarcoma. An enlarging fibroid in a postmenopausal woman raises concern for sarcomatous change as fibroids should regress with menopause. Leiomyosarcoma has poor outcomes even at early stage and radiation does not improve control or survival.

**Q:** What are the four major types of uterine sarcoma and their key distinguishing features?

**A:** 1. Leiomyosarcoma — most common, average age 55, >10 mitoses/HPF, necrosis, atypia, poor prognosis. 2. Endometrial Stromal Sarcoma (ESS) — perimenopausal, AUB, good prognosis, hormone-sensitive. 3. Undifferentiated Sarcoma — rare, severe nuclear pleomorphism, poor prognosis. 4. Adenosarcoma (mixed) — rarest, low malignant potential, polypoid uterine mass, mixture of benign epithelium + malignant sarcoma.

Distinguishing these subtypes is important because prognosis and treatment vary significantly. ESS responds to hormonal therapy (progestins), while leiomyosarcoma does not. Adenosarcoma is of mixed malignant potential and is often found incidentally at time of hysterectomy for PMB.

**Q:** What is the incidence order of malignant gynecological cancers in Canada?

**A:** Order of incidence: endometrium > ovary > cervix > vulva > vagina > fallopian tube.

Endometrial carcinoma is the most common gynecological malignancy. Ovarian cancer, while less common, causes more deaths due to its typically advanced-stage presentation. Cervical cancer rates have declined significantly with screening and HPV vaccination.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of ovarian cancer in Canada?

**A:** Ovarian cancer: lifetime risk 1.4%, majority of cases in patients >50 years, 4th leading cause of cancer death in women. Causes more deaths than all other gynecological malignancies combined. 85% epithelial, 15% non-epithelial. 70% of epithelial ovarian cancers present at Stage III disease.

Ovarian cancer's poor prognosis reflects its typical advanced-stage presentation, as early-stage disease is usually asymptomatic. Most women present with Stage III (advanced disease) with a 24% 5-year survival. Stage I has a 90% 5-year survival.

**Q:** What are the risk factors and protective factors for epithelial ovarian cancer?

**A:** Risk factors: early menarche/late menopause, personal or family history of breast/colon/endometrial/ovarian cancer, advanced age, BRCA mutation (serous), Lynch syndrome (non-serous, non-mucinous), fertility drugs (limited evidence). Protective factors: OCP (50% risk reduction after 5 years), pregnancy/breastfeeding.

The incessant ovulation hypothesis posits that repeated ovulatory rupture/repair of the ovarian epithelium increases cancer risk. OCP suppresses ovulation and is protective. BRCA1/2 mutations confer a 39-44% (BRCA1) and 11-18% (BRCA2) lifetime risk of ovarian cancer.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of epithelial ovarian cancer?

**A:** Most patients present with advanced-stage (Stage III/IV) disease. Symptoms: abdominal symptoms (nausea, bloating, pain, dyspepsia, anorexia, early satiety), symptoms of mass effect, increased abdominal girth (from ascites or tumour), urinary urgency/frequency, constipation.

The vague, non-specific nature of symptoms leads to late diagnosis. 'Omental cake' refers to ascites plus a fixed upper abdominal and pelvic mass — almost always signifying ovarian cancer. Any adnexal mass in a postmenopausal woman should be considered malignant until proven otherwise.

**Q:** What is the screening recommendation for ovarian cancer?

**A:** There is no effective screening method for ovarian cancer in average-risk women. Routine CA-125 or pelvic ultrasound are NOT recommended for screening. For high-risk patients (BRCA mutation carriers), prophylactic BSO or salpingectomy may be recommended.

RCTs have shown no significant reduction in ovarian cancer mortality with CA-125 or transvaginal ultrasound screening. These tests have high false-positive rates leading to unnecessary surgeries with significant complication rates (3-15% major complications). Benefits do not outweigh harms in average-risk populations.

**Q:** What is the treatment for epithelial ovarian cancer?

**A:** Debulking surgery: total hysterectomy, BSO, omentectomy, and removal of all visible disease. Chemotherapy: platinum-based (carboplatin) + taxol (paclitaxel). Neo-adjuvant chemotherapy can shrink tumours prior to debulking. Adjuvant chemotherapy treats microscopic disease.

Optimal cytoreduction (residual disease <1 cm) is associated with improved survival. Most epithelial ovarian cancers are chemosensitive. Intraperitoneal chemotherapy for Stage III disease may provide survival advantage. BRCA-mutated tumours are particularly sensitive to platinum agents and PARP inhibitors.

**Q:** What are the tumour markers for ovarian cancer subtypes?

**A:** Epithelial (serous/endometrioid): CA-125. Granulosa cell tumour: Inhibin. Dysgerminoma: LDH. Yolk sac tumour: AFP. Choriocarcinoma: beta-hCG. Embryonal cell: AFP + beta-hCG. Immature teratoma: none.

CA-125 has only 50% sensitivity for early-stage ovarian cancer, limiting its utility as a screening tool. It is more useful for monitoring treatment response and detecting recurrence. Germ cell tumours occur mostly in young women and have specific tumour markers that guide diagnosis and follow-up.

**Q:** What are borderline (low malignant potential) ovarian tumours and their key features?

**A:** Borderline tumours (15% of epithelial ovarian tumours): tumour cells with histologically malignant characteristics arising from ovarian surface but NOT invading ovarian stroma. One-third in women <40 years. Generally slow-growing, excellent prognosis (5-year survival >99%). Treated with surgery (BSO/omental biopsy +/- hysterectomy); chemotherapy has limited benefit. Young patients may have fertility-sparing treatment (cystectomy or unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy).

Borderline tumours occupy a histological middle ground between benign and malignant. They can metastasize but uncommonly. Late recurrences may be associated with low-grade serous carcinoma. The excellent prognosis supports conservative management, especially in young patients desiring fertility.

**Q:** What is a follicular cyst and how is it managed?

**A:** A follicular cyst develops when a follicle fails to rupture during ovulation. Usually asymptomatic, 4-8 cm, unilocular, lined with granulosa cells. May rupture/bleed/tort causing pain. Management: if <6 cm, wait 6 weeks and re-examine (usually regresses with next cycle). OCP prevents development of new cysts. Symptomatic/suspicious masses warrant surgical exploration (laparoscopic cystectomy or oophorectomy).

Follicular cysts are the most common functional ovarian cyst. They are benign and most resolve spontaneously within 1-2 menstrual cycles. Surgical intervention is reserved for large (>6 cm), persistent, symptomatic, or suspicious cysts.

**Q:** What is a corpus luteum cyst and how does it differ from a follicular cyst?

**A:** A corpus luteum cyst develops when the corpus luteum fails to regress after ovulation, becoming cystic or hemorrhagic. Larger (10-15 cm) and firmer than follicular cysts. More likely to cause pain and may delay onset of next period. Management is the same as for follicular cysts.

Corpus luteum cysts can be hemorrhagic and present with acute pelvic pain mimicking ectopic pregnancy. They are associated with elevated progesterone levels. A pregnancy test should always be performed to exclude ectopic pregnancy in a woman with acute pelvic pain and an adnexal mass.

**Q:** What is a theca-lutein cyst and what is its association?

**A:** Theca-lutein cysts arise from atretic follicles stimulated by abnormal beta-hCG levels. Associated with molar pregnancy and ovulation induction with clomiphene. Management is conservative — cysts regress as beta-hCG levels fall.

Theca-lutein cysts are caused by excessive gonadotropin stimulation of the ovary. They are classically bilateral and can become very large. In molar pregnancy, they regress following uterine evacuation as hCG levels decline. In ovulation induction, they are a marker of ovarian hyperstimulation.

**Q:** What is a benign cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst) and what are its ultrasound findings?

**A:** Benign cystic teratoma (dermoid) is the single most common ovarian germ cell neoplasm. Contains elements of all 3 germ cell lines including dermal appendages (sweat/sebaceous glands, hair follicles, teeth). 20% bilateral. Ultrasound: smooth-walled, mobile, unilocular cyst — may show calcification (pathognomonic, from teeth). Treatment: laparoscopic cystectomy (may recur).

Dermoid cysts occur across the reproductive years (20% outside reproductive years). The presence of calcification on ultrasound (teeth) is pathognomonic. Complications include rupture, torsion, and infarction. Chemical peritonitis can occur from sebaceous material spillage.

**Q:** What are the malignant germ cell tumours of the ovary and their tumour markers?

**A:** Malignant germ cell tumours (2-3% of ovarian cancers, predominantly in children/young women <30 years): Dysgerminoma (LDH marker, 10-15% bilateral), Immature Teratoma (no tumour marker), Yolk Sac Tumour (AFP marker), Ovarian Choriocarcinoma (beta-hCG marker, precocious puberty), Embryonal Cell (AFP + beta-hCG).

Malignant germ cell tumours are rapidly growing but usually chemosensitive, making fertility-sparing surgery (unilateral salpingo-oophorectomy + nodes) followed by chemotherapy feasible even in advanced disease. Dysgerminoma Stage IA requires no adjuvant treatment and is highly responsive to chemotherapy if advanced.

**Q:** What are sex cord stromal tumours of the ovary and their key distinguishing features?

**A:** Fibroma/Thecoma (benign): non-functioning; associated with Meigs syndrome (triad of benign ovarian tumour + ascites + pleural effusion). Granulosa-Theca Cell Tumour: inhibin marker; estrogen-producing (precocious puberty, menorrhagia, PMB, risk of endometrial cancer); Call-Exner bodies on histology. Sertoli-Leydig Cell Tumour: elevated androgens; virilizing effects (hirsutism, deep voice, hairline recession).

These functional tumours produce hormones and present accordingly. Granulosa cell tumours cause hyperestrogenism and endometrial hyperplasia/cancer risk. Call-Exner bodies (small groups of cells with central eosinophilic material) are the histologic hallmark. Meigs syndrome resolves after tumour removal.

**Q:** What is Meigs syndrome?

**A:** Meigs syndrome is the triad of: (1) benign ovarian tumour (classically fibroma/thecoma), (2) ascites, and (3) pleural effusion. It resolves after tumour removal.

Meigs syndrome is important to recognize because the combination of ovarian mass, ascites, and pleural effusion may mimic malignancy (e.g., ovarian cancer with peritoneal spread). CA-125 can be elevated in Meigs syndrome, further mimicking ovarian cancer. The key distinguishing feature is the benign histology of the ovarian tumour.

**Q:** What is a Krukenberg tumour?

**A:** A Krukenberg tumour is a metastatic ovarian tumour from a primary GI (commonly stomach or colon) or breast malignancy. Usually bilateral (80%). Histology shows characteristic 'signet-ring' cells. Accounts for 4-8% of ovarian malignancies.

Krukenberg tumours are important to recognize as the ovarian lesion may be the initial presentation, and identifying the primary site is essential for treatment planning. Signet-ring cells contain mucin displacing the nucleus to the periphery. They carry a poor prognosis because they represent metastatic disease.

**Q:** What are the epithelial ovarian cancer subtypes and their key features?

**A:** Serous (most common, 50% of ovarian cancers, 75% of epithelial; psammoma bodies on histology, 20-30% bilateral). Mucinous (20% of epithelial; may cause pseudomyxoma peritonei; remove appendix to rule out primary). Clear Cell (10%; associated with endometriosis; poor response to chemotherapy; more common in Asians). Endometrioid (10%; associated with endometriosis and endometrial neoplasm; responds well to chemotherapy).

Serous tumours are the most clinically significant, often presenting at advanced stage with BRCA mutations. Pseudomyxoma peritonei (implants producing large quantities of mucin seeding the abdominal cavity) is a dreaded complication of mucinous tumours. Clear cell carcinoma has a particularly poor chemotherapy response.

**Q:** What are the causes of an elevated CA-125, and what are its limitations as a screening test?

**A:** Gynecologic causes of elevated CA-125: endometriosis, pregnancy, fibroids, PID, benign ovarian neoplasms. Non-gynecologic: pancreas, stomach, colon, rectum cancer; cirrhosis, pancreatitis, general inflammation. Limitations: only 50% sensitivity for early-stage ovarian cancer — poor screening test; not specific.

CA-125 is useful for monitoring treatment response and surveillance for recurrence in known ovarian cancer patients, but its low sensitivity for early-stage disease and poor specificity (elevated in many benign conditions) make it unsuitable as a population screening tool. Diagnosis of ovarian tumours requires surgical pathology.

**Q:** What is pseudomyxoma peritonei and which ovarian tumour subtype is it associated with?

**A:** Pseudomyxoma peritonei is a condition where implants seed the abdominal cavity and produce large quantities of mucin, filling the peritoneal cavity. It is associated with mucinous ovarian tumours. When mucinous ovarian cancer is found, the appendix should also be removed to rule out a possible appendiceal primary source.

The appendix is the most common primary site of pseudomyxoma peritonei. In women, it can first present as an ovarian mucinous tumour (often an implant from an appendiceal mucinous neoplasm). Removing the appendix is both diagnostic and potentially curative if the appendix is the primary site.

**Q:** What is the investigation approach for a suspicious ovarian mass?

**A:** Referral to gynecologic oncologist. Bimanual exam (solid, irregular, fixed pelvic mass = suspicious). RMI (Risk of Malignancy Index) is best tool to assess likelihood of malignancy. Blood work: CBC, LFTs, electrolytes, Cr, tumour markers (CA-125, inhibin, beta-hCG, LDH, AFP, androgens, CEA, Ca19-9). Transvaginal U/S (best to visualize ovaries). CT abdomen/pelvis for metastases. Biopsy NOT recommended (tumour spillage risk) — cytological diagnosis from paracentesis or omental cake biopsy if extensive disease.

Avoiding biopsy of a potentially malignant ovarian mass prevents tumour spillage into the peritoneum, which would upstage an otherwise localized cancer. PET scan and bone scan are not routinely indicated. When ovarian cancer is suspected, preoperative assessment aims to plan for optimal cytoreductive surgery.

**Q:** What is the 5-year survival by stage for ovarian cancer?

**A:** Stage I: >90%. Stage II: 60-75%. Stage III: 24%. Stage IV: 1%.

The dramatic difference in survival between Stage I and Stage III/IV underscores the importance of early detection. Unfortunately, approximately 70% of epithelial ovarian cancers present at Stage III, contributing to the overall poor prognosis of this disease.

**Q:** A 62-year-old woman presents with vaginal bleeding. Transvaginal ultrasound shows an endometrial thickness of 8 mm. What is the next step?

**A:** Endometrial biopsy (office endometrial sampling). Endometrial thickness >5 mm in a postmenopausal woman with bleeding is abnormal and warrants tissue sampling to rule out endometrial carcinoma.

PMB = endometrial carcinoma until proven otherwise. 95% of endometrial carcinomas present with vaginal bleeding. An endometrial thickness >5 mm in a symptomatic postmenopausal woman meets the threshold for biopsy. Office endometrial biopsy is minimally invasive and highly sensitive for endometrial carcinoma.

**Q:** A 35-year-old woman on combined OCP for 5 years asks about her ovarian cancer risk. How does OCP use affect risk?

**A:** OCP significantly reduces ovarian cancer risk — likely due to ovulation suppression. Risk reduction occurs even after 1 year of use, and is approximately 50% after 5 years of use.

The incessant ovulation hypothesis postulates that repetitive ovulatory events damage ovarian surface epithelium, increasing cancer risk. OCP suppresses ovulation, allowing less cumulative epithelial damage. This protective effect persists for years after stopping OCP and is one of its most significant non-contraceptive benefits.

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman with acute pelvic pain has a right adnexal mass on ultrasound with calcification visible on imaging. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Benign cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst). Calcification (from teeth) is pathognomonic on ultrasound. Dermoid cysts are the single most common ovarian germ cell neoplasm. Pain can result from torsion, rupture, or infarction.

Dermoid cysts contain elements of all three germ cell lines including teeth, hair, and sebaceous material. The calcification from teeth on ultrasound or plain radiograph is pathognomonic. Treatment is laparoscopic cystectomy, though recurrence is possible.

**Q:** A postmenopausal woman presents with a unilateral solid ovarian mass, ascites, and a pleural effusion. What is in the differential diagnosis and how do you differentiate?

**A:** Differential: Meigs syndrome (benign ovarian fibroma/thecoma + ascites + pleural effusion) vs. malignant ovarian cancer with metastases. Key: CA-125 can be elevated in both. Definitive differentiation requires surgical pathology showing benign fibroma histology (in Meigs syndrome) vs. malignant tumour. Meigs syndrome resolves after tumour removal.

Meigs syndrome mimics advanced ovarian cancer clinically and biochemically. Any adnexal mass in a postmenopausal woman should be considered malignant until proven otherwise. However, recognizing Meigs syndrome avoids unnecessary radical surgery. The pleural effusion (usually right-sided) and ascites in Meigs syndrome resolve after oophorectomy.

## Obstetrics

**Q:** What is the most common cause of DIC in pregnancy?

**A:** Placental abruption

Placental abruption is the most common cause of DIC in pregnancy, occurring in approximately 20% of abruptions. DIC results from release of thromboplastin from the damaged placenta into the maternal circulation.

**Q:** How is vasa previa differentiated from other causes of antepartum bleeding using bedside testing?

**A:** Apt test: NaOH mixed with blood — fetal blood turns the supernatant pink, maternal blood turns it yellow

The Apt test uses sodium hydroxide to distinguish fetal from maternal hemoglobin. Fetal hemoglobin is resistant to denaturation by NaOH, keeping the supernatant pink, while adult hemoglobin denatures and turns yellow-brown.

**Q:** What is the perinatal mortality rate of undiagnosed vasa previa, and what is survival if diagnosed antenatally?

**A:** Undiagnosed: 50% mortality (75% if membranes rupture). Diagnosed antenatally: 97% survival.

Vasa previa carries extremely high mortality when undiagnosed because rupture of the unprotected fetal vessels causes fetal exsanguination. Antenatal diagnosis by ultrasound allows planned cesarean delivery at 35-36 weeks, dramatically improving survival.

**Q:** What is the most important risk factor for spontaneous preterm labour?

**A:** Prior history of spontaneous preterm labour

A previous spontaneous preterm birth is the single most important predictor of recurrent preterm labour. Other risk factors include short cervical length, cervical procedures, infections, and multiple gestation.

**Q:** What cervical length measurement on transvaginal ultrasound has a high negative predictive value for preterm labour before 34 weeks?

**A:** >30 mm

A cervical length greater than 30 mm measured by transvaginal ultrasound has a high negative predictive value for preterm labour before 34 weeks GA. Transvaginal ultrasound is the preferred route for cervical assessment.

**Q:** What is the role of fetal fibronectin in predicting preterm labour, and what is its key clinical value?

**A:** Positive if >50 ng/mL. Main value is its high NPV (>95%): a negative result means delivery is unlikely in 7-14 days.

Fetal fibronectin is a glycoprotein found in amniotic fluid and placental tissue. Its negative predictive value exceeds 95%, making it most useful for ruling out imminent delivery. It is done at 24-34 weeks with intact membranes and <3 cm dilation.

**Q:** What are the tocolytic requirements (all must be satisfied) before tocolysis can be given for preterm labour?

**A:** Live immature fetus, intact membranes, cervical dilatation <4 cm, absence of maternal or fetal contraindications

Tocolysis does not inhibit preterm labour completely but delays delivery up to 48 hours to allow administration of betamethasone for fetal lung maturity and/or transfer to an appropriate care centre.

**Q:** What is the dosing regimen for antenatal corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity?

**A:** Betamethasone 12 mg IM q24h × 2 doses OR dexamethasone 6 mg IM q12h × 4 doses, given between 24-34+6 weeks GA if delivery expected within 7 days

Antenatal corticosteroids enhance fetal lung maturity and reduce perinatal death, severe RDS, IVH, necrotizing enterocolitis, and neonatal sepsis. Active TB is a specific maternal contraindication.

**Q:** What is the role of MgSO4 in preterm labour, and at what gestational age is it indicated?

**A:** Neuroprotection — reduces risk of cerebral palsy. Given as 4 g bolus + 1 g/h infusion for ≥4 hours if imminent delivery expected at <33+6 weeks GA.

MgSO4 is given for neuroprotection in preterm labour, not as a tocolytic. It reduces the risk of cerebral palsy in premature infants when administered before delivery.

**Q:** How is membrane rupture confirmed on sterile speculum exam?

**A:** Pooling of fluid in posterior fornix, cascading (fluid leaking from cervix with cough/Valsalva), positive nitrazine test (blue), and ferning on microscopy

Nitrazine tests the pH of amniotic fluid which is basic, turning the paper blue. However, it has low specificity as blood, urine, and semen can also cause a positive result. Ferning occurs due to salt crystallization in amniotic fluid.

**Q:** At what gestational age is induction recommended for PROM at term?

**A:** ≥37 weeks GA — induction of labour is recommended since the risk of death from sepsis is greater than from RDS

PROM management is guided by gestational age. At ≥37 weeks, the risk of neonatal sepsis from prolonged ROM outweighs the risk of respiratory distress syndrome, so induction is recommended.

**Q:** What antibiotic should NOT be used for PPROM and why?

**A:** Amoxicillin/clavulanic acid — increased risk of necrotizing enterocolitis in neonates

While antibiotics are used in PPROM to increase latency to delivery and reduce maternal/neonatal morbidity, amoxicillin/clavulanic acid is specifically avoided due to its association with neonatal necrotizing enterocolitis. Amoxicillin alone is safe.

**Q:** What defines a postterm pregnancy, and when is induction recommended?

**A:** Postterm = pregnancy >42 weeks GA. Induction is recommended at 41+3 weeks GA if no contraindications to vaginal delivery.

Perinatal mortality is 2-3x higher after 42 weeks due to progressive uteroplacental insufficiency. Induction at 41+3 weeks reduces risks of stillbirth, macrosomia, and meconium aspiration.

**Q:** What differentiates symmetric (Type I) from asymmetric (Type II) IUGR?

**A:** Symmetric (25-30%): early onset, reduced head AND abdomen, associated with congenital anomalies/TORCH. Asymmetric (70%): late onset, abdomen smaller than head (brain sparing), associated with placental insufficiency, better prognosis.

In asymmetric IUGR, blood flow is preferentially redirected to the brain (brain sparing effect), resulting in a disproportionately smaller abdomen compared to the head. The HC/AC ratio is increased.

**Q:** What is the definition of macrosomia, and at what estimated fetal weight is planned cesarean delivery reasonable?

**A:** Macrosomia = >90th percentile for GA or >4000 g. Planned CD reasonable if EFW >5000 g (non-diabetic) or >4500 g (diabetic).

Macrosomia increases the risk of CPD, shoulder dystocia, and birth injuries. The threshold for planned CD is lower in diabetic patients due to the different body fat distribution in diabetic macrosomic infants.

**Q:** What is the definition and clinical significance of polyhydramnios and oligohydramnios?

**A:** Polyhydramnios: AFI >25 cm or single deepest pocket >8 cm. Oligohydramnios: AFI <5 cm or single deepest pocket <2 cm.

Polyhydramnios is associated with fetal anomalies (GI atresia, anencephaly), maternal diabetes, and multiple gestation. Oligohydramnios is associated with IUGR, renal anomalies, uteroplacental insufficiency, and ruptured membranes.

**Q:** What defines preeclampsia?

**A:** Pre-existing or gestational HTN (sBP ≥140 or dBP ≥90 after 20 weeks GA) with new-onset proteinuria (protein:creatinine ratio >30 mg/mmol) or adverse conditions (end-organ dysfunction)

Preeclampsia is caused by placental malperfusion leading to release of soluble factors that cause maternal vascular endothelial injury, hypertension, and multi-organ damage. It can progress to eclampsia.

**Q:** What is HELLP syndrome?

**A:** Hemolysis, Elevated Liver enzymes, Low Platelets — a severe complication of preeclampsia

HELLP syndrome represents a severe form of preeclampsia with microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, hepatocellular injury, and thrombocytopenia. It requires urgent delivery and supportive care.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment and prophylaxis for eclamptic seizures?

**A:** MgSO4 — 4 g IV loading dose, then 1 g/h maintenance. Continue for 12-24 hours postpartum.

MgSO4 is used for both treatment and prophylaxis of eclamptic seizures. The risk of seizure is highest in the first 24 hours postpartum. Definitive treatment of eclampsia is delivery after maternal stabilization.

**Q:** Which antihypertensives are contraindicated in pregnancy?

**A:** ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics (unless pulmonary edema/cardiac failure), prazosin, and atenolol

ACEIs and ARBs are teratogenic and can cause fetal renal failure and oligohydramnios. Safe options include labetalol, nifedipine, and methyldopa. For severe HTN, IV labetalol, nifedipine, or hydralazine are used.

**Q:** What is the 2-step screening approach for gestational diabetes recommended in Canada?

**A:** Step 1: Random 50 g OGCT at 24-28 weeks. Normal if 1h PG <7.8. GDM if ≥11.1. If 7.8-11.0, proceed to Step 2: Fasting 75 g OGTT — GDM if ≥1 of: FPG ≥5.3, 1h PG ≥10.6, 2h PG ≥9.0 mmol/L.

The Canadian Diabetes Association recommends this 2-step screening approach for all pregnant women at 24-28 weeks GA. First-line management of GDM is diet modification and exercise; insulin if glycemic targets not met within 2 weeks.

**Q:** What are the glycemic targets during pregnancy for women with diabetes?

**A:** FPG <5.3 mmol/L, 1h postprandial PG <7.8 mmol/L, 2h postprandial PG <6.7 mmol/L

Tight glycemic control is essential in pregnancy to prevent macrosomia, congenital anomalies (in pre-existing DM), and neonatal hypoglycemia. During labour, blood glucose should be maintained between 4.0-7.0 mmol/L.

**Q:** What are the congenital anomalies associated with pre-gestational diabetes (T1DM/T2DM) in pregnancy?

**A:** 2-7x increased risk of cardiac (VSD), neural tube defects, GU (cystic kidneys), GI (anal atresia), and MSK (sacral agenesis) anomalies

These anomalies occur in T1DM and T2DM but NOT in GDM, because GDM develops after the critical period of organogenesis in the first trimester. Pre-conception glycemic control (HbA1c <7%) reduces this risk.

**Q:** When should GBS prophylaxis be given in labour, and what is the first-line antibiotic?

**A:** Penicillin G 5 million IU IV then 2.5 million IU q4h until delivery. Should be given ≥4 hours prior to delivery to be considered adequate.

GBS screening is done at 35-37 weeks GA. Prophylaxis is indicated for positive GBS culture, GBS bacteriuria, previous infant with GBS disease, unknown GBS status with risk factors (preterm, ROM >18h, fever ≥38°C).

**Q:** Why should asymptomatic bacteriuria be treated in pregnancy?

**A:** Increased risk of progression to cystitis, pyelonephritis, and probable increased risk of preterm labour

UTI is the most common medical complication of pregnancy. Asymptomatic bacteriuria occurs in 2-7% of pregnant women and should be treated due to the increased urinary stasis from mechanical and hormonal factors of pregnancy.

**Q:** Why is warfarin contraindicated in pregnancy?

**A:** Warfarin crosses the placenta and has teratogenic effects (nasal hypoplasia, stippled epiphyses). LMWH is preferred for anticoagulation in pregnancy.

Warfarin is a vitamin K antagonist that crosses the placenta and can cause warfarin embryopathy. LMWH does not cross the placenta and should be discontinued at least 24 hours prior to delivery.

**Q:** What are the cardinal movements of the fetus during delivery?

**A:** Engagement → Descent → Flexion → Internal rotation → Extension → Restitution (external rotation) → Expulsion

These movements allow the fetus to navigate the maternal pelvis. The head enters in the occiput transverse position, rotates to occiput anterior during internal rotation, and extends to deliver over the perineum.

**Q:** What are the components of the Bishop Score?

**A:** Position, Consistency, Effacement, Dilatation, and Station of fetal head. Score <3 = unfavourable cervix; ≥6 = favourable; 9-13 = high likelihood of vaginal delivery.

The Bishop Score predicts the likelihood of successful labour induction. An unfavourable cervix (score <6) warrants cervical ripening with prostaglandins or mechanical methods (Foley catheter) prior to induction.

**Q:** What are the three types of fetal heart rate decelerations, and which is ominous?

**A:** Early (head compression, benign), Variable (cord compression, most common in labour), Late (uteroplacental insufficiency — ominous sign)

Late decelerations begin after the peak of contractions and reflect uteroplacental insufficiency with fetal hypoxia/acidemia. They are the most concerning pattern and warrant urgent evaluation and possible delivery.

**Q:** What is the 'turtle sign' and what does it indicate?

**A:** The fetal head delivers but retracts against the perineum — indicates shoulder dystocia

Shoulder dystocia occurs when the anterior shoulder is impacted above the pubic symphysis. It is a life-threatening emergency managed with the ALARMER mnemonic: Ask for help, Legs (McRoberts), Anterior shoulder disimpaction (suprapubic pressure), etc.

**Q:** What maneuvers are used for shoulder dystocia, and which combination resolves 90% of cases?

**A:** McRoberts maneuver (legs in full flexion) + suprapubic pressure resolves 90% of cases. Additional maneuvers: posterior shoulder rotation (Wood's corkscrew), delivery of posterior arm, Zavanelli maneuver.

McRoberts maneuver flattens the lumbar lordosis and rotates the pubic symphysis cephalad, which often frees the impacted anterior shoulder. Suprapubic pressure is applied to disimpact the anterior shoulder.

**Q:** What is the most common breech presentation, and which type is most likely to be delivered vaginally?

**A:** Frank breech (60%) is most common and most likely to be delivered vaginally. Complete breech is 10%, incomplete/footling is 30%.

In frank breech, the hips are flexed and knees extended with the buttocks presenting. External cephalic version (ECV) should be offered at >36 weeks GA with a 40-60% success rate.

**Q:** What is twin-twin transfusion syndrome (TTTS)?

**A:** Arteriovenous placental anastomoses in monochorionic twins causing unequal blood flow: donor twin develops IUGR, anemia, oligohydramnios; recipient twin develops hypervolemia, CHF, polyhydramnios.

TTTS occurs in 10% of monochorionic twins. Treatment is fetoscopic laser ablation of placental vascular anastomoses (preferred at 16-26 weeks GA). Serial amniocentesis can decompress the recipient twin.

**Q:** What are the risk factors and management of uterine rupture?

**A:** Risk: previous uterine scar (especially classical incision, 12% risk), oxytocin tachysystole, grand multiparity. Presents with sudden abdominal pain, FHR abnormalities, loss of station. Emergency CD required.

Uterine rupture is a complete separation of all layers of the uterine wall. The incidence is 0.5-0.8% overall but up to 12% with classical incision. It is a catastrophic emergency requiring immediate surgical intervention.

**Q:** A pregnant woman at 20 weeks gestation has chronic hypertension with BP 145/95 mmHg. What antihypertensives are safe to use and which must be avoided?

**A:** Safe first-line: labetalol, methyldopa, long-acting oral nifedipine. MUST AVOID: ACE inhibitors and ARBs (fetotoxic to renal system). Also avoid thiazide diuretics.

ACEi and ARBs cause fetal renal toxicity and are absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy. The CHAP trial showed that treating mild chronic HTN in pregnancy (target <140/90) reduces preeclampsia and adverse outcomes.

**Q:** What medications should be avoided in pregnancy for HTN management, and what are first-line options?

**A:** Avoid: ACEIs, ARBs (fetotoxic to renal system), thiazide diuretics. First-line: labetalol, methyldopa, long-acting oral nifedipine.

The CHAP trial demonstrated that treating mild chronic HTN (<140/90) in pregnancy with antihypertensives reduces preeclampsia, preterm birth, and fetal/neonatal death compared to no treatment.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacological treatment for nausea and vomiting of pregnancy (NVP)?

**A:** Pyridoxine (vitamin B6) monotherapy OR doxylamine/pyridoxine (Diclectin®) combination: 4 tablets/day (1 q AM, 1 q lunch, 2 q HS), up to 8 tablets/day maximum.

Diclectin® (doxylamine + pyridoxine) is the only Health Canada-approved medication specifically for NVP. It is recommended as first-line pharmacological therapy by SOGC guidelines before escalating to H1 antagonists, metoclopramide, phenothiazines, or ondansetron.

**Q:** A pregnant patient at 10 weeks GA has intractable vomiting, ketonuria, and electrolyte disturbances. What is the diagnosis, and what complication must be prevented with early supplementation?

**A:** Hyperemesis gravidarum. Wernicke's encephalopathy must be prevented with thiamine supplementation.

Hyperemesis gravidarum affects ~1% of pregnancies. Thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency from prolonged vomiting can cause Wernicke's encephalopathy (confusion, ophthalmoplegia, ataxia). Thiamine supplementation must be given before IV dextrose to avoid precipitating encephalopathy.

**Q:** What steroid should be avoided in T1 for hyperemesis gravidarum and why?

**A:** Methylprednisolone (steroids) should be avoided in the first trimester due to increased risk of oral clefting in the fetus.

While steroids (methylprednisolone) can be effective for refractory hyperemesis gravidarum, their use in T1 is associated with an increased risk of cleft palate/lip. They may be used with caution after T1 if other therapies have failed.

**Q:** What is the SOGC-recommended prenatal visit schedule for uncomplicated pregnancies?

**A:** Every 4-6 weeks until 30 weeks GA, every 2-3 weeks from 30-36 weeks GA, then every 1-2 weeks from 36 weeks GA until delivery.

This schedule balances adequate surveillance for common complications (hypertension, GDM, preterm labour) with minimizing unnecessary healthcare utilization. High-risk pregnancies require more frequent monitoring.

**Q:** At what gestational age does the uterine fundus reach the umbilicus, and what does SFH less than dates suggest?

**A:** The fundus reaches the umbilicus at 20 weeks GA. SFH < dates suggests: date miscalculation, oligohydramnios, IUGR, fetal demise, or early engagement.

Symphysis-fundal height (SFH) in centimetres should roughly equal gestational age in weeks (±2 cm) from 20-36 weeks. Fundus at umbilicus = 20 wks, halfway between symphysis and umbilicus = 16 wks. Discordance prompts further investigation including ultrasound.

**Q:** What are the four Leopold's maneuvers and what does each determine?

**A:** 1st: which fetal part is furthest from pelvic inlet (fundal); 2nd: location of fetal back; 3rd: which fetal part is above the pelvic inlet (presenting part); 4th: location of fetal brow/degree of engagement.

Leopold's maneuvers are performed after 30-32 weeks GA to determine fetal lie, presentation, position, and engagement. They help plan delivery and identify abnormal presentations requiring further assessment.

**Q:** Which populations require carrier screening for Tay-Sachs disease, and what test is used?

**A:** Ashkenazi Jewish, French Canadians, and Cajun populations. Test: enzyme assay for hexosaminidase A (HEXA) or DNA analysis of the HEXA gene.

Tay-Sachs is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder. If both partners are Ashkenazi Jewish, also screen for Canavan disease and Familial Dysautonomia. If both partners screen positive, refer for genetic counselling.

**Q:** What investigations are performed at the first prenatal visit (8-12 weeks GA) in Canada?

**A:** CBC, blood group and Rh status, antibody screen, ferritin; HIV, VDRL, HBsAg, Rubella IgG; urine R&M and C&S, urine PCR for chlamydia/gonorrhea; dating U/S; possible Pap smear; varicella IgG if no history; parvovirus IgG/IgM if high risk.

The first prenatal visit establishes baseline risk and screens for asymptomatic infections. Early detection of HIV, syphilis, rubella non-immunity, and bacteriuria can significantly reduce maternal and fetal morbidity.

**Q:** What does the enhanced First Trimester Screen (eFTS) measure and at what gestational age?

**A:** At 11-13+3 weeks GA. It combines: nuchal translucency (NT) ultrasound + β-hCG + PAPP-A + placental growth factor + MSAFP to screen for trisomy 21, 18, and other aneuploidies.

The eFTS has ~85% sensitivity for trisomy 21 with a 5% false positive rate. It is the preferred screening test when results are desired in T1. An increased NT also warrants cardiac assessment. Positive results should be followed by CVS, amniocentesis, or NIPT.

**Q:** What is the Maternal Serum Screen (MSS/Triple/Quadruple Screen)? When is it done and what does it measure?

**A:** Done at 15-20 weeks GA. Measures: 1) MSAFP, 2) β-hCG, 3) unconjugated estriol (uE3), and (if Quad Screen) 4) Inhibin A. Screens for NTDs (elevated MSAFP), trisomy 21, and trisomy 18.

The MSS (Triple Screen) or Quad Screen is offered if the patient missed eFTS. For trisomy 21: decreased MSAFP + increased β-hCG + decreased uE3 + increased inhibin A, sensitivity ~65-80%. For NTD: elevated MSAFP (sensitivity 80-90%).

**Q:** What are the detection rate and false positive rate of NIPT for trisomy 21?
