**A:** Detection rate: 99%; False positive rate: 0.1%. NIPT is a screening test, not diagnostic — positive results must be confirmed with CVS or amniocentesis.

NIPT analyzes cell-free fetal DNA (ccffDNA) in maternal blood from 10 weeks GA onward. While highly accurate for common trisomies and sex chromosome aneuploidies, it does not screen for open neural tube defects and requires confirmatory invasive testing.

**Q:** What are two major disadvantages of NIPT compared to amniocentesis or CVS?

**A:** 1) Does not screen for open neural tube defects (ONTDs). 2) Is a screening test — positive results must be confirmed with invasive testing (CVS or amniocentesis). Also: not universally publicly funded in Canada, and may fail if fetal fraction is insufficient (especially with elevated BMI).

NIPT gives no result in 1-5% of cases due to insufficient fetal fraction. BMI >30 is associated with lower fetal fraction. Insufficient fetal fraction is itself a risk factor for aneuploidy and should not simply be repeated without clinical assessment.

**Q:** What is amniocentesis, when is it performed, and what are its key advantages and risks?

**A:** Ultrasound-guided transabdominal extraction of amniotic fluid, performed from 15 weeks GA onward. Advantages: screens for ONTDs (96% accurate), karyotyping, L/S ratio for fetal lung maturity. Risk: 1/200-1/900 procedure-related pregnancy loss.

Amniocentesis is more accurate than CVS and also screens for NTDs via acetylcholinesterase and amniotic AFP. In women >40 years, the risk of chromosomal anomaly (1/180) exceeds the procedural risk of miscarriage. Results take 14-28 days (QF-PCR/FISH can provide preliminary results in 24 hours).

**Q:** What is chorionic villus sampling (CVS), when is it performed, and what are its key advantages and disadvantages compared to amniocentesis?

**A:** Biopsy of fetal-derived chorion via transabdominal needle or transcervical catheter at 10-12 weeks GA. Advantages: earlier results than amniocentesis, rapid karyotyping in 48 hours. Disadvantages: 1% miscarriage risk (higher than amniocentesis), does NOT screen for ONTDs, 1-2% genetic mosaicism causing false negatives.

CVS enables earlier pregnancy termination if a severe anomaly is found. The inability to screen for ONTDs is an important limitation — amniocentesis remains the diagnostic test for NTD confirmation. Mosaicism is a significant source of false-negative results with CVS.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of Rh isoimmunization and how common is it in an Rh-negative mother with an ABO-compatible Rh-positive infant?

**A:** Rh-negative mother is sensitized to Rh antigen from fetal RBCs crossing the placenta, producing anti-Rh IgG antibodies that can cross the placenta in subsequent pregnancies and cause fetal hemolytic anemia. Risk of sensitization is 16% without prophylaxis.

Sensitization is more likely to occur with ABO compatibility (incompatibility provides some natural protection). The first Rh-positive pregnancy rarely causes problems as initial sensitization produces IgM (does not cross placenta), but subsequent pregnancies trigger an IgG response that does cross the placenta.

**Q:** When and to whom is Rhogam® (Rh IgG) administered in pregnancy?

**A:** Given to all Rh-negative, antibody screen-negative patients: 1) Routinely at 28 weeks GA (lasts ~12 weeks); 2) Within 72 hours of birth of Rh-positive infant; 3) After any invasive procedure (CVS, amniocentesis); 4) With ectopic pregnancy; 5) After miscarriage/therapeutic abortion; 6) After antepartum hemorrhage; 7) After trauma.

Rhogam® 300 mcg provides sufficient prophylaxis for 30 mL of fetal Rh-positive whole blood. If >30 mL fetal blood in maternal circulation (detected by Kleihauer-Betke test or flow cytometry), additional doses are required. If the antibody screen is already positive, Rhogam® provides no benefit.

**Q:** What test is used to determine the volume of fetomaternal hemorrhage, and when is additional Rhogam® required?

**A:** Kleihauer-Betke test or flow cytometry. Additional Rhogam® is required if >30 mL of fetal blood enters the maternal circulation (standard 300 mcg dose covers only 30 mL).

The Kleihauer-Betke test identifies fetal hemoglobin-containing cells in maternal blood. One additional 10 mcg of Rhogam® is given for every 1 mL of fetal blood over 30 mL. Middle cerebral artery Dopplers are used to assess degree of fetal anemia non-invasively.

**Q:** What is hydrops fetalis and what causes it in the context of Rh isoimmunization?

**A:** Hydrops fetalis is edema in at least two fetal compartments (e.g., ascites, pleural effusion, pericardial effusion, skin edema) due to fetal heart failure secondary to severe anemia caused by maternal anti-Rh IgG antibodies destroying fetal RBCs.

Anti-Rh IgG crosses the placenta and causes fetal RBC hemolysis → fetal anemia → high-output cardiac failure → generalized edema/hydrops. Erythroblastosis fetalis refers to the hemolytic anemia itself. Treatment involves intrauterine transfusion of O-negative packed RBCs between 18-35 weeks GA.

**Q:** What constitutes a normal Non-Stress Test (NST) for a fetus >32 weeks GA?

**A:** At least 2 accelerations of FHR ≥15 bpm above baseline, each lasting ≥15 seconds, within a 20-minute period.

The NST assesses fetal wellbeing using the relationship between fetal movement and FHR accelerations. For fetuses <32 weeks GA, the criteria are lower (≥10 bpm for ≥10 seconds). An abnormal NST is <2 accelerations in 40 minutes, prompting further assessment with BPP or delivery planning.

**Q:** What are the four parameters scored on the ultrasound component of the Biophysical Profile (BPP), and which is a marker of chronic vs. acute hypoxia?

**A:** Four ultrasound parameters (2 points each): 1) Tone (limb extension then flexion), 2) Movement (3 discrete movements), 3) Breathing (≥1 episode ≥30 seconds), 4) Amniotic Fluid Volume (fluid pocket ≥2 cm in 2 axes). AFV is a marker of CHRONIC hypoxia; all others indicate ACUTE hypoxia.

The BPP combines the NST (2 points) with 4 U/S parameters for a total of 10/10. A score of 8/10 with normal fluid or 10/10 has a perinatal mortality of 1:1000. A score of 0-4/10 has perinatal mortality of 91-600:1000 and delivery should be considered.

**Q:** What BPP score indicates possible fetal compromise requiring consideration of delivery?

**A:** BPP 0-4/10: perinatal mortality rate 91-600:1000 — consider delivery for fetal indications.

BPP interpretation: 8-10/10 normal fluid = reassuring (1:1000 mortality); 6-8/10 abnormal fluid = perinatal mortality 9:1000 (deliver at term, continue surveillance if <34 wks); 6/10 normal fluid = equivocal (repeat in 24 hours); 0-4/10 = consider immediate delivery.

**Q:** What are the EFM tracing classifications and what defines an abnormal (Category 3) tracing?

**A:** Category 3 (Abnormal): Baseline bradycardia <100 bpm; absent variability (<5 bpm) for >80 min; sinusoidal pattern; repetitive complicated variable decelerations; recurrent late decelerations; single prolonged deceleration >3 min. Indicates possible fetal compromise — requires urgent intervention.

Category 1 (Normal): baseline 110-160 bpm, moderate variability 6-25 bpm, no significant decelerations. Category 2 (Atypical): intermediate features requiring clinical interpretation. Category 3 prompts urgent assessment and delivery planning. Per SOGC Fetal Health Surveillance guidelines (2020).

**Q:** What are the folate supplementation recommendations in Canada for women at standard and high risk for NTDs?

**A:** Standard risk: 0.4-1 mg/day starting 2-3 months preconception until 4-6 weeks postpartum. High risk (e.g., prior NTD, antiepileptic drugs, DM): 4 mg/day starting at least 3 months preconception until 12 weeks GA, then 0.4-1 mg until 4-6 weeks postpartum or while breastfeeding.

Folate supplementation reduces NTD incidence by 50-70%. Iron is the only known nutrient whose requirements in pregnancy cannot be met by diet alone. High-risk factors for NTD include previous child with NTD (risk increases to 2-5%), maternal DM, antiepileptic drugs (especially valproate/carbamazepine), and consanguinity.

**Q:** What is the recommended calcium, vitamin D, and iron intake during pregnancy?

**A:** Calcium: 1200-1500 mg/day; Vitamin D: 1000-2000 IU/day; Iron: 16-20 mg elemental iron/day (supplementation required as dietary intake is insufficient).

Iron is unique among pregnancy nutrients because requirements cannot be met by diet alone — supplementation is mandatory. Calcium maintains maternal bone integrity and fetal skeletal development. Vitamin D promotes calcium absorption. Iron supports maternal blood volume expansion and fetal/placental tissue growth.

**Q:** What is the safe upper limit of caffeine consumption during pregnancy?

**A:** Less than 300 mg/day is considered safe. SOGC states 1-2 cups of coffee per day are safe.

Caffeine readily crosses the placenta and acts as a diuretic and stimulant. The relationship between caffeine and IUGR is unclear. Common caffeine content: 8 oz coffee ~135 mg, 12 oz cola ~46 mg, Red Bull ~67 mg, dark chocolate bar ~10 mg.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to physical activity during pregnancy?

**A:** Ruptured membranes, preterm labour, hypertensive disorders of pregnancy, incompetent cervix, IUGR, multiple gestations (>3), placenta previa after 28 weeks GA, persistent T2/T3 bleeding, uncontrolled T1DM, uncontrolled thyroid disease, serious cardiovascular or respiratory disease.

Pregnant women without contraindications should aim for 150 minutes of moderate-intensity exercise per week. The 'talk test' (able to speak while exercising) indicates appropriate intensity. The supine position should be avoided after 20 weeks GA due to aortocaval compression.

**Q:** What are the effects of smoking during pregnancy?

**A:** Increased risk of: decreased birth weight, placenta previa, placental abruption, spontaneous abortion, preterm labour (PTL), and stillbirth.

Smoking cessation is strongly encouraged. Psychosocial interventions are first-line. Nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) is preferred over pharmacotherapy if counselling alone fails. Bupropion and varenicline have limited safety data in pregnancy. Intermittent/low-dose NRT is preferred to minimize fetal exposure.

**Q:** What are the fetal risks associated with alcohol, cocaine, and cannabis use in pregnancy?

**A:** Alcohol: fetal alcohol spectrum disorder, spontaneous abortion, stillbirth, congenital anomalies — NO safe amount. Cocaine: microcephaly, growth retardation, prematurity, placental abruption. Cannabis: low birth weight, neurobehavioural abnormalities in childhood.

No amount of alcohol has been proven safe in pregnancy — abstinence is recommended. Cannabis is increasingly used but is associated with adverse neurodevelopmental outcomes. Cocaine causes vasoconstriction leading to placental insufficiency and abruption.

**Q:** What medications are contraindicated in pregnancy and what are their specific fetal effects?

**A:** ACE inhibitors: fetal renal defects, IUGR, oligohydramnios. Valproate: congenital malformations (NTD) up to 9%. Warfarin: fetal warfarin syndrome (nasal hypoplasia, epiphyseal stippling, optic atrophy, ICH). Retinoids (Accutane): CNS/craniofacial/cardiac/thymic anomalies. Tetracycline: stains teeth, bone effects. Lithium: Ebstein's anomaly. NSAIDs after 30 weeks: premature ductus arteriosus closure.

The preferred analgesic in pregnancy is acetaminophen. NSAIDs (specifically indomethacin) can be used before 30 weeks for tocolysis but cause premature ductus arteriosus closure if used after 30 weeks. Warfarin crosses the placenta — switch to LMWH for anticoagulation in pregnancy.

**Q:** What vaccines are safe and which are contraindicated during pregnancy?

**A:** Safe: tetanus toxoid, diphtheria, influenza (recommended for all), hepatitis B, pertussis (Tdap every pregnancy, ideally 27-32 weeks GA). Contraindicated/Avoid: live vaccines — MMR, varicella, oral poliovirus, oral typhoid.

Public Health Agency of Canada recommends all pregnant patients receive influenza vaccine and Tdap every pregnancy regardless of immunization history. Live vaccines pose risk of placental/fetal infection. Rubella vaccine should be given postpartum to non-immune patients, with pregnancy deferred ≥1 month post-vaccination.

**Q:** What is the threshold radiation dose to cause miscarriage vs. fetal malformations, and which imaging studies deliver the highest fetal dose?

**A:** Miscarriage: >5 cGy. Malformations: >20-30 cGy. Highest fetal doses: CT pelvis (~25 cGy), CT lumbar spine (0-24 cGy), CT abdomen (0-8 cGy), plain pelvis (~1.5 cGy). Chest X-ray: <0.001 cGy (safe).

Ultrasound and MRI have no known adverse fetal effects (gadolinium should be avoided if possible — long-term effects unknown). Radioactive iodine isotopes are absolutely contraindicated. CT of the chest delivers negligible fetal dose. When imaging is clinically indicated for maternal health, it should be performed.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis of vaginal bleeding in the third trimester?

**A:** Most common physiologic cause: bloody show (cervical changes/early dilation). Most common pathological cause: placental abruption. Others: placenta previa, vasa previa, cervical lesion (cervicitis/polyp/cancer), uterine rupture, bleeding from bowel/bladder, coagulopathy.

Third-trimester bleeding requires urgent evaluation. Digital vaginal exam must be avoided until placenta previa is excluded by ultrasound. Placenta previa presents with painless bright red bleeding; abruption typically presents with painful bleeding and uterine tenderness.

**Q:** Compare placenta previa and placental abruption in terms of presentation, risk factors, and epidemiology.

**A:** Placenta previa: 0.5-0.8% of pregnancies, PAINLESS bright red bleeding, risk factors include previous previa (4-8% recurrence), multiparity, advanced age, prior uterine surgery, multiple gestation, fibroids. Abruption: 1-2% of pregnancies, usually PAINFUL bleeding, risk factors include previous abruption (5-16% recurrence), maternal HTN (50% of abruptions), smoking, cocaine, multiparity, trauma, rapid uterine decompression.

Placenta previa: placenta implanted in lower uterine segment covering the internal os — do NOT perform digital vaginal exam. Abruption: premature separation of a normally implanted placenta after 20 weeks GA — may have concealed hemorrhage. Both can lead to maternal and fetal compromise.

**Q:** What is the dating ultrasound, when is it performed, and what is its margin of error?

**A:** The dating ultrasound is performed at 8-12 weeks GA (measures crown-rump length, CRL). Margin of error: ±5 days. EDD should be based on T1 ultrasound if available. A pregnancy without U/S confirmation of EDD prior to 22+0/7 weeks GA is considered sub-optimally dated.

T1 U/S is the most accurate method for establishing GA up to 13+6/7 weeks. The T2 U/S (18-20 weeks) has a margin of error of ±10 days and is less accurate for dating. Dating U/S should be offered when the fetus has a CRL of at least 10 mm or is ≥7+0 weeks GA. Changes to EDD must be documented and discussed.

**Q:** What are the nucleotide markers for trisomy 21 on the MSS/Quad Screen?

**A:** Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) on MSS: decreased MSAFP + increased β-hCG + decreased unconjugated estriol (uE3) + increased Inhibin A. Sensitivity ~65-80%.

The memory mnemonic for T21 on MSS: 'Down is down' — MSAFP ↓, uE3 ↓, but β-hCG ↑ and Inhibin A ↑. For trisomy 18 (Edwards): all four analytes are decreased (↓MSAFP, ↓β-hCG, ↓uE3, ↓Inhibin A), sensitivity ~80%.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for neural tube defects (NTDs)?

**A:** Previous child with NTD (risk increases from 0.1% to 2-5%), consanguinity, chromosomal abnormalities, Hispanic ethnicity (3-fold higher than African Americans), insufficient folate or zinc, maternal DM, antiepileptic drug use (especially valproate, carbamazepine — NTD risk 1-2% and 12% respectively).

The general population NTD risk is 0.1%. High-risk patients should receive 4 mg folate daily starting at least 3 months preconception. Carbamazepine use in pregnancy carries a 1% NTD risk. Both amniocentesis (96% accurate) and elevated MSAFP can detect ONTDs, but CVS cannot.

**Q:** What fetal condition is assessed using the L/S (lecithin-sphingomyelin) ratio from amniocentesis, and what value is reassuring?

**A:** Fetal lung maturity. An L/S ratio >2:1 indicates sufficient surfactant production and that respiratory distress syndrome (RDS) is less likely to occur after delivery.

Amniocentesis in T3 can assess fetal lung maturity to guide timing of delivery. This is particularly relevant when early delivery is being considered (e.g., maternal complications, IUGR). An L/S ratio <2:1 suggests immature lungs and risk of neonatal RDS.

**Q:** When should fetal movement (FM) counting begin, and what finding requires urgent assessment?

**A:** FM counting is recommended after 26 weeks GA. Normally ≥6 perceived movements in 2 hours. If it takes more than 2 hours to feel 6 discrete movements, further assessment is indicated and the patient should present to labour and delivery triage.

Fetal movement (quickening) is first noticed at 18-20 weeks in primigravidas, 1-2 weeks earlier in multigravidas, and 1-2 weeks later if the placenta is anterior. Decreased FM may indicate fetal demise, decreased amniotic fluid, fetal sleep cycle, or hypoxia. NST and BPP are used for further assessment.

**Q:** What weight gain is recommended during pregnancy based on pre-pregnancy BMI?

**A:** BMI <18.5 (underweight): 12.5-18 kg. BMI 18.5-24.9 (normal): 11.5-16 kg. BMI 25-29.9 (overweight): 7-11.5 kg. BMI ≥30 (obese): 5-9 kg. Caloric increase: ~100 kcal/day in T1, ~300 kcal/day in T2/T3, ~450 kcal/day during lactation.

Gestational weight gain recommendations are based on Institute of Medicine guidelines adapted for Canadian practice. BMI ≥30 at baseline already increases risk of GDM, preeclampsia, and DVT — excessive weight gain amplifies these risks. Women with BMI >25 should gain less during lactation.

**Q:** What is the significance of an Rh antibody titre ≥1:16 during pregnancy?

**A:** A positive titre (≥1:16) indicates an increased risk of fetal hemolytic anemia. The mother should be followed with serial monthly antibody titres, serial ultrasounds, and serial amniocentesis as needed. Rhogam® provides no benefit if the antibody screen is already positive.

Once sensitization has occurred (antibody screen positive), Rhogam® prophylaxis is ineffective because B cells and antibodies are already in circulation. Management focuses on surveillance of fetal anemia via MCA Dopplers (peak systolic velocity correlates with anemia severity), intrauterine transfusion, or early delivery.

**Q:** What is the nuchal translucency (NT) measurement used for, and what conditions can an increased NT detect?

**A:** NT measures the fluid behind the fetal neck at 11-13+3 weeks GA. Increased NT is an early screen for: trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), trisomy 18, Turner syndrome, other aneuploidies, and cardiac anomalies.

NT is a component of the eFTS (combined with β-hCG and PAPP-A). NT measurement is mandatory for the eFTS. Isolated increased NT without chromosomal anomaly warrants detailed cardiac anatomy assessment. NT >3.5 mm is associated with significant increase in aneuploidy risk.

**Q:** What are the indications for diagnostic prenatal testing (CVS or amniocentesis)?

**A:** Age >40 years, risk factors in current pregnancy, abnormal ultrasound, abnormal prenatal screening (eFTS, MSS, or NIPT), previous chromosomal anomaly or genetic disease, either parent a known carrier of a genetic disorder or balanced translocation, or consanguinity.

In women >40 years, the risk of chromosomal anomaly (1/180) exceeds the procedural risk of amniocentesis (1/200-1/900), making invasive testing appropriate. Screening tests (NIPT, eFTS, MSS) are not diagnostic — positive results require CVS or amniocentesis for confirmation.

**Q:** What are the intimate partner violence (IPV) screening recommendations during prenatal care?

**A:** Screen ALL pregnant patients — not just those with suspicious presentations. Screen as early as possible in pregnancy. Warning signs: bruising, improbable injuries, depression, late prenatal care (presenting in T2/T3), missed prenatal visits, appointments cancelled on short notice.

IPV is common and underdiagnosed in pregnancy. Universal screening is recommended regardless of perceived risk. A private, non-judgmental approach is essential. Documentation and referral to appropriate resources (social work, shelters) should follow positive screens.

**Q:** What are the complications of hyperemesis gravidarum for both mother and fetus?

**A:** Maternal: dehydration, electrolyte and acid-base disturbances, Mallory-Weiss tear, Wernicke's encephalopathy, death. Fetal: usually none; however, IUGR is 15x more common in patients losing >5% of pre-pregnancy weight.

Hyperemesis gravidarum causes significant maternal morbidity. Wernicke's encephalopathy (from thiamine deficiency) is a potentially fatal complication. Despite severe maternal illness, fetal outcomes are generally preserved unless significant maternal weight loss occurs (>5% of pre-pregnancy weight).

**Q:** What is the lithium-associated cardiac malformation in neonates exposed in utero?

**A:** Ebstein's anomaly (downward displacement of the tricuspid valve into the right ventricle), along with goitre and hyponatremia.

Lithium is used for bipolar disorder but is teratogenic. Ebstein's anomaly is associated with T1 lithium exposure. Women requiring mood stabilization in pregnancy may need to switch medications — valproate (NTD risk up to 9%) and carbamazepine (NTD risk 1%) also pose significant risks. Risk-benefit analysis is essential.

**Q:** What foods should pregnant women avoid due to risk of Listeria and Toxoplasma?

**A:** Avoid: raw/undercooked meat and fish, raw hotdogs, raw eggs, raw sprouts (especially alfalfa), unpasteurized dairy products/juices, unpasteurized soft cheeses, deli meats, smoked salmon, and patés (Listeria risk). Also avoid top predator fish (shark, swordfish, king mackerel, tilefish) due to mercury.

Listeria monocytogenes causes listeriosis, which can lead to miscarriage, stillbirth, premature delivery, or severe neonatal infection. Toxoplasma gondii is acquired from raw meat and cat feces. Mercury in top predator fish can cause fetal neurotoxicity — Health Canada recommends limiting these fish.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to External Cephalic Version (ECV)?

**A:** Absolute contraindications include: (1) conditions where vaginal delivery is contraindicated (placenta previa, previous classical caesarean section, previous myomectomy), (2) PROM, (3) uteroplacental insufficiency, (4) abnormal NST, (5) multiple gestation.

ECV is a procedure using external uterine pressure to turn a non-vertex fetus to vertex. Absolute contraindications represent situations where the procedure would be unsafe or futile. Relative contraindications include mild/moderate oligohydramnios, suspected IUGR, HTN, previous T3 bleed, and nuchal cord.

**Q:** What are the eligibility criteria for External Cephalic Version (ECV)?

**A:** Criteria: (1) >36 weeks GA, (2) singleton pregnancy, (3) unengaged presenting part, (4) reactive NST, (5) intact membranes.

ECV has an overall success rate of 40-60%. It is performed with external pressure on the uterus under U/S guidance with intermittent FHR assessment. Rh-negative patients require Rhogam after the procedure. Better prognosis if multiparous, good fluid volume, small baby, skilled obstetrician, and posterior placenta.

**Q:** What are the risks of ECV (External Cephalic Version)?

**A:** Risks include: abruption, cord compression, cord accident, ROM, labour, fetal bradycardia requiring CD (<1% risk), alloimmunization, and fetal death (1/5000).

ECV risk of emergency CD due to fetal bradycardia is <1%. Rhogam must be given post-procedure if the patient is Rh-negative to prevent alloimmunization. These risks must be discussed when obtaining informed consent.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to vaginal breech delivery?

**A:** Contraindications: (1) cord presentation, (2) footling breech, (3) fetal factors incompatible with vaginal delivery (e.g., hydrocephalus, macrosomia, IUGR), (4) clinically inadequate maternal pelvis.

Vaginal breech delivery is associated with risks including head entrapment, prolapsed umbilical cord, birth trauma, and perinatal death. CD is recommended if the breech has not descended to the perineum in the second stage after 2 h without active pushing, or if vaginal delivery is not imminent after 1 h of active pushing.

**Q:** Define pre-existing hypertension in pregnancy.

**A:** sBP ≥140 mmHg or dBP ≥90 mmHg prior to 20 weeks GA; BP must be elevated on >2 occasions at least 15 minutes apart, on the same arm, seated with an appropriately sized cuff.

If HTN persists >12 weeks postpartum, the diagnosis is 'revised' to pre-existing HTN that was masked by the early pregnancy physiologic decrease in BP. Pre-existing HTN is associated with increased risk of gestational HTN, placental abruption, IUGR, IUFD, and CD.

**Q:** Define gestational hypertension.

**A:** sBP ≥140 mmHg or dBP ≥90 mmHg after 20 weeks GA without proteinuria in a patient known to be normotensive before pregnancy.

Gestational HTN (de novo) is distinguished from pre-existing HTN by its onset after 20 weeks. If it persists >12 weeks postpartum, it is reclassified as pre-existing HTN. It is a risk factor for progression to preeclampsia.

**Q:** Define preeclampsia.

**A:** Pre-existing or gestational HTN with new onset proteinuria (urinary protein:creatinine ratio >30 mg/mmol) OR adverse conditions (end-organ dysfunction).

Preeclampsia results from placental malperfusion leading to soluble factor release, maternal vascular endothelial injury, hypertension, and multi-organ injury. Adverse maternal conditions include sBP >160 mmHg, thrombocytopenia, elevated creatinine, oliguria <500 mL/day, pulmonary edema, elevated liver enzymes, and neurologic symptoms. Adverse fetal conditions include IUGR, oligohydramnios, and absent/reversed umbilical artery end-diastolic flow.

**Q:** Define eclampsia.

**A:** The occurrence of ≥1 generalized convulsion and/or coma in the setting of preeclampsia and in the absence of other neurologic conditions.

Eclamptic seizures occur in approximately 0.5% of mildly preeclamptic patients and 2-3% of severely preeclamptic patients. Seizures are typically tonic-clonic, lasting 60-75 seconds. Approximately 25% of cases present in the postpartum period. Women with typical eclamptic seizures without focal neurologic deficits or prolonged coma generally do not require neuroimaging.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for hypertensive disorders of pregnancy?

**A:** Key risk factors: primigravida (80-90% of gestational HTN), first conception with new partner, PMHx/FMHx of gestational HTN or preeclampsia, DM, chronic HTN, renal insufficiency, obesity, APS or inherited thrombophilia, extremes of maternal age (<18 or >40 yr), previous stillbirth/IUFD, vascular/connective tissue disease, placental abruption in previous pregnancy, IUGR, oligohydramnios, multiple gestation, fetal hydrops ('mirror syndrome').

These risk factors help identify patients who should receive early preventive intervention such as low-dose ASA (150-162 mg/d) from before 16 weeks GA to 36 weeks GA. Calcium supplementation (<900 mg/d) is also recommended for calcium-deficient women.

**Q:** What is the management of non-severe hypertension (BP 140-159/90-109 mmHg) in pregnancy?

**A:** Target BP <140/90 mmHg (without comorbidities) or <140/85 mmHg (with comorbidities). First-line antihypertensives: labetalol 100-400 mg PO BID-TID, nifedipine XL 20-60 mg PO once daily or BID, or alpha-methyldopa 250-500 mg PO BID-TID.

ACEIs, ARBs, diuretics (except for pulmonary edema/cardiac failure), prazosin, and atenolol are contraindicated in pregnancy. For initial treatment of non-severe HTN (BP 140-159/90-109 mmHg), labetalol, nifedipine, or methyldopa are preferred per SOGC guidelines.

**Q:** What is the management of severe hypertension (BP ≥160/110 mmHg) in pregnancy?

**A:** Target sBP <160 mmHg and dBP <110 mmHg. Options: (1) labetalol 20 mg IV, then 20-80 mg IV q30 min (max 300 mg), then switch to oral; (2) nifedipine immediate release 5-10 mg capsule q30 min; (3) hydralazine 5 mg IV, repeat 5-10 mg IV q30 min or 0.5-10 mg/h IV (max 20 mg IV or 30 mg IM).

Severe HTN in pregnancy requires prompt IV treatment to prevent hemorrhagic stroke (accounts for 50% of deaths). ACEIs, ARBs, atenolol, and prazosin remain contraindicated. Diuretics may be used in cases of pulmonary edema or cardiac failure.

**Q:** What is the management of preeclampsia based on gestational age?

**A:** • Stable, no adverse conditions (24-33+6 wk GA): expectant management; delivery as approaching 34-36 wk GA. Consider antenatal corticosteroids if <35 wk GA.
• ≥37 wk GA: delivery recommended.
• Severe preeclampsia: stabilize and deliver regardless of GA.
• Seizure prevention: MgSO₄ 4 g IV loading dose, then 1 g/h.

Post-delivery, risk of seizure is highest in the first 24 hours postpartum — continue MgSO₄ for 12-24 hours after delivery. Most patients return to a normotensive BP within 2 weeks postpartum. Continuous FHR monitoring and hourly I&O are required during labour in severe preeclampsia.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for eclampsia and its definitive management?

**A:** First-line treatment for prevention of recurrent convulsions: MgSO₄ (treatment and prophylaxis). Definitive treatment: delivery after maternal stabilization, irrespective of GA or fetal distress.

Acute eclampsia management: ABCs, left lateral decubitus position, supplemental O₂, bed rails up. Aggressive antihypertensive therapy for sustained sBP ≥160 mmHg or dBP ≥110 mmHg with hydralazine or labetalol. Delivery is definitive to reduce maternal morbidity/mortality; mode depends on clinical situation.

**Q:** What are the components of HELLP syndrome?

**A:** HELLP = Hemolysis, Elevated Liver enzymes, Low Platelets. It is a severe complication of preeclampsia.

HELLP syndrome is one of the most serious complications of hypertensive disorders of pregnancy. It can lead to DIC (from release of placental thromboplastin causing consumptive coagulopathy), hepatic dysfunction, and renal dysfunction. Hemorrhagic stroke accounts for 50% of maternal deaths from preeclampsia/eclampsia.

**Q:** What laboratory evaluation should be done for hypertensive disorders of pregnancy?

**A:** CBC (evaluate for thrombocytopenia), PTT/INR/fibrinogen (if abnormal LFTs or bleeding), ALT/AST, creatinine, uric acid, 24-hour urine protein or protein:creatinine ratio. Consider PIGF testing or sFlt-1:PIGF ratio as early screening for suspected preeclampsia.

Low-risk normotensive women should not be routinely screened for proteinuria. Home blood pressure monitoring can be used to monitor BP between visits. Lab workup identifies end-organ involvement that defines adverse conditions in preeclampsia.

**Q:** What ASA regimen is recommended to prevent preeclampsia, and when should it be started?

**A:** Low-dose ASA 150-162 mg/day at night, started before 16 weeks GA and continued until 36 weeks GA, for women at increased risk of preeclampsia.

Per SOGC guidelines, low-dose ASA is recommended for high-risk women (e.g., those with prior preeclampsia, DM, chronic HTN, renal disease, multiple gestation, APS). It is also recommended for pre-existing DM patients to reduce the risk of preeclampsia.

**Q:** What is the management of iron deficiency anemia in pregnancy?

**A:** Prevention (non-anemic): 30 mg elemental iron daily (met by most prenatal vitamins). Treatment (anemic): 30-120 mg elemental iron daily. Note: mother needs 1g of elemental iron per fetus.

Iron deficiency is responsible for 80% of non-physiologic anemia during pregnancy. Iron requirements increase due to fetal/placental growth (500 mg), increased maternal RBC mass (500 mg), and losses (200 mg). Common preparations: ferrous sulfate 325 mg = 65 mg elemental Fe; ferrous fumarate 325 mg = 106 mg elemental Fe; ferrous gluconate 325 mg = 36 mg elemental Fe.

**Q:** What are the fetal complications of iron deficiency anemia in pregnancy?

**A:** Decreased oxygen-carrying capacity leading to fetal distress, IUGR, low birth weight, and impaired fetal neurodevelopment.

Maternal complications include angina, CHF, infection, slower recuperation, and preterm labour. Iron requirements are greater for multiple gestations. Folate deficiency (not iron deficiency) is associated with neural tube defects.

**Q:** What are the fetal complications of folate deficiency in pregnancy?

**A:** Neural tube defects (T1), low birth weight, and prematurity.

Folic acid is necessary for neural tube closure by 28 days GA. Prevention requires 0.4-1 mg folic acid PO daily for 1-3 months preconceptually and throughout T1. Minimum daily requirement is 0.4 mg. Folate deficiency is most often associated with iron deficiency anemia.

**Q:** What is the etiology of gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM)?

**A:** Anti-insulin factors produced by the placenta and high maternal cortisol levels create increased peripheral insulin resistance, leading to GDM and/or exacerbating pre-existing DM.

GDM complicates 2-6% of pregnancies and is usually tested for at 24-28 weeks GA. Unlike T1DM and T2DM, GDM develops after the critical period of organogenesis, so it does not manifest an increased risk of congenital anomalies.

**Q:** Describe the 2-step screening process for gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) recommended by the Canadian Diabetes Association.

**A:** Step 1: Random non-fasting 50 g OGCT. 1h PG <7.8 mmol/L = normal; ≥11.1 mmol/L = GDM; 7.8-11.0 mmol/L → proceed to Step 2.
Step 2: Fasting 75 g OGTT; GDM if ≥1 of: FPG ≥5.3 mmol/L, 1h PG ≥10.6 mmol/L, 2h PG ≥9.0 mmol/L.

Screening is performed between 24-28 weeks GA in all pregnant women. An alternative 1-step fasting 75 g OGTT can be used: GDM if FPG ≥5.1, 1h PG ≥10.0, or 2h PG ≥8.5 mmol/L. Fasting plasma glucose values appear to be the most effective for determining pregnancy outcomes.

**Q:** What are the glycemic targets during pregnancy for patients with GDM?

**A:** FPG <5.3 mmol/L (95 mg/dL); 1-hour postprandial PG <7.8 mmol/L (140 mg/dL); 2-hour postprandial PG <6.7 mmol/L (120 mg/dL).

First-line management of GDM is diet modification and increased physical activity. Insulin therapy is initiated if glycemic targets are not achieved within 2 weeks of lifestyle modification. Metformin can be used (off-label) and is safe during breastfeeding. Other oral antihyperglycemic agents should not be used during pregnancy.

**Q:** What are the intrapartum glucose management goals for diabetic patients in labour?

**A:** Maintain blood glucose between 4.0-7.0 mmol/L during labour (using insulin and dextrose drip for T1DM/T2DM, monitoring q1h) to reduce the risk of neonatal hypoglycemia.

After delivery, insulin requirements dramatically drop with expulsion of the placenta (the source of insulin antagonists). Monitor glucose q6h postpartum; restart insulin at two-thirds of pre-pregnancy dosage when glucose >8 mmol/L. For GDM, stop insulin and diabetic diet postpartum and follow up with 75g OGTT at 6 weeks to 6 months postpartum.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for GDM?

**A:** Age >35 yr, obesity (BMI >30 kg/m²), ethnicity (Indigenous, Hispanic, South Asian, Asian, African), family history of DM, previous history of GDM, previous infant birthweight >4.0 kg, polycystic ovarian syndrome, current use of glucocorticoids, essential HTN or pregnancy-related HTN.

GDM affects 2-6% of pregnancies. Women with previous GDM have a 50% risk of developing T2DM in the next 20 years. Breastfeeding for 3-4 months postpartum is encouraged to prevent childhood obesity and diabetes.

**Q:** What congenital anomalies are associated with pre-existing diabetes (T1DM/T2DM) in pregnancy but NOT with GDM?

**A:** Cardiac (ventricular septal defect), neural tube defects, GU (cystic kidneys), GI (anal atresia), and MSK (sacral agenesis) anomalies — 2-3x increased risk. GDM does NOT increase congenital anomaly risk.

GDM develops after organogenesis (which occurs in the first trimester), so it does not cause congenital anomalies. In contrast, T1DM and T2DM cause hyperglycemia during organogenesis. Sacral agenesis is a pathognomonic association with maternal diabetes.

**Q:** What are the neonatal complications of maternal diabetes in pregnancy?

**A:** Hypoglycemia (pancreatic hyperplasia → excess insulin), hyperbilirubinemia/jaundice (prematurity and polycythemia), hypocalcemia (possible functional hypoparathyroidism), polycythemia (hyperglycemia stimulates fetal erythropoietin production).

Macrosomia results from maternal hyperglycemia → fetal hyperinsulinism → accelerated anabolism. Macrosomia increases risk of shoulder dystocia and birth trauma. Fetal lung immaturity (RDS) results from hyperglycemia interfering with surfactant synthesis. Elective CD is considered for predicted birth weight >4500 g.

**Q:** Why does maternal hyperglycemia cause polyhydramnios?

**A:** Maternal hyperglycemia → fetal hyperglycemia → fetal polyuria → increased amniotic fluid (polyhydramnios). Fetal urine is a major source of amniotic fluid.

This is a direct consequence of the glucose osmotic effect. The increased urine output from the fetal kidneys leads to polyhydramnios. Conversely, IUGR from placental vascular insufficiency in diabetes can cause oligohydramnios.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for early-onset neonatal Group B Streptococcus (GBS) disease?

**A:** <37 weeks GA at birth, prolonged ROM ≥18 hours, maternal intrapartum GBS colonization in current pregnancy, GBS bacteriuria at any time in current pregnancy, previous infant with invasive GBS disease, maternal fever (temperature ≥38°C).

The recto-vaginal GBS carrier rate is 15-40%. GBS increases risk of endometritis, vertical transmission (neonatal sepsis, meningitis, pneumonia, death). GBS bacteriuria at any trimester warrants intrapartum prophylaxis. Screening with vaginal and anorectal swabs is offered at 35-37 weeks GA.

**Q:** What antibiotic regimens are used for intrapartum GBS prophylaxis?

**A:** First-line: penicillin G 5 million IU IV, then 2.5 million IU IV q4h until delivery (give ≥4h before delivery for adequate prophylaxis). Penicillin allergy (low anaphylaxis risk): cefazolin 2g IV, then 1g q8h. Penicillin allergy (high anaphylaxis risk): vancomycin 1g IV q12h OR clindamycin 900 mg q8h (only if susceptible isolate).

Vancomycin and clindamycin levels in amniotic fluid do not reach therapeutic levels; all babies should be screened for GBS despite treatment. If maternal fever occurs, broad-spectrum antibiotic coverage is given regardless of GBS status and GA. If <37 wk GA in labour or with ROM, IV GBS prophylaxis for minimum 48 hours.

**Q:** Why must asymptomatic bacteriuria be treated in pregnancy?

**A:** Asymptomatic bacteriuria in pregnancy carries increased risk of progression to cystitis, pyelonephritis, and probable increased risk of preterm labour (PTL).

UTI is the most common medical complication of pregnancy, with asymptomatic bacteriuria occurring in 2-7% of pregnant women. Increased urinary stasis from mechanical and hormonal (progesterone) factors predisposes to infection. Complications if untreated include acute cystitis, pyelonephritis, and possible PTL.

**Q:** What is the first-line antibiotic treatment for uncomplicated UTI in pregnancy?

**A:** First-line: amoxicillin 250-500 mg PO q8h x 7 days. Alternatives: nitrofurantoin 100 mg PO BID x 7 days or cephalosporins. Follow with monthly urine cultures.

Major organisms causing pyelonephritis: E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterobacter, Proteus. Pyelonephritis requires hospitalization and IV antibiotics. Recurrence is common; offer continuous or post-coital prophylaxis with nitrofurantoin or cephalexin, except during the last 4 weeks of pregnancy (avoid nitrofurantoin near term).

**Q:** What are the fetal effects and management of varicella (chickenpox) infection during pregnancy?

**A:** Fetal effects: congenital varicella syndrome (limb aplasia, chorioretinitis, cataracts, cutaneous scars, cortical atrophy, IUGR, hydrops) — greatest risk at 13-30 weeks GA and 5 days pre- to 2 days post-delivery. Management: Varicella-zoster immunoglobulin (VZIG) for exposed patient; if maternal infection 5 days before delivery, give infant VZIG. Do NOT administer live attenuated vaccine during pregnancy.

Vertical transmission can occur transplacentally. The live attenuated varicella vaccine is contraindicated in pregnancy. VZIG administered to the exposed patient decreases risk of congenital varicella syndrome.

**Q:** What are the fetal effects of Cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection during pregnancy?

**A:** 5-10% of infected infants develop CNS involvement: mental retardation, cerebral calcification, hydrocephalus, microcephaly, deafness, and chorioretinitis.

CMV is a DNA herpes family virus. Transmission to baby is transplacental, during delivery, or via breast milk. Mother may be asymptomatic or have flu-like symptoms. Diagnosis by serology and virus isolated from urine or secretion culture. No specific treatment; maintain good hygiene and avoid high-risk situations.

**Q:** What are the fetal effects of Parvovirus B19 (Fifth Disease) during pregnancy?

**A:** Greatest risk at 10-20 weeks GA: spontaneous abortion, stillbirth, hydrops fetalis (in utero). If fetal hydrops occurs, consider fetal transfusion.

Parvovirus B19 is transmitted by respiratory droplets and infected blood products. Mother may be asymptomatic or have flu-like symptoms, rash, and arthritis. Diagnosis: serology, viral PCR, maternal AFP. If maternal IgM is present, follow fetus with U/S for hydrops.

**Q:** What is the management of Hepatitis B in pregnancy and the neonate?

**A:** Screen all pregnancies serologically. Treat neonate with Hepatitis B immunoglobulin (HBIG) and vaccine at birth, 1 month, and 6 months — 90% effective. Vertical transmission rate: 10% if asymptomatic and HBsAg+; 85-90% if both HBsAg and HBeAg positive.

HBV is a DNA virus transmitted via blood, saliva, semen, and vaginal secretions. Fetal effects include prematurity, low birth weight, and neonatal death. Mother may present with fever, N/V, fatigue, jaundice, and elevated liver enzymes. Transmission to baby is transplacental or through breast milk.

**Q:** When is elective caesarean delivery indicated for HIV-positive pregnant women?

**A:** Elective CD is indicated if: no previous antiviral treatment or monotherapy only, viral load unknown or >500 RNA copies/mL, unknown prenatal care, or patient request.

HIV in pregnancy is managed with triple antiretroviral therapy, which decreases transmission to <1%. Transmission routes to baby are in utero (1/3), at delivery (1/3), and breastfeeding (1/3). All pregnant women are offered HIV screening. Fetal effects include IUGR, PTL, and PROM.

**Q:** What are the features of congenital rubella syndrome?

**A:** Hearing loss, cataracts, cardiovascular lesions (mitral regurgitation), IUGR, hepatitis, CNS defects, and osseous changes.

Rubella is highly contagious (ssRNA togavirus). Greatest fetal risk is in T1. Maternal symptoms: rash (50%), fever, posterior auricular/occipital lymphadenopathy, arthralgia. Do NOT administer live attenuated vaccine during pregnancy; offer vaccination following pregnancy. No specific treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for syphilis in pregnancy?

**A:** Benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million IU IM x1 dose for early syphilis; 3 doses for late syphilis. Monitor VDRL monthly. No approved alternatives in pregnancy; if beta-lactam allergy, recommend desensitization then treat with penicillin.

Syphilis is caused by Treponema pallidum (spirochete), transmitted sexually to the mother and transplacentally to the baby. Greatest fetal risk is in T1. VDRL screening is performed for all pregnancies; positive results require confirmatory testing. Fetal effects include PTL, multisystem involvement, and fetal death.

**Q:** What is the timing and management of toxoplasmosis transmission during pregnancy?

**A:** Greatest fetal transmission risk in T3, but most severe fetal effects if infected in T1. Management: spiramycin decreases fetal morbidity but not rate of transmission. Diagnosis: IgM and IgG serology; PCR of amniotic fluid.

Toxoplasma gondii is a protozoa transmitted via raw meat, unpasteurized goat's milk, and cat feces/urine. Most mothers are subclinical; may have flu-like symptoms. Congenital toxoplasmosis: chorioretinitis, hydrocephaly, intracranial calcification, microcephaly — 75% initially asymptomatic at birth. Only primary infection during pregnancy is concerning.

**Q:** What are the VTE risk factors specific to pregnancy?

**A:** Hypercoagulability: increased factors II, V, VII, VIII, IX, X, XI, fibrinogen; increased platelet aggregation; decreased protein S, tPA, factors XI, XIII; increased resistance to activated protein C; antithrombin can be normal or reduced. Stasis: increased venous distensibility, decreased venous tone, 50% decrease in venous flow in lower extremity by T3, uterus mechanically impedes venous return. Endothelial damage: vascular damage at delivery (CD or SVD), uterine instrumentation, peripartum pelvic surgery.

These factors correspond to Virchow's Triad (hypercoagulability, stasis, endothelial damage). Incidence of DVT is 12.1/10,000 and PE is 5.4/10,000 pregnancies. Highest DVT risk is in T3; highest PE risk is in the first 6 weeks postpartum. Most DVTs occur in iliofemoral or calf veins with predilection for the left leg.

**Q:** What is the management of VTE in pregnancy?

**A:** Preferred: LMWH (discontinue ≥24h before delivery). Alternative: unfractionated heparin 80 IU/kg bolus (max 5000 IU) → 18 IU/kg/h infusion; maintain aPTT 1.5-2x normal. Warfarin is CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy (teratogenic).

Before initiating treatment, obtain baseline CBC including platelets and aPTT. HIT occurs in approximately 3% with unfractionated heparin. VTE prophylaxis postpartum should be considered if absolute risk >1.0%. Women on long-term anticoagulation require full therapeutic anticoagulation throughout pregnancy and 6-12 weeks postpartum.

**Q:** How is warfarin different from heparin in terms of safety during pregnancy?

**A:** Warfarin is CONTRAINDICATED in pregnancy due to teratogenic effects. LMWH is preferred for VTE treatment and does not cross the placenta; unfractionated heparin is an alternative.

This distinction is clinically important: warfarin crosses the placenta and is a known teratogen (warfarin embryopathy). Heparins (both LMWH and unfractionated) do not cross the placenta and are safe in pregnancy. Postpartum VTE prophylaxis considerations include risk factors such as BMI >30, smoking >10 cigarettes/day, preeclampsia, emergency CD, and blood loss >1L.

**Q:** Define the stages of labour and their cervical dilatation.

**A:** First stage: 0-10 cm cervical dilatation (latent phase: 0-4 cm; active phase: 4-10 cm). Second stage: 10 cm to delivery of the baby. Third stage: delivery of placenta. Fourth stage: first postpartum hours.

Duration of first stage: nulliparous latent phase 6-18h (multiparous 2-10h); nulliparous active phase: dilatation >1.0 cm/h; multiparous >1.2 cm/h. Second stage: nulliparous up to 3h (or 4h with epidural); multiparous up to 30 min (or 1h with epidural). Third stage: 5-30 min for both.

**Q:** Differentiate true labour from false labour (Braxton-Hicks contractions).

**A:** True labour: regular, painful contractions of increasing intensity associated with progressive cervical dilatation, effacement, and descent. False labour (Braxton-Hicks): irregular contractions with unchanged intensity and long intervals, not associated with cervical changes, often relieved by rest or hydration.

The key differentiator is progressive cervical change (dilatation + effacement) and descent of presenting part in true labour. Bishop score is used to assess cervical readiness. Latent phase has slow dilatation to 4 cm; active phase has rapid dilatation to 10 cm with contractions every 2-3 min lasting 45-60 seconds.

**Q:** Define fetal station in labour.

**A:** Station describes the position of the presenting bony part of the fetal skull relative to the ischial spines, measured in 1 cm intervals. Station 0 = at ischial spines = engaged; -5 to -1 = above spines; +1 to +5 = below spines.

Station is determined by vaginal examination. Engagement means the leading edge of the skull has reached the level of the ischial spines (station 0). Progress is measured by descent in relation to maternal ischial spines during the second stage of labour.

**Q:** What are the fetal positions and which is most common?

**A:** Most common: occiput anterior (OA), specifically left OA. Other positions: occiput posterior (OP) — most rotate spontaneously to OA but may cause prolonged second stage; occiput transverse (OT) — if persistent, leads to arrest of dilatation.

Normally, the fetal head enters the maternal pelvis and engages in the OT position, then rotates to OA (or OP in a small percentage). OP presentations may require rotational manoeuvres or result in operative delivery. Fetal position is described using the reference point of the presenting part (occiput for cephalic, sacrum for breech, mentum for face).

**Q:** What fetal attitudes/head presentations require caesarean delivery?

**A:** Brow presentation (head partially extended) requires CD. Mentum posterior (face presentation with fully extended head) always requires CD. Mentum anterior can deliver vaginally.

Vertex presentation (head fully flexed) is normal. Face presentation (head fully extended) can deliver vaginally if mentum anterior. Brow presentation cannot deliver vaginally due to the largest presenting diameter. These are assessed via vaginal examination and/or U/S.

**Q:** What are the signs of placental separation during the third stage of labour?

**A:** Gush of blood, lengthening of the cord, and uterus becomes globular (fundie rise).

The third stage of labour is from delivery of the baby to delivery of the placenta. Signs of placental separation indicate it is ready to be delivered. Active management of the third stage (oxytocin administration) reduces postpartum hemorrhage risk. Duration is 5-30 minutes for both nulliparous and multiparous women.

**Q:** What is asynclitism in the context of labour?

**A:** Asynclitism is the lateral tilt of the sagittal suture relative to the axis of the birth canal. It can be anterior (sagittal suture displaced posteriorly) or posterior (sagittal suture displaced anteriorly), and may impact descent during labour.

Synclitism is the normal alignment where the sagittal suture is equidistant from the symphysis and sacral promontory. Asynclitism is a normal mechanism of labour allowing the fetal head to navigate the pelvis but can also indicate cephalopelvic disproportion if persistent.

**Q:** What are the HbA1c targets for pre-existing diabetes (T1DM/T2DM) before and during pregnancy?

**A:** Before pregnancy: optimize glycemic control (HbA1c <7%). During pregnancy: aim for HbA1c <6.5%.

Preconception planning includes commencing folic acid 1.0 mg daily (3 months prior), reviewing medications (discontinue ACEIs, ARBs, statins), and assessing for complications. During pregnancy, fetal echocardiogram is performed in T2 if HbA1c was high in T1. Start 162 mg ASA at night before 16 weeks GA to reduce preeclampsia risk.

**Q:** A patient with preeclampsia at 35 weeks GA develops a tonic-clonic seizure lasting 70 seconds. What is the first-line treatment to prevent recurrent seizures?

**A:** MgSO₄ (magnesium sulfate) is the first-line treatment for eclampsia, used for both treatment and prophylaxis of convulsions. Loading dose: 4g IV, then 1g/h maintenance.

Management also includes: ABCs, left lateral decubitus position (prevent aspiration), supplemental O₂, bed rails up for safety, and aggressive antihypertensive therapy if sBP ≥160 mmHg or dBP ≥110 mmHg. Definitive treatment is delivery after maternal stabilization, irrespective of GA. The seizure risk is highest in the first 24h postpartum — continue MgSO₄ for 12-24h after delivery.

**Q:** A GDM patient has inadequate glycemic control after 2 weeks of diet and exercise. What medication is initiated, and what are the glycemic targets?

**A:** Initiate insulin therapy. Glycemic targets: FPG <5.3 mmol/L, 1-hour PG <7.8 mmol/L, 2-hour PG <6.7 mmol/L. Metformin can be used off-label after discussion with patient.

Metformin and glyburide are safe during breastfeeding; other antihyperglycemic agents should not be used due to lack of safety data. IOL is offered between 38-39 weeks GA for GDM on insulin or metformin. Breastfeeding for 3-4 months is encouraged to prevent childhood obesity and diabetes.

**Q:** What are the TORCH infections in pregnancy?

**A:** TORCH = Toxoplasmosis, Other (syphilis, varicella, parvovirus B19, HIV, Hepatitis B, influenza), Rubella, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes Simplex Virus.

TORCH infections are important causes of congenital infection and fetal anomaly. All are transmitted transplacentally. Key distinguishing features: CMV is the most common congenital viral infection; toxoplasmosis is worst if infected in T1 despite greatest transmission risk in T3; rubella causes the classic triad of hearing loss, cataracts, and cardiac defects.

**Q:** What are the fetal complications of placenta previa?

**A:** Perinatal mortality (low but higher than normal pregnancy), prematurity (bleeding often dictates early CD), intrauterine hypoxia (acute or IUGR), fetal malpresentation, PPROM, risk of fetal blood loss from placenta (especially if incised during CD).

Placenta previa creates a physical barrier at the cervical os, predisposing to preterm delivery and compromised placental perfusion. Fetal malpresentation occurs because the previa occupies the lower uterine segment. Fetal blood loss risk is highest if the placenta is inadvertently incised during caesarean delivery.

**Q:** What are the major maternal complications of placenta previa?

**A:** <1% maternal mortality; hemorrhage and hypovolemic shock; anemia; acute renal failure; pituitary necrosis (Sheehan syndrome); placenta accreta (especially with previous uterine surgery or anterior previa); hysterectomy.

Massive hemorrhage from placenta previa can cause hypovolemic shock leading to renal cortical necrosis and, classically, Sheehan syndrome from pituitary ischemia. Anterior placentas overlying previous uterine scars carry the highest risk of morbidly adherent placenta (accreta spectrum), often necessitating hysterectomy.

**Q:** Which imaging modality is most accurate for diagnosing placenta previa, and when should it be repeated?

**A:** Transvaginal ultrasound (TVU) is more accurate than transabdominal U/S at any gestational age. If the placenta covers the internal os by <20 mm at 20 weeks, spontaneous resolution is likely; TVU should be repeated in the third trimester as the distance from the cervix typically increases.

The lower uterine segment elongates with advancing gestation, often drawing an apparent previa away from the os — a phenomenon called placental migration. TVU is safe in placenta previa and provides superior visualization of the cervical os. Repeat scanning in T3 confirms resolution or persistence.

**Q:** Outline the expectant management of placenta previa at <37 weeks with minimal bleeding.

**A:** Admit to hospital; limited physical activity; pelvic rest (no douching, enemas, or sexual intercourse); consider corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity; deliver when fetus is mature or hemorrhage indicates maternal/fetal compromise. Rhogam if Rh-negative; large bore IV, volume replacement, O2 for hypotensive patients; EFM; U/S assessment.

The goal is to prolong the pregnancy until the risks of prematurity are outweighed by those of continued expectant management. Pelvic rest prevents mechanical stimulation of the lower uterine segment. Corticosteroids accelerate fetal lung maturity if preterm delivery becomes necessary. Delivery by CD at ≥37 weeks is standard.

**Q:** Define placental abruption and state the most significant maternal haematologic complication.

**A:** Partial or total premature placental detachment caused by bleeding at the decidual-placental interface, occurring after 20 weeks GA. The most significant haematologic complication is DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation), which occurs in 20% of abruptions. Placental abruption is the most common cause of DIC in pregnancy.

The decidual-placental interface hemorrhage releases thromboplastin into the maternal circulation, triggering consumptive coagulopathy. DIC in abruption is associated with concealed hemorrhage and fetal demise. Fibrinogen, FDP, PTT/PT, and platelet counts must be monitored closely.

**Q:** Differentiate external (revealed) from internal (concealed) placental abruption.

**A:** External/revealed/apparent (majority): blood dissects downward toward cervix → presents with vaginal bleeding. Internal/concealed/occult (20%): blood dissects upward toward the fetus; may not have visible vaginal bleeding. Most are mixed. Shock and anemia may be out of proportion to apparent blood loss in concealed abruption.

Because concealed hemorrhage is not visible externally, the degree of blood loss may be severely underestimated. This makes clinical signs of shock disproportionate to apparent bleeding a key diagnostic clue. Concealed abruption also carries higher risk of DIC due to larger volume of retroplacental hematoma.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of placental abruption?

**A:** Painful (80%) vaginal bleeding (not always present if concealed); uterine tenderness; uterine contractions/hypertonus (lack of relaxation between contractions); sudden onset, constant pain localized to lower back and uterus; shock/anemia out of proportion to apparent blood loss; fetal distress or demise (15% present with demise); bloody amniotic fluid; coagulopathy.

The combination of painful vaginal bleeding with uterine rigidity ('board-like uterus') and fetal distress is the hallmark triad. Fetal presentation is typically normal. The pain in abruption is constant (unlike labour contractions) because the retroplacental hematoma causes sustained uterine tension.

**Q:** What is the sensitivity of ultrasound for diagnosing placental abruption, and what is the primary basis for diagnosis?

**A:** U/S sensitivity for diagnosing abruption is only 15%. Placental abruption is primarily a clinical diagnosis based on history and examination findings.

The retroplacental hematoma can be isoechoic with the placenta on U/S, making it difficult to detect. A negative U/S does not exclude abruption. Clinical features (painful bleeding, uterine rigidity, fetal distress, disproportionate shock) remain the cornerstone of diagnosis.

**Q:** Outline the management of severe placental abruption with fetal/maternal compromise.

**A:** Hydrate and restore blood loss; correct coagulation defect if present; vaginal delivery if no contraindication and no fetal/maternal distress; CD if live fetus with fetal or maternal distress develops despite resuscitation, labour fails to progress, or vaginal delivery is otherwise contraindicated.

Rapid delivery is essential to prevent ongoing fetal hypoxia and to allow uterine contraction to tamponade bleeding. Blood products (red cells, platelets, cryoprecipitate) must be available due to DIC risk. Vaginal delivery is preferred when safe as it achieves uterine contraction more rapidly than CD in some situations.

**Q:** Define vasa previa and explain why it carries high perinatal mortality.

**A:** Vasa previa: unprotected fetal vessels pass over the cervical os, associated with velamentous cord insertion or succenturiate lobe. Undiagnosed: 50% perinatal mortality (75% if membranes rupture) because the vessels are fetal — even a small amount of blood loss causes fetal exsanguination. If diagnosed antenatally: 97% survival with planned CD at 35-36 weeks.

Unlike placenta previa where bleeding is maternal, bleeding in vasa previa is entirely fetal. Rupture of membranes tears these unprotected vessels, and the fetus can exsanguinate rapidly because fetal blood volume is small. The Apt test (NaOH — pink = fetal blood) or Wright's stain for nucleated RBCs can confirm fetal source.

**Q:** What is the Apt test and when is it used?

**A:** The Apt test mixes blood with NaOH: if the supernatant turns pink → fetal blood (fetal Hb is alkali-resistant); if yellow → maternal blood (adult Hb is denatured). Used immediately when vasa previa or other fetal vessel rupture is suspected to distinguish fetal from maternal source of vaginal bleeding.

Fetal hemoglobin (HbF) resists alkali denaturation, maintaining the pink colour. Adult hemoglobin (HbA) is denatured, turning yellow-brown. This bedside test is rapid and critical in vasa previa where confirming fetal source mandates immediate emergency CD to prevent exsanguination.

**Q:** Define preterm labour (PTL) and state its incidence.

**A:** Labour between 20 and 37 weeks GA. Complicates approximately 10% of pregnancies. It is the most common cause of neonatal mortality in the United States and among the top 5 causes of neonatal mortality in Canada.

The 20–37 week window defines PTL; before 20 weeks is classified as abortion. The neonatal consequences of prematurity — respiratory distress, IVH, NEC, sepsis — drive the high morbidity and mortality, making PTL prevention a major obstetric priority.

**Q:** What is the single most important risk factor for preterm labour?

**A:** Prior history of spontaneous preterm labour is the most important risk factor for PTL.

A previous spontaneous PTL increases recurrence risk approximately 2-3 fold. Other important risk factors include cervical length <25 mm on TVU, bacterial vaginosis, multiple gestation, prior cervical procedures (cone biopsy, D&C), smoking, and cocaine use. Identifying high-risk women allows targeted prevention strategies.

**Q:** What cervical length on transvaginal U/S has high negative predictive value for PTL before 34 weeks?

**A:** Cervical length >30 mm on transvaginal ultrasound has a high negative predictive value for PTL before 34 weeks GA. A short cervix (<25 mm at <24 weeks GA) is an indication for progesterone therapy.

Transvaginal ultrasonography (not transabdominal) is the preferred and more accurate method for cervical length assessment. A long cervix (>30 mm) effectively excludes imminent PTL. Progesterone (200 mg vaginally daily from diagnosis to 36 weeks) reduces PTL risk in women with a short cervix in singleton pregnancies.

**Q:** What is fetal fibronectin (fFN) and how is it used clinically in suspected preterm labour?

**A:** Fetal fibronectin is a glycoprotein found in amniotic fluid and placental tissue. Positive if >50 ng/mL in cervicovaginal secretions at 22-34 weeks GA. NPV >95%: a negative result means very unlikely to deliver in 7-14 days. Positive result indicates increased risk of delivery — may prompt admission, tocolysis, and/or corticosteroids. Contraindicated if cerclage, active vaginal bleeding, vaginal exam or sex in last 24 hours.

fFN serves best as a rule-out test (high NPV). A positive result has low PPV (~25%) but identifies women at increased risk who may benefit from corticosteroids and/or transfer to a tertiary centre. It is only valid with intact membranes, cervical dilation <3 cm, and established fetal well-being.

**Q:** What are the indications and definition of cervical cerclage?

**A:** Cerclage = placement of cervical sutures at the level of the internal os (usually end of T1 or T2, removed in T3). Indication: cervical incompetence (cervical dilation and effacement without increased uterine contractility). Diagnosis based on: Hx of silent cervical dilation, recurrent T2 losses, prior cervical procedures. Proven benefit in women with primary structural cervical abnormality or connective tissue disorders. Cerclage is inferior to progesterone for preventing PTL in singletons not due to cervical incompetence.

Cervical cerclage mechanically reinforces the internal os. TVU of cervical length is recommended only for high-risk pregnancies before 28 weeks GA. Physical examination-indicated cerclage (for visible dilation) is associated with significant reductions in perinatal mortality and preterm birth. Progesterone is superior for cervical shortening without true incompetence.

**Q:** What are the requirements (all must be satisfied) to use tocolysis in preterm labour?

**A:** Live, immature fetus; intact membranes; cervical dilatation <4 cm; absence of maternal or fetal contraindications.

Tocolysis does not stop PTL completely but may delay delivery up to 48 hours, allowing betamethasone (antenatal corticosteroids) to enhance fetal lung maturity and/or arrange transfer to a tertiary centre. It is not used when delivery is safer than continuation of pregnancy.

**Q:** List the contraindications to tocolysis.

**A:** Maternal contraindications: bleeding (placenta previa or abruption), maternal disease (HTN, DM, heart disease), preeclampsia or eclampsia, chorioamnionitis. Fetal contraindications: erythroblastosis fetalis, severe congenital anomalies, fetal distress/demise, IUGR, multiple gestation (relative).

In these conditions, continuation of pregnancy is more dangerous than preterm delivery, making tocolysis inappropriate. Chorioamnionitis is an absolute contraindication because prolonging pregnancy risks overwhelming fetal and maternal sepsis.

**Q:** What are the tocolytic agents used in preterm labour and their key dosing?

**A:** 1. Nifedipine (calcium channel blocker): 20 mg PO loading → 20 mg PO 90 min later → 20 mg q3-8h for 72h (max 180 mg); or 10 mg PO q20 min x4 doses. 2. Indomethacin (prostaglandin synthesis inhibitor): for early PTL (<32 wk) or polyhydramnios; 50-100 mg PR loading → 25-50 mg q6h x8 doses for 48h. Absolute contraindication to nifedipine: maternal CHF, aortic stenosis.

Nifedipine relaxes smooth muscle by blocking L-type calcium channels in the myometrium. Indomethacin inhibits prostaglandin production, reducing uterine contractility; avoid after 32 weeks due to fetal ductal constriction risk. Both agents delay delivery by up to 48 hours but do not prevent PTL.

**Q:** What are the indications, dosing, and benefits of antenatal corticosteroids in preterm labour?

**A:** Betamethasone valerate (Celestone) 12 mg IM q24h x2 doses OR dexamethasone 6 mg IM q12h x4 doses. Given at 24–34+6 weeks GA if expected delivery within 7 days. Benefits: enhance fetal lung maturity; reduce perinatal death; reduce severe RDS, IVH, necrotizing enterocolitis, and neonatal sepsis. Specific contraindication: active TB.

Antenatal corticosteroids are one of the most impactful interventions in preterm labour, reducing neonatal mortality by ~30%. At 22+0–23+6 weeks, multidisciplinary counselling is required and steroids considered if early intensive care is requested. Betamethasone is preferred in most Canadian centres.

**Q:** What is MgSO4 used for in preterm labour and what is the dosing?

**A:** MgSO4 is used for fetal neuroprotection: reduces the risk of cerebral palsy. Dose: 4 g IV bolus followed by 1 g/h infusion for at least 4 hours, given if imminent delivery is expected and GA is <33+6 weeks.

MgSO4 neuroprotection is now standard of care at <34 weeks when preterm delivery is imminent. The mechanism is thought to involve stabilization of neuronal membranes and reduction of glutamate-mediated excitotoxicity. It is distinct from MgSO4 used for eclampsia prevention.

**Q:** What are the gestational age-based survival rates for preterm infants?

**A:** 24 weeks GA: ~50% survival (may be higher in tertiary centres with level 3-4 NICU). 30 weeks GA or 1500 g: ~90% survival. 33 weeks GA or 2000 g: ~99% survival.

These benchmarks inform counselling and decision-making around tocolysis and delivery. Below 24 weeks, intensive care is often considered non-beneficial; the 22-24 week 'grey zone' requires individualized multidisciplinary discussion with parents. Improved NICU care continues to push viability thresholds lower.

**Q:** What are the morbidities associated with prematurity?

**A:** Respiratory distress syndrome (RDS), intraventricular hemorrhage (IVH), apnea, feeding difficulties, seizures, asphyxia/hypoxia, sepsis, thermal instability, retinopathy of prematurity, bronchopulmonary dysplasia, necrotizing enterocolitis.

Prematurity is the leading cause of perinatal morbidity and mortality. Each week of additional gestation significantly reduces the incidence of these complications. This drives the rationale for antenatal corticosteroids (reduces RDS, IVH, NEC) and MgSO4 (reduces cerebral palsy).

**Q:** Define PROM, prolonged ROM, and PPROM.

**A:** PROM: prelabour rupture of membranes (at any gestational age). Prolonged ROM: >18 hours elapsed between ROM and onset of labour. PPROM: preterm (before 37 weeks GA) prelabour rupture of membranes.

These definitions are critical for clinical management. PPROM before 34 weeks usually warrants expectant management with antibiotics to extend latency. Prolonged ROM (>18h) increases GBS transmission risk and is an indication for GBS prophylaxis if GBS status is unknown.

**Q:** How is PROM/PPROM confirmed clinically?

**A:** Sterile speculum exam (avoid digital exam to minimize infection risk): pooling of fluid in posterior fornix; cascading (fluid leaking from cervix with cough/Valsalva); nitrazine test (amniotic fluid is basic — turns nitrazine paper blue, but low specificity as blood, urine, semen also positive); ferning (amniotic fluid crystallizes in fern pattern on microscopy). Decreased AFV on U/S.

The combination of pooling, cascading, ferning, and nitrazine is highly sensitive. Nitrazine alone is non-specific (false positives with blood, urine, semen). Ferning is more specific — amniotic fluid forms a fern pattern due to sodium chloride crystallization. U/S finding of oligohydramnios supports PROM.

**Q:** Outline the PROM management by gestational age.

**A:** 22-25 wk: individual consideration with parental counselling regarding risks of prematurity. 26-34 wk: expectant management (prematurity complications are significant). 34-36 wk ('grey zone'): risk of death from RDS equals risk of death from neonatal sepsis. ≥37 wk: induction of labour (risk of death from sepsis > risk from RDS).

The key decision point is when the risk of infection (chorioamnionitis, neonatal sepsis) exceeds the risk of prematurity. At ≥37 weeks, expectant management risks overwhelming sepsis without benefit. At 26-34 weeks, benefits of continued gestation outweigh infection risk with antibiotic prophylaxis.

**Q:** What antibiotics are recommended for PPROM, and which combination should be avoided?

**A:** Recommended options (from large PPROM RCTs): (1) Ampicillin 2g IV q6h + erythromycin 250mg IV q6h followed by amoxicillin + erythromycin orally; OR (2) Erythromycin 250mg orally for 10 days. Amoxicillin/clavulanic acid (co-amoxiclav) must NOT be used due to increased risk of necrotizing enterocolitis in neonates. Amoxicillin without clavulanic acid is safe.

Antibiotics in PPROM extend the latency period (time from ROM to delivery), reducing chorioamnionitis and neonatal morbidity. The clavulanate component of co-amoxiclav has been specifically associated with NEC in neonates in the ORACLE trial — a critical Canadian licensing point.

**Q:** What are the complications of PROM?

**A:** Cord prolapse; intrauterine infection (chorioamnionitis); premature delivery; limb contracture; pulmonary hypoplasia (especially with very early GA PROM); maternal coagulopathy.

Pulmonary hypoplasia results from prolonged oligohydramnios (lack of amniotic fluid for fetal breathing movements) and is most severe with PROM before 22 weeks. Chorioamnionitis is the most common serious infectious complication and mandates urgent delivery regardless of gestational age.

**Q:** Define postterm pregnancy and state its management recommendation.

**A:** Postterm pregnancy: >42 weeks GA (up to 27% reach 41 weeks; 5.5% exceed 42 weeks). Management: labour induction is recommended at 41+3 weeks GA if no contraindications to vaginal delivery.

Postterm pregnancy carries 2-3x higher perinatal mortality due to progressive uteroplacental insufficiency. Canadian guidelines (SOGC) recommend induction at 41+3 weeks to prevent this excess mortality. Most cases are idiopathic; rare causes include placental sulfatase deficiency and anencephaly.

**Q:** What are the complications of postterm pregnancy (>42 weeks)?

**A:** Perinatal mortality 2-3x higher (progressive uteroplacental insufficiency); intrauterine infection; asphyxia; meconium aspiration syndrome; placental insufficiency/ageing/infarction; macrosomia; dystocia; fetal distress; operative deliveries; pneumonia; seizures; NICU admission; stillbirth.

The post-mature placenta undergoes progressive degeneration, reducing oxygen and nutrient delivery. Oligohydramnios with cord compression and meconium passage into reduced amniotic fluid drives the increased risk. Macrosomia from prolonged nutrient delivery paradoxically coexists with uteroplacental insufficiency in late postterm.

**Q:** Define intrauterine fetal demise (IUFD) and state its incidence in Canada.

**A:** IUFD: fetal demise in utero after 20 weeks GA (before 20 weeks = spontaneous abortion). Incidence: <1% of pregnancies; in 2021, there were 3,174 stillbirths in Canada.

The 20-week threshold distinguishes stillbirth from miscarriage in Canadian vital statistics and clinical practice. IUFD is increased in high-risk pregnancies (HTN, DM, APS, congenital anomalies). Approximately 50% are idiopathic; the remaining 50% have identifiable causes.

**Q:** What investigations should be ordered after intrauterine fetal demise?

**A:** Maternal: HbA1c, fasting glucose, TSH, Kleihauer-Betke, VDRL, ANA, CBC, anticardiolipins, antibody screens, INR/PTT, serum/urine toxicology, cervical and vaginal cultures, TORCH screen. Fetal: karyotype, cord blood, skin biopsy, genetics evaluation, autopsy, amniotic fluid culture for CMV, parvovirus B19, herpes. Placenta: pathology, bacterial cultures.

A systematic workup identifies the cause in ~50% of cases. APS (antiphospholipid syndrome — anticardiolipins, lupus anticoagulant) is an important and treatable cause. Kleihauer-Betke identifies fetomaternal hemorrhage. TORCH infections (Toxoplasma, Rubella, CMV, Herpes) are a key fetal cause.

**Q:** How is intrauterine fetal demise managed, and what complication must be monitored?

**A:** IOL with vaginal misoprostol (Cytotec) for >20 weeks GA. Monitor for maternal coagulopathy (10% risk of DIC). Provide parental psychological care/bereavement support per hospital protocol. Comprehensive discussion within 3 months about investigation results and plans for future pregnancies.

DIC risk in IUFD results from release of thromboplastin from degenerating fetal and placental tissue into the maternal circulation. Risk increases with time since fetal death. Serial fibrinogen, PT/PTT, and platelet counts guide blood product use. Misoprostol is preferred for cervical ripening and induction in IUFD.

**Q:** Define IUGR and differentiate symmetric (Type I) from asymmetric (Type II) IUGR.

**A:** IUGR: failure of a fetus to reach its biologically determined growth potential due to pathological factors. Symmetric (Type I, 25-30%): early onset, reduced growth of both head and abdomen; HC/AC ratio may be normal; associated with congenital anomalies or TORCH infections. Asymmetric (Type II, 70%): late onset; fetal abdomen disproportionately smaller than head (brain spared → HC/AC ratio increased); associated with placental insufficiency; more favourable prognosis.

In asymmetric IUGR, the fetus preferentially shunts blood to the brain (brain-sparing reflex), preserving head growth at the expense of liver glycogen and subcutaneous fat. This is detected by an increased HC/AC ratio. Symmetric IUGR affecting all organs suggests an early insult (chromosomal, infectious) before organ differentiation.

**Q:** What are the neonatal complications of IUGR?

**A:** Meconium aspiration; asphyxia; polycythemia; hypoglycemia; hypocalcemia; hypophosphatemia; hyponatremia; intellectual disability; greater risk of perinatal morbidity and mortality.

Chronic intrauterine hypoxia in IUGR stimulates erythropoietin release, causing polycythemia. Depleted glycogen stores cause hypoglycemia. IUGR fetuses have reduced stress tolerance during labour, increasing caesarean delivery rates. Long-term sequelae include Barker hypothesis cardiovascular and metabolic programming.

**Q:** How is IUGR investigated and managed?

**A:** Investigations: SFH at every visit; if high-risk or SFH lags >2 cm: U/S (BPD, HC/AC ratio, FL, EFW, AFV), BPP, Doppler umbilical cord blood flow analysis. Management: risk modification (smoking, alcohol, nutrition, treat maternal illness); serial BPP; delivery when extrauterine existence is less dangerous (abnormal function tests, absent growth, severe oligohydramnios) especially if >34 wk; betamethasone + MgSO4 if anticipated preterm delivery; IUGR fetuses are more likely to require CD.

Absent or reversed end-diastolic flow on umbilical Doppler indicates severe placental insufficiency and is a critical sign requiring urgent delivery. Serial monitoring allows timing of delivery to minimize prematurity while avoiding stillbirth from progressive placental failure.

**Q:** Define macrosomia and state the thresholds for planned caesarean delivery.

**A:** Macrosomia: infant weight >90th percentile for GA or >4000 g. Planned CD is reasonable when: EFW >5000 g in non-diabetic patients; EFW >4500 g in diabetic patients.

Diabetic macrosomia causes disproportionate shoulder and trunk growth (not head), increasing shoulder dystocia risk at lower birth weights than non-diabetic macrosomia. Ultrasound EFW has a ±10-20% error, so clinical judgment is required. Prevention focuses on glycemic control in diabetes and limiting excessive gestational weight gain.

**Q:** Define polyhydramnios and oligohydramnios by AFI and single deepest pocket (SDP) criteria.

**A:** Polyhydramnios: AFI >25 cm OR single deepest pocket >8 cm. Oligohydramnios: AFI <5 cm OR single deepest pocket <2 cm.

AFI (amniotic fluid index) sums the deepest fluid pockets in four uterine quadrants. The SDP method measures only the largest vertical pocket. Both methods are used; SDP may be preferred in multiple gestations. Amniotic fluid normally decreases after 35 weeks GA, and oligohydramnios is common in pregnancies >41 weeks (~12%).

**Q:** What are the causes of polyhydramnios?

**A:** Idiopathic (most common). Maternal: T1DM (abnormal transchorionic flow). Maternal-fetal: chorioangiomas, multiple gestation, fetal hydrops. Fetal: chromosomal anomaly (up to 2/3 of fetuses with severe polyhydramnios); CNS anomalies (anencephaly, hydrocephalus, meningocele); GI anomalies (TE fistula, duodenal atresia, facial clefts — interfere with swallowing); respiratory (cystic adenomatoid malformed lung); fetal demise/chronic hypoxemia.

Polyhydramnios arises from either overproduction or impaired reabsorption of amniotic fluid. GI obstruction prevents fetal swallowing (a key route of fluid reabsorption).
