CNS anomalies impair the swallowing reflex. Chromosomal anomalies are found in up to 2/3 of severe cases, making genetic workup essential.

**Q:** What are the causes of oligohydramnios?

**A:** Idiopathic (most common). Maternal: uteroplacental insufficiency (preeclampsia, nephropathy); medications (ACE inhibitors). Fetal: congenital urinary tract anomalies (renal agenesis/bilateral agenesis = Potter sequence, obstruction, posterior urethral valves); fetal demise/chronic hypoxemia (blood shunted away from kidneys to brain). IUGR. Ruptured membranes (prolonged amniotic fluid leak). Amniotic fluid normally decreases after 35 weeks.

Fetal urine is the primary source of amniotic fluid after 16 weeks. Renal agenesis (Potter sequence) → absent urine production → severe oligohydramnios → pulmonary hypoplasia + limb deformities. ACE inhibitors are teratogenic in T2/T3, causing renal tubular dysgenesis and oligohydramnios — a critical prescribing safety point.

**Q:** What are the obstetrical complications of polyhydramnios vs. oligohydramnios?

**A:** Polyhydramnios: cord prolapse, placental abruption, malpresentation, PTL, uterine dysfunction, PPH (uterine atony from overdistension); maternal pressure symptoms (dyspnea, edema, hydronephrosis). Oligohydramnios: cord compression; pulmonary hypoplasia (late onset); fetal anomalies (15-25%); amniotic fluid bands (T1) → Potter facies, limb deformities, abdominal wall defects; marker for infants who may not tolerate labour well.

Over-distension in polyhydramnios leads to uterine atony and PPH. The large fluid volume allows cord prolapse with membrane rupture. Oligohydramnios removes the cushioning effect around the cord, causing compression during labour contractions — a sign of fetal compromise on CTG (variable decelerations).

**Q:** How is severe polyhydramnios managed?

**A:** Determine underlying cause; screen for maternal disease/infection; complete fetal U/S evaluation. Mild-to-moderate: no treatment required. Severe: hospitalize and consider therapeutic amniocentesis (decompression). Prognosis: 2-5 fold increase in perinatal mortality risk.

Therapeutic amnioreduction relieves maternal pressure symptoms and may reduce preterm labour from uterine overdistension. Addressing the underlying cause (e.g., glycemic control in T1DM) is paramount. Chromosomal anomaly workup is important given the high association in severe cases.

**Q:** What is antenatal depression, its incidence, and first-line treatment?

**A:** Major depression occurring during pregnancy (onset may pre-date pregnancy). Incidence: 7-9% of pregnancies. Screening: 10-item Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (most widely used). First-line treatment for mild-to-moderate cases without prior MDD history: structured psychotherapy. Antidepressants + psychotherapy ± ECT for: severe features, psychosis, catatonia, high suicide risk, food/fluid refusal.

Antenatal depression is associated with adverse pregnancy outcomes (preterm birth, IUGR), abnormal infant development, and increased risk of postpartum depression and conversion to bipolar disorder. Screening at the first antenatal visit is recommended. SSRIs are generally considered safe in pregnancy when benefits outweigh risks.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for antenatal depression?

**A:** Prior history of depression or anxiety; unintended or unwanted pregnancy; life stress; intimate partner violence or history of abuse; poor social support; chronic medical conditions (especially hypothyroidism).

Hypothyroidism is specifically highlighted as a risk factor for antenatal depression — thyroid function testing (TSH) is warranted in at-risk patients. Screening with the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (also validated antenatally) should be done for all pregnant women at the first prenatal visit per SOGC guidelines.

**Q:** State the incidence and chorionicity features of twin pregnancies.

**A:** Twins: 1 in 80; triplets: 1 in 6400 in North America. 2/3 of twins are dizygotic (fraternal). Monozygous twinning occurs at a constant rate worldwide (1 in 250). Risk factors for dizygotic twins: IVF, increased maternal age, newly discontinued OCP, ethnicity. Chorionicity must be determined by U/S within T1 (ideally 8-12 weeks).

Chorionicity (number of placentas) is the most important prognostic factor in twin pregnancies. Monochorionic twins share a placenta and are at risk for twin-twin transfusion syndrome (TTTS). Chorionicity determination is most accurate in T1 (lambda sign = dichorionic; T-sign = monochorionic).

**Q:** What are the maternal complications of multiple gestation?

**A:** Hyperemesis gravidarum; gestational HTN; anemia; increased physiological stress on all systems; increased compressive symptoms; thrombosis; postpartum depression.

Multiple gestation doubles or triples the physiological demands of pregnancy. The higher circulating blood volume and uterine size increase preeclampsia risk 3-fold. Uterine overdistension predisposes to PTL, malpresentation, PPH from atony, and cord prolapse.

**Q:** What is twin-twin transfusion syndrome (TTTS), what is its incidence, and how is it treated?

**A:** TTTS: placental intertwin vascular anastomoses cause arterial blood from donor twin to pass into veins of recipient twin. Occurs in 10% of monochorionic twins; concern if >30% EFW discordance. Donor: IUGR, hypovolemia, hypotension, anemia, oligohydramnios. Recipient: hypervolemia, HTN, CHF, polycythemia, edema, polyhydramnios, kernicterus. Treatment: fetoscopic laser ablation of placental anastomoses (preferred 16-26 weeks GA); therapeutic serial amniocentesis; intrauterine blood transfusion to donor if necessary.

TTTS is the most serious complication specific to monochorionic twins. Without treatment, mortality is >80%. Fetoscopic laser coagulation (Nd:YAG laser) is the preferred treatment between 16-26 weeks as it addresses the underlying vascular connections, achieving survival in ~75% of cases.

**Q:** What are the types of breech presentation and their relative frequencies?

**A:** Frank breech (60%): hips flexed, knees extended — buttocks at cervix; most common type; most common to be delivered vaginally. Complete breech (10%): hips and knees both flexed. Incomplete/footling breech (30%): both or one hip partially flexed with feet or knees presenting first. Overall incidence: 3-4% at term (25% at <28 weeks GA).

Most fetuses are breech in early pregnancy and convert to vertex by 36 weeks. Frank breech is the most favourable for vaginal delivery as the presenting part is uniformly firm. Footling breech carries the highest risk of cord prolapse and is generally a contraindication to vaginal delivery.

**Q:** What are the criteria for vaginal breech delivery?

**A:** Complete or frank breech at ≥36 weeks GA; EFW 2500-3800 g (5.5-8.5 lb) based on clinical and U/S assessment; adequate pelvis; continuous fetal monitoring; two experienced obstetricians, assistant, and anesthetist present; ability to perform emergency CD within 30 minutes if required; mother motivated and understands risks and benefits.

The Term Breech Trial (2000) shifted most centres toward routine CD for breech. However, SOGC acknowledges that vaginal breech delivery remains an acceptable option in experienced centres when strict criteria are met. External cephalic version (ECV) at 36-37 weeks should be offered first to all eligible women.

**Q:** What is the Kleihauer-Betke test used for in obstetrics?

**A:** The Kleihauer-Betke test quantifies fetal cells in the maternal circulation (fetomaternal hemorrhage). It is used to: (1) determine the extent of fetomaternal transfusion and guide Rhogam dosing in Rh-negative women; (2) may help confirm placental abruption (not diagnostic); (3) part of the IUFD workup to detect large fetomaternal hemorrhage as a cause.

Fetal RBCs resist acid elution (due to HbF) and appear as bright pink cells on an acid-eluted smear, while adult RBCs appear as ghost cells. Flow cytometry is an alternative. The standard 300 mcg Rhogam dose covers 30 mL of fetal blood; larger hemorrhages require proportionally higher doses calculated from the Kleihauer-Betke result.

**Q:** What is the role of progesterone in preventing preterm labour, and what is the recommended dosing?

**A:** Progesterone maintains uterine quiescence (exact mechanism unclear). Indicated if cervical length <25 mm at <24 weeks GA: 200 mg vaginally once daily from diagnosis to 36 weeks. If risk factors or history of PTL: start vaginally at 16-20 weeks GA. Superior to cerclage for preventing PTL in singletons without cervical incompetence.

Vaginal progesterone is the first-line intervention for cervical shortening without frank incompetence. It reduces preterm birth before 34 weeks in women with short cervix by approximately 45%. The mechanism may involve reducing gap junction formation, inhibiting prostaglandin production, and blocking oxytocin receptor expression.

**Q:** Why should amoxicillin-clavulanic acid (Augmentin) NOT be used in PPROM?

**A:** Amoxicillin-clavulanic acid is contraindicated in PPROM because of an increased risk of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC) in neonates, demonstrated in the ORACLE I trial. Amoxicillin alone (without clavulanic acid) is safe.

The ORACLE I trial showed that while antibiotics in PPROM reduce chorioamnionitis and extend latency, co-amoxiclav was associated with a significantly higher rate of NEC in neonates. The clavulanate component is believed to disrupt the neonatal gut microbiome. This is a key prescribing safety point tested on the MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the mode of delivery recommendations for twin pregnancies based on chorionicity?

**A:** Dichorionic diamniotic or monochorionic diamniotic twins: may attempt vaginal delivery if twin A is vertex and growth discordance <25% (otherwise CD). 40-50% of twin deliveries are by CD; 10% of cases have twin A vaginally and twin B by CD. Monochorionic monoamniotic (MCMA) twins: all must be delivered by CD.

MCMA twins have a single amniotic cavity, creating risk of cord entanglement and interlocking — making CD mandatory. For other twin types, vaginal delivery is feasible when twin A presents cephalic. If twin B becomes non-cephalic after delivery of twin A, internal podalic version and breech extraction are options.

**Q:** What is Sheehan syndrome and which obstetric conditions cause it?

**A:** Sheehan syndrome (pituitary necrosis): ischemic necrosis of the anterior pituitary gland due to severe postpartum hemorrhage and hypovolemic shock. The enlarged pituitary gland of pregnancy is particularly susceptible to ischemia. Associated obstetric conditions: placenta previa, placental abruption, and any cause of massive obstetric hemorrhage.

The pituitary gland enlarges during pregnancy due to lactotroph hyperplasia, making it vulnerable to ischemia during severe hypotension. Clinical features of Sheehan syndrome include failure of lactation (first sign — loss of prolactin), amenorrhea, hypothyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, and diabetes insipidus — presenting months to years later.

**Q:** A 28-year-old G2P1 at 30 weeks presents with painless bright red vaginal bleeding. She had a prior uterine surgery. What is the most likely diagnosis and what complication must be specifically anticipated?

**A:** Most likely diagnosis: placenta previa (painless bleeding is the hallmark). With prior uterine surgery and anterior placenta previa, the most important anticipated complication is placenta accreta spectrum (morbidly adherent placenta), which may require hysterectomy. Transvaginal U/S is the investigation of choice. Rhogam if Rh-negative.

Painless antepartum bleeding in T3 is placenta previa until proven otherwise. Prior uterine incision creates a decidua basalis defect that the placenta invades (accreta), penetrates (increta), or perforates through (percreta). This risk increases with each additional uterine surgery. Planning in a centre with interventional radiology and blood bank support is critical.

**Q:** A 32-week pregnant woman presents with sudden onset severe abdominal pain, uterine rigidity, and fetal bradycardia. Her blood pressure is 80/50 mmHg with minimal vaginal bleeding. What is the diagnosis and what blood product should be prepared?

**A:** Diagnosis: concealed placental abruption (internal/occult abruption — shock disproportionate to visible blood loss, uterine rigidity, fetal distress). Blood products to prepare: red blood cells, platelets, cryoprecipitate (for DIC) — DIC occurs in 20% of abruptions, and abruption is the most common cause of DIC in pregnancy.

The concealed nature explains why shock is disproportionate to visible bleeding. The uterus is hypertonic because the retroplacental hematoma causes sustained tension. Fibrinogen is the first clotting factor to fall in obstetric DIC — cryoprecipitate is the most efficient fibrinogen replacement. Emergency CD is indicated given fetal distress.

**Q:** What is the definition of an episiotomy and what are the two main types?

**A:** An episiotomy is an incision in the perineal body at the time of delivery — essentially a controlled 2nd degree laceration. Types: (1) Midline: incision through central tendinous portion of perineal body — heals better but increases risk of extension to 3rd/4th degree tear. (2) Mediolateral: incision at 60° from midline through bulbocavernosus, superficial transverse perineal, and levator ani muscles — reduces risk of extensive tear but more painful.

Midline episiotomies are easier to repair and heal better but carry higher risk of sphincter injury (3rd/4th degree tears). Mediolateral (preferred in many Canadian centres) reduces severe perineal trauma. Current evidence (restricted use) favours allowing perineum to tear naturally and repair as needed rather than routine episiotomy.

**Q:** What does current evidence recommend regarding episiotomy use during vaginal delivery?

**A:** Current evidence supports RESTRICTED use of episiotomy — it is preferable to let the perineum tear naturally and repair as needed rather than routine episiotomy. Episiotomy should be reserved for specific indications such as unyielding perineum obstructing delivery, expediting delivery (abnormal FHR), or instrumental delivery.

Cochrane reviews and SOGC guidelines support selective/restricted use. Routine episiotomy does not prevent severe perineal trauma and increases morbidity. Mediolateral episiotomy is preferred when an episiotomy is needed to reduce sphincter injury risk.

**Q:** What are the complications of episiotomy?

**A:** Infection, hematoma, pain, extension into anal musculature or rectal mucosa (3rd/4th degree tear), fistula formation, and incontinence (fecal or urinary).

Extension into anal sphincter (3rd degree) or rectal mucosa (4th degree) is the most serious complication and is more common with midline episiotomy. Fistula formation between rectum and vagina is rare but devastating.

**Q:** What is the overall rate of Caesarean delivery (CD) in Canada and what are the main indications?

**A:** Overall CD rate in Canada is approximately 28% (range 18.5–35.3% by province/territory). Indications: Maternal — obstruction (fibroids), active herpetic vulvar lesion, invasive cervical cancer, previous uterine surgery (most common), eclampsia, HELLP, heart disease. Maternal-fetal — failure to progress, placental abruption/previa, vasa previa. Fetal — abnormal FHR, malpresentation, cord prolapse, certain congenital anomalies, some multiple gestations.

Previous CD is the most common indication for repeat CD. Active genital herpes at time of labour is a maternal indication because of risk of neonatal herpes transmission. Canada's CD rate has risen substantially over recent decades.

**Q:** What are the two main skin incision types for Caesarean delivery and their advantages/disadvantages?

**A:** 1) Transverse (e.g., Pfannenstiel or Joel-Cohen): decreased abdominal exposure but improved strength, cosmesis, and reduced pain. 2) Vertical midline: rapid peritoneal entry and increased exposure (e.g., large fibroids, emergency), but increased risk of dehiscence.

Pfannenstiel is the standard for elective and most emergency CDs. Vertical midline is reserved for obese patients, massive fibroids, or situations requiring rapid access (e.g., maternal cardiac arrest). Joel-Cohen (higher, more transverse) is associated with less blood loss and faster recovery than Pfannenstiel.

**Q:** What are the three types of uterine incisions for Caesarean delivery and when is each used?

**A:** 1) Low transverse (most common): in non-contractile lower uterine segment — decreased chance of rupture in subsequent pregnancies. 2) Low vertical: for very preterm infants or poorly developed lower uterine segment. 3) Classical (rare): in thick, contractile upper segment — used for transverse lie with fetal back down, preterm breech, fetal anomaly, >2 fetuses, lower segment adhesions, obstructing fibroid, morbid obesity.

Low transverse incision is standard as it heals well and permits TOLAC. Classical incision carries ~6% rupture risk in subsequent pregnancies (vs. 0.47% for low transverse) and is a contraindication to TOLAC. Low vertical may extend upward into the contractile segment.

**Q:** What prophylactic antibiotic should be given for Caesarean delivery and what is the dosing?

**A:** Cefazolin: 2 g IV if <120 kg, 3 g IV if ≥120 kg. Should be administered as a single dose approximately 30–60 minutes prior to skin incision.

Prophylactic antibiotics significantly reduce the risk of post-CD endometritis and wound infection. Weight-based dosing ensures adequate tissue concentrations in obese patients. This is a SOGC-endorsed practice aligned with ACOG guidelines.

**Q:** What are the risks/complications of Caesarean delivery?

**A:** Anesthetic complications (aspiration), hemorrhage (~1000 mL blood loss), infection (UTI, wound, endometritis), injury to surrounding structures (bowel, bladder, ureter, uterus), thromboembolism (DVT, PE), increased recovery time/hospital stay, maternal mortality (<0.1%), subsequent placenta accreta spectrum.

Placenta accreta spectrum is an increasingly important long-term complication of prior CD — risk increases with each subsequent CD. Compared to vaginal delivery, CD carries higher short-term maternal morbidity but may be necessary for maternal or fetal indications.

**Q:** What is TOLAC and who should be offered it? What is the success rate?

**A:** Trial of Labour after Caesarean (TOLAC) should be recommended to patients with no contraindications after a previous low transverse uterine incision. Success rate varies with indication for previous CD, generally 60–80%. Risk of uterine rupture is 0.47% with prior low transverse incision; increased by inter-delivery interval <18 months and oxytocin use.

SOGC and ACOG recommend offering TOLAC as the relative risk of maternal death is actually higher with elective repeat CD than TOLAC, though absolute risks are low for both. Successful TOLAC (VBAC) avoids the morbidity of repeat surgery.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to TOLAC (Trial of Labour after Caesarean)?

**A:** 1) Previous classical, inverted-T, or unknown uterine incision, or complete transection of uterus (6% rupture risk). 2) Any contraindication to vaginal birth (e.g., placenta previa). 3) Inadequate facilities or personnel for emergency CD.

The type of previous uterine incision is the key determinant of TOLAC eligibility. Classical incision carries ~6% rupture risk making TOLAC unsafe. Unknown scar type is treated as classical. Facilities must be capable of performing emergency CD within 30 minutes (the 'decision to incision' standard).

**Q:** What is the definition of postpartum hemorrhage (PPH)?

**A:** Loss of >1000 mL of blood after CD, >500 mL after vaginal delivery, OR bleeding associated with signs/symptoms of hypovolemia within 24 hours of the birthing process regardless of mode of delivery. Primary PPH: within first 24 h. Secondary PPH: after 24 h but within first 12 weeks postpartum.

The updated definition aligns with SOGC Guideline No. 431 (2022). Clinical signs of hypovolemia are used when blood loss estimation is difficult. Incidence is 5–15%. PPH remains a leading cause of maternal mortality globally.

**Q:** What are the 4 Ts of postpartum hemorrhage etiology?

**A:** 1) Tone (uterine atony) — most common cause (>80%): overdistended uterus, muscle exhaustion, distortion, infection, or bladder distension. 2) Tissue — retained placental products, blood clots, GTN, abnormal placentation (accreta). 3) Trauma — laceration, episiotomy, hematoma, uterine rupture, inversion. 4) Thrombin — coagulopathy (hemophilia, DIC, ITP, TTP, VWD, therapeutic anticoagulation).

Uterine atony accounts for >80% of PPH. Active management of the 3rd stage (oxytocin + uterine massage + cord traction) prevents atony. Late PPH (>24h) is most commonly due to retained products of conception or endometritis.

**Q:** What is active management of the third stage of labour and why is it important?

**A:** Active management involves three components: 1) Oxytocin administration (10 IU IM preferred for low-risk vaginal deliveries). 2) Uterine massage. 3) Umbilical cord traction for placental delivery. This approach prevents uterine atony — the most common cause of PPH (>80%).

SOGC Clinical Practice Guideline No. 235 supports active management of the third stage. Oxytocin is the uterotonic of choice. Studies show active management reduces PPH incidence compared to expectant management.

**Q:** What is the initial management of postpartum hemorrhage?

**A:** ABCs, call for help. 2 large bore IVs, run crystalloids wide open. Labs: CBC, coagulation profile, fibrinogen, cross and type packed RBCs. Foley catheter to empty bladder and monitor urine output. Treat underlying cause (based on 4 Ts).

Rapid resuscitation and identifying the cause are simultaneous priorities. Red-top tube clot test: no clot in 7–10 minutes indicates coagulopathy. Massive transfusion protocol should be activated for severe hemorrhage with a 1:1:1 ratio of RBCs:FFP:platelets.

**Q:** What are the medical therapies for uterine atony causing PPH?

**A:** 1) Oxytocin 10 IU IM (preferred low-risk vaginal) or IV infusion 20–40 IU in 1000 mL at 150 mL/h. 2) Carbetocin 100 µg IV bolus over 1 min for elective CD or 100 µg IM for vaginal delivery with ≥1 PPH risk factor. 3) Methylergonovine (Ergotamine®) 0.25 mg IM/slow IV q2h (contraindicated in HTN). 4) Carboprost (Hemabate®) 250 µg IM/IMM q15 min, max 2 mg (contraindicated in asthma, CV, renal, hepatic disease). 5) Misoprostol 600–800 µg PO/SL or PR/PV. 6) Tranexamic acid 1 g IV.

Carbetocin is a long-acting oxytocin analogue used as a single dose for elective CD, avoiding need for continuous infusion. Methylergonovine causes vasoconstriction — contraindicated in hypertension. Carboprost (PGF2α) is highly effective but has major side effects. Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic that reduces mortality from PPH.

**Q:** What local/mechanical control measures are used for uterine atony in PPH?

**A:** 1) Bimanual uterine massage (elevate and compress uterus through abdomen). 2) Uterine packing (mesh with antibiotic treatment). 3) Bakri Balloon tamponade — slows hemorrhage, allows time for coagulopathy correction or OR preparation. 4) Manual removal if retained placenta.

Bakri balloon is particularly useful as a temporizing measure. The intrauterine balloon is inflated with 250–500 mL saline. If balloon + medical therapy fails, surgical intervention is required.

**Q:** What surgical options are available for intractable PPH due to uterine atony?

**A:** 1) Embolization of uterine artery or internal iliac artery (interventional radiology). 2) Laparotomy with bilateral uterine artery ligation or internal iliac artery ligation + compression sutures (B-Lynch or Cho sutures). 3) Hysterectomy (last resort) — with angiographic embolization if post-hysterectomy bleeding.

B-Lynch suture compresses the uterus longitudinally to reduce blood flow; fertility-preserving. Hysterectomy is definitive but means permanent loss of fertility. Uterine artery embolization is minimally invasive and preserves uterus but requires IR capability.

**Q:** What is retained placenta and how is it defined and managed?

**A:** Definition: Placenta undelivered after 30 minutes postpartum. Causes: trapped/incarcerated placenta (behind partially closed cervix), placenta adherens (adherent to wall), or abnormal implantation (accreta/increta/percreta). Management: 2 large bore IVs, type & screen; Brandt maneuver (cord traction + suprapubic counterpressure); oxytocin 10 IU in 20 mL NS into umbilical vein; manual removal if above fails; D&C (U/S guided); cefazolin 2 g IV if manual removal or D&C.

The Brandt maneuver prevents uterine inversion by supporting the uterus at the suprapubic level while applying cord traction. Placenta accreta spectrum may require hysterectomy. Risk factors include placenta previa, prior CD, prior curettage.

**Q:** What is uterine inversion and how is it managed?

**A:** Definition: Inversion of the uterus through the cervix ± vaginal introitus. Often iatrogenic (excessive cord traction with fundal placenta). Epidemiology: 1 in 1500–2000 deliveries; more common in grand multiparous women. Features: profound vasovagal response — bradycardia, vasodilation, hypovolemic shock disproportionate to blood loss. Management: urgent — call anesthesia, ABCs + IV crystalloids; tocolytic or nitroglycerin IV to relax uterus; replace uterus WITHOUT removing placenta first; then manually remove placenta and withdraw slowly; oxytocin IV ONLY after uterus replaced; re-explore uterus; may require GA + laparotomy.

The key teaching point is to NOT remove the placenta before replacing the uterus — doing so increases blood loss and makes replacement more difficult. Oxytocin before replacement would contract cervix and trap the inverted uterus. Nitroglycerin is the fastest-acting uterine relaxant.

**Q:** What is the definition of postpartum fever?

**A:** Fever >38°C on any two of the first 10 days postpartum, EXCEPT the first day (day 1 excluded due to normal physiologic response to delivery).

This is a clinical definition used to trigger investigation and treatment of puerperal infections. The exclusion of day 1 avoids over-diagnosing fever from the stress of delivery itself.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic for causes of postpartum fever and list the causes?

**A:** Mnemonic: The '5 Ws' or 'B-5W': Breast (engorgement, mastitis), Wound (episiotomy, CD site infection), Walking (DVT, thrombophlebitis), Womb (endometritis), Wind (atelectasis, pneumonia) + UTI. Key causes: endometritis (most common), wound infection, mastitis/breast engorgement, UTI, atelectasis/pneumonia, DVT/pelvic thrombophlebitis.

Endometritis is the most common cause of significant postpartum fever. Timing helps distinguish causes: breast engorgement (day 3–4), wound infection (day 5+), endometritis (day 2–5). Pelvic thrombophlebitis should be considered if fever persists despite adequate antibiotic therapy.

**Q:** How is postpartum endometritis treated?

**A:** Treatment depends on severity: oral antibiotics if well; IV antibiotics with hospitalization for moderate-to-severe cases (ensure no retained products of conception). Empiric IV: clindamycin + gentamicin/tobramycin. Risk factors: CD, intrapartum chorioamnionitis, prolonged labour, prolonged ROM, multiple vaginal exams. Prophylaxis: cefazolin 2–4 g IV (based on BMI) 30 min prior to skin incision for CD.

Clindamycin + gentamicin covers the polymicrobial flora of postpartum endometritis (including anaerobes and GBS). Failure to improve on this regimen should prompt consideration of pelvic abscess or resistant organisms. Ruling out RPOC (retained products) is critical.

**Q:** Compare lactational vs. non-lactational mastitis.

**A:** Lactational: more common; often 2–3 wk postpartum; caused by S. aureus; unilateral localized pain, tenderness, erythema; treatment = heat/ice packs + continue nursing/pumping + cloxacillin/cephalexin; abscess requires I&D + IV antibiotics (nafcillin/oxacillin). Non-lactational (periductal mastitis): most common type in non-lactating; mean age 32 yr; may be sterile or S. aureus/anaerobes; smoking is risk factor; subareolar pain, possible subareolar mass, nipple inversion, variable discharge; treatment = broad-spectrum antibiotics + I&D; total duct excision is definitive; may develop mammary duct fistula.

Periductal mastitis is associated with smoking and mammary duct ectasia. It tends to recur and may require total duct excision. Must rule out inflammatory breast carcinoma. Non-lactational abscess tends to recur more than lactational abscess.

**Q:** What is the antibiotic of choice for lactational mastitis and what if penicillin-allergic?

**A:** First-line: cloxacillin OR cephalexin. If penicillin-allergic: erythromycin. For abscess requiring hospitalization: nafcillin or oxacillin IV + incision and drainage (I&D).

S. aureus (including occasionally MRSA) is the predominant pathogen in lactational mastitis. Cloxacillin/cephalexin cover penicillinase-producing S. aureus. Continued breastfeeding or pumping from the affected breast is recommended to prevent engorgement and worsening.

**Q:** Define postpartum blues, postpartum depression, and postpartum psychosis and distinguish them.

**A:** Blues: onset day 3–10 postpartum; 40–80% of mothers; self-limited — resolves within 2 weeks; mood lability, tearfulness, anxiety, irritability, poor concentration — normal hormonal adjustment. PPD: major depression within 6 months of childbirth; 10–15%; risk of recurrence 50%; suspect if blues last >2 wk or severe symptoms (disinterest in baby, suicidal/infanticidal ideation); treat with antidepressants, psychotherapy, ECT if refractory. Postpartum Psychosis: acute psychosis within first 2 weeks (can last months); rare (0.2%); 50% recurrence in next pregnancy; psychiatric emergency — risk of infanticide; treat with hospitalization, mood stabilizer, antipsychotics.

Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) is the validated screening tool for PPD in Canada. Bipolar disorder and prior psychotic illness are major risk factors for postpartum psychosis. Postpartum psychosis is a psychiatric emergency requiring immediate hospitalization.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for postpartum depression (PPD)?

**A:** Personal or family history of depression (including PPD), prenatal depression or anxiety, stressful life situation, poor support system, unwanted pregnancy, colicky or sick infant. Recurrence risk is 50%.

PPD affects 10–15% of new mothers and is likely underdiagnosed. Screening with the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) is recommended routinely in Canada. PPD interferes with mother-infant bonding and may have long-term effects on child development.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and treatment of postpartum psychosis?

**A:** Epidemiology: rare (0.2%), but 50% recurrence risk in next pregnancy. Increased risk with bipolar disorder, schizoaffective disorder, schizophrenia, other psychotic illness, or personal/family history of postpartum psychosis. Treatment: psychiatric emergency (risk of infanticide) — typically requires hospitalization, mood stabilizer, and antipsychotics.

Postpartum psychosis usually presents within the first 2 weeks postpartum with rapid onset of psychotic symptoms — hallucinations, delusions, disorganized thinking. The risk of harm to self or infant necessitates immediate psychiatric assessment and usually inpatient care.

**Q:** What are the 10 Bs of postpartum office visit care?

**A:** 1) Be careful (no douches/tampons 4–6 wk). 2) Be fit (walking, Kegel exercises). 3) Birth control assessment. 4) Breastfeeding (not reliable contraception alone). 5) Bladder (urinary incontinence). 6) Blood pressure (especially if gestational HTN). 7) Blood tests (CBC, TSH, 75g OGTT if GDM). 8) Blues (postpartum mood assessment). 9) Bowel (fluids, fibre, laxatives; hemorrhoid treatment). 10) Breast and pelvic exam (+ Pap smear if due).

The 6-week postpartum visit is a comprehensive assessment. Blood tests are targeted: CBC if PPH occurred, TSH if subclinical hypothyroidism in pregnancy, OGTT if gestational diabetes. Lactational amenorrhea is WHO-approved contraception for up to 6 months if: amenorrheic + fully/nearly fully breastfeeding (no interval >4–6h) + <6 months postpartum.

**Q:** What are the criteria for lactational amenorrhea as contraception and how long is it approved?

**A:** WHO-approved for up to 6 months postpartum if ALL three criteria met: 1) Amenorrhea (no menstrual periods). 2) Fully or nearly fully breastfeeding (no interval of >4–6 hours between breastfeeds). 3) Less than 6 months postpartum.

Lactational amenorrhea method (LAM) suppresses ovulation via prolactin-mediated GnRH suppression. Failure of any one criterion significantly reduces efficacy. Non-lactating patients resume ovulation ~45 days after birth; lactating patients ovulate later but timing is unpredictable.

**Q:** Describe the normal physiological changes of lochia postpartum.

**A:** Lochia is normal vaginal discharge postpartum due to uterine decidual tissue sloughing. Sequence: Lochia rubra (red, days 1–3, erythrocytes) → Lochia serosa (pale) → Lochia alba (white/yellow, residual leukorrhea) over 3–6 weeks. Foul-smelling lochia suggests endometritis.

Lochia progression reflects normal uterine involution. Abnormal findings include excessive bleeding (PPH), foul odour (infection), or resumption of red lochia after transition to serosa/alba (retained products or infection). The uterus should involute approximately 1 cm below the umbilicus per day in the first 4–5 days.

**Q:** What are the postpartum physiological changes in the uterus and when does ovulation resume?

**A:** Uterine weight diminishes rapidly through catabolism; cervix loses elasticity and regains firmness. Uterus involutes ~1 cm below umbilicus per day in first 4–5 days; reaches non-pregnant state in 4–6 weeks postpartum. Ovulation resumes ~45 days after birth; non-lactating patients usually ovulate sooner than lactating patients.

Uterine involution is facilitated by breastfeeding (oxytocin release). Subinvolution (failure to involute) may indicate endometritis or retained products. Return of ovulation before first menses means contraception is needed before the 6-week visit if pregnancy is not desired.

**Q:** What is the management of postpartum bladder dysfunction?

**A:** Pelvic floor prolapse and stress/urge urinary incontinence are common after vaginal delivery. Risk increased with instrumental delivery or prolonged second stage. Conservative management: pelvic floor retraining with Kegel exercises/pelvic physiotherapy, vaginal cones or pessaries, lifestyle modifications (limit fluid/caffeine), and local vaginal estrogen in breastfeeding patients to strengthen vaginal mucosa.

Urinary incontinence postpartum is often underreported. Pelvic floor physiotherapy is first-line per SOGC guidelines. Surgical options (mid-urethral slings) are deferred until childbearing is complete. Stress incontinence is most common after vaginal delivery.

**Q:** Which drugs are safe during breastfeeding and which are contraindicated?

**A:** SAFE: Analgesics (acetaminophen, NSAIDs), anticoagulants (heparin), antidepressants (sertraline, fluoxetine, TCAs), antiepileptics (phenytoin, carbamazepine, valproic acid), antihistamines, antimicrobials (penicillins, aminoglycosides, cephalosporins), β-adrenergics (propranolol, labetalol), insulin, steroids, low-dose OCP (though estrogen may decrease milk production). CONTRAINDICATED: Chloramphenicol (bone marrow suppression), cyclophosphamide (immune suppression), sulfonamides (hemolysis in G6PD deficiency), nitrofurantoin (hemolysis in G6PD deficiency), tetracycline, lithium, phenindione, bromocriptine, antineoplastics/immunosuppressants, psychotropic drugs (relative CI).

This is high-yield for MCCQE1. Key points: warfarin is safe (does not cross into breast milk in significant amounts) — heparin listed as safe; lithium crosses into milk causing toxicity; tetracycline causes staining of infant's developing teeth; sulfonamides and nitrofurantoin cause hemolysis only if infant has G6PD deficiency.

**Q:** What are the dosing and indications for betamethasone and dexamethasone for fetal lung maturity?

**A:** Betamethasone valerate (Celestone®): 12 mg IM q24h × 2 doses. Dexamethasone: 6 mg IM q12h × 4 doses. Both used for enhancement of fetal pulmonary maturity in preterm labour (PTL).

Antenatal corticosteroids are indicated between 24–34 weeks (and up to 36+6 in some guidelines) when preterm birth is anticipated within 7 days. They reduce RDS, IVH, NEC, and neonatal mortality. Betamethasone is preferred over dexamethasone in most centres (fewer doses, evidence of lower IVH risk).

**Q:** What are the dosing and indications for Rh immunoglobulin (RhIgG/RhoGAM®)?

**A:** Dose: 300 µg IM × 1 (50 µg IM for first trimester events). Indications: Rh-negative women — routinely at 28 weeks GA; within 72 hours of birth of Rh+ fetus; positive Kleihauer-Betke test; any invasive procedure in pregnancy; ectopic pregnancy; antepartum hemorrhage; first trimester bleeding; miscarriage or therapeutic abortion (50 µg IM dose).

RhIgG prevents sensitization of Rh-negative mothers by Rh-positive fetal red cells. If sensitization occurs (positive anti-D antibody), subsequent pregnancies are at risk for hemolytic disease of the fetus and newborn. Kleihauer-Betke test quantifies feto-maternal hemorrhage to determine if additional RhIgG is needed.

**Q:** What is the dosing of oxytocin for induction/augmentation of labour and for PPH prevention/treatment?

**A:** Induction/augmentation: 0.5–2.0 mU/min IV, increase by 1–2 mU/min q20–60 min. PPH prevention (3rd stage): 10 IU IM at delivery of anterior shoulder (or after placenta). PPH treatment: 20 IU/L normal saline or Ringer's lactate IV continuous infusion.

Oxytocin is the most important uterotonic. Low-dose protocols reduce hyperstimulation risk during induction. For elective CD, the bolus regimen (5–10 IU slow IV) is used. Carbetocin (single bolus) may be preferred for elective CD to avoid need for continuous infusion.

**Q:** What is the dosing and indication for carbetocin?

**A:** Carbetocin (long-acting oxytocin): 100 µg IV bolus over 1 minute for elective CD, OR 100 µg IM for vaginal deliveries with ≥1 risk factor for PPH (instead of continuous oxytocin infusion).

Carbetocin has a longer half-life than oxytocin, allowing a single-dose administration instead of continuous infusion. It is particularly useful in elective CD where continuous IV oxytocin infusion would otherwise be needed. Not used for induction of labour.

**Q:** What is the dosing and contraindications of carboprost (Hemabate®) for PPH?

**A:** Carboprost (synthetic PGF2α analog): 250 µg IM/intramyometrial (IMM) q15 min to maximum 2 mg total. Contraindicated in cardiovascular disease, pulmonary disease (especially asthma), renal dysfunction, and hepatic dysfunction. Major prostaglandin side effects (diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, fever, bronchospasm).

PGF2α causes uterine contraction and vasoconstriction. Bronchospasm is the key contraindication in asthma patients. Despite side effects, it is highly effective for atony unresponsive to oxytocin. The maximum dose of 2 mg (8 doses) limits its use.

**Q:** What are the dosing and indications for misoprostol (Cytotec®) in obstetrics?

**A:** PPH treatment: 600–1000 µg PR × 1 dose. Medical abortion/retained products: 400 µg PO/SL × 1 dose or 800 µg PV × 1 dose (3–7 days after methotrexate). Note: misoprostol 600–800 µg PO/SL or PR/PV — side effect of pyrexia if >600 µg.

Misoprostol is a PGE1 analogue, orally active, inexpensive, and stable at room temperature — key advantages in resource-limited settings. Fever is common with doses >600 µg. Sublingual route has faster absorption than oral. It can be used for PPH when IV access is unavailable.

**Q:** What are the dosing and indications for methylergonovine (Ergotamine®) and its key contraindication?

**A:** Methylergonovine maleate: 0.25 mg IM or slow IV q2h, up to maximum 1.25 mg; or IV bolus 0.125 mg. Indication: treatment of uterine atony (PPH). Key contraindication: HYPERTENSION (causes vasoconstriction and severe HTN).

Methylergonovine acts on smooth muscle causing sustained uterine contraction and vasoconstriction. It must never be given IV rapidly (causes acute HTN and stroke). Contraindicated in preeclampsia, gestational HTN, or any hypertensive state.

**Q:** What is the TOLAC success rate and what factors increase failure?

**A:** General TOLAC success rate (achieving VBAC): 60–80%. Factors affecting success: the indication for previous CD influences success rate (e.g., non-recurring indication like malpresentation has higher success than prior CPD). Factors increasing failure/rupture risk: inter-delivery interval <18 months, oxytocin use during TOLAC.

Patients with a non-recurring indication (e.g., breech presentation) have the highest VBAC success rates (~85–90%). Those with prior CPD or failure to progress have lower rates (~50–60%). Oxytocin augmentation during TOLAC increases rupture risk but is not absolutely contraindicated.

**Q:** What is the Meis Trial (NEJM 2003) and its significance for preterm labour?

**A:** The Meis Trial (NEJM 2003) studied 17α-hydroxyprogesterone caproate (17P) for prevention of recurrent preterm birth. Women with prior spontaneous preterm delivery received weekly 250 mg IM 17P or placebo until delivery or 36 weeks GA. Results: 17P significantly reduced delivery at <37 wk (36.3% vs. 54.9%), <35 wk, and <32 wk. Infants had lower rates of enterocolitis, IVH, and need for supplemental oxygen. Conclusion: 17P substantially reduces recurrent preterm delivery.

This landmark trial established 17P as a standard preventive therapy for women with prior spontaneous preterm birth. It is administered weekly from 16–36 weeks gestation. Note: subsequent meta-analyses and the PROLONG trial (2020) have questioned its efficacy, leading to re-evaluation of its use.

**Q:** What did the Twin Birth Study (NEJM 2013) conclude about planned CD vs. vaginal delivery in twins?

**A:** The Twin Birth Study (NEJM 2013) randomized women at 32–38+6 weeks GA with twin pregnancy and first twin in cephalic position to planned CD vs. planned vaginal delivery. Results: NO significant difference in composite adverse perinatal outcomes between groups (2.2% vs. 1.9%; p=0.49). Conclusion: No benefit from planned CD over planned vaginal delivery of twins at 32–38 weeks if first twin is cephalic.

This trial changed practice — previously many centres routinely performed CD for twins. The results support vaginal delivery as safe when the leading twin is cephalic at 32–38 weeks. The second twin's presentation does not need to be cephalic, as internal podalic version or breech extraction can be performed.

**Q:** What did the TermPROM Trial (NEJM 1992) find regarding management of PROM at term?

**A:** TermPROM Trial compared immediate induction (oxytocin or PGE2) vs. expectant management in 5041 women with PROM at ≥37 weeks GA. Results: Neonatal infection and CD rates were similar across all groups (2–3% and 9.6–10.9%). Chorioamnionitis rate was lower with oxytocin induction than expectant management (4.0% vs. 8.6%, p<0.001), as was postpartum fever (1.9% vs. 3.6%). Conclusion: IV oxytocin induction results in lower maternal infection risk; similar neonatal infection and CD rates as expectant management.

This trial supports active management (induction) rather than watchful waiting for PROM at term. Delayed delivery after PROM increases infection risk. Current Canadian practice recommends induction at term PROM — typically with IV oxytocin, especially if GBS positive.

**Q:** What did the Canadian Multicenter Post-term Pregnancy Trial (NEJM 1996) find?

**A:** Randomized 3407 women with uncomplicated post-term pregnancies (≥41 weeks) to induction of labour vs. serial antenatal monitoring. Results: Induction had LOWER CD rate than serial monitoring (21.2% vs. 24.5%, p=0.03), with fewer CDs due to fetal distress (5.7% vs. 8.3%). Perinatal and neonatal mortality rates were similar. Conclusion: In post-term pregnancy, induction results in lower CD rate than serial antenatal monitoring.

This landmark Canadian trial countered the belief that induction of labour increases CD rates in post-term pregnancies. Current SOGC guidelines recommend induction at 41+0 to 42+0 weeks. Serial monitoring is an acceptable alternative but carries higher CD rates due to fetal distress.

**Q:** What are the dosing schedules and indications for penicillin and cefazolin for GBS prophylaxis?

**A:** Penicillin (preferred): 5 million IU IV loading dose, then 2.5 million IU IV q4h until delivery. Cefazolin (for penicillin-allergic, not at risk for anaphylaxis): 2 g IV loading dose, then 1 g IV q8h until delivery. Indication: GBS prophylaxis during labour.

Group B Streptococcus (GBS) prophylaxis is recommended for GBS-positive women, those with unknown GBS status and risk factors, or prior GBS-affected infant. Penicillin is preferred due to narrow spectrum. For patients at high risk of anaphylaxis to penicillin, clindamycin or vancomycin is used based on sensitivity testing.

**Q:** What are the indications and dosing of folic acid supplementation in pregnancy?

**A:** Standard dose: 0.4–1 mg PO daily — start 1–3 months preconception and continue through first trimester. Indication: Prevention of neural tube defects (NTD). High-risk dose: 4 mg PO daily — for women with previous NTD or at high risk (anti-epileptics, diabetes, obesity, family history of NTD).

Folate reduces the risk of spina bifida and anencephaly by ~70%. The higher dose (4 mg) is required when there is a prior NTD-affected pregnancy. SOGC recommends universal preconception folic acid supplementation. Food fortification in Canada (since 1998) has reduced NTD rates significantly.

**Q:** What is the dosing of erythromycin for preterm premature rupture of membranes (PPROM) and what is its role?

**A:** Erythromycin: 250 mg PO q6h × 10 days. Indication: To prolong pregnancy and decrease maternal and neonatal morbidity in patients NOT in labour with PPROM.

The ORACLE trial established erythromycin as the antibiotic of choice for PPROM not in labour. It reduces chorioamnionitis, prolongs latency, and reduces neonatal complications. Amoxicillin-clavulanate (co-amoxiclav) should be avoided in PPROM due to increased risk of neonatal necrotizing enterocolitis.

**Q:** What is doxylamine succinate (Diclectin®) and how is it used for nausea and vomiting in pregnancy?

**A:** Doxylamine succinate (Diclectin®): each tablet contains 10 mg doxylamine succinate + vitamin B6 (pyridoxine). Dosing: 2 tablets at hs + 1 tablet in AM + 1 tablet in PM (max 8 tablets/day). First-line treatment for nausea and vomiting in pregnancy, including hyperemesis gravidarum.

Diclectin® is the only Health Canada-approved medication specifically for NVP. Delayed-release formulation targets nighttime and early morning dosing. If Diclectin® is insufficient, antihistamines, metoclopramide, ondansetron, and corticosteroids can be added stepwise.

**Q:** What is dinoprostone (Cervidil®) and how is it used?

**A:** Dinoprostone (Cervidil®): PGE2-impregnated vaginal thread, 10 mg PV (remove after 12 hours), maximum 3 doses. Indication: Induction of labour (cervical ripening). Key advantage: Can be removed if uterine tachysystole (hyperstimulation) occurs.

Cervidil is the controlled-release PGE2 insert used for cervical ripening. Its removability is a significant advantage over PGE2 gel (Prostin® gel) in the event of hyperstimulation. PGE2 gel: 1 mg or 2 mg PV q6–12h, max 3 doses.

**Q:** A 28-year-old G2P1 presents with heavy vaginal bleeding 15 minutes after vaginal delivery of a 4.2 kg infant. The uterus is soft and boggy. What is the most likely diagnosis and initial management?

**A:** Uterine atony (most common cause of PPH, >80%). Management: ABCs, call for help; 2 large-bore IVs + crystalloids; bimanual uterine massage; empty bladder (Foley); labs (CBC, coagulation, fibrinogen, type & crossmatch); uterotonics in order — oxytocin IV infusion, then methylergonovine (if normotensive), then carboprost (if no asthma/CV/hepatic/renal disease), then misoprostol PR/SL; Bakri balloon if refractory; surgery if all else fails.

Macrosomia (4.2 kg) is a risk factor for atony due to overdistension. Uterine massage and oxytocin should be initiated immediately. Rapid escalation to surgical management (B-Lynch suture, uterine artery embolization, hysterectomy) is required if medical therapy fails. Active management of third stage prevents this complication.

**Q:** A patient 2 hours after vaginal delivery develops sudden hemorrhagic shock with blood loss disproportionate to visible bleeding. Exam shows a smooth round mass protruding through the cervix. What is the diagnosis and immediate management?

**A:** Uterine inversion. Immediate management: Call anesthesia urgently; ABCs + IV crystalloids; DO NOT remove placenta (removal increases bleeding and makes replacement harder); use tocolytic (e.g., nitroglycerin IV) to relax uterus; manually replace uterus; THEN remove placenta; administer IV oxytocin ONLY AFTER uterus is replaced; re-explore; may require GA + laparotomy.

Key distinguishing feature: shock disproportionate to visible blood loss (vasovagal mechanism). The most critical error is removing the placenta before replacing the uterus. The cervical ring contracts around the inverted uterus and worsens with time — urgent replacement is essential.

**Q:** Which drugs are contraindicated during breastfeeding and why?

**A:** Chloramphenicol (bone marrow suppression in infant), cyclophosphamide (immune suppression), sulfonamides (hemolysis in G6PD-deficient infant), nitrofurantoin (hemolysis in G6PD deficiency), tetracycline (staining/abnormality of developing teeth), lithium (toxicity — apnea, cyanosis, hypotonia), phenindione (anticoagulant effect in infant), bromocriptine (suppresses lactation), antineoplastics/immunosuppressants, psychotropic drugs (relative contraindication).

Lithium concentrations in breast milk are 40–50% of maternal plasma levels — significant neonatal toxicity. Tetracycline causes permanent discoloration of deciduous teeth. Most antibiotics are safe. Warfarin (not listed) is actually safe in breastfeeding. This is a common MCCQE1 topic.

**Q:** A patient 4 days postpartum develops fever, foul-smelling lochia, uterine tenderness, and foul vaginal discharge. What is the diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Postpartum endometritis. Treatment: mild cases — oral antibiotics; moderate-to-severe — hospitalization with IV antibiotics (clindamycin + gentamicin/tobramycin); ensure no retained products of conception (RPOC). Risk factors: CD, intrapartum chorioamnionitis, prolonged labour, prolonged ROM, multiple vaginal exams.

Clindamycin covers anaerobes and gram-positives; gentamicin covers gram-negatives — together they provide broad spectrum coverage for the polymicrobial flora of endometritis. Failure to improve in 48–72 hours suggests RPOC, abscess, or resistant organism. RPOC can be diagnosed by ultrasound and managed with D&C.

## Gynecology Contraception

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of the copper IUD as a contraceptive?

**A:** The copper IUD causes a mild foreign body reaction in the endometrium that is toxic to sperm and alters sperm motility.

The copper IUD (Nova-T) does not release hormones; instead, copper ions create a hostile environment for sperm. It can last up to 5 years and is also used for disorders of menstruation. Unlike hormonal IUDs, it does not suppress ovulation.

**Q:** How does the progesterone-releasing IUD (Mirena/Kyleena) work?

**A:** It causes decidualization of the endometrium and thickening of cervical mucus, and may suppress ovulation.

The levonorgestrel-releasing IUD (Mirena, Kyleena) primarily acts locally on the endometrium and cervical mucus. Ovulation suppression is a variable and incomplete effect. Contraceptive effects last up to 5 years (Mirena) or 3 years (Kyleena). It is also used for disorders of menstruation.

## Gynecology Medications

**Q:** What is leuprolide (Lupron) and what are its indications?

**A:** Leuprolide is a synthetic GnRH agonist that induces a reversible hypoestrogenic state. Indications include endometriosis, leiomyomata (fibroids), dysfunctional uterine bleeding (DUB), and precocious puberty.

Despite being called a 'GnRH antagonist' in some older references, leuprolide acts as a GnRH agonist causing receptor downregulation and thus hypoestrogenism. Treatment is usually limited to 6 months due to bone density effects; retreatment with leuprolide alone is not recommended. Key side effects include hot flashes, vaginitis, and reduced bone density.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to leuprolide (Lupron)?

**A:** Contraindications: pregnancy, undiagnosed vaginal bleeding, breastfeeding.

Leuprolide induces a hypoestrogenic state and is teratogenic, making pregnancy an absolute contraindication. Retreatment after 6 months requires bone density monitoring due to risk of osteoporosis. It is dosed as 3.75 mg IM monthly or 11.25 mg IM every 3 months.

**Q:** What is menotropin (Pergonal) and what are its main side effects?

**A:** Menotropin is a human gonadotropin with FSH and LH effects used for infertility. Side effects include bloating, abdominal/pelvic pain, headache, N/V, and risk of multiple pregnancy.

Menotropin stimulates ovarian follicle development and induces ovulation. It is dosed 75-150 IU FSH/LH IM daily for 7-12 days, followed by 10,000 IU HCG 1 day after the last dose. Contraindications include primary ovarian failure, intracranial lesion, uncontrolled thyroid/adrenal dysfunction, ovarian cysts (not PCOS), pregnancy, and undiagnosed uterine bleeding.

**Q:** What are the indications, dosing, and a key drug interaction of metronidazole in gynecology?

**A:** Indications: bacterial vaginosis, trichomonas vaginitis. Dosing: 2 g PO single dose or 500 mg PO BID x 7 days. Key interaction: disulfiram-like reaction with alcohol (flushing, tachycardia, N/V).

Metronidazole is bactericidal via toxic metabolites that damage bacterial DNA. It is contraindicated in first-trimester pregnancy. Other drug interactions include cisapride, warfarin, cimetidine, lithium, amiodarone, milk thistle, and carbamazepine. The disulfiram-like reaction is a clinically important and high-yield interaction to remember.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and indication of oxybutynin (Ditropan)?

**A:** Oxybutynin is an anticholinergic that relaxes bladder smooth muscle and inhibits involuntary detrusor contractions. It is used for overactive bladder (urge incontinence).

Oxybutynin is dosed 5 or 10 mg/d PO, titrated by 5 mg weekly to a maximum of 30 mg/d. Side effects are typical anticholinergic: dry mouth/eyes, constipation, palpitations, urinary retention, dizziness. Contraindicated in narrow-angle glaucoma, GI ileus, severe colitis, and obstructive uropathy.

**Q:** What is tolterodine (Detrol) used for and what are its serious side effects?

**A:** Tolterodine is an anticholinergic used for overactive bladder (urge incontinence). Serious side effects include anaphylaxis, psychosis, and tachycardia.

Like oxybutynin, tolterodine inhibits detrusor contractions via anticholinergic mechanisms. It is dosed 1-2 mg PO BID. In contrast to oxybutynin, tolterodine carries risks of anaphylaxis and psychosis, which are less common with oxybutynin. Contraindicated in glaucoma, gastric/urinary retention.

**Q:** What is tranexamic acid (Cyklokapron), its mechanism, indication, and key contraindications in gynecology?

**A:** Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic (reversibly inhibits plasminogen activation) used for menorrhagia. Contraindicated in thromboembolic disease, acquired colour vision disturbances, subarachnoid hemorrhage, and age <15 years.

Tranexamic acid is dosed 1-1.5 g TID-QID for the first 4 days of the menstrual cycle (max 4 g/day). It reduces menstrual blood loss without hormonal effects. Ophthalmic monitoring is recommended with prolonged use. Rare cases of thrombosis have been reported.

**Q:** Why was ulipristal acetate (Fibristal) withdrawn from the Canadian market?

**A:** Fibristal was voluntarily withdrawn from the Canadian market in 2020 due to risk of drug-induced liver injury.

Ulipristal acetate is a selective progesterone receptor modulator (SPRM) that was used for preoperative treatment of leiomyoma (uterine fibroids). Despite its efficacy, post-marketing surveillance identified a risk of serious hepatotoxicity, leading to withdrawal. This is a notable Canadian regulatory event.

**Q:** What is urofollitropin (Metrodin) and what are its contraindications?

**A:** Urofollitropin is FSH used for ovulation induction in PCOS. Contraindicated in primary ovarian failure, intracranial lesion (e.g., pituitary tumour), uncontrolled thyroid/adrenal dysfunction, ovarian cysts (not PCOS), pregnancy, and abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB).

Urofollitropin is dosed 75 IU SC x 7-12 days. Side effects include ovarian enlargement/cysts, edema, arterial thromboembolism, fever, abdominal pain, headache, and multiple pregnancy. The distinction between PCOS (not a contraindication) and other ovarian cysts (a contraindication) is clinically important.

## Gynecology Oncology

**Q:** What did the PORTEC-3 trial conclude about adjuvant therapy for high-risk endometrial cancer?

**A:** For women with stage 3 or serous endometrial cancers, chemoradiotherapy should be recommended over radiotherapy alone, with improved 5-year overall survival (81.4% vs. 76.1%) and failure-free survival (76.5% vs. 69.1%).

PORTEC-3 (Lancet 2019) was a randomized phase 3 trial comparing adjuvant chemoradiotherapy vs. pelvic radiotherapy alone in high-risk endometrial cancer. Distant metastases were reduced in the chemoradiotherapy group (78/330 vs. 98/330). This trial established the standard of combined adjuvant therapy for high-risk endometrial cancer.

**Q:** What did the LACC trial demonstrate about surgical approach for early-stage cervical cancer?

**A:** Minimally invasive radical hysterectomy (laparoscopic/robotic) was associated with lower disease-free survival (86% vs. 96.5% at 4.5 years) and lower overall survival compared to open abdominal radical hysterectomy.

The LACC trial (NEJM 2018) included patients with stage IA1, IA2, or IB1 cervical cancer. Despite the appeal of minimally invasive surgery, open surgery was significantly superior in both disease-free and overall survival. This landmark trial changed practice, leading to open surgery being preferred for cervical cancer radical hysterectomy.

**Q:** What did the LION trial conclude about lymphadenectomy in advanced ovarian cancer?

**A:** Pelvic and para-aortic lymphadenectomy in patients with advanced ovarian cancer who underwent macroscopically complete resection and had clinically negative nodes did NOT improve overall survival and increased complication rates.

The LION trial (NEJM 2019) randomized patients with advanced epithelial ovarian cancer after complete resection with negative lymph nodes to lymphadenectomy vs. no lymphadenectomy. Median OS was 65.5 months vs. 69.2 months (no benefit). Repeat laparotomy was higher in the lymphadenectomy group (12.4% vs. 6.5%). This changed practice by eliminating routine lymphadenectomy in this setting.

## Obstetrics Antepartum

**Q:** What is the GTPAL format used in obstetric history?

**A:** G = Gravidity (total pregnancies including current); T = Term deliveries (>37 weeks); P = Preterm deliveries (20+0 to 36+6 weeks); A = Abortions and ectopic pregnancies (<20 weeks, both induced and spontaneous); L = Living children.

GTPAL provides a structured, standardized format for documenting obstetric history. Gravidity counts each pregnancy once (even multiple gestations). This format is used across Canadian obstetric practice (SOGC guidelines) for clear communication of pregnancy history.

**Q:** What are the physical signs of early pregnancy and at what gestational ages do they appear?

**A:** Goodell's sign (cervical softening): 4–6 weeks; Chadwick's sign (bluish cervix/vagina from venous engorgement): 6 weeks; Hegar's sign (cervical isthmus softening): 6–8 weeks. Also: uterine enlargement, breast engorgement, areola darkening.

These classical signs reflect the increased pelvic vascularity (Chadwick's) and progesterone-mediated cervical and uterine softening (Goodell's, Hegar's) of early pregnancy. Recognition on physical exam can confirm pregnancy before investigations.

**Q:** What are the β-hCG 'Rule of 10s' levels during normal pregnancy?

**A:** 10 IU at the time of missed menses; 100,000 IU peak at approximately 10 weeks GA; then levels decline but remain measurable throughout pregnancy.

β-hCG doubles every 48h in normal early pregnancy and peaks at 8–10 weeks (up to ~100,000 IU/L). Suboptimal doubling suggests ectopic pregnancy or miscarriage; elevated levels suggest molar pregnancy, multiple gestation, or trisomy 21. Important for interpreting prenatal screening.

**Q:** What does transvaginal ultrasound show at 5 weeks, 6 weeks, and 6–8 weeks gestational age?

**A:** 5 weeks: gestational sac visible; 6 weeks: fetal pole visible; 6–8 weeks: fetal heart activity visible (FHR visible after 6 weeks on TVUS). Transabdominal USS: intrauterine pregnancy visible at 6–8 weeks.

TVUS is the modality of choice for early pregnancy evaluation due to superior resolution. Failure to see expected structures at these timeframes raises concern for ectopic pregnancy, missed abortion, or inaccurate dating—all requiring further investigation.

**Q:** How is the estimated date of delivery (EDD) calculated using Naegele's rule?

**A:** Naegele's rule: Add 1 year + 7 days, then subtract 3 months from the first day of the LMP. Example: LMP = April 1, 2021 → EDD = January 8, 2022. Adjust for cycle length variations (add days if >28d cycle, subtract if <28d).

Naegele's rule assumes a regular 28-day cycle and ovulation on day 14. If LMP is uncertain or cycle is irregular, early ultrasound (ideally <12 weeks) provides more reliable dating and is used to confirm or revise the EDD in Canadian practice (SOGC guidelines).

**Q:** What are the cardiovascular changes of normal pregnancy?

**A:** Increased: cardiac output (CO), heart rate, blood volume. Decreased: blood pressure (from decreased PVR due to progesterone's vasodilation) and venous return (uterine compression of IVC). Risk of varicose veins, hemorrhoids, and leg edema from increased venous pressure.

Pregnancy creates a hyperdynamic circulation. BP typically falls in the 1st and 2nd trimesters and returns to normal in the 3rd. Understanding these changes prevents misdiagnosis of physiologic changes (e.g., systolic murmurs, supine hypotension) as pathology.

**Q:** What hematologic changes occur in normal pregnancy and what is gestational thrombocytopenia?

**A:** Hemodilution causes physiologic anemia (decreased Hgb/Hct). Leukocyte count increases but function is impaired. Hypercoagulable state (increased DVT/PE risk but decreased bleeding at delivery). Gestational thrombocytopenia: mild (platelets >70,000/μL), asymptomatic, normalizes 2–12 weeks postpartum.

Gestational thrombocytopenia (platelet counts 100,000–150,000/μL) is the most common cause of thrombocytopenia in pregnancy and is benign. Must be distinguished from ITP, HELLP syndrome, and TTP-HUS. The hypercoagulable state reflects increased clotting factors and decreased fibrinolysis.

**Q:** What respiratory changes occur in normal pregnancy?

**A:** Increased: O₂ consumption, minute ventilation (causing mild respiratory alkalosis helping CO₂ cross placenta). Decreased: TLC, FRC, RV. No change in: vital capacity, FEV₁. Elevated diaphragm. Increased nasal congestion.

The compensated respiratory alkalosis of pregnancy (PCO₂ ~30 mmHg) creates a gradient for CO₂ diffusion from fetal to maternal circulation. Decreased FRC means pregnant patients desaturate more rapidly with apnea—critical for anesthetic management and intubation.

**Q:** What GI changes occur in pregnancy and what is their mechanism?

**A:** Progesterone causes: increased gallstones (gallbladder stasis), constipation (decreased GI motility), predisposition to GERD (reduced lower esophageal sphincter tone + delayed gastric emptying + uterine compression). Hemorrhoids result from constipation and increased intra-abdominal pressure.

Progesterone's smooth muscle relaxation effects throughout the GI tract underlie most pregnancy-related GI symptoms. GERD symptoms affect up to 80% of pregnant patients. These changes also increase aspiration risk in obstetric anesthesia (Mendelson syndrome risk).

**Q:** What genitourinary changes occur in pregnancy and why does creatinine decrease?

**A:** Increased CO → increased GFR → decreased creatinine (normal in pregnancy: 35–44 mmol/L), uric acid, and BUN. Ureteral and renal pelvis dilation (R>L) from progesterone and uterine pressure. Glycosuria can be physiologic (especially T3) due to GFR increase exceeding tubular reabsorption capacity. Increased UTI/pyelonephritis risk from urinary stasis.

Creatinine normal values must be adjusted in pregnancy—a 'normal' non-pregnant creatinine of 80 mmol/L may represent significant renal impairment in pregnancy. Glycosuria in T1 or T2 should prompt diabetes testing; in T3 alone it may be physiologic.

**Q:** What endocrine changes occur in the thyroid during pregnancy?

**A:** Moderate thyroid enlargement; increased total thyroxine (T4) and thyroxine-binding globulin (TBG); normal FREE thyroxine index and TSH. Physiologic TSH suppression in T1 due to β-hCG cross-reactivity with TSH receptors.

TBG rises due to estrogen stimulation, increasing total T4 but not free T4. Physiology TSH suppression in T1 (β-hCG shares structural homology with TSH) can mimic hyperthyroidism—free T4 and clinical context are key. This is why free hormone levels and clinical symptoms guide thyroid management in pregnancy.

**Q:** What folic acid supplementation does the CTFPHC recommend for all patients capable of becoming pregnant?

**A:** 0.4 mg/day of folic acid supplementation for all patients capable of becoming pregnant (CTFPHC Grade II-2-A recommendation). Higher doses (4–5 mg/day) are recommended for high-risk patients (prior NTD, diabetes, antiepileptic drug use).

Folic acid reduces the risk of neural tube defects (NTDs) by ~70%. Supplementation must begin BEFORE conception as the neural tube closes by 4 weeks GA—often before pregnancy is confirmed. This is a key preconception counselling point in Canadian family medicine and obstetrics.

**Q:** When should the initial prenatal visit occur and what conditions warrant earlier assessment?

**A:** Initial prenatal visit should occur within 8–12 weeks of the first day of LMP. Earlier assessment is warranted for: age <20 or >35 years, vaginal bleeding, severe nausea/vomiting, or other risk factors.

Early prenatal care optimizes outcomes by establishing accurate gestational age dating, identifying medical conditions requiring modification, initiating appropriate supplementation, and providing counselling. First trimester screening (weeks 11–13+6) depends on timely presentation.

**Q:** What is the critical period of organogenesis in pregnancy and why does it matter for preconception counselling?

**A:** Weeks 3–8 GA is the critical period of organogenesis (most organ systems develop). Early preparation is vital because teratogenic exposures during this window can cause structural fetal anomalies, often before pregnancy is recognized.

Preconception counselling focuses on medication review and teratogen avoidance, folic acid initiation, and optimizing maternal health BEFORE conception, since organogenesis occurs at 3–8 weeks—when many patients are unaware they are pregnant.

**Q:** What infections should be screened for at preconception/initial prenatal visit in Canada?

**A:** Rubella, HBsAg (hepatitis B), VDRL (syphilis), Pap smear, gonorrhea/chlamydia, HIV. Additionally: TB testing based on travel/healthcare work history; varicella history or vaccination; parvovirus immunity if exposed to small children.

This comprehensive SOGC-aligned screening panel detects infections that can cause vertical transmission, congenital anomalies, or maternal morbidity. Early identification allows for interventions (immunoglobulin, antivirals, prophylaxis) to reduce fetal and neonatal harm.

**Q:** What does the placenta produce and what is its structural composition?

**A:** Composition: discoid mass of fetal tissue (chorion frondosum) and maternal tissue (decidua basalis), divided by fissures into cotyledons on the uterine side. Produces: progesterone, placental lactogen, estrogen, relaxin, β-hCG, and infant growth factors.

The placenta is both the interface for nutrient/gas exchange and a major endocrine organ. Its hormonal products maintain the corpus luteum (β-hCG), support uterine quiescence (progesterone), and regulate fetal growth. Abnormal implantation causes antepartum hemorrhage (placenta previa, abruption).

**Q:** What trimesters are defined in obstetrics and what is considered a normal term pregnancy?

**A:** T1 (first trimester): weeks 1–14; T2 (second trimester): weeks 14–28; T3 (third trimester): weeks 28–42. Normal term pregnancy: 37–42 weeks GA.

Trimester definitions help structure clinical care—first trimester screening occurs in T1, gestational diabetes screening in T2 (24–28 weeks), and Group B Streptococcus (GBS) screening in T3 (35–37 weeks). Preterm is defined as <37 weeks and post-term as >42 weeks.

**Q:** What skin changes are normal in pregnancy and what causes them?

**A:** Increased pigmentation of perineum and areola; chloasma (melasma—pigmentation under eyes/nose bridge); linea nigra (midline abdominal pigmentation). Also: proliferation of skin tags; spider angiomas; palmar erythema (estrogen); striae gravidarum (connective tissue changes).

Most skin changes reflect elevated estrogen (palmar erythema, spider angiomas) and melanocyte-stimulating hormone activity (increased pigmentation). These are physiologic and usually resolve postpartum, though striae and linea nigra may persist.

**Q:** What calcium changes occur in pregnancy and how is bone density maintained?

**A:** Decreased total maternal Ca²⁺ (due to decreased albumin). Free ionized Ca²⁺ remains normal due to decreased PTH → increased bone resorption, gut absorption, and bone turnover. Bone density is maintained by estrogen inhibition of excessive resorption.

Fetal calcium demands are met primarily through increased maternal gut absorption and decreased renal losses. Despite increased bone turnover markers, net bone density is preserved in pregnancy (unlike lactation). This is clinically relevant for vitamin D and calcium supplementation guidance.

## Ob Labour Stages

**Q:** What are the signs indicating placental separation during the third stage of labour?

**A:** 1. Gush of fresh blood
2. Umbilical cord lengthening
3. Uterine fundus becoming firm and globular
4. Uterine fundus rising upward

These four signs collectively indicate placental separation from the uterine wall. Recognition is important as the third stage (delivery of placenta) can last up to 30 minutes before intervention is indicated. Active management with oxytocin reduces PPH risk by >40%.

**Q:** What is the active management of the third stage of labour and what is its benefit?

**A:** Administration of oxytocin 10 IU IM or 5 mg IV push (or IV drip) after delivery of the anterior shoulder (or after placental delivery). Benefit: reduces risk of PPH by >40%.

Active management of the third stage of labour with oxytocin is standard of care in Canada. It can be given after delivery of the anterior shoulder in anticipation of placental delivery, or after the placenta is delivered. Routine oxytocin in the third stage significantly reduces postpartum hemorrhage risk.

**Q:** What monitoring occurs in the fourth stage of labour?

**A:** - Monitor vital signs and bleeding
- Repair lacerations
- Ensure uterus is contracted (palpate uterus, monitor uterine bleeding)
- Inspect placenta for completeness and umbilical cord for 2 arteries and 1 vein

The fourth stage of labour is the first postpartum hour, and is considered the most dangerous period for the mother along with the third stage due to risk of hemorrhage. Ensuring the uterus is contracted and placenta is complete prevents PPH. A single umbilical artery may indicate fetal anomalies.

## Ob Labour Mechanics

**Q:** List the cardinal movements of fetal delivery in order.

**A:** 1. Head floating (before engagement)
2. Engagement
3. Descent + flexion
4. Further descent + internal rotation beginning
5. Complete internal rotation
6. Extension (complete)
7. Restitution (external rotation)
8. Delivery of anterior shoulder
9. Delivery of posterior shoulder

The cardinal movements describe the sequence of fetal head and body movements as the fetus navigates the birth canal. Internal rotation brings the occiput to the pubic symphysis (OA position). Extension delivers the head under the pubic arch. Restitution/external rotation aligns the head with the shoulders for delivery.

## Ob Analgesia

**Q:** What non-pharmacologic pain relief techniques are available in labour?

**A:** - Reduction of painful stimuli: movement, position change, counter-pressure, abdominal compression
- Peripheral sensory activation: heat/cold, water immersion, touch/massage, acupuncture, TENS, intradermal sterile water over sacrum, aromatherapy
- Enhancement of descending inhibitory pathways: attention focusing, distraction, hypnosis, music, biofeedback

Non-pharmacologic techniques work through various mechanisms including gate control theory (TENS, massage), endorphin release (immersion, exercise), and cognitive/psychological modulation (hypnosis, distraction). These are important first-line options and are often used in combination with pharmacologic methods.

**Q:** What pharmacologic methods are available for pain relief in labour?

**A:** - Nitrous oxide (e.g., Entonox® — self-administered)
- Narcotics (usually combined with anti-emetic)
- Pudendal nerve block
- Perineal infiltration with local anesthetic
- Regional anesthesia: epidural block, combined spinal-epidural, spinal

Regional anesthesia (epidural) is the most effective form of labour analgesia in Canada. Nitrous oxide provides moderate analgesia with rapid onset and offset. Narcotics cross the placenta and can cause neonatal respiratory depression if given close to delivery. Pudendal nerve block is useful for perineal repair and outlet forceps.

## Ob Fetal Monitoring

**Q:** What are the indications for continuous electronic fetal heart rate monitoring (CTG) in labour?

**A:** - Abnormal auscultation
- Prolonged labour
- Induced or augmented labour
- Meconium present
- Multiple gestation or fetal complication
- Concerns about fetus tolerating labour

Intermittent auscultation with Doppler is appropriate for low-risk labours (q15-30 min in active first stage, q5 min in second stage when pushing). Continuous CTG is reserved for higher-risk situations. While CTG reduces neonatal seizures by half, it is associated with higher intervention rates (increased CD and instrumental delivery) without reducing cerebral palsy or perinatal mortality.

**Q:** What does the baseline FHR represent and what is the normal range?

**A:** Normal baseline FHR: 110-160 bpm. It is a parameter of fetal well-being vs. distress.

Baseline FHR is the average FHR during a 10-minute segment when not in a deceleration or acceleration. Fetal tachycardia (>160 bpm) and bradycardia (<110 bpm) are abnormal. Baseline FHR reflects the balance between sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous system tone and is affected by multiple maternal, fetal, and uteroplacental factors.

**Q:** How is FHR variability classified?

**A:** - Absent: undetectable amplitude
- Minimal: >0 to <6 bpm
- Moderate (normal): 6-25 bpm
- Marked: >25 bpm
Measured over a 15-minute period. Normal (moderate) variability indicates acceptable fetal acid-base status.

FHR variability reflects the integrity of the fetal autonomic nervous system and is the most important indicator of fetal well-being. Moderate variability indicates the fetus is not significantly acidotic. Absent or minimal variability can indicate fetal acidemia, CNS depression (narcotics, sedatives, fetal sleep), or CNS anomalies. Variability can only be assessed by CTG (not intermittent auscultation).

**Q:** What defines an FHR acceleration?

**A:** Increase of ≥15 bpm lasting ≥15 seconds (or ≥10 bpm for ≥10 seconds if <32 weeks GA).

Accelerations are reassuring signs of fetal well-being indicating intact fetal neurological response. Their presence generally excludes significant fetal acidemia. The lower threshold at <32 weeks GA accounts for the normally lower fetal heart rate reactivity in preterm fetuses.

**Q:** Compare the three types of FHR decelerations: cause, shape, and timing.

**A:** **Early decelerations:**
- Cause: vagal response to fetal head compression
- Shape: uniform, mirrors contraction
- Timing: nadir at peak of contraction; returns to baseline by end of contraction
- Significance: benign

**Variable decelerations:**
- Cause: cord compression (or forceful pushing in 2nd stage)
- Shape: variable in shape, onset, duration
- Timing: variable relative to contractions
- Significance: most common type; can become severe

**Late decelerations:**
- Cause: fetal hypoxia/acidemia, uteroplacental insufficiency, maternal hypotension, uterine hypertonus
- Shape: uniform, gradual onset and recovery after contraction peak
- Timing: onset, nadir, and recovery AFTER peak of contraction (30s lag)
- Significance: ominous sign of uteroplacental insufficiency

Differentiating deceleration types is critical for intrapartum management. Late decelerations are the most concerning and suggest uteroplacental insufficiency; they may also decrease variability and alter baseline FHR. Variable decelerations are the most common type and become concerning when severe (FHR drops <60 bpm for >60s, loss of variability, slow return to baseline, biphasic pattern, tachycardia/bradycardia after).

**Q:** What are the 'Rule of 60s' features suggesting severe variable decelerations?

**A:** - FHR drop <60 bpm for >60 seconds
- Loss of variability or decrease in baseline after deceleration
- Biphasic deceleration
- Slow return to baseline
- Baseline tachycardia or bradycardia
- May be associated with fetal acidemia

Severe variable decelerations are a warning sign of potential fetal acidemia. The Rule of 60s (drop <60 bpm below baseline OR to <60 bpm for >60s) helps identify high-risk variable decelerations. When present, resuscitative measures and potentially fetal scalp sampling or delivery should be considered.

**Q:** What are causes of fetal tachycardia vs. fetal bradycardia?

**A:** **Fetal tachycardia (>160 bpm):**
- Maternal: fever, hyperthyroidism, anemia, dehydration
- Fetal: arrhythmia, anemia, infection, prolonged activity, chronic hypoxemia, congenital anomalies
- Drugs: sympathomimetics, anesthetics
- Uteroplacental: early hypoxia, chorioamnionitis, hypercontractility

**Fetal bradycardia (<110 bpm):**
- Maternal: hypothermia, hypotension, hypoglycemia, positioning, umbilical cord compression
