- Fetal: rapid descent, dysrhythmia, heart block, hypoxia, vagal stimulation (head compression), hypothermia, acidosis
- Drugs: β-blockers, magnesium sulfate
- Uteroplacental: late hypoxia (abruption, HTN), acute cord prolapse

Understanding causes of FHR abnormalities allows targeted intervention. Maternal fever is a common cause of fetal tachycardia and may indicate chorioamnionitis. Acute cord prolapse causing fetal bradycardia is an obstetric emergency requiring immediate delivery. Fetal heart block is a fetal arrhythmia causing persistent bradycardia and may be associated with maternal anti-Ro/La antibodies (SLE/Sjogren's).

**Q:** What are the indications and interpretation of fetal scalp blood sampling (pH and lactate)?

**A:** **Indications:** Atypical or abnormal FHR with heavy meconium or abnormal FHR patterns (unexplained low variability, repetitive late decelerations, complex variable decelerations, fetal cardiac arrhythmias)

**Prerequisites:** Adequately dilated cervix

**Interpretation:**
- pH ≥7.25, lactate <4.2 mmol/L: Normal → repeat if abnormal FHR persists
- pH 7.21-7.24, lactate 4.2-4.8 mmol/L: Borderline → repeat in 30 min or consider delivery if rapid fall
- pH <7.20, lactate >4.8 mmol/L: Fetal acidosis → delivery indicated

**Contraindications:** Known/suspected fetal blood dyscrasia (hemophilia, VWD); active maternal infection (HIV, genital herpes, Hep B)

Fetal scalp blood sampling is used to confirm or exclude fetal acidemia when CTG shows abnormal patterns. pH <7.20 or lactate >4.8 mmol/L indicates significant fetal metabolic acidosis requiring prompt delivery. Lactate measurement is increasingly preferred as it requires smaller blood volumes and has better reproducibility.

**Q:** A patient in active labour at 6 cm develops repetitive late decelerations on CTG with baseline FHR 155 bpm and minimal variability. What is the appropriate management?

**A:** Management of abnormal CTG (late decelerations + decreased variability — Category III/ominous):

1. **Reposition patient** (left lateral decubitus — relieve aortocaval compression)
2. **Supplemental O₂** (100% by mask if abnormal SpO₂)
3. **IV fluids** (correct maternal hypotension)
4. **Stop oxytocin** (if infusing)
5. **Fetal scalp stimulation** (if accelerates → reassuring)
6. **Consider fetal scalp electrode** for more accurate FHR tracing
7. **Fetal scalp pH or lactate** (if available and cervix adequate)
8. **Notify physician**
9. **Rule out:** cord prolapse, fever, dehydration, drug effects, prematurity
10. **If above fails → consider CD**

Late decelerations with minimal variability indicate significant uteroplacental insufficiency and potential fetal acidemia. This is an ominous pattern requiring urgent intervention. The initial steps are resuscitative (repositioning, O₂, fluids, stop oxytocin). Fetal scalp pH/lactate confirms or excludes acidosis to guide delivery decision. Failure to respond to resuscitation mandates expedited delivery.

**Q:** What are the key findings of the Cochrane review on continuous CTG vs. intermittent auscultation during labour?

**A:** **Cochrane Review (2017, 13 trials, 37,000 women):**

Continuous CTG vs. intermittent auscultation:
- **No difference** in overall perinatal death rate or cerebral palsy
- **Reduced neonatal seizures** (approximately halved)
- **Significantly increased** CD rate: RR 1.63, 95% CI 1.29-2.07
- **Significantly increased** instrumental vaginal birth: RR 1.15, 95% CI 1.01-1.33

**Conclusion:** CTG may reduce neonatal seizures but has NO effect on cerebral palsy rates, infant mortality, or neonatal well-being measures, while significantly increasing intervention rates.

This evidence underpins current Canadian guidelines recommending intermittent auscultation for low-risk labours. The lack of improvement in cerebral palsy or mortality despite increased interventions suggests that intrapartum CTG has limited ability to prevent chronic neurological injury (most cerebral palsy is antepartum in etiology). Continuous CTG should be reserved for high-risk situations where benefits outweigh intervention risks.

**Q:** What are the factors causing decreased FHR variability?

**A:** - CNS anomalies
- Dysrhythmia
- Inactivity/sleep cycle (normal intermittent decrease)
- Preterm fetus
- Narcotics and sedatives
- Fetal infection
- Dehydration
- Hypoxia

FHR variability reflects the integration of multiple neurological inputs to the fetal heart via the vagus nerve. Decreased variability can be physiological (fetal sleep cycle lasting 20-40 min) or pathological (acidosis, drug effects, neurological injury). Distinguishing physiological from pathological decreased variability requires clinical context and often fetal scalp stimulation — if FHR accelerates, acidosis is excluded.

## Ob Fetal Physiology

**Q:** Describe the fetal physiologic response to hypoxia/asphyxia.

**A:** 1. Decreased fetal movement, tone, and breathing activities
2. Anaerobic metabolism → decreased pH
3. Transient fetal bradycardia → followed by fetal tachycardia
4. Redistribution of fetal blood flow:
   - Increased flow: brain, heart, adrenals
   - Decreased flow: kidneys, lungs, gut, liver, peripheral tissues
5. Increased fetal blood pressure

This 'brain-sparing' redistribution of fetal blood flow in hypoxia is a critical adaptive mechanism. The diving reflex analogy helps understand the initial bradycardia. Redistribution away from kidneys leads to oligohydramnios; away from gut leads to gut ischemia and meconium passage. These changes underpin the biophysical profile assessment of fetal well-being.

**Q:** What maternal, uteroplacental, and fetal factors affect fetal oxygenation?

**A:** **Maternal:**
- Decreased O₂ carrying capacity (anemia, carboxyhemoglobin)
- Decreased uterine blood flow (hypotension, regional anesthesia, supine positioning)
- Chronic conditions (vasculopathies: SLE, T1DM, chronic HTN; APS, cyanotic heart disease, COPD)

**Uteroplacental:**
- Uterine hypertonus (abruption, tachysystole from oxytocin/prostaglandins)
- Uteroplacental dysfunction (abruption, infarction, chorioamnionitis, DM, hydrops, post-dates)

**Fetal:**
- Cord compression (oligohydramnios, cord prolapse, entanglement)
- Decreased O₂ carrying capacity (isoimmunization, fetomaternal bleed, carboxyhemoglobin)

Multiple factors at maternal, placental, and fetal levels can compromise fetal oxygenation. Understanding these mechanisms guides appropriate monitoring and intervention. Left lateral positioning corrects supine hypotension (aortocaval compression). Tachysystole from oxytocin requires dose reduction or discontinuation.

## Ob Induction

**Q:** What is the Bishop score and how is it interpreted?

**A:** The Bishop score assesses cervical favourability for induction. Components (scored 0-3 each):
- **Position:** Posterior (0), Mid (1), Anterior (2)
- **Consistency:** Firm (0), Medium (1), Soft (2)
- **Effacement (%):** 0-30 (0), 40-50 (1), 60-70 (2), >80 (3)
- **Dilatation (cm):** 0 (0), 1 (1), 3-4 (2), ≥5 (3)
- **Station of fetal head:** -3 (0), -2 (1), -1/0 (2), +1/+2/+3 (3)

**Interpretation:**
- <3: Unfavourable → cervical ripening required
- ≥6: Favourable
- 9-13: High likelihood of vaginal delivery

The Bishop score is used to predict the likelihood of successful labour induction and need for cervical ripening. An unfavourable cervix (Bishop score <6) warrants cervical ripening before induction. The mnemonic PCED-S (Position, Consistency, Effacement, Dilatation, Station) helps recall the components.

**Q:** What are the indications for induction of labour?

**A:** **Most common:** Late term (40-41 wk) and postterm (≥42 wk GA)
**Second most common:** Diabetes mellitus

**Maternal indications:**
- DM
- Gestational HTN ≥38 wk GA
- Preeclampsia (balance severity vs. GA)
- Other maternal comorbidities (renal/lung disease, chronic HTN, cholestasis)
- Significant but stable antepartum hemorrhage
- Advanced maternal age >40 yr at ≥39 wk GA (increased stillbirth risk)

**Maternal-fetal:** Isoimmunization, PROM, chorioamnionitis

**Fetal:** Suspected fetal compromise, macrosomia, fetal demise, IUGR, oligo/polyhydramnios, anomalies requiring surgery, discordant twins, previous stillbirth or low PAPP-A

**>41 wk GA:** Offer IOL if no contraindications; IOL decreases CD, FHR changes, meconium staining, macrosomia, and death vs. expectant management

In Canada, IOL at ≥41 weeks is standard of care and is shown to reduce perinatal mortality and CD rates compared to expectant management. The ARRIVE trial demonstrated that elective IOL at 39+0 to 40+6 weeks in low-risk nulliparous women does not increase adverse perinatal outcomes and actually reduces CD rates.

**Q:** What are the prerequisites for labour induction?

**A:** **Capability:** CD capability if necessary

**Maternal:**
- Favourable cervix (soft, effaced, anterior, dilated) OR cervical ripening performed
- No contraindications to vaginal delivery

**Fetal:**
- Normal FHR tracing
- Cephalic presentation
- Adequate fetal monitoring available

**Consider the 5 Cs before induction:**
- Fetal presentation
- Potential for CPD
- Fetal well-being/FHR
- Membrane status

All prerequisites must be met before proceeding with labour induction. If the cervix is unfavourable (Bishop score <6), cervical ripening must precede induction. The ability to perform emergent CD is mandatory as IOL may result in failed induction requiring operative delivery.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to induction of labour?

**A:** **Maternal:**
- Any contraindication to vaginal delivery (prior classical/inverted T-incision CD or uterine surgery e.g. myomectomy)
- Unstable maternal condition
- Active maternal genital herpes
- Invasive cervical carcinoma
- Pelvic structure deformities
- Previous uterine rupture

**Maternal-fetal:**
- Placenta previa or vasa previa
- Cord prolapse or cord presentation

**Fetal:**
- Fetal distress (abnormal FHR tracing) or malpresentation

Contraindications to IOL are generally those that preclude vaginal delivery or would increase risk of uterine rupture. Classical uterine incisions carry a 4-9% rupture risk with labour (vs. ~0.5-0.8% for low transverse), making them a contraindication. Placenta previa risks catastrophic hemorrhage with cervical changes.

**Q:** What methods are used for cervical ripening?

**A:** **Mechanical:**
- Foley catheter placement: first-line when feasible; preferred for outpatient ripening
- Cook's catheter

**Pharmacologic:**
- Intravaginal PGE2 gel (Prostin®): long and closed cervix; 6-12h, up to 3 doses
- Intravaginal PGE2 insert (Cervidil®): continuous release; assess at 12h and 24h; can be removed if needed; advantage: oxytocin can start 30 min after removal (vs. 6h after gel)
- Intravaginal misoprostol/PGE1 (Cytotec®): long and closed cervix; inexpensive; more effective than PGE2; oxytocin can start 4h after insertion; **contraindicated in 3rd trimester with prior CD**

**Note:** Oxytocin alone is NOT effective for cervical ripening

Cervical ripening is required when Bishop score is <6. Foley catheter is preferred for outpatient management and is not associated with uterine hyperstimulation. Misoprostol (PGE1) is more effective than PGE2 for achieving vaginal delivery but is contraindicated with prior CD due to increased uterine rupture risk. The timing intervals before starting oxytocin after prostaglandins are clinically important.

**Q:** What is amniotomy and when is it used for induction?

**A:** Amniotomy = artificial rupture of membranes (AROM) to stimulate prostaglandin synthesis and secretion.

**Prerequisites:** Cervix dilated and soft, membranes can be felt, head well applied to cervix.

**Amniotomy + IV oxytocin together:** More patients delivered vaginally at 24h than amniotomy alone (RR=0.03) with fewer instrumental deliveries (RR=5.5).

**Amniotomy alone:** Limited evidence for effectiveness.

Amniotomy stimulates prostaglandin release from decidua and membranes, promoting labour. It is most effective when combined with oxytocin augmentation. Risks include cord prolapse (especially if head not engaged), chorioamnionitis (especially with prolonged rupture), and fetal heart rate changes. Ensuring the presenting part is well-applied to the cervix before amniotomy reduces cord prolapse risk.

**Q:** What are the dosing and potential complications of oxytocin for induction/augmentation?

**A:** **Dosing:** 10 U in 1 L normal saline; start at 0.5-2 mU/min IV; increase by 1-2 mU/min every 20-60 min
- Reassess at 20 mU/min; use minimum dose for active labour
- Half-life: 3-5 minutes

**Timing after prostaglandins:**
- ≥30 min after Cervidil® removal
- ≥6h after PGE2 gel insertion
- ≥4h after PGE1 (misoprostol) insertion

**Complications:**
- Tachysystole/tetanic contraction → fetal distress or uterine rupture
- Uterine muscle fatigue → uterine atony → PPH
- Vasopressin-like action → anti-diuresis (water intoxication)
- Caution with previous CD or uterine surgery

Oxytocin is a synthetic version of the natural neurohypophyseal hormone. Its short half-life (3-5 min) allows rapid titration. Tachysystole is defined as >5 contractions in 10 minutes averaged over 30 minutes and requires dose reduction or discontinuation. The vasopressin-like action can cause hyponatremia with large volumes of hypotonic solution.

**Q:** A woman at 38 weeks gestation is admitted with a Bishop score of 3. What are the appropriate cervical ripening options?

**A:** A Bishop score of 3 indicates an unfavourable cervix requiring cervical ripening before induction. Options:
1. **Foley catheter** (mechanical): first-line when feasible; preferred for outpatient management; no risk of hyperstimulation
2. **Intravaginal PGE2 gel (Prostin®):** 6-12h, up to 3 doses
3. **Intravaginal PGE2 insert (Cervidil®):** Controlled release; can be removed if hyperstimulation occurs; wait ≥30 min before starting oxytocin
4. **Misoprostol/PGE1 (Cytotec®):** More effective than PGE2; inexpensive; wait ≥4h before oxytocin; **AVOID if prior CD**

Oxytocin alone is NOT effective for cervical ripening.

A Bishop score <6 (especially <3) indicates an unfavourable cervix with low likelihood of successful induction. Cervical ripening agents work by softening, effacing, and dilating the cervix to achieve a favourable Bishop score before oxytocin induction. The choice depends on outpatient vs. inpatient management, prior uterine surgery, and available resources.

**Q:** What are the key findings of the ARRIVE trial and their implications for clinical practice?

**A:** **ARRIVE Trial (NEJM 2018):** Elective induction of labour between 39+0 and 40+6 weeks GA in low-risk nulliparous women

**Results:**
- No increase in adverse perinatal outcomes (perinatal death or severe neonatal complications: 4.3% IOL vs. 5.4% expectant management — not statistically significant)
- **Significant reduction in CD:** 18.6% (IOL) vs. 22.3% (expectant management), RR 0.84, 95% CI 0.76-0.93, p<0.001
- No significant differences in other secondary outcomes

**Implications:** Elective IOL at 39+0-41+0 weeks does NOT increase adverse perinatal outcomes and reduces CD rates → supports offering IOL to low-risk nulliparous women at 39 weeks

The ARRIVE trial challenged the traditional belief that elective induction increases CD rates. The counter-intuitive result (IOL reducing CD) may be explained by the fact that the alternative is expectant management to ≥40 weeks, during which CD risk accumulates. These findings support offering IOL at 39 weeks to low-risk nulliparous women who desire it, while maintaining that it should not be mandated.

## Ob Labour Complications

**Q:** What is dystocia and what are the 5 Ps of its etiology?

**A:** **Dystocia:** Failure of expected patterns of descent of the presenting part and cervical dilatation to occur in the appropriate time frame.

**Definition by stage:**
- Active phase: >2h of <1 cm/h dilatation
- 2nd stage: >1h with no descent during active pushing

**5 Ps:**
1. **Power** (leading cause): hypotonic or uncoordinated contractions, inadequate maternal expulsive efforts
2. **Passenger:** fetal position, attitude, size, anomalies (e.g., hydrocephalus)
3. **Passage:** pelvic structure (CPD), soft tissue factors (tumours, full bladder/rectum, vaginal septum)
4. **Psyche:** hormonal response to stress contributing to dystocia
5. **Practitioner:** understanding and expectations of normal labour progress

Dystocia is the most common indication for primary CD. Management involves ruling out CPD, then augmenting with IV oxytocin + amniotomy, optimizing fetal position, and optimizing pain control. An intrauterine pressure catheter measuring contraction adequacy (Montevideo units >200) can confirm adequate power before diagnosing arrest.

**Q:** What are the risks of dystocia?

**A:** - Maternal stress
- Maternal infection
- PPH
- Need for neonatal resuscitation
- Fetal compromise (from tachysystole)
- Uterine rupture
- Hypotension

Dystocia-associated complications arise from the prolonged labour itself and from the interventions used. Prolonged labour increases infection risk (chorioamnionitis). Tachysystole from oxytocin augmentation can cause fetal distress. Uterine atony after prolonged labour leads to PPH. These risks must be weighed against the risks of operative delivery.

**Q:** Define shoulder dystocia, its incidence, and risk factors.

**A:** **Definition:** Fetal anterior shoulder impacted above the pubic symphysis after fetal head has been delivered. Life-threatening emergency.

**Incidence:** 0.2-3% of deliveries

**Mechanism:** Breadth of shoulders greater than biparietal diameter of head

**Risk factors:**
- Maternal: obesity, DM, multiparity, previous shoulder dystocia
- Fetal: prolonged gestation, macrosomia (especially with GDM)
- Labour: prolonged 2nd stage, instrumental delivery

Shoulder dystocia is largely unpredictable; most cases occur in women without identified risk factors. Macrosomia (especially associated with GDM) is the strongest risk factor. The 'turtle sign' (head delivered then retracts against perineum) is the classic presentation. Ultrasound is unreliable for predicting macrosomia (±20% error).

**Q:** What are the maneuvers for managing shoulder dystocia (ALARMER mnemonic)?

**A:** Stepwise approach:
1. **Ask for help** (call team)
2. **Legs in full flexion** (McRoberts maneuver) — hyperflexion of maternal thighs on abdomen
3. **Anterior shoulder disimpaction** (suprapubic pressure)
4. **Rotate posterior shoulder anteriorly** — hand in vagina under adequate anesthesia
5. **Manual corkscrew** — rotate fetus by posterior shoulder until anterior shoulder emerges from behind symphysis
6. **Episiotomy** — provides space for internal maneuvers (does NOT relieve bony dystocia)
7. **Rollover** — on hands and knees position

**McRoberts + suprapubic pressure resolves ~90% of cases**

**Last resort options:**
- Cleidotomy (deliberate fracture of neonatal clavicle)
- Zavanelli maneuver (replacement of fetus + emergent CD)
- Symphysiotomy

McRoberts maneuver (maternal thigh hyperflexion) flattens the lumbar lordosis, rotates the pubic symphysis superiorly, and increases the relative AP diameter of the pelvis. Combined with downward suprapubic pressure, it resolves the majority of shoulder dystocia cases. Time is critical as cord compression can cause HIE within minutes.

**Q:** What are the fetal and maternal complications of shoulder dystocia?

**A:** **Fetal:**
- Hypoxic ischemic encephalopathy (HIE) from chest/cord compression
- Brachial plexus injury: Erb's palsy (C5-C7), Klumpke's palsy (C8-T1) — 90% partially resolve within 6 months
- Fractures: clavicle, humerus, cervical spine
- Death

**Maternal:**
- Perineal injury
- PPH (uterine atony or lacerations)
- Uterine rupture

**Prognosis:** 1% risk of long-term disability for infant

Erb's palsy (upper brachial plexus, C5-C7) is the most common brachial plexus injury, presenting with the 'waiter's tip' posture (arm adducted and internally rotated, forearm pronated). Klumpke's palsy (lower plexus, C8-T1) is rarer and affects hand intrinsics. Most brachial plexus injuries from shoulder dystocia resolve spontaneously within months.

**Q:** Define umbilical cord prolapse and outline its management.

**A:** **Definition:** Descent of the cord to a level adjacent to or below the presenting part, causing cord compression between presenting part and pelvis.

**Incidence:** 0.16-0.18% of deliveries

**Risk factors:** Prematurity/PROM, fetal malpresentation (~50%), low-lying placenta, polyhydramnios, multiple gestation, CPD

**Presentation:** Visible/palpable cord; FHR changes (variable decelerations, bradycardia)

**Management:**
1. Emergency CD if not fully dilated and delivery not imminent
2. O₂ to mother, monitor FHR
3. Keep cord warm and moist (replace into vagina + warm saline soaks)
4. Relieve cord compression:
   - Manually elevate fetal head (pelvic exam)
   - Steep Trendelenburg or knee-to-chest position
   - Retrofill bladder with 500-700 mL normal saline
5. If fetal demise or <22 wk GA: allow labour and delivery

Cord prolapse is an obstetric emergency with high perinatal mortality if not promptly managed. The bladder retrofilling technique (500-700 mL NS) pushes the presenting part upward, relieving cord compression while preparing for CD. The Trendelenburg/knee-chest position uses gravity to reduce pressure on the cord. Time to delivery is critical.

**Q:** What is uterine rupture: definition, etiology, risk factors, and presentation?

**A:** **Definition:** Complete separation of all layers of the uterine wall

**Incidence:** 0.5-0.8% overall; up to 12% with classical incision

**Etiology:**
- Previous uterine scar (40% of cases)
- Tachysystole with oxytocin
- Grand multiparity
- Previous intrauterine manipulation

**Risk factors:** Uterine scarring (prior CD especially classical incision, D&C perforation, myomectomy), excessive stimulation (oxytocin, prostaglandins), trauma (ECV), multiparity, uterine abnormalities, malpresentation, placenta accreta

**Presentation:**
- Prolonged fetal bradycardia (most common)
- Acute constant lower abdominal pain (may be absent with epidural)
- Hyper/hypotonic contractions
- Vaginal bleeding
- Intra-abdominal hemorrhage
- Loss of station of presenting part
- Maternal tachycardia, hypotension, shock

Uterine rupture is a catastrophic obstetric emergency. The classical CD scar has a 4-9% rupture risk (typically before labour or early in labour), while low transverse scar has 0.5-0.8% risk. Loss of fetal station and sudden cessation of contractions with fetal bradycardia should raise immediate suspicion. Treatment requires immediate delivery and maternal stabilization, potentially including hysterectomy.

**Q:** What are the treatment and complications of uterine rupture?

**A:** **Treatment:**
- Rule out other causes (e.g., placental abruption)
- Maternal stabilization (may require hysterectomy)
- Treat hypovolemia
- Immediate delivery for fetal survival

**Complications:**
- Maternal mortality: 1-10%
- Maternal: hemorrhage, shock, DIC, amniotic fluid embolus, hysterectomy
- Fetal: distress, infant mortality up to 15%

Uterine rupture is among the most dangerous obstetric emergencies. Maternal mortality 1-10% reflects the severity of hemorrhagic shock and coagulopathy. Hysterectomy may be necessary for hemorrhage control. Neonatal outcome depends heavily on time to delivery — immediate cesarean delivery after recognition is critical for fetal survival.

**Q:** What is amniotic fluid embolism (AFE): definition, epidemiology, presentation, and management?

**A:** **Definition:** Amniotic fluid debris in maternal circulation triggering an anaphylactoid immunologic response

**Epidemiology:**
- Rare: 1 in 8,000-80,000 births
- 13-30% maternal mortality rate
- Leading cause of maternal death in induced abortions and miscarriages

**Risk factors:** Placental abruption, rapid labour, multiparity, uterine rupture, uterine manipulation, induction medications/procedures

**Presentation:**
- Sudden onset: respiratory distress + cardiovascular collapse (hypotension, hypoxia) + coagulopathy
- Seizure in 10%
- Survivors: ARDS and left ventricular dysfunction
- Aura in up to 1/3 of patients

**Management:**
- ICU with multidisciplinary team
- Supportive: high-flow O₂, ventilation support, fluid resuscitation, inotropic support ± intubation
- Correct coagulopathy

AFE is an unpredictable catastrophic event. The classic triad of sudden hypoxia, hypotension, and coagulopathy in a peripartum patient should immediately raise suspicion. DIC is a major component, requiring aggressive replacement of clotting factors. The DDx includes pulmonary embolus, anaphylaxis, septic shock, eclampsia, and HELLP syndrome. AFE is a clinical diagnosis of exclusion.

**Q:** Define chorioamnionitis, its etiology, and clinical features.

**A:** **Definition:** Infection of the chorion, amnion, and amniotic fluid

**Incidence:** 1-5% of term pregnancies; up to 25% in preterm deliveries

**Mechanism:** Ascending infection from vagina

**Organisms:** GBS, Bacteroides and Prevotella species, E. coli, anaerobic Streptococcus

**Risk factors:** Low parity, prolonged ROM, long labour, multiple vaginal exams, internal monitoring, bacterial vaginosis

**Clinical features:**
- Diagnostic: maternal fever (single ≥39°C OR persistent ≥38°C for >30 min) + fetal tachycardia + foul/purulent cervical discharge
- Non-specific: maternal tachycardia, uterine tenderness

**Investigations:** CBC (leukocytosis, elevated lactate); amniotic fluid (Gram stain, glucose, culture)

Chorioamnionitis is a clinical diagnosis based on maternal fever plus at least one other sign. It is the most common serious complication of PROM. GBS is particularly important as it causes neonatal sepsis and meningitis. The diagnosis should prompt antibiotic treatment and appropriate labour management (not necessarily immediate CD).

**Q:** What is the treatment of chorioamnionitis and what complications can occur?

**A:** **Treatment:**
- IV antibiotics:
  - Ampicillin 2 g IV q6h + gentamicin 2 mg/kg load, then 1.5 mg/kg IV q8h
  - Add anaerobic coverage: clindamycin 900 mg IV q8h
  - Continue postpartum (especially if CD) to reduce endometritis risk
- Antipyretics
- Allow appropriate labour progression (NOT an indication for immediate CD)

**Complications:**
- Bacteremia (mother or fetus)
- Wound infection (if CD)
- Pelvic abscess
- Neonatal meningitis
- Maternal or neonatal sepsis
- Neonatal death
- **Long-term infant:** cerebral palsy, bronchopulmonary dysplasia

Chorioamnionitis requires prompt IV antibiotics but does not mandate immediate operative delivery unless obstetric indications exist. Antibiotics reduce maternal and neonatal morbidity. The ampicillin + gentamicin combination provides broad coverage; clindamycin adds anaerobic coverage especially important for CD. Neonatal risks include early-onset sepsis, pneumonia, and long-term neurodevelopmental sequelae.

**Q:** What is meconium in the context of labour and what does it signify?

**A:** **Definition:** Substance forming the first feces of the developing fetus

**Epidemiology:** Present in ~10% early in labour; up to 25% of all labours; more common in postterm pregnancies

**Clinical significance:**
- Usually NOT associated with poor outcome
- **Particulate (thickened) meconium:** associated with lower APGARs, meconium aspiration syndrome, neonatal respiratory distress, perinatal death; darker green or black colour
- **Thin meconium:** yellow to light green; less concerning
- **Always abnormal** if seen in preterm fetus
- May indicate: cord compression, uterine hypertonia, or undiagnosed breech
- Increasing meconium during labour may indicate fetal distress

**Management:** Call respiratory therapy/neonatology/pediatrics to delivery room; closely monitor FHR

Meconium passage results from increased vagal tone causing intestinal peristalsis. Particulate (thick) meconium is more concerning than thin meconium because it is more likely to cause meconium aspiration syndrome (MAS). The presence of meconium is an indication for continuous electronic FHR monitoring. Meconium in a preterm fetus is always pathological as the preterm fetal gut normally does not pass meconium.

**Q:** What are the immediate management steps when a 'turtle sign' is observed during delivery?

**A:** The 'turtle sign' (head delivers then retracts against perineum) indicates shoulder dystocia. Immediately:

1. **Call for help** (additional obstetric, anesthetic, neonatal team)
2. **McRoberts maneuver** — hyperflexion of maternal thighs onto abdomen
3. **Suprapubic pressure** — downward pressure on anterior shoulder (NOT fundal pressure)
4. If not resolved: **episiotomy** + internal maneuvers (rotation of posterior shoulder anteriorly, manual corkscrew)
5. **Rollover** (hands and knees position)
6. **Last resort:** cleidotomy, Zavanelli maneuver + emergency CD, symphysiotomy

**McRoberts + suprapubic pressure resolves ~90% of cases**

Document: time of head delivery, maneuvers used, duration, neonatal outcomes

Shoulder dystocia is a time-critical emergency — progressive hypoxia from cord compression develops rapidly. Fundal pressure is CONTRAINDICATED as it worsens impaction of the anterior shoulder. The systematic ALARMER approach ensures no step is missed. After delivery, careful neonatal assessment for brachial plexus injury and fractures is mandatory, and thorough documentation is essential.

## Ob Maternal Outcomes

**Q:** What are the leading causes of maternal mortality in Canada?

**A:** 1. Thromboembolism
2. Cardiac event
3. Suicide
4. Sepsis
5. Ectopic pregnancy
6. Hypertension (HTN)
7. Amniotic fluid embolism
8. Hemorrhage

*In Canada (2013), lifetime risk of maternal death is 1/5,200*

Understanding causes of maternal mortality is important for preventive strategies. Thromboembolism is the leading cause, highlighting the importance of VTE prophylaxis. Suicide/mental health is a significant cause, emphasizing perinatal mental health screening. Hemorrhage (PPH) is a major but largely preventable cause through active management of the third stage.

## Ob Operative Delivery

**Q:** What are the prerequisites (ABCDEFGHIJK) for operative vaginal delivery?

**A:** - **A:** Anesthesia (adequate)
- **B:** Bladder empty
- **C:** Cervix fully dilated and effaced with ROM
- **D:** Determine position of fetal head
- **E:** Equipment ready (including facilities for emergent CD)
- **F:** Fontanelle (posterior fontanelle midway between thighs)
- **G:** Gentle traction
- **H:** Handle elevated
- **I:** Incision (episiotomy)
- **J:** Jaw visible, remove forceps
- **K:** Knowledgeable operator

Operative vaginal delivery (forceps or vacuum) requires strict adherence to prerequisites to minimize maternal and fetal complications. An empty bladder prevents bladder injury. Knowing the exact fetal head position is mandatory before applying forceps. The requirement for CD capability is absolute — if operative delivery fails, emergency CD must be immediately available.

**Q:** What are the indications and contraindications for operative vaginal delivery?

**A:** **Indications:**
- Fetal: atypical/abnormal FHR tracing, evidence of fetal compromise, prolonged second stage
- Maternal: need to avoid voluntary expulsive effort (cardiac/cerebrovascular disease), exhaustion, lack of cooperation, excessive analgesia

**Contraindications:**
- Cervix not fully dilated
- Membranes intact
- Unknown fetal head position
- Unengaged head
- Fetal bone demineralization disorder (osteogenesis imperfecta)
- Fetal bleeding disorder (hemophilia, VWD)
- Brow or face presentation

Operative vaginal delivery is indicated when benefits outweigh risks and prerequisites are met. Fetal bleeding disorders are contraindications because the compression forces from forceps or vacuum can cause intracranial hemorrhage. Face presentation can be delivered with forceps in mento-anterior position but brow presentation cannot. An unengaged head with an operative attempt risks severe trauma and is absolutely contraindicated.

**Q:** Classify forceps deliveries by station and describe the types of forceps.

**A:** **Classification by station:**
- **Outlet forceps:** Head visible between labia between contractions; sagittal suture in/close to AP diameter; rotation ≤45°
- **Low forceps:** Presenting part at station ≥+2 cm; subdivided by rotation (<45° or >45°)
- **Mid forceps:** Presenting part below spines but above +2 cm

**Types of forceps:**
- **Simpson or Tucker-McLane:** OA presentations
- **Kielland (rotational) forceps:** Rotation of head or correction of asynclitism
- **Piper forceps:** After-coming head in breech delivery
- **Wrigley's:** Preterm babies

Forceps classification by station has important medicolegal implications — mid-cavity forceps carry higher maternal and fetal complication rates. The choice of forceps depends on indication: Simpson forceps are most commonly used, Kielland forceps for rotational delivery, Piper forceps for the head in breech delivery (rotated to bring the posterior fossa into view).

**Q:** Compare forceps vs. vacuum extraction: advantages, disadvantages, and complications.

**A:** **Forceps:**
- Advantages: Higher overall success rate for vaginal delivery
- Disadvantages: Greater incidence of maternal injury; requires more anesthesia
- Complications: Maternal — cervical/vaginal/perineal lacerations (including OASIS), bladder/uterus/bone injury, pelvic nerve damage, PPH, infections; Fetal — fractures, facial nerve palsy, face/scalp trauma, intracranial hemorrhage, cephalohematoma, cord compression

**Vacuum extraction:**
- Advantages: Easier to apply; less anesthesia required; less maternal soft tissue injury than forceps
- Disadvantages: Suitable only for vertex presentations; contraindicated <34 weeks GA
- Complications: Maternal — perineal lacerations/OASIS, PPH, infection; Fetal — cephalohematoma, retinal hemorrhages, jaundice (increased vs. forceps), subgaleal hemorrhage, spine injuries, soft tissue trauma
- Limits: Stop after 3 pulls over 3 contractions with no progress; 3 pop-offs; or 20 min if delivery not imminent

The choice between forceps and vacuum depends on clinical situation, GA, and operator expertise. Vacuum is contraindicated <34 weeks due to higher risk of subgaleal (subaponeurotic) hemorrhage in preterm neonates. Subgaleal hemorrhage is a serious complication as the potential space can accommodate significant blood volume. Vacuum has higher neonatal jaundice rates due to increased red blood cell breakdown from cephalohematoma.

**Q:** Classify perineal lacerations and describe their management.

**A:** **Classification:**
- **1st degree:** Involves skin and vaginal mucosa only (not underlying fascia/muscle)
- **2nd degree:** Involves fascia and muscles of perineal body but not anal sphincter
- **3rd degree:** Involves the anal sphincter:
  - 3A: <50% of external anal sphincter
  - 3B: >50% of external anal sphincter
  - 3C: External AND internal anal sphincters
- **4th degree:** Extends through entire anal sphincter complex into rectal mucosa

**3rd and 4th degree (OASIS) management:**
- Single prophylactic dose of IV antibiotics (2nd-generation cephalosporin, e.g., cefoxitin or cefotetan) OR cefazolin + metronidazole
- Laxatives; avoid constipation
- Postpartum pelvic physiotherapy
- Endoanal U/S at 3-4 months post-repair to assess anal sphincter integrity

OASIS (Obstetric Anal Sphincter Injuries) include 3rd and 4th degree tears. Risk factors include vacuum delivery (highest odds, OR 4.23), prolonged second stage, forceps, VBAC, and episiotomy. Failure to identify and properly repair OASIS leads to fecal incontinence. Prophylactic antibiotics reduce post-repair wound complications and infection.
